Stress-Testing the Reasoning Competence of LLMs With Proofs Under Minimal Formalism (Extended Technical Report ) Thanks: This article is an extended version of the 2025 EMNLP Findings paper Stress-Testing the Reasoning Competence of LLMs With Formal Proofs . It contains substantial new material beyond the conference version, including new tasks, results, methods, and analyses.
Konstantine Arkoudas, Serafim Batzoglou

Abstract
We introduce ProofGrid , a challenging benchmark suite for evaluating the reasoning competence of language models through machine-checkable proofs rather than final answers alone. ProofGrid spans 15 tasks organized around proof writing, proof checking, proof masking, and proof gap-filling. The tasks are expressed in deliberately minimal formal notation, most notably NDL, a stripped-down language for natural deduction that fits in a short prompt and supports precise, auditable checking. As a result, correctness judgments are mechanical, reproducible, and fine-grained rather than dependent on human or LLM judges. ProofGrid discriminates model capabilities with high resolution and covers a carefully calibrated difficulty spectrum, from foundational reasoning tests to structurally rich challenge tasks that no model currently solves, while avoiding reliance on domain knowledge, problems that can be easily outsourced to solvers, or long-context artifacts. The paper develops a detailed comparative framework for reasoning benchmarks and uses it to survey and systematize a broad and previously fragmented literature. This analysis clarifies how ProofGrid differs from existing benchmarks not only in difficulty, but in representational choices, verification guarantees, reasoning depth, and vulnerability to shortcut-based success. A key methodological contribution of the paper is an instrumented proof-checking pipeline that tolerates minor surface-level deviations while explicitly identifying the first substantive failure in a stretch of reasoning. This significantly sharpens measurement resolution, separating high-level proof planning from low-level execution noise, and reveals that current frontier models often possess surprisingly strong proof-strategy capabilities even when their formal proof execution is brittle. Using this pipeline, we conduct an extensive evaluation and analysis of a broad set of open and proprietary models released from late 2024 until now. Our results demonstrate strikingly rapid advances as well as sharp remaining limitations. Frontier systems now perform strongly on several foundational tasks, yet difficult ProofGrid tasks (especially those requiring global combinatorial reasoning or low-level proof synthesis) remain far from solved. Beyond task-level accuracy, we identify widespread epistemic instability, e.g. , models often generate proofs containing local logical errors that they can nevertheless recognize and reject when those same inferences are presented in isolation. We formalize this phenomenon via an Epistemic Stability Index (ESI), a quantitative measure of cross-context coherence between model outputs for different but related tasks (such as generated proofs and corresponding entailment judgments). Finally, we complement raw accuracies with 2PL Item Response Theory (IRT) analyses, Wright maps, and a new normalized measure of task discrimination based on Fisher information and latent model abilities estimated from their responses. Taken together, these results position ProofGrid not merely as a reasoning benchmark, but as a diagnostic framework for studying the emerging structure, limits, and internal coherence of reasoning in contemporary language models.



1
Introduction

Large language models (LLMs) have advanced rapidly, finding applications in increasingly complex
problems well beyond their original scope of narrow NLP tasks like summarization, information extraction,
and composition of short texts like emails.
The recent emergence of the inference-compute paradigm
[
80
]
has given rise to so-called

large reasoning models
(LRMs), such as OpenAI’s o-series of models along with GPT-5 and its successors;
DeepSeek’s R1 and related offerings; Anthropic’s Claude Sonnet and Opus lines; and Google’s Gemini family.
These models have been trained with reinforcement learning (RL) and can perform test-time search
to identify promising completions that are more likely to yield correct answers, at the expense
of higher inference costs. Powered by test-time scaling and RL, language models have demonstrated
dramatic improvements in logical and mathematical reasoning, with some of them surpassing human performance
on highly challenging benchmarks, such as FrontierMath
[
34
]
.

However, most existing test batteries for logical reasoning are focused only
on the accuracy of the answers, which are typically either binary (in response to yes/no questions);
or answer keys to multiple-choice questions; or, increasingly, numbers
or other
\boxed{
\cdots
}
literals,
such as closed-form expressions.
While this approach enables automatic evaluation by simply comparing model answers against canonical solutions,
it has a serious drawback: it ignores the reasoning that gives rise to the answers. This can inflate
performance, because models sometimes arrive at the right answers for the wrong reasons, through plausible but
ultimately spurious inferences. More fundamentally, this evaluation mode is particularly unsuitable for
mathematics, where proofs are not just a means to an end but the very foundation of the discipline.
A mathematical claim is accepted not because it seems likely or even because it happens to be
correct in some instances, but because a rigorous and logically sound proof
demonstrating its truth is given.

Therefore, emphasizing answer accuracy while overlooking the validity of the underlying reasoning
is fundamentally misaligned with mathematical practice, which typically rejects a result
that is derived by faulty reasoning, even if it is numerically correct.
To properly assess the reasoning abilities of language models, especially in mathematics,
it is essential to evaluate whether they can construct and verify proofs.
ProofGrid
is a step in that direction.

ProofGrid
is a benchmark suite for evaluating proof-based reasoning, requiring models not merely
to produce correct answers but to construct, verify, and repair proofs expressed in a minimalist
natural-deduction language (NDL) for propositional logic, and also in a pared-down formulation
of pure equational logic. The benchmark comprises multiple tasks (proof writing, proof checking,
proof masking, and proof gap-filling), designed to probe complementary aspects of reasoning,
from generation of complex inference chains to error localization and structural reconstruction.
In this paper we present and discuss all tasks in detail, evaluate 24 well-known models (both closed- and open-source)
on every task, present and discuss the results, and perform extensive error analysis.

All tasks in
ProofGrid
are formulated in uninterpreted formal logic. As a result,
the problems are entirely abstract and thus require what psychologists call
fluid intelligence
, as opposed to

crystallized
intelligence
[
84
]
. Fluid intelligence refers to innate reasoning ability employed
to solve novel problems, while crystallized intelligence reflects the declarative knowledge and
problem-solving know-how that has accumulated through explicit instruction.
Most current benchmarks allow models to leverage their vast stores of crystallized
knowledge, acquired through their pretraining,
which often enables them to essentially retrieve correct answers,
either through brute memorization or by employing shortcuts that are sometimes
valid but are other times derived from superficial patterns.

There is nothing wrong with shortcuts, of course,
especially when they are based on sound theory. On the contrary, they are invaluable in practice.
But when it comes to assessing native reasoning ability, they can act as confounders. If we ask a model whether a graph
can be colored with 2 colors, it can answer immediately if it notices an odd-length cycle
and it happens to know that a graph is 2-colorable iff it contains no odd-length cycles.
By minimizing reliance on memorized content, symbolic-logic problems are much
better gauges of pure reasoning competence.

To make the ramifications of this concrete with an example: o4-mini was touted as achieving 99.5% pass
@1

on the 2025 AIME exam
,
slightly beating o3. Even more impressively,
o4-mini-medium
scored
\approx 22\%

on the competition subset of FrontierMath,

outperforming the average team of human mathematicians

(
\approx 19\%
).
On
ProofGrid
, by contrast, it consistently places in the bottom half of the 24 models we have evaluated,
frequently scoring less than half of the absolute score of o3,
1
1

1

For instance,
in the
PL3-PW
division of
ProofGrid
, the accuracy of o4-mini is 6% compared to o3’s 22%.

and well below open-source models
like DeepSeek R1 or even DeepSeek 3.1. This performance profile is more consistent
with the experiences of users who report that impressive benchmark numbers
often fail to translate to strong real-world performance on reasoning-intensive tasks,
especially those that gain little from memorized knowledge.
An advantage of abstract reasoning problems like those of
ProofGrid
is that they can tease apart stronger from weaker models much more reliably.
To quantify this with precision, we introduce a simple notion of spread we call DI
(discriminability index), which we compute and report for every task in
ProofGrid
.
We also draw on IRT and statistical information theory to introduce a
new normalized metric of task discrimination on select bands of latent
model ability.

In addition, many existing benchmarks have become ceiling-saturated and no longer
pose a serious challenge.
ProofGrid
features demanding tasks that reveal the limitations of even
the most capable models. We hope this will help to drive continued advances
in the intersection of reasoning and language models.

Beyond the issue of ceiling effects, with leading scores essentially at 100%, many existing benchmarks
cluster together even mid- and third-tier models. On MATH 500, for example,
GPT-4.1
scores 87.6%
, while Gemini 2.5 Pro and DeepSeek 3.1
receive 91% and 91.5%, respectively. This amounts to a spread of 3.9 points between the strongest and weakest of these three models,
and a spread of only 0.5% between the top two of the three. By comparison, the corresponding scores from a randomly selected
ProofGrid
task,
PL1-PW
, are 35% for GPT-4.1, 49% for DeepSeek 3.1, and 94% for Gemini 2.5 Pro, yielding
a 59-point separation between first and last and a 45-point gap between the top two.

In this work we focus on reasoning tasks that demand careful and deep conceptual engagement rather than mere procedural manipulation,
and cannot be straightforwardly reduced to off-the-shelf algorithms, whether implemented by external systems
or coded by the model itself. This rules out puzzles like Sudoku, Towers of Hanoi, or string
manipulation problems, which admit simple algorithmic solutions, and even tasks like propositional satisfiability,
which can be readily outsourced to SAT solvers. By contrast, there is no standard algorithm for writing proofs
in nontrivial proof systems, and while some of our equational problems could be approached with heuristics like beam search,
it is not realistic for a model to engineer and execute a complete—and efficient—proof system for such problems on the fly.
Even proof checking, though easily solvable by algorithm in principle, would require the one-shot design, construction,
and debugging of a full software pipeline. Building such a system de novo during a reasoning task is infeasible.
As a result, our benchmark compels models to perform genuine logical reasoning instead of
relying on canned procedures.
Moreover, by centering on proof writing, our tasks more closely reflect the kinds of reasoning
challenges that arise in mathematics, science, and engineering, where the construction and justification of arguments
is central. This makes
ProofGrid
not only more demanding, but also more ecologically valid as a test
of the reasoning abilities we ultimately want models to develop.

Some related work has probed reasoning limits using very large problems or solutions,
whose size might approach or exceed model context windows (
e.g.
, problems where the output
length grows exponentially with input).
With problems of such large sizes, failure is rarely
surprising and not very diagnostic, as it is hard to disentangle genuine reasoning shortcomings from token-budget limits,
truncation artifacts, or decoding constraints. Moreover, solutions to such problems (e.g., towers of Hanoi)
tend to adhere to a fixed recursive pattern and thus stress-test symbolic bookkeeping and bandwidth
more than they stress-test reasoning.
By contrast, both the inputs and the expected outputs of
ProofGrid
problems
are of relatively small or modest sizes and fit easily within standard context limits,
typically occupying only a few pages of text. Consequently,
model failures cannot be attributed to context truncation or insufficient output budget, but instead
reflect an inability to sustain long-horizon symbolic reasoning. Models must discover invariants,
manage global constraints, select relevant premises under noise, reconstruct hidden combinatorial
structure, and intelligently compose deep and coherent chains of inference,
not simply manage context length and repetition.

In particular, our main contributions in this paper are the following:

•

We introduce
ProofGrid
, a rigorous reasoning benchmark that does not equate success with simple answer accuracy but instead requires
models to justify and explain their deliberations by producing machine-checkable proofs. The proofs are written in extraordinarily
simple formal proof languages (or, as in the case of equational logic, no formal language at all other than simple equations).
The benchmark blends:

(a)

accessible tasks serving as foundational or calibration tests, intended to gauge minimal proof-backed reasoning competence;

(b)

more difficult tasks that are not yet quite solved by the top 2-3 models but are likely to be relatively soon;

(c)

frontier challenges that are markedly more difficult than the rest, leaving more headroom for meaningful progress.

ProofGrid
adheres to the following design principles:

1.

With the exception of the frontier challenges, all tasks, including the easier/foundational ones,
have sharp disciminating power at the time of writing.

This principle relates directly to the issue of benchmark saturation. There are two different notions of saturation, and
they are often conflated in the community:
ceiling saturation
occurs when the strongest 2-3 models start scoring
around 95% or higher. By contrast,
general
or
discriminative
saturation occurs when
most
models,
not just the elite, start scoring above 90%. These are very different conditions. It is discriminative saturation that renders
a benchmark obsolete, because it can no longer separate different levels of ability.
But as long as a task effectively separates respondents of low, medium, and high ability, there is nothing objectionable
about the top few models reaching 90% or higher.
2
2

2

A school exam that gives As to most students would provide
very little signal about true ability and would not be useful. But as long as an exam successfully
separates students of different skill levels, it should not be dismissed just because a few students ace it. (Indeed,
the top students are usually expected to earn As, otherwise the exam might be too difficult to function as a broad
test of competence.)

2.

Solutions do not rely on content or crystallized mathematical knowledge.

3.

Tasks are not readily solvable by code, either by external off-the-shelf solvers or by the models themselves.

4.

Tasks do not use excessively long inputs or require excessively long outputs.

5.

Tasks do not move up the difficulty ladder by introducing adversarial tricks or obscure rules, or by exploiting model quirks.

•

We use the benchmark to measure and analyze the reasoning performance of a wide
selection of well-known large models spanning releases from late 2024 until now. Errors in every task are grouped
into categories and detailed error statistics per category are given for every model.

•

We quantify each task’s discriminative power using both a simple measure we
introduce based on Gini mean difference, the discriminative index (DI); as well as a new
normalized score based on the Fisher information of task items and latent model abilities
estimated from task results, using IRT (item response theory).

•

We introduce novel proof-manipulation tasks (proof masking and proof gap-filling)
that evaluate a model’s ability to reason explicitly
about
proofs, reconstruct missing
inference steps, and manipulate proof structure. This extends evaluation beyond proof generation
and checking to meta-level structural understanding: do models grasp the internal structure
of a reasoning artifact well enough to manipulate it successfully?

•

Through extensive output analysis, we uncover and document a range
of widespread behaviors, including self-contradictory explanations, shifting
notions of reasoning correctness, verdict reversals mid-output, and other failure modes.

•

We develop a structural framework for comparing reasoning benchmarks along orthogonal axes
(input form, output structure, logical expressiveness, inferential depth, and task diversity),
and use it to situate
ProofGrid
within the broader reasoning-evaluation landscape.

•

We develop an instrumented proof checker that tolerates minor surface-level
deviations while explicitly identifying the first substantive error in a proof attempt. This allows
us to disentangle failures of procedural control from failures of higher-level reasoning, enabling
fairer evaluation and substantially richer error analysis than is possible with standard pass/fail checking.

•

We report formal calibration results on the task of equational gap-filling,
quantifying how well models’ self-reported confidence aligns with empirical correctness.
Using bin-level calibration gaps and expected calibration error (ECE), we find pervasive
miscalibration across many models (with especially pronounced overconfidence),
making naive confidence-thresholding unreliable. The resulting calibration benchmarks
establish a foundation for calibration-aware training and evaluation on proof-manipulation tasks.
This separates raw competence from epistemic reliability and yields a quantitative yardstick
for progress on calibrated reasoning

•

Beyond qualitative analysis and cataloging of reasoning pathologies,
we investigate questions of reasoning
coherence across different tasks
,
an analysis that is enabled by the presence of multiple related problems in the benchmark.
We give a rigorous formalization of a notion that we call
epistemic stability

and provide the first quantitative results capturing the coherence of a model’s reasoning
across epistemic contexts, by quantifying the degree of instability between different sets
of judgments (such as generated proofs and independent entailment judgments).
We show empirically that even top-tier models suffer from high epistemic instability,
even when their performance on a task may be high in isolation.

The rest of this paper is structured as follows. Section
2
discusses and organizes related work.
Section
3
introduces the
\textsc{PL}_{1}
division of
ProofGrid
, simple propositional logic, which includes
the following tasks (note: tasks whose names end in
-c
are frontier challenge tasks):

1.

PL1-PW
(proof writing, discussed in Section
3.1
);

2.

PL1-PC
and
PL1-PC-c
(proof checking, Section
3.2
);

3.

PL1-PM
and
PL1-PM-c
(proof masking, Section
4
);

4.

PL1-GF
and
PL1-GF-c
(proof gap-filling, Section
5
).

Section
6
introduces the
\textsc{PL}_{2}
division, with only one task,
PL2-PW
.
Section
7
describes the
\textsc{PL}_{3}
division, with two tasks,
PL3-PW
and
PL3-PC
.
Section
9
introduces
\textsc{PL}_{4}
, comprising two tasks
PL4-PW
and
PL4-PW-c
.
Section
9.3
presents the equational-reasoning tasks (
EQ-PC
,
EQ-ER
, and
EQ-GF
)
and corresponding results. Section
8
formalizes a notion of epistemic stability
and presents related results, while Section
10
concludes. Appendix
A
defines NDL, a natural-deduction
proof language widely used in
ProofGrid
, Appendix
B
discusses IRT and its application to
ProofGrid
, while
Appendix
C
specifies various technical/operational details.

2
Related Work

Reasoning has emerged as a core yardstick for assessing the performance of language models. However, there are
many types of reasoning and many different research directions exploring the intersection of reasoning with
AI models. In this section we aim to clarify this complicated and fragmented landscape, and explain where our own work fits
into the broader picture. While we try to be comprehensive, our primary focus in this paper is deductive
reasoning—the bedrock of mathematics. Although copious and significant research exists on other forms of reasoning also
(induction, causality, and abduction, to name a few), deduction has historically dominated the literature on AI
and reasoning.

Because of the central role that deductive reasoning has played in the field, and its
prominence in
ProofGrid
, it will be helpful to clarify an aspect of it that is sometimes misunderstood.
Deduction is often said to be a
non-ampliative
species of reasoning,
meaning that there is no information in the conclusion that is not, in some sense, already contained in the premises.
This supposedly contrasts with other types of reasoning, such as induction, where the conclusion conveys
information beyond what is found in the premises.
But this is a somewhat crude distinction that can be misleading,
even though it is common both in and out of the AI community (
e.g.
, it is
embraced in the HELM framework’s discussion of reasoning evaluation
[
53
, p. 38]
).
It is true, of course, that every interpretation which satisfies
the premises of a valid deductive argument also satisfies the conclusion, and in that strict model-theoretic sense,
the conclusion cannot say anything that is not already forced by the premises.
So the claim that deduction is not ampliative is true enough in the narrow technical sense
of truth-conditional semantics. But it breaks down in several important respects,
particularly when examined from epistemic, computational, or representational perspectives.

Epistemically and cognitively, deduction routinely yields vast amounts of information,
particularly in the mathematical sciences, revealing structure, compressing information,
providing explanations and warrants, and connecting distant concepts.
It would be untenable to say that Euclid’s theorems
don’t provide any information that is not already
contained in his postulates, or that Fermat’s last theorem doesn’t tell us anything
that is not already in the axioms of ZFC. Before the proof by Andrew Wiles was published in 1995,
we didn’t even know whether Fermat’s last theorem was true; we only suspected that it was.
It was the deductive reasoning of Wiles that provided justification for believing that
the theorem is true, explained why it is true, connected it to elliptic curves and
modular forms, and introduced powerful new techniques and rich conceptual structures.

In general, the distinction between ampliative and non-ampliative inference
is much more fragile than usually assumed, as is the very distinction
between deductive and nondeductive arguments. The topic is too complex
to discuss in depth here, but it is worth noting that expert opinions on the subject
run a wide gamut, with some argumentation theorists (who study
everyday natural-language argumentation and reasoning), such as

36
, claiming that all natural-language reasoning is
deductive, and that all arguments should be understood as attempts
at deductive arguments. While we do not quite endorse this strong thesis
here, it is important to understand that there are compelling arguments for it.
In the context of AI benchmarks, the position is perhaps easier to understand
when one considers problems in datasets such as MATH, like a problem asking for the roots of a quadratic equation
or the area of a rectangular field. Strictly speaking, numeric answers like
\{2,5\}

for the equation roots or 138 for the rectangle area are not formally entailed
by the problem context, because the context omits the necessary mathematical background:
definitions (what is a root, what is a rectangle,
etc.
), relevant theorems and results
(the quadratic formula, area formulas,
\ldots
), and so on. Nevertheless, the underlying reasoning
remains entirely deductive. The abridged problem formulation doesn’t shift the
reasoning into the domain of abduction or induction, nor does it make the answer ampliative.
It simply reflects the economy of expression in our representations of mathematical reasoning.

Philosophers of science such as
65

have defended equally strong positions regarding deduction in scientific reasoning,
claiming, for example, that “the only valid arguments are deductively valid arguments,
and that deductive logic is the only logic that we have or need”
[
66
]

and that instances of ‘‘inductive’’ or ‘‘abductive’’ reasoning are really
enthymematic deductions. Indeed, one could plausibly argue that even in what appear to be inherently
inductive settings, such as applications of statistical techniques that seem to go
beyond what has been strictly observed, the reasoning often remains primarily deductive.
In Bayesian confirmation theory, for example, the move from prior to posterior
is, mathematically speaking, an application of the probability axioms, and the
update itself is a deduction made inside a richer calculus. So what might
look ampliative in a bare propositional language is non-ampliative
once the relevant probabilistic assumptions are made explicit in the model
specification.
3
3

3

Of course, Bayesian updating
by itself does not resolve the further questions of which prior to adopt, which
likelihood is appropriate, or which model family to use. But that says nothing
about the indispensability of induction.
To make that stronger claim, one would need to show that the reasoning by which
such choices are made is itself irreducibly nondeductive, rather than merely
dependent on substantive assumptions or principles that are not fixed by logic alone.
Uncertainty or underdetermination at the level of premises
does not entail the presence of any distinctively inductive inferential mechanisms.
Such uncertainty is manifested even in mathematics,
e.g.
, consider the
historical doubts regarding Euclid’s
fifth postulate, the axiom of choice, the continuum hypothesis and large-cardinal axioms,
and even the law of the excluded middle and double negation elimination.

Likewise, deduction plays a key
role in empirical-error-minimization methods like regression or SVM, or indeed any
technique that can be viewed through the lens of statistical learning theory (SLT).
This is not just because these techniques are deductively analyzed and proven to
minimize error, but also because when we apply a model to a new input that we have
not seen before, the reasoning is often best understood as a deduction.

The point can be illustrated with ordinary linear regression, especially when formulated in Vapnik’s
SLT framework. Suppose we fix a hypothesis class
\mathcal{H}
of linear
functions
f_{\beta}(x)=\beta_{0}+\beta^{\top}x
, a loss function such as squared error, and a learning
rule that selects from
\mathcal{H}

the function minimizing empirical risk, perhaps with an added regularization term to control capacity.
Suppose further that we assume the training sample and a previously unseen instance are
drawn i.i.d. from a common underlying distribution, that the feature representation is
stable across train and test cases, and that the target relation is appropriately approximated
within the chosen hypothesis class. Then, given the observed data, it follows
deductively

which parameter vector minimizes the specified objective, which hypothesis is therefore
selected by the learning rule, what prediction that hypothesis assigns to a new input, and (given the SLT machinery)
what kinds of bounds relate empirical risk to expected risk as a function of sample size and
hypothesis-class capacity. On this picture, the reasoning is not an irreducibly ampliative
leap from observed to unobserved cases. Rather, it is a chain of deductions within a richer
premise set that includes the hypothesis space, the loss function, the sampling assumptions,
and the generalization theory. The genuine uncertainty lies not in the mathematical
derivation of the fitted line or the associated risk bound, but in whether the assumptions
underwriting that derivation are good assumptions for the problem at hand. But that is
business as usual for deduction, which distinguishes between validity and soundness.
Whether the premises entail the conclusion is one thing, and logic can tell us that;
whether the premises are
true
(
e.g.
, whether the test instances are indeed drawn i.i.d.
from a common underlying distribution) is up to the way the world is and must be settled
by empirical investigation.

In any event, whether all reasoning is ultimately deductive (or whether it can be
fruitfully reconstructed as such) is a complex and contentious question, and we do not aim to take
a definitive position here. Our more limited point is that the distinction between ampliative and non-ampliative
reasoning (and the characterization of deduction as non-ampliative) is of questionable
stability and value; and that, when scrutinized, many allegedly ampliative cases of
inference are far more deductive in structure than the standard contrast suggests.

In what follows we begin with a broad overview of reasoning datasets, grouped into four categories:
natural-language reasoning; mathematical and scientific reasoning; generalist reasoning; and formal reasoning.
We then introduce a set of analytical dimensions that provide a finer-grained understanding of this landscape,
and use these axes to classify a broad range of benchmarks, including
ProofGrid
. The section concludes
with a brief comparison of
ProofGrid
and other major datasets.

2.1
A Bird’s-Eye Survey

2.1.1
Natural-Language Reasoning

Within the larger NLP community, a major thread of work involving AI models and reasoning investigates
the ability of such models to perform natural-language inference (NLI), and particularly their ability to
determine whether a set of natural-language sentences entails a target sentence.
The general problem is this:

Given a set of sentences (or more generally, a text corpus)
T
and a hypothesis
h
(representing, for example,

a potential answer to a question) determine whether
T\models h
,
i.e.
, whether
T
entails
h
.

(1)

Common alternative terms for
T
include
context
,
theory
,
knowledge base
, or simply the set of
premises
,
while the sentence
h
is also referred to as the—putative—
conclusion
, and sometimes as the
target sentence
or the
query
.

Note that entailment here is usually (but not always, see below)
semantic
, it is
not
formal or strictly logical entailment.
In formal logic, if
T
entails
h
then it does so purely by virtue of the syntactic
form
of the sentences in
T
and
h
,
not by virtue of the
meanings
of the words in those sentences. We thus say that
Tom is tall and thin
formally entails

Tom is tall
because the entailment has nothing to do with what
tall
and
thin
actually mean.
We could replace those adjectives with any
others and the entailment would still hold. In fact, that is how formal entailment is defined: We quantify over all possible

interpretations
of the extra-logical symbols, and we say that no matter how you interpret
Tom
,
tall
, and
thin
,

i.e.
, for any possible way of assigning meanings (denotations) to those terms, the conclusion comes out true whenever
the premises are true. By contrast, the usual textbook definition of
semantic
entailment is this:
T\models h
iff
the truth of the sentences in
T
“guarantees” or makes “certain” the truth of
h
, or equivalently, iff the falsity of
h
guarantees
or makes certain the falsity of at least one element of
T
.

However, this common textbook gloss is loaded with subjective psychological notions like certainty.
A crisper definition of semantic entailment can be given via possible worlds:
T
entails
h
iff in
every possible world (or model) where all sentences in
T
are true,
h
is also true. This sounds very much like
the definition of entailment in formal logic, but there is a crucial difference. In this definition we do not quantify
over completely arbitrary interpretations of non-logical symbols. Instead, those meanings are (largely)
fixed
.
Proper names like
Tom
, for example, are fixed: they are “rigid designators” that pick out the same constant element across all
possible worlds.
Lexical items that are not proper names (say, words like
politician
and
smoker
) are allowed some degree of cross-world variation
but only in the sense that the sets of individuals answering to these descriptions (the
extensions
of these words) may vary from world to world,
as one world may have a different set of politicians from another, and someone who smokes in this world might not smoke in another. But the
concepts expressed by these words are uniform across all possible worlds.
4
4

4

See, for instance,
41
.

So the proper way to understand semantic entailment is this:
once we fix the meanings of the extra-logical words
Tom
,
thin
, and
tall
, and
overweight
, in any possible world in which
Tom is thin

is true,
Tom is not overweight
is also true. Thus,
Tom is thin
semantically entails
Tom is not overweight
, even though
it does not formally entail it.

Sometimes the entailment is neither formal nor semantic but rather one of implicature. Here is a textbook example:
Some students managed to pass the exam

strongly suggests that some students did not pass the exam, and so in an NLP context we might say that
Some students did not pass the exam
is entailed by

Some students managed to pass the exam
, even though it is neither semantically nor formally entailed by it. It is possible to tease out a number of other commonsensical
types of entailment that are disjoint from the above kinds, but ultimately, entailment judgments in NLP are made empirically; labeled examples are annotated
based on what typical annotators perceive to be consequences of a given context
T
.
This purely descriptive framing—entailment by democracy, so to speak—is reasonable if the goal
is to study entailment as an empirical phenomenon, as it functions in everyday language use.
But it does not offer a normative basis for adjudicating the correctness of an inference, and this
may have implications for models trained on such data.
The heuristics-and-biases literature has documented a vast range of systematic reasoning
fallacies to which most people (including reasoning experts) are susceptible
[
93
]
.
If what counts as entailment becomes conflated with what is commonly perceived as such,
even when these perceptions are flawed, a model could score well
on such benchmarks by mimicking incorrect or biased reasoning patterns.

Problem (
1
) originated under the name RTE (Recognizing Textual Entailment).
5
5

5

The term “NLI” started gaining
wider currency after MacCartney’s influential 2009 PhD dissertation
[
56
]
.

The first RTE challenge was launched
in 2005
[
31
]
. The fourth RTE challenge, in 2008, introduced an alternative and more general formulation of the problem:
Given
T
and
h
, decide whether
T
entails
h
,
contradicts
p
, or leaves the status of
p
unknown—corresponding to the labels
entailment
,
contradiction
,
and
neutral
. This three-way setup was later adopted and popularized by large-scale benchmarks such as
SNLI
[
17
]
and MNLI
[
103
]
, helping to standardize the task in modern NLP.

Historically, NLI work studied real-world natural language, usually drawn from
naturally occurring or crowdsourced text. The context
T
was typically a single sentence or a short paragraph at most,
and the emphasis was on lexical, syntactic, and pragmatic nuances (arising,
e.g.
, from various types of implicatures)
in the relevant sentences. As already mentioned, entailment was typically defined
informally,
6
6

6

58
argued early on that
a textual inference task should “adopt as the standard of inference what a human would be happy to infer from a piece
of text. In particular, items would be assessed by people that are awake, careful, moderately intelligent and informed,
and with reasonable document interpretation expertise,
but not by semanticists or similar academics
[our emphasis].
These people would use whatever background and real world knowledge that they usually bring to interpreting texts.”

although logic-based techniques were also used from the beginning
[
15
,
1
]
, albeit with
underwhelming results
[
16
]
.
Early logic-based efforts still dealt with short
sentence pairs drawn from real-world contexts and attended closely to lexical and syntactic features
(captured,
e.g.
, by wide-coverage parsers), but they grounded entailment in logic and formal
semantic representations, such as those of DRT (Discourse Representation Theory)
[
48
]

or MRS (Minimal Recursion Semantics)
[
30
]
.
So even within mainstream NLI, there was always a thread of research that treated entailment
as a rigorously defined, logic-driven relation, but applied to ecologically authentic
and linguistically rich text. However, the limited success of that approach in early NLI
made way for statistical models with much more shallow representations that nonetheless
proved more effective in practice.

There is a relatively newer strand of NLI work that is more explicitly inspired by and tied to formal logic,
exemplified by datasets like RuleTaker
[
28
]
and systems like ProofWriter
[
88
]
. The main difference of these
approaches from traditional NLI is that their data sources are expressed in
controlled
natural language (CNL),
or quasi-natural language, usually templated English sentences synthetically generated from formal logical data with the help
of grammars.
7
7

7

Representative examples include
Dave is green
,
Bob is round
,

Big people are rough
, and
If someone is young and round then they are kind
.
The extent to which these may be viewed as “natural” language inference problems is debatable.

Because the starting point is a synthetically generated formal dataset,
8
8

8

This is typically
a set of pairs
(S,p)
consisting of a collection of formal sentences
S
and a single sentence
p
—the
same type of objects that we call
arguments
in
ProofGrid
. (These pairs are known
as
sequents
in logic, but we avoid that term here because it tends to be
associated with Gentzen-style proof systems, which are not our focus in this paper.)

it can be adjusted to support experiments with deeper reasoning and precisely defined entailment conditions,
often grounded in subsets of logic such as stratified Datalog or various description logics. These datasets
emphasize multi-step inference chains, closed-world reasoning, and deterministic label generation and
evaluation, enabling fine-grained control over complexity and semantic scope. While the language is
less naturalistic and rather inauthentic, the trade-off is a more transparent link between linguistic input and symbolic inference,
so these datasets have been more widely used to probe the reasoning abilities of neural
models. Besides RuleTaker
[
28
]
and ProofWriter
[
88
]
, datasets in this
category include ProntoQA
[
75
]
, LogicInference
[
68
]
, LogicAsker
[
95
]
,
LogicNLI
[
91
]
, and FLD
[
64
]
. Folio
[
39
]
can arguably be placed in the same group.
Although its sentences are not programmatically derived from logical forms, they are nevertheless synthetic
insofar as they do not occur naturally; they were manually created by human annotators based on randomly selected
Wikipedia pages. The resulting style is conspicuously stilted in the interest of enabling straightforward mappings to formal
(FOL) notation.
9
9

9

A representative FOLIO problem features the hypothesis/conclusion

Sea eel is an eel
, while the corresponding context consists of the following sentences:
All eels are fish. No fish are plants.
A thing is either a plant or animal. Nothing that breathes is paper. All animals breathe. If a sea eel is either an eel or a plant,
then a sea eel is an eel or an animal
.

In general, NLI-oriented datasets (certainly those from the mainstream NLI tradition, but to a lesser extent
those of the CNL variety as well), tend to focus on the inferences that are found
in natural language, which are characterized by relatively shallow deductions, are strongly impacted by
linguistic nuances, and incorporate commonsense—and often non-monotonic—reasoning principles such
as the closed-world assumption and negation-as-failure. In this setting, AI models are viewed
as “soft” reasoners
[
28
]
or soft theorem provers, simulating logical reasoning
using natural language. By contrast, we will see that math- and logic-oriented datasets like
ProofGrid
are usually expressed in formal symbolic notation and focus on long chains of deductions whose
correctness is underwritten by strict logical and mathematical norms. They gauge the degree
to which AI models can serve as “hard” reasoners or theorem provers for logic and mathematics.

While much of the work on natural-language reasoning centers around entailment, whether
formally or informally defined, there are other reasoning tasks that depart from this framework.
For instance, some benchmarks focus on abductive reasoning, where, given a natural-language context, the goal
is to select the most plausible explanation or continuation (from a number of candidates).
A representative example is HellaSwag
[
106
]
, in which models must choose the
most plausible ending to a scenario from a set of options. Although this task is not about entailment,
solving it requires integrating background knowledge, commonsense expectations,
and causal plausibility, all of which can be said to involve (informal) inference.
These benchmarks therefore probe reasoning in a broader and more intuitive sense,
often drawing on patterns learned from real-world event sequences rather than strict logical inference.
In contrast to deduction-focused benchmarks, the reasoning here is contextual, gradient, and
plausibility-based, highlighting the diversity of what “reasoning” can mean in NLP.

2.1.2
Mathematical and Scientific Reasoning

Another class of benchmarks regularly invoked in conversations about AI models and reasoning
comprises tests specifically designed to evaluate mathematical competence.
Here the inputs are math problems (from various branches of mathematics and levels of difficulty) and the
outputs are the respective answers. Unlike NLI, the focus is on longer chains of deductions whose
correctness is underwritten by strict and explicitly formulated logical and mathematical norms,
not by tacit informal conventions or pragmatics. The idea is to gauge the extent to which models
can be used as “hard” logico-mathematical problem solvers.
Representative samples include GSM8K
[
29
]
,
MATH
[
43
]
, OlympiadBench
[
40
]
, Omni-MATH
[
14
]
,
and the more demanding and recent OlymMATH
[
85
]
and FrontierMath
[
34
]
.

The problems in these benchmarks are expressed in natural language
and the expected answers are what we might call
literals
, typically
numbers or symbolic expressions like
x^{2}-5
. Models are expected
to output their final answers in a special designated format, usually within the
braces of a
L
a
T
e
X
command like
\boxed{
\cdots
}
.
Questions are culled from many sources: standardized school curricula from various countries
(such as K-12 textbooks); statewide and national exams (SATs, ACT, AP Calculus and Statistics,
CBSE/ICSE/ISC exams in India, etc.); math competitions and Olympiads
(
AMC
,

Putnam
,

AIME
,

USAMO
,

IMO
, and so on); materials from educational organizations like

AoPS
(the Art of Problem Solving); undergraduate math problem
sets (
e.g.
, MIT OCW, Harvard, etc.); crowdsourcing; and synthetic data as well.

Some of the more recent math benchmarks are remarkably challenging.
FrontierMath

[
34
]
, for example,
introduced in late 2024 by the research institute Epoch AI with the explicit aim of evaluating AI models on advanced mathematics,
comprises hundreds of original research-level problems across a wide range of
domains, such as algebraic geometry, number theory, and category theory. These problems were created by expert mathematicians,
vetted for originality, and selected for their theoretical depth and difficulty. The creators note that even seasoned mathematicians
may require hours or days to solve many of them. According to Epoch AI,
as of September 2025, no model reached a mean accuracy of
25% on
FrontierMath
,
10
10

10

In December 2024, OpenAI claimed that o3 attained an accuracy
in excess of 25%, but that claim
has been controversial

and Epoch AI was not able to reproduce that figure.

though GPT-5 came close at 24.83%, with OpenAI’s o4-mini at second place, followed by
Gemini 2.5 Pro, Grok-4, and o3.
11
11

11

These results are for the easier tiers, 1–3. The hardest tier is 4, currently led by
GPT-5 with 8.33%.
By early March 2026, the top performer was GPT-5.2, at 31.3%.

However, regardless of their difficulty level, the extent to which these datasets actually test
mathematical
reasoning
is not obvious. They certainly don’t do so in an explicit sense,
given that they follow an outcome-based evaluation paradigm. That is, models are judged
on whether they produce the correct final answer, typically a single literal value,
without requiring them to provide logically correct arguments in support of those answers.
12
12

12

COT (chain-of-thought) prompting is widely used for these tests, and model outputs are
(potentially long) sequences of intermediate steps culminating
in a final
\boxed{
\cdots
}
answer. However, this is done
mostly in order to improve the question-answering performance of the models, not to evaluate the reasoning
contained in the COT traces. Model performance is still judged only on whether the final answer
matches the ground truth. What little preliminary work has been done in this direction so far has found
that “there exists a significant disparity between answer accuracy and faithfulness of the CoT reasoning generated
by LLMs, indicating that they often arrive at correct answers through incorrect reasoning”
[
67
]
.
Some newer datasets, such as NuminaMath, include
a certain proportion of “proof-based problems”
[
52
, p. 12]
, but they do not currently
support proof evaluation.

This is rather incongruous with the subject matter, because, since the time of Euclid,
mathematics has valued proof over mere assertion. But if performance
is gauged only by inspecting the final answer, models may get full credit even if they arrive
at the correct result through memorization, pattern-matching, heuristic shortcuts, or statistical artifacts
rather than sound reasoning. Benchmark creators do attempt to guard against guesswork (
e.g.
, through “guessproofness” constraints

[
34
, p. 3]

and large or intricate answers), but these measures
are only partial defenses against the core issue: These benchmarks don’t verify
whether the model arrived at the right solution for the right
reasons.
13
13

13

While acknowledging the technical difficulty of
FrontierMath
, a number of Fields Medalists
“pointed out that the numerical format of the questions feels somewhat contrived,” with Richard Borcherds
remarking, in a bit of understatement, that “these benchmark problems aren’t quite the
same as coming up with original proofs”
[
34
, p. 11]
.

A related issue with benchmarks in this category, at least as diagnostic tools for reasoning,
is that they inevitably favor models with vast stores of crystallized mathematical knowledge.
Large language models are trained on enormous corpora, including textbooks,
research articles, lecture notes, problem banks, explanations, proofs, tutorials, and forums like StackExchange, giving them access
to a breadth and depth of mathematical content far beyond what any human mathematician could hope to internalize.
This gives AI models a significant advantage. Problems are built around familiar mathematical objects (polynomials,
inequalities, divisibility conditions, geometric relations, and so on), where the underlying concepts, as well as common
tricks and transformations, are very well-represented in the training data. Even when problems are novel or cleverly
constructed or transformed, the models’ ability to draw on a tremendous body of heuristics and solution templates
might enable them to perform relatively well without necessarily engaging in deep reasoning,
aided mostly by pattern recognition, recall, and analogy.
This is especially true if the problems are heavily
computational, as is the case with many of these benchmarks (including
FrontierMath
), which allows the models to leverage their coding skills.
By contrast, as mentioned in the introduction,
ProofGrid
is deliberately designed to neutralize this advantage. Its problems are fully symbolic,
devoid of meaningful names or context, and grounded in logic systems that offer no opportunity to exploit real-world
mathematical knowledge. It thus forces models to rely primarily on fluid reasoning, by constructing and
verifying proofs from abstract premises, using inferential strategies that are common in mathematical practice.
This offers a clearer lens into a model’s true reasoning abilities, unassisted by prior knowledge.

A similar class of benchmarks target scientific problem solving.
A historically important sample is the AI2 Reasoning Challenge (ARC) benchmark
[
27
]
,
introduced by the Allen Institute for AI and aiming to evaluate understanding and reasoning in AI models within
the domain of elementary and middle school science. It contains 7,787 natural-language multiple-choice
questions sourced from publicly available U.S. standardized science exams and split into two subsets:

•

ARC-Easy: Questions that can often be answered by surface-level lexical cues or
information retrieval techniques.

•

ARC-Challenge: A curated subset of 2,590 questions that are supposed to defeat retrieval-based methods
and are intended to test deeper forms of scientific and commonsense reasoning.

The questions span a broad range of science topics typically covered in grades 3–9, including the life sciences
(biology, ecosystems, nutrition, human body systems,
etc.
); physical science (forces and motion, energy, matter, electricity);
earth and space science (geology, weather, solar system); and scientific method and reasoning (experimental design,
measurement, cause-effect relationships, and so on). The benchmark was explicitly designed to resist shallow
statistical methods and require multi-step inference, background knowledge, and commonsense reasoning.
At the time of its release in 2018, ARC represented a significant step towards evaluating genuine science-related
reasoning ability in language models, but by now it has been effectively saturated, with many models achieving
superhuman performance even on the Challenge subset. ARC has been superseded by the science-focused parts of
relatively more recent benchmarks like BBH and Multi-MLU, although these have also been largely saturated by now.

ARC and related science-focused benchmarks bear directly on our earlier remarks regarding the distinction between deductive and
nondeductive reasoning, raising important questions about how deduction should be understood and identified.
Consider this ARC problem:
A student riding a bicycle observes that it moves faster on a smooth road than on a rough road.
This happens because the smooth road has: (A) less gravity (B) more gravity (C) less friction [correct] (D) more friction.

This may appear to rely on simple empirical knowledge and commonsense reasoning. But if a model—or human—has internalized
appropriate generalizations like “Friction impedes motion” and “Rough surfaces increase friction,” then a correct
answer may be reached through a chain of deterministic (and arguably deductive) reasoning. From this perspective,
the distinction between deductive and nondeductive reasoning may depend less on the task formulation and more on the implicit
premises available to the test taker. This ambiguity reinforces the need for benchmarks that make the role of deduction
explicit and verifiable—where the logical structure of the argument can be scrutinized and the conclusion’s validity traced
to its premises.

Finally, the same issues we discussed in the context of math benchmarks apply here as well. The actual inferences
of the model are not tested, only its final answer. Accordingly, these benchmarks do not directly test
reasoning either, but rather question-answering ability. Likewise, crystallized knowledge plays a crucial
role in successful performance in these benchmarks, and can be a confounding factor in assessing
the extent to which models arrive at their answers through genuine reasoning rather than recall
or statistical association. The exploitation of shallow statistical patterns has been repeatedly demonstrated
for earlier and smaller transformer models, such as BERT, even on relatively abstract logical reasoning
problems expressed in natural language, with,
e.g.
,
107
concluding that “instead of learning
to emulate the correct reasoning function, BERT has in fact learned statistical features that inherently exist in
logical reasoning problems.” But similar concerns arise for newer, larger, and more powerful models.
For example,
67
evaluated 5 families of instruction-tuned causal LLMs
ranging from 7B to 175B parameters, including an early version of ChatGPT, on two multi-hop QA benchmarks.
They found that the models often make the correct guess for the wrong reasons when they are asked to
think step-by-step, showing a significant gap (around 16 percentage points) between answer correctness
and COT correctness and concluding that “The correct final answer may not necessarily follow from faithful reasoning. We observe a
significant gap between answer accuracy and reasoning faithfulness. It highlights the necessity
of directly evaluating the reasoning steps rather than solely scoring the final answers.”
This is confirmed by our results,
e.g.
, models do very well when we ask them
to simply guess whether a proof is correct, but when we ask them to justify their answers with details, performance drops significantly
(see Table
6
). However,
67
also reported that “the performance gap between the final answer
and reasoning worsens as the model size increases. As the answer accuracy also increases with the model size,
it suggests that the bigger models may have the knowledge of the final answer without the need to perform reasoning.”
By contrast, we find that the gap shrinks for the best and largest models, because they rely less on superficial patterns
and more on honest reasoning toil.

The methodological issue of assessing models indirectly, by their final answers only,
extends to frontier models and to the one domain that is supposed to be the epitome
of abstract deductive reasoning: mathematics.
While AI models have undeniably made great strides here,
the recent buzz about such models being able to solve Olympiad-level math problems is premature.
A recent manual analysis of 455 carefully curated shortlist IMO (International Math Olympiad) problems
from 2009 to 2023 found that “frontier models often generate incorrect solutions, that is, solutions that
contain reasoning errors despite getting the final answer right.” Specifically, once the model’s full reasoning
was taken into account, the study found that the conditional probability of an answer being fully correct given
that the final answer was correct was 12.5% for o1, 14.3% for o3-mini, and a rather astounding 0%
for o1-mini, DeepSeek R1, and Gemini 2.0
[
57
, p. 8]
. The authors write that “
\cdots
current LLMs fall significantly short of solving
challenging Olympiad-level problems and frequently fail to distinguish correct mathematical
reasoning from clearly flawed solutions. Our analyses demonstrate that the occasional correct
final answers provided by LLMs often result from pattern recognition or heuristic shortcuts rather
than genuine mathematical reasoning. These findings underscore the substantial gap between
LLM performance and human expertise in advanced mathematical reasoning and highlight the
importance of developing benchmarks that prioritize the soundness of the reasoning used to arrive
at an answer rather than the mere correctness of the final answers.”

2.1.3
Generalist/Hybrid Reasoning

In this category we find more recent and wider-ranging benchmarks that touch on reasoning somewhat
indirectly or only in part, even though they often end up informing public claims about the reasoning
abilities of AI models. Notable examples include BBH
[
87
]
and its successor
BBEH (“Big Bench Extra Hard”)
[
35
]
, MMLU
[
42
]

and its successor MMLU Pro
[
98
]
, HLE (Humanity’s Last Exam)
[
76
]
, SuperGPQA
[
90
]
.
These benchmarks aim to evaluate models across a wide array of subjects and tasks, many of which
do involve reasoning to some degree. These tasks range from math and logic puzzles to legal analysis, ethics scenarios,
high-school and college science, the social sciences, professional/standardized exams (SAT, GRE, LSAT,
etc.
),
and so on. In some cases, the required inference resembles formal deduction (e.g., logic grid puzzles or syllogisms);
in others, the inference relies on background knowledge, commonsense, or pragmatic reasoning.

Although these benchmarks provide a valuable broad-strokes view of model capabilities, they offer a relatively
coarse-grained signal about reasoning, as they conflate different cognitive demands (e.g., domain knowledge,
reading comprehension, cultural context, symbolic manipulation, and so on). Moreover, the evaluation remains
outcome-based, judging models only on their final answers without assessing whether those answers are supported
by correct or coherent reasoning steps. As such, these benchmarks complement more focused and diagnostic tools
that aim to isolate and evaluate more precisely defined forms of reasoning.

A well-known benchmark that can be placed in this category, motivated in part by the same
considerations as
ProofGrid
, is the Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus for Artificial General Intelligence (ARC-AGI,
or simply ARC), introduced by
26
. It was designed to measure human-like fluid intelligence,
rather than the crystallized intelligence typically assessed by standard AI benchmarks.
Because of its prominence, the rest of this section is devoted to a detailed examination of ARC.

ARC comprises few-shot visual grid-transformation tasks,
where the test taker must conjecture an underlying pattern from a small
number of input-output examples and successfully apply it to a novel test grid.
The tasks are exclusively visual, strictly precluding not just domain-specific knowledge
but also language, with the aim of relying only on fundamental
core knowledge
priors
[
82
]
like
objects, numbers, actions, and space (or basic geometry).
14
14

14

According to the work of
developmental psychologist
83
(see also
82
),
human cognition starts with a small
set of evolutionarily old, domain-specific representational systems, the four main ones
being objects, numbers, actions, and space (a fifth domain-specific ability,
for social interaction, is acknowledged as a distinct possibility). These systems are supposed to appear early in development,
be shared in some form with other animals, and provide the foundations for all subsequent learning.
The core-knowledge thesis is clearly nativist, or at least anti-blank-slate, but it differs
in one major respect from the more conventional nativism of Chomsky and Fodor: the role
of language. The latter posit language as
the
most highly distinctive computational system
of the human mind, set apart by its uniqueness, its abstract combinatorial structure,
and its explanatory power. Core knowledge theorists, by contrast, tend to view language
as a powerful add-on—a coordinator and amplifier of more basic cognitive systems
that predate human language, insofar as they appear in infants before the development
of language, and even in other animals. However, many cognitive scientists would maintain
that language is so transformative that it cannot be understood as just an important add-on;
on this view, language doesn’t just organize or decorate cognition, it fundamentally reorganizes it.

Humans are generally able to solve ARC tasks without much difficulty.
As models scaled and researchers began applying massive test-time compute and brute-force
search techniques to the original dataset, ARC-AGI-2 was introduced in early 2025 to
re-establish a rigorous capability boundary
[
24
]
.
ARC-AGI-2 retains the visual
grid format of the original ARC-AGI (which has come to be known as ARC-AGI-1) but
introduces more complex tasks (requiring, for example, a sequence of object-manipulating
actions that must respect multiple rules and contextual constraints). These new tasks
are not as easily solved as those in ARC-AGI-1, but humans still succeed with probability 66%
when attempting to solve a randomly sampled task from the new benchmark
[
24
]
[p. 7].

A key aim of ARC, which is shared by
ProofGrid
, is to minimize
the memorization and data-retrieval advantages of LLMs, and to test
fluid problem-solving ability.
But ARC goes further by precluding language and keeping the tasks strictly visual,
with the goal of testing whether models can solve these tasks using only the few foundational priors
that seem available to humans, roughly those singled out by core-knowledge theorists.
Chollet speaks of “a test of human-like general intelligence”
and writes that “an actionable test of general intelligence that would be fair for
both humans and machines should only feature tasks that assume the four core knowledge systems
listed above, and should not involve any acquired knowledge outside of these priors”

[
26
]
[p. 27].

ARC is an elegant benchmark that has been influential in the field,
but several issues are worth noting. First, ARC claims to be a measure of general fluid intelligence, but as was
acknowledged by
26
[p. 54], no empirical evidence has been presented in support of that contention,
e.g.
,
no evidence that performance on ARC correlates meaningfully with
gF
, or even
with
g
.
15
15

15

The famous
g
-factor (the
g
stands for “general intelligence”), introduced
by
81
, is a latent factor inferred from positive correlations
among diverse cognitive test scores (people who do well on one kind of mental task tend to do well
on others, on average). It is a statistical construct from psychometrics.
Spearman believed that everyone has a relatively fixed intellectual capacity,
a core mental ability that plays a role in virtually every cognitive task,
and that the
g
-factor measured precisely this ability.

Gf
(fluid intelligence) is a related factor, representing the capacity to reason and
solve novel problems independently of previously acquired knowledge. In psychometric
models
[
21
,
22
,
20
]
,
it is typically distinguished from crystallized intelligence (
Gc
),
which reflects knowledge and skills acquired through learning and experience.

This is not implausible, given
that ARC has qualitative similarities to well-known nonverbal intelligence tests like RPM
(Raven’s Progressive Matrices
[
59
]
[ch. 11]),
and the latter
have
been psychometrically validated (
e.g.
, RPM is known
to load fairly strongly on
g
, although see
33
for
recently discovered limitations).
But superficial task similarity is not enough. Psychometrically,
two tasks can look alike and yet differ a lot in what drives performance: visuospatial search,
working-memory load, perceptual segmentation, task-format familiarity, response-generation demands,
and so on. ARC, in particular (and especially ARC-AGI-2), differs from RPM in significant ways:
it is open-ended rather than multiple-choice, it requires constructing an output rather than
recognizing one, it imposes heavier search demands, and uses a pixel-grid micro-world
with its own idiosyncratic priors. Such differences could substantially increase or decrease correlation
with
g
. So while there is a plausible case for expecting some positive correlation between ARC
and
Gv
16
16

16

In the Cattell-Horn-Carroll family of psychometric theories of intelligence

[
20
]
, the
Gv
factor captures the ability to perceive, analyze, manipulate,
and think with visual patterns and spatial information.

94
write that “
Gv
involves a range of visual processes,
ranging from fairly simple visual perceptual tasks to higher-level, visual, cognitive processes.
Woodcock and Mather (1989, p. 15) define
Gv
in part: “In Horn-Cattell theory,
‘broad visualization’ requires fluent thinking with stimuli that are visual in the
mind’s eye.”” They further note that “Engineers, auto mechanics, architects, nuclear physicists,
sculptors, carpenters, and parts department managers all use
Gv
to deal with the
demands of their job,”
although “many writers seem to consider
Gv
a relatively low-level cognitive ability, more
perceptual than intellectual.”
,
and perhaps even with
g
or
Gf
, to the best of our knowledge that remains
an open empirical question.

Second, barring the use of language in a general test of intelligence (especially
one that is routinely applied to language models) seems questionable, both on a priori
and on empirical grounds. Language alone does not give models an unfair advantage.
Language is a faculty that all neurobiologically normal humans share.
It is not, by itself, crystallized intelligence, nor is it a bias; it is a key innate mental capacity
that cognitive science recognizes as central to thought. It is particularly important for
the most prominent forms of human reasoning, from argumentation in politics and everyday life
to scientific and mathematical reasoning, which have been deeply intertwined
with language
17
17

17

We understand language here in a broad sense, as a structured
system of symbols governed by rules that enable the representation and communication of meaning.
The only essential features are the use of discrete symbolic elements, combinatorial principles
that determine how these elements can be arranged, and systematic relationships between syntax (form) and semantics.

for thousands of years, especially with reason giving.
The relevant word in ancient Greek is
logos
, a rich term that can mean speech, word,
account, explanation, or argument—all of them inherently linguistic objects. Likewise for
ratio
,
the Latin root of
reasoning
, which also revolves around giving an account, explaining, reckoning, and
justifying. These are the core practices of both everyday reasoning as well as mathematical and scientific
reasoning, where one articulates premises and derives conclusions, makes and justifies claims and
other inferential commitments, and explains why something is the case.

The importance of language is widely recognized in psychometric theories of intelligence and testing,
even by advocates of nonverbal tests like Wechsler, who as far back as 1939 was stressing the need
to include both verbal and nonverbal tasks in intelligence tests, since doing so “makes possible
a comparison between a subject’s facility in using words and symbols and his ability to manipulate
objects, and to perceive visual patterns”
[
99
]
[p. 146]. He also made the empirical observation
that “Subjects of superior intelligence generally do better on the verbal, and subjects of
inferior intelligence do better on the performance (nonverbal) part of the examination” [p. 147].
A “critical finding”
[
50
]
[p. 531] of what is perhaps the most
prominent and “widely accepted”
[
60
]
scientific theory of intelligence,
the CHC (Cattell-Horn-Carroll) theory
[
61
]
, is that

the general reasoning factor (
Gf
) may be decomposed into subfactors: (1) deductive
reasoning (termed sequential reasoning by Carroll and largely comprising verbal
tasks), (2) quantitative reasoning (inductive or deductive reasoning with quantitative
concepts), and (3) inductive reasoning (often measured with figural tasks; Carroll, 1993).
A good reasoning test, then, should probably measure all three of these
reasoning factors – or at least not be strongly biased toward one (Wilhelm, 2005).
This fact is commonly overlooked in studies that represent fluid reasoning abilities
with a single figural reasoning test, such as Raven’s Progressive Matrices test (Raven, Court, & Raven, 1977).

[
50
]
[p. 531]

Like RPM, ARC only taps subfactor (3) above, and even there we would claim that
serious inductive reasoning (as done, e.g., in science) almost always involves language
and the explicit articulation of hypotheses and evidence.
The importance of language is also recognized by rival theories to CHC,
such as BIS (the Berlin Intelligence Structure)
[
11
]
, which holds that
“measuring reasoning requires tasks that sample across the main content areas,”
of which there are three: verbal, numeric, and figural; and “suggests that the diversity
of content required to measure
Gf
consistently will require going beyond the
figural tasks (e.g., Raven’s Progressive Matrices)”
[
50
]
[p. 532].
Likewise, recent work on the limitations of exclusively figural tasks like RPM
advises that “researchers are discouraged to include only Raven’s in an investigation,
if a valid estimate of
g
is sought.”
[
33
]
[p. 71].

ARC also aims to be a test of abstraction, but it is important to be precise about
what sort of abstraction mechanisms are tapped by nonverbal tests.
Cognitive science has traditionally distinguished
between two types of abstraction:

•

low-level
perceptual abstraction
, which allows us to recognize objects across
viewpoints, strip away visual noise to see that two shapes are both triangles, and so on; and

•

conceptual abstraction
, which is the process of creating higher-order categories based
on invisible, functional, or relational properties, e.g., understanding that a thermometer, a clock,
and a yardstick all belong to the category of measuring instruments, or understanding concepts
like justice and entropy.

The sort of abstraction tested by visual tasks like ARC and RPM sits somewhere in between,
and much closer to the first. It allows us to extract relational structure from perceptual representations,
by identifying visual progression (e.g., increases in shape size), rotation or reflection, and
patterns like same-colored regions across portions of a grid.
The corresponding type of reasoning is often called
visuospatial
relational
reasoning or
perceptual analogy
.
While it’s true that this intermediate level of perceptual abstraction has been historically important
in the testing of fluid intelligence, it is conceptual abstraction that is
central to science and mathematics
and is considered to be the defining cognitive capacity behind theoretical reasoning.
As already mentioned, conceptual abstraction involves the formation of general ideas and
principles that are detached from any particular sensory inputs.
They range from ordinary concepts like cause and effect or kinship (
e.g.
, the notion of an uncle)
to scientific quantities like acceleration, the idea of natural selection in biology,
a random variable in statistics, a semigroup in mathematics, and so on. It is these linguistically scaffolded
abstractions that allow generalization across widely different contexts and enable
sophisticated reasoning, explanation, and prediction. None of these are exercised by visual tests.
So, while success on visual tasks might well provide some
signal on intelligence, it should not be understood as indicative of general
or high-level abstraction ability. The latter goes much farther than what is
visually given, by relying on language to represent categories that
depend on inferential roles rather than perceptual relations.
18
18

18

Modern developmental science indicates that conceptual abstraction is produced
by different learning systems than perceptual abstraction, emerges
at different developmental stages, and is subserved by different mechanisms,
requiring cross-modal integration, lexicalization, executive function,
and the learning of rule-based categories. According to
79
,
the brain contains two distinct category-leaning systems. One is a similarity-based
system characterized by compression, which learns from perceptual correlations,
operates on many overlapping features, is present early in development, and is tied to the
inferotemporal cortex and perceptual systems; this is the system that learns “dense” categories
like dogs and cats. The other is a rule-based system characterized by selection,
which selects specific diagnostic dimensions, supports rules, requires attention and executive control,
is tied to the prefrontal cortex, and develops later; this is the system that learns “sparse” categories,
like prime numbers and moral agency.
79
points out that sparse categories tend to depend
on unobservable properties and require selective attention and rule learning;
and that abstract reasoning in mathematics and science
depends on representational resources that go far beyond visual structure: the
ability to formulate and manipulate concepts whose defining properties are
unobservable, inferred, and linguistically articulated.

It might be objected that ARC was never intended to test high-level reasoning or abstraction;
what has made it notable is that powerful AI models have found it challenging even though it
is a fairly simple test that many ordinary people are able to pass.
However, ARC aims to be a test of “abstraction and reasoning” simpliciter,
not a test of perceptual abstraction and visuospatial reasoning. The paper that introduced
the benchmark speaks of a general measure of intelligence, and
the relevant Kaggle competition presents itself as a test to find the next
frontier of AGI, implying human-level mastery across all cognitive domains.
As for the observation that models are struggling with something that even children can do,
and the resulting temptation to conclude that models are incapable of reasoning,
it should be kept in mind that a 5-year-old human has a 500-million-year evolutionary
head start in visual processing. The human brain dedicates a massive portion of its cortex to
automatic, unconscious visual grouping (Gestalt principles like proximity, similarity, and closure).
For humans, “seeing” the grid and grouping the pixels requires zero effort.
This raises the question of whether the exclusion of language in order to be fair to humans
might not tip the scales the other way and make the test unfair to language models.

A brief historical aside might be helpful here: nonverbal intelligence tests originated in the 1800s
because scientists were curious about the cognitive abilities of language-deprived people
who could not speak. During World War I, the US Army needed “methods to assess
abilities of foreign born and illiterate military recruits” and a committee to design
such methods was formed
[
59
]
[p. 5].
Later in the 20th century, fairness came to be an increasingly prominent
motivation behind nonverbal intelligence tests, some of which (such as the Universal
Nonverbal Intelligence Test
[
18
]
) had fairness as their “overarching
goal”
[
59
]
[p. 88].
After all, if one hardly speaks English, it is not fair to give them an intelligence
test that requires fluency in English.
By the same token, however, it is not fair to give a visual intelligence test to a subject
who is visually impaired.
And to this day, the perceptual abilities of language models,
even multi-modal ones, remain relatively poor, lagging far behind their language skills.
This is largely because transformer-based models, which were designed for sequence statistics,
lack the geometric and physical priors encoded in the human brain,
i.e.
, they lack most
of the core-knowledge priors that humans have (objects, actions, and space).
19
19

19

Just as they lack grounding
in the physical world, with no direct experience of physical interactions such as movement
and object manipulation, and no experience of temporal continuity.
A baby learns about object persistence by interacting with the physical world,
not by ingesting a static Internet snapshot.

Given that ARC seems heavily gated by perceptual access to the relevant tasks,
visual ability may be acting as a confound. This is essentially the argument made by recent work
[
97
]
,
which found that models frequently fail on ARC tasks before reasoning even begins,
simply because they are unable to reliably identify objects, shapes, and spatial relations
(something which is effortless for humans), and therefore end up misinterpreting the visual input.
Manual error analysis carried out by the authors suggests that 80% of model failures are due to perceptual errors.
They also found that when the task is split into a 2-stage pipeline, a perception stage that takes
the image and outputs a natural-language description of it, followed by a “reasoning” stage that takes
the natural-language description and reasons with it, performance improves significantly when the results
of the first (perceptual) step are improved,
e.g.
, simply by using a stronger visual model, while keeping
the reasoning model fixed. The authors thus argue that ARC results can overestimate the
reasoning gap between humans and AI, because humans have much stronger visual object recognition
and perceptual grouping.

These considerations suggest a divergence between the methodological guidelines
in the paper that introduced ARC and the empirical benchmark ecosystem that
grew around it.
26
[p. 45] writes that a general benchmark of intelligence
“should work for both humans and machines, fairly, by only assuming the same priors as possessed
by humans (e.g. Core Knowledge) and only requiring a human-sized amount of practice time or training data”
and “we recommend only comparing the intelligence of systems that assume a sufficiently similar
set of priors” (p. 44). But in practice the test is routinely used to compare the intelligence
of humans and transformer-based models, which have radically different priors.

To be clear, we are not claiming that all reasoning is linguistic, nor that ARC fails to
test reasoning altogether. There are well-established non-linguistic forms of
reasoning, visuospatial reasoning being one of them, and ARC does appear to tap abilities in this domain;
we would not be surprised if ARC performance is shown to correlate with
gV
.
What remains empirically unclear is exactly which abilities ARC measures
and how strongly performance on the benchmark correlates with broader measures of intelligence,
and particularly which of the prominent CHC factors it loads on most strongly.
Our claim is that a vast and very important portion of human reasoning and abstraction
is conceptual or theoretical: it concerns categories, relations, and entities that are not
directly perceptible and whose representation and manipulation are inextricably linked with language.
Because nonverbal tests like ARC exclude language by design and operate over
