fully observable structures, they leave this large and very significant
class of reasoning capabilities untested.
20
20

20

An analogy between
reasoning and humor might help to clarify our position.
Language is not strictly necessary for reasoning, in the same way that it is not necessary for humor—consider Chaplin’s
silent films, slapstick, timing, visual surprise, incongruous or absurd gesture (Monty Python’s
ministry of silly walks), and so on. At the same time, the most effective and intelligent humor
is conveyed with the use of language, which enables irony, parody, understatement, narrative framing,
double meaning, register shifts, self-reference, and the management of multiple perspectives at once.
We would likewise claim that the most sophisticated forms of reasoning
are enabled by and expressed through language and symbolism, which allow for explicit premises and conclusions,
scope distinctions, quantification, modality, counterfactuals, recursive embeddings, and above all
the articulation of why something follows. Taking silent pratfalls as the essence of comedy
would be like taking grid transformations as the essence of reasoning.

Finally, even though ARC was designed as a test that
does not use language, largely in an effort to eliminate reliance on memorized patterns
and to measure fluid intelligence, many of the systems that perform best on ARC
are neuro-symbolic pipelines that do not perform simple end-to-end prediction
of the output grid
[
25
]
. Instead, they parse
the grid into objects, regions, and colors; they express candidate
solutions in a DSL (domain-specific language) or restricted Python-like program space;
they explicitly search over compositions of primitives, keeping only candidates that fit
the training examples; and then run the resulting program on the test input.
This process effectively maps these problems back into a symbolic
language
(Python or DSLs)
and relies on formal syntax, semantics, and logic to synthesize a solution.
And given that models are pretrained on terabytes of Python code, this allows them
to lean on the massive linguistic and structural priors they memorized
during pretraining, which seems at odds with a core-knowledge test.
While it’s true that the tasks themselves are purely visual, if the solver is a giant
model pretrained on vast bodies of Python, iteration patterns, grid manipulation idioms, and so on,
and relies on that know-how during problem solving, then language priors
re-enter through the back door.

2.1.4
Formal Reasoning

The final category comprises benchmarks whose input problems are fully formal, expressed in symbolic logic or formal mathematical notation.
These benchmarks are designed to probe reasoning in a highly controlled setting, abstracted away from the vagaries of natural language
or domain-specific conventions.

Unlike natural-language or generalist benchmarks, which often involve a mix of cognitive competencies and inferential strategies,
formal reasoning benchmarks aim to isolate and evaluate deductive capacity in its purest form. The tasks typically involve proving
or refuting precise claims, or applying logical rules within a constrained formal system.
Most benchmarks in the category comprise contentful but formally structured math problems (as in MiniF2F
[
108
]
);
datasets like
ProofGrid
that aim to capture content-agnostic reasoning are relatively rare
(Rosetta
[
7
]
is arguably another example, but see our comments below).

A key advantage of such benchmarks is that they are interpretable and verifiable: because the logic is fully specified,
it is possible to determine unambiguously whether a response is correct and to assess the structure of the reasoning that led to it.
This makes them especially well-suited for fine-grained diagnostic evaluation of a model’s inferential abilities. Moreover,
the absence of linguistic ambiguity or commonsense knowledge demands allows these benchmarks to more cleanly separate
reasoning skill from linguistic fluency or background knowledge.

That said, formal reasoning benchmarks introduce their own complications. They tend to require a high degree of familiarity
with the underlying logic or proof calculus, which makes them less accessible to general-purpose models or to researchers unfamiliar
with the target formalism. Some benchmarks are grounded in complex systems like higher-order logic or dependent type theory,
or rely on interactive proof protocols
(such as Lean-style tactic traces) that complicate the relationship between proof generation and proof explanation,
as we discuss later.
ProofGrid
mitigates these challenges by adopting a minimalist
natural deduction
language,
featuring the same general style of reasoning commonly found in mathematics and logic textbooks.
That language is fully described in a concise self-contained prompt.

All in all, formal reasoning benchmarks play an essential role in the ecosystem of reasoning evaluation,
offering a uniquely rigorous lens on deductive competence.

2.2
Reasoning Dimensions

To better understand the diversity of reasoning datasets and benchmarks in NLP and AI in general,
we position these along three main orthogonal axes:

1.

an
input representation
axis;

2.

an
output representation
axis; and a

3.

a
content
or
crystallized knowledge
axis.

These axes reflect conceptually independent dimensions that shape the kinds of reasoning that a benchmark can
evaluate.
21
21

21

The axes are not continuous,
but orthogonality is more important than continuity in a conceptual framework of this kind.
A more granular analysis could refine each independent dimension into a categorical
or ordinal feature with a specific set of interpretable values.
We have already taken preliminary steps in this direction,
e.g.
,
we introduce a number of discrete levels for the output-representation axis:

binary
,
multiple-choice
,
literal
,
chain-of-thought
,
entailment tree
,

Python code
, and
formal proof
. We leave the development of a finer-grained and exhaustive
classification scheme to future work. Note also that while we treat the input representation
and content axes as conceptually orthogonal, in practice they are not always independent.
In particular, benchmarks with natural language inputs almost inevitably carry
some
semantic
content, even when synthetically constructed. Although it is possible to design grammatically
well-formed yet semantically vacuous sentences in Jabberwocky style (
e.g.
, “Every glorb is a zibble”),
such examples are highly contrived. Thus, while the axes are formally distinct, they
may be somewhat statistically correlated in practice.

The first axis captures the form in which the inputs of a dataset are expressed. This could be naturalistic
human language, or synthetically generated natural language, or formal symbolic notation, or something in between.
This choice usually reflects the motivation behind the benchmark. Datasets like SNLI and MNLI are squarely in
the mainstream NLI tradition and are thus invariably focused on natural language. Their main aim is to test
whether language models can simulate human-like reasoning from textual input.
Towards the middle of this axis we find datasets like RuleTaker and FLD, which are formulated in the sort
of controlled natural language (CNL) described earlier: templated English sentences generated from symbolic rules.
This results in data that is expressed in natural language but is formulaic, with very limited linguistic diversity,
and semantically aligned with formal logic. Finally, at the right end of this axis we find datasets like
Rosetta, whose problems are expressed entirely in formal symbolic notation, using logics
of varying representational power (like propositional, first-order, or higher-order logic).

The second axis characterizes the structure and complexity of the required model outputs.
On one end we have tasks that demand only a binary decision, such as whether a hypothesis follows from a context (
e.g.
, entailment vs. non-entailment),
or the selection of a single correct answer from multiple choices. These setups are straightforward
to evaluate and model, but offer limited insight into the actual reasoning that models perform.
On the other end of the spectrum are tasks requiring more structured reasoning artifacts, such as chains
of deductive steps, natural-language justifications (
e.g.
, COT traces), entailment trees
(as in EntailmentBank), or even full formal proofs (as in
ProofGrid
). These richer outputs enable
finer-grained analysis of a model’s reasoning process and can support interpretability,
proof checking, and compositional generalization, but typically demand more supervision and
are harder to generate.

The third axis characterizes reasoning benchmarks in terms of their content demands,
capturing the type and extent of domain knowledge required to solve them. At one end of this spectrum
we have content-free benchmarks, such as
ProofGrid
, which involve purely symbolic structural reasoning
tasks in uninterpreted settings. These tasks rely on the internal structure of the inference tasks and
require little external knowledge. Farther along this axis we find
benchmarks grounded in commonsense or everyday content, such as SNLI or bAbI
[
102
]
, where understanding is based
on familiar types of objects, events, and social conventions. Moving along, we have benchmarks involving
scientific content, like GPQA
[
72
]
or ARC (Challenge Set)
[
27
]
, which require reasoning over concepts and relations
introduced by scientific theories, and are thus accessible only to those with some scientific education.
At the far end of the axis we have pure math benchmarks such as MATH, GSM8K, or Lean-based proof datasets,
where the content is fully mathematical, and reasoning requires both mathematical knowledge and
symbolic manipulation. Although diverse, these benchmarks fall along a coherent continuum that reflects
increasing degrees of semantic specificity, abstraction, and crystallized domain knowledge.
Framing this as a content axis allows us to compare reasoning tasks not just by how they are formulated,
but also by what kind of knowledge they demand.

In addition to these three primary axes, we identify three further dimensions that shed light on the internal
structure and reasoning demands of a benchmark: the expressiveness of the underlying formalism,
the range of inferential mechanisms required, and the diversity of its tasks.
These axes are secondary and only relevant for datasets that are grounded in formal logic or in natural language rendered from logical forms.

Formalism Expressiveness:

For benchmarks that are defined in, or translated from, a formal logic, including both
fully symbolic datasets like
ProofGrid
and CNL benchmarks like RuleTaker, a key consideration
is the expressive power of the underlying logical system. This axis captures the range of propositions that the
formalism can represent and thereby constrains the structural and semantic complexity of the problem instances.
For example, RuleTaker is grounded in stratified Datalog, which supports recursive Horn clauses and limited negation
but disallows full disjunction, unrestricted negation, quantifier alternation, and function symbols. Other benchmarks might be based on
propositional logic, first-order logic fragments, equational logic, description logics,
epistemic, modal, temporal, or spatial logics, or more powerful systems like higher-order logic or dependent type theory. Each of these formalisms permits
a different set of logical constructs (such as quantifiers, equality, modal operators, or arithmetic) which
in turn determine the space of conceivable problems. Two benchmarks may both use formal representations
yet differ dramatically in the depth and nuance of statements they can encode, and thus on the types of
reasoning that they can test, depending on where they lie on this dimension.

Inferential Repertoire:

This axis captures the range and sophistication of inference mechanisms
that a model must deploy to explain and justify its answers to a benchmark’s problems.
It is mostly relevant for benchmarks that require models to produce
structured reasoning artifacts

that are amenable to automated analysis, such as entailment trees or formal proofs.
For benchmarks that only require final answers, the model’s reasoning process remains opaque
and this axis becomes inert; without observable reasoning traces, it is not possible to determine
what inference methods were used, short of speculating about latent cognitive or
model-internal dynamics. The same applies to benchmarks that elicit
unstructured
reasoning
artifacts, such as chain-of-thought traces expressed in arbitrary natural language. Even if such traces
are generated during inference, they are typically not evaluated and are therefore of little help
in analyzing the underlying reasoning mechanisms.

When structured reasoning outputs
are
generated, this axis ranges from benchmarks that require
only simple chaining or forward inference using a small set of rudimentary rules (such as
modus ponens and conjunction elimination) to tasks that demand complex reasoning strategies such as
hypothetical proof, arbitrarily nested case analyses, universal generalization, reasoning by contradiction,
existential instantiation, and various forms of mathematical induction (natural, structural, strong,
etc.
).

While this dimension often correlates with the expressiveness of the formalism,
it is not reducible to it. A benchmark may employ an expressive logic in theory while
exercising only a weak fragment in practice. In such cases, the inferential repertoire axis
provides an independent informative lens on the range of reasoning tested by a benchmark.

Task Diversity:

Another important dimension for characterizing a reasoning benchmark is task breadth—the number
and variety of distinct reasoning tasks it includes. Some benchmarks are narrowly focused, posing a single core problem,
such as entailment: “Does hypothesis
h
follow from context
T
?” Even within that core task, variations can arise
depending on the output format (e.g., binary label vs. a supporting explanation in COT style), but
the central reasoning objective remains the same. Other benchmarks expand the task scope by supporting or requiring multiple
types of reasoning, each probing different varieties of inferential ability. For example, in addition to entailment
verification, a benchmark might include tasks like countermodel generation (demonstrating that
T\not\models h
),
error diagnosis (identifying why a hypothesis fails), or hypothesis repair (suggesting minimal modifications that ensure
T\models h
).
The number and diversity of these subtasks can be treated as a measure of a benchmark’s functional richness.

Greater task diversity broadens what a dataset can reveal and enables finer-grained assessment of a model’s reasoning,
but it also complicates evaluation and interpretation. Benchmarks with many distinct subtasks can suffer from a lack of cohesion, potentially
conflating heterogeneous cognitive demands. This can make it difficult to attribute model success or failure to specific competencies.
Finally, aggregating performance across many diverse tasks tends to lack a principled foundation,
raising questions about how to interpret a single combined score. As such, while broad task coverage
offers diagnostic benefits, there is also value in relatively focused benchmarks with tightly
defined reasoning objectives. Diversity is most effective when the subtasks are conceptually coherent
and mutually reinforcing, rather than arbitrarily bundled together. This axis thus highlights
a tension between diagnostic richness and interpretive clarity.

Together, these auxiliary axes complement the existing framework and offer finer-grained tools for analyzing what
makes a benchmark challenging.

lists a number of datasets that are intended, either entirely or in part,

along with a few additional attributes such as the maximum reasoning depth required (a rough ceiling on problem difficulty);
current saturation status; and whether a model’s reasoning is verified programmatically.

—
) under

means that the dataset has no fixed size. This usually means that the authors provide a script
that can generate any number of data points, often set to a certain default value
(
e.g.
, 5K in the case of LogicInference
[

, p. 10]
).

Table 1:
Qualitative comparison of 33 reasoning datasets. A long dash (
—
) means NA (not applicable).
Expr.
represents the expressiveness of the underlying logic
(when one exists).
Range
represents the diversity of reasoning mechanisms tested by a benchmark.
R. Depth
is the maximum reasoning depth required.

Satur.
is the benchmark’s saturation level.
Verif.
indicates whether a benchmark programmatically verifies the model’s reasoning. NL: Natural Language;
CNL: Controlled Natural Language; COT: Chain of Thought; MCQA: Multiple-Choice Question Answering; PL: Propositional Logic;
S. Datalog: Stratified Datalog; PHL: Positive Horn Logic;
\mbox{FOL}_{0}
= propositional logic plus a very small fragment of FOL;
EQL: (unconditional) Equational Logic; HOL: Higher-Order Logic.

ProntoQA’s logic is positive Horn logic, which is reducible to Datalog over unary predicates.
The few negated properties in ProntoQA are just specially named predicates; they do not introduce or need
the semantic machinery—and potential non-monotonicity—of true stratified negation.
Accordingly, the only reasoning mechanism that is needed for ProntoQA proofs is modus ponens.

Although the
EntailmentBank
paper
[
32
]
outlines three tasks, these are all varying
scales of the same problem, entailment tree construction, so we have consolidated them under a single task designation.
We have listed
ProofGrid
’s task number as 4/15 to indicate that there are 4 general types of tasks but with 15
separate datasets/instantiations.
We report MMLU as comprising 57 tasks, following the paper that introduced the benchmark
[
42
]
, but note that
these are not 57 distinct task types in terms of input/output format or basic problem-solving requirements.
Instead, they all share the same basic task structure, namely, multiple-choice question answering (MCQA), typically
with 4 answer choices. The variation across the 57 datasets lies entirely in the domain or subject matter (STEM questions,
social-science questions, anatomy, logical fallacies, moral disputes, and so on).
22
22

22

It’s an unfortunate
trend that
task
is becoming increasingly conflated with
subject matter
. As another example,
the paper that introduced the HLE benchmark (Humanity’s Last Exam) characterizes benchmarks as
“collections of questions which assess model performance on tasks such as
math, programming, or biology”
[
76
, p. 4]
.

A more recent version of this benchmark, MMLU-Pro
[
98
]
, was introduced in 2024 with the aim of filtering
out trivial questions from MMLU; reducing the number of categories from 57 to 14; incorporating new questions from
TheoremQA
[
23
]
, SciBench, and the STEM website; and adding several additional distractor options to multiple-choice questions.
Nevertheless, even that version is nearing saturation, with o3, Grok-4, DeepSeek-R1, every Gemini model from Gemini-2.5 onward
and every GPT model from GPT-5 onward all clustering between 84% and 92% accuracy.

Hybrid benchmarks like BBH and BBEH are marked as MCQA even though they typically include COT-based prompting setups,
because the elicited COT traces are not evaluated.

Note that some datasets include structured reasoning artifacts but we have nonetheless marked them as unable to verify
a model’s reasoning because the models themselves are not required to produce any such artifacts.
This is the case,
e.g.
, for LogicNLI
[
91
]
. Its hypotheses are synthetically generated by starting
with premises and then applying a sequence of
n\leq 5
inference rules until a final conclusion
p
is obtained.
The resulting proof trace
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}=p
is stored in the dataset as the canonical derivation of
p
.
When a model correctly classifies
p
as entailed by the starting premises, the authors then check to see if the
model also accepts the canonical proof’s intermediate conclusions
p_{i}
, a test they dub “proof traceability”

[
91
, p. 3741]
. However, this is mostly a behavioral test of response coherence or consistency
rather than proof traceability. Since the model produces no explicit reasoning and its internal process is opaque,
agreement on
p_{i}
offers no evidence that the model actually followed the canonical derivation path.

Some CNL datasets exercise a relatively large number of inference rules
but are still classified as low-diversity, meaning that they test a small range of reasoning mechanisms.
This is either because the large majority of the rules are drawn from a single logic (
e.g.
,
LogicInference supports 19 inference rules, but 17 of them are for propositional logic and
only 2 for predicate logic); or because they represent superficial template
variants that don’t capture any fundamentally new abilities.
It could be argued that inference rules in general are just one
type of reasoning mechanism, so rule count alone is a poor indicator of
reasoning breadth. In addition, there are several inference idioms,
pervasive in everyday mathematical and logical discourse,
that cannot be captured by simple inference rules operating on sentences.
Chief among them is conditional reasoning, where one makes a provisional hypothesis
and proceeds to derive consequences from it and other background assumptions, ultimately discharging
it and deducing a conditional. To represent this type of reasoning faithfully, one needs a notion
of assumption scope, which simple inferences rules do not provide. In principle, hypothetical reasoning
could be captured by inference rules that operate on sequents rather than plain
sentences, but none of these datasets work with sequents.
23
23

23

A CNL benchmark based on
sequent reasoning would be somewhat at odds with the genre. These benchmarks strive to retain natural language,
even if it’s at the level of a synthetic veneer, as an implicit gesture toward
cognitive plausibility—they aim to simulate, however imperfectly, the surface form of human reasoning.
By contrast, formal abstractions like sequents, while useful for rigorous analysis of logic and for proof theory,
depart significantly from the style in which humans express their inferences in practice,
and so their use would introduce structural artificiality into the benchmark.

Even if they did, something important would
be lost in translation because sequents lack the hierarchical assumption nesting
that is a defining feature of conditional reasoning in the
wild.
24
24

24

This is not to say that sequent systems can’t represent conditional reasoning
at all (clearly they can), only that they do so in a very different style.
The situation is analogous to the distinction between structured programming languages and assembly/machine languages.
The latter don’t have the hierarchical nesting that is needed for natural descriptions of algorithms,
but they are Turing-complete nevertheless.

Reasoning by contradiction is essentially of the same type. Eigenvariables used in universal
generalization (“Consider an arbitrary
x
such that
\cdots
”) provide yet another example
of a structured and scope-based reasoning mechanism that cannot be faithfully captured
by simple inference rules.
Mental-model theorists
[
46
]
would go further and argue that reasoning is primarily semantic, driven by
model-based entailment analysis rather than inference rules. This rings true in mathematics,
where a typical step is “
p
follows from
\cdots
,” not an application of a specific low-level
rule like modus tollens. It is up to the reader to verify that there is no model
in which
\cdots
are true and
p
is false.
(In the setting of propositional logic,
ProofGrid
captures this pervasive idiom
with
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
, which does away with all inference rules in favor of semantic entailment;
see Section
7.1
.)

We have listed the depth of reasoning of GSM8K
[
29
]
as 8 even though, strictly speaking,
the dataset does not contain any proof trees or other reasoning artifacts. However, the paper
that introduced the benchmark notes that “problems take between 2 and 8 steps to solve, and solutions
primarily involve performing a sequence of elementary calculations using basic arithmetic operations
to reach the final answer,” so we use this quantity as a proxy for reasoning depth.
Even though OlympiadBench also has 2334 physics questions, we list its size as 6142 because that’s
the number of math questions it contains. OlympiadBench contains detailed solutions as well (in natural
language), although models are only evaluated on their final answers.
We have classified Omni-Math as highly saturated because o3-mini’s accuracy on that benchmark already
exceeded 80% in 2025
[
9
, p. 2]
, without falling below 50% on any categories.

We have listed NL for the input form of the problems of LogicQA 2.0
[
54
]
, because these were obtained from naturally occurring text,
adapted from the Chinese Civil Service Examination.
The English version was obtained by machine translation and may inject ambiguities and other issues into a
problem (though the authors remark that the quality of the translation is substantially better in version 2.0).

For FLD
[
64
]
, 30K is the size of each variant of the corpus, but there are multiple “dot variants” of the form FLD.
k

for
k=0,1,\ldots,5
. Each FLD.
k
dataset flips at most one design knob at a time (such as the syntactic complexity
of the sentences, the depth of the proof trees, the number of distractors, and so on), so that
controlled ablation experiments can be run to see which aspect of reasoning is more challenging for
language models.
25
25

25

Multiple corpus variants with different profiles are common in this
line of research (a tradition going back to RuleTaker, which introduced five datasets varying by reasoning depth).

In theory, FLD supports full first-order logic, albeit without equality or function symbols. In practice,
the fragment it exercises is far more limited, essentially confined to monadic predicates without quantifier alternations.
This reflects a broader trend among CNL benchmarks.
Even though these are solidly framed in logic, many end up targeting
very weak subsets of predicate logic. This is partly due to the sheer complexity
of the bookkeeping infrastructure that is needed for a correct implementation of full-blown proofs in first-order logic.
Implementing a sound and complete inference system for full FOL is technically demanding.
It requires careful handling of variable scope, alpha-conversion, avoiding variable capture,
and enforcing a number of subtle caveats. For instance, the witness variable in an existential
elimination must be fresh and have no free occurrences in the conclusion. These provisos
are not even mentioned in the formulation of existential instantiation in FLD
[
64
, Fig. B.3(a), p. 15]
.
This raises questions.
None of these benchmarks provide
any guarantee that the generated proofs are correctly implemented and tested.
Soundness rests entirely on a Python codebase, typically built from scratch for the purposes of the benchmark.
The risk of error is mitigated by the severe restrictions imposed on the richness
of the sentences and proofs that are allowed, but the restrictions also mean that
these benchmarks are far from exercising the more challenging—and interesting—parts
of predicate logic. Using a preexisting and already-vetted proof-engineering system as an off-the-shelf
component to handle the drudgery of proof representation and manipulation
has the advantage of minimizing the benchmark’s trusted computing base.
The creators of FLD note that they avoid the use of external logic systems in order to maintain
greater control over data generation, but such use need not constrain the selection of axioms,
the depth of proofs, the size of formulas, or most of the parameters that benchmarks are
typically interested in tweaking.

NLSat
[
74
]
is notable in that, like the
\textsc{PL}_{3}
division of
ProofGrid
, it attempts to build
computationally hard inference problems. But it does so not by drawing from well-known
families of complex problems but rather by random sampling near the critical region of the 3-SAT
phase transition. The resulting problems pose simple yes/no (
sat
or
unsat
) questions.
There are no proofs or other reasoning artifacts, so reasoning depth is impossible to measure.
The authors do provide an algorithm-specific measure of complexity, namely the average and median numbers
of backtracking conflicts and variable assignments reported by the
\mbox{DPPL}(T)
algorithm used by Z3
when solving the generated problems; the maximum average and median was 22 and 13, respectively.

LogicBench
[
69
]
and Multi-LogicEval
[
70
]
include a number of non-monotonic
inference patterns embedded in FOL. Multi-LogicEval does not require any proofs, but their results were
obtained with zero-shot COT prompting and they performed a manual analysis of the output COT traces.

FrontierMath
has 300 core problems, organized into 3 tiers of difficulty, with an extra 50 in a fourth tier,
so we have listed 350 as the total size.
The table does not include recent math datasets like NuminaMath
[
52
]
and Big-Math
[
2
]
,
because these are much bigger and primarily intended for training (particularly via RL) rather than benchmarking.
We likewise omit logic-based datasets like Rosetta
[
7
]
, as they are also mainly intended for training.
Rosetta could be used for evaluation as well, but only after one has fine-tuned a model on the training split,
because the input format of the problems is a deliberately obfuscated notation (even though the problem structure
is propositional), in order to force models to focus on reasoning rather than memorization of surface-syntax patterns.
As a result, off-the-shelf models would not fare well on these problems, especially in a zero-shot setting,
and the dataset does not provide any scaffolding for ICL prompting. Either way, models are not require to produce
proofs or any other reasoning artifacts.

Similar issues arise in datasets like HOL Step
[
47
]
and LeanDojo
[
104
]
.
These are designed to train models to perform highly specific tasks in tactic-based
proof systems like HOL and Lean, such as:

•

Premise selection
: Given a goal
g
and an available result
p
, determine whether
p
will
be useful in proving
g
.

•

Next-tactic selection
: Given the current proof-search state (a notion that is specific to tactic-based
theorem provers), encoded as a string, along with a ranked list of premises (generated by a premise-selection component)
representing available lemmas, predict the next tactic to apply (a function that takes a sequent as input and produces another sequent as output).

Neither was designed to test a model’s general ability to reason with or about proofs, and
neither provides prompt scaffolding for ICL-testing of off-the-shelf models.
If one wrote prompts from scratch (see footnote
28
), one could attempt to use LeanDojo
as a test set to be given to an off-the-shelf model, but doing so would mostly gauge the model’s ability
to generate a Lean tactic that is syntactically valid and semantically applicable to the input proof state.
This requires the model to be deeply familiar not just with general Lean syntax
and semantics but also with its extensive tactic library; and more importantly, the result would not tell us whether the
predicted tactic would eventually lead to success.

In general, tactic-based interactive theorem-proving frameworks are primarily geared towards verification
rather than presenting reasoning in the structured and hierarchical style that is characteristic of human-written
proofs.
26
26

26

That said, broader ecosystems grown around such frameworks increasingly support mixed styles,
structured proofs, comments, local lemmas, and higher-level proof organization.

The main representation of a proof in such a framework is a sequence of system-specific
tactic applications. These tactics are usually applied in a backward fashion, decomposing a goal into subgoals until
the process reaches axioms or trivially solvable leaves. Although this analytical process does operationalize
the
search
for a proof object,
it does not directly function as a proof in the usual expository or narrative sense;
instead, it’s an execution trace that records a proof-search trajectory.
This is an important distinction. The tactic trace can convey key aspects of strategy
(
e.g.
, which lemmas drive the argument, where cases split, or which
invariant is maintained), but the overall structure of the reasoning is encoded implicitly and unevenly.
It is dispersed across highly procedural steps that can obscure the high-level organization of the argument,
especially for non-expert users of the system, instead of presenting it in a stable, declarative, and
hierarchically articulated proof scaffold. Lean’s core does ultimately generate a proof term, a fully elaborated term in the Calculus of Inductive Constructions
that certifies the correctness of the theorem. However, these proof terms are typically opaque and unwieldy,
even for experienced users. They are often closer to compiled machine code than to conventional mathematical argument.
In practice, even seasoned Lean users seldom engage with these proof terms directly, instead treating the sequence of tactics
itself as the canonical proof.
As a result, benchmarks based on such systems are better viewed as evaluations of system-specific
verification abilities than as direct tests of declarative, human-interpretable
reasoning.
27
27

27

Of course, even if tactic sequences aren’t structured like natural deduction,
they still represent how real users build proofs interactively and hence they do capture
useful reasoning artifacts. For users working
within
a system like Lean, tactic sequences
are familiar and perfectly functional. But while they are useful operational artifacts, they are not
accessible and structurally clear explanations of deductive inference, which is what would be needed for
measuring interpretable reasoning.

In addition, because Lean is a complex domain-specific formal language, such benchmarks are accessible
mostly to models with substantial prior exposure to it. Barring fine-tuning, meaningful performance is
unlikely for models that have not been heavily pretrained on Lean code and proofs, and even then,
performance can remain sensitive to nuances in the training distribution.
28
28

28

One might
point out that a system like Lean is just another formal language, and any formal language can be
described in a prompt. After all, this is exactly what was done with NDL in
ProofGrid
.
In principle, this is a reasonable point. If Lean was a simple language, then in-context learning via prompting could suffice.
But Lean is a large and multi-layered system with all the inevitable idiosyncrasies that come with such scale.
It has a dependent type theory core (the Calculus of Inductive Constructions); thousands of predefined tactics and lemmas;
a rich module system; a highly non-trivial unification and elaboration behavior; and so on.
In practice, even advanced models fail to generate correct Lean code unless heavily pretrained on it.
Prompting might help to guide or structure a solution, but it can’t replace deep preexisting familiarity with Lean.

This makes such benchmarks less well suited for evaluating a model’s general reasoning abilities.
Additionally, running such benchmarks requires substantial Lean-specific infrastructure,
including specialized parsing, proof-checking, and goal-state tracking machinery, often tightly coupled to particular
versions of the system. The associated tooling overhead places additional constraints on the
accessibility and extensibility of such benchmarks.

Finally, the content of benchmarks like LeanDojo is not abstract logic or untyped
symbolic reasoning, but rather interpreted formal mathematics from subjects like number theory.
As such, the benchmark is deeply contentful: solving its problems depends not only on
fluency in manipulating Lean tactics but also on substantial crystallized mathematical knowledge, including
a large number of domain-specific results (as well as notational conventions and standard proof idioms).
This positions LeanDojo and similar datasets at
the far end of the content axis in our taxonomy, where reasoning is tightly entangled with deep formalized subject matter.
This makes them useful for evaluating mathematical assistants or theorem provers in specialized domains
within the Lean ecosystem, but less suitable as general-purpose benchmarks for testing fluid or broadly transferable reasoning.

The table also omits datasets like
NaturalProofs

[
100
]
, which contains a large number of mathematical
proofs (32K) in natural language, mostly culled from
ProofWiki
. Although it is a useful
resource, this dataset presents a significant challenge for the streamlined benchmarking of proof-writing ability,
as it is not currently possible to reliably evaluate an arbitrary natural-language proof attempt.
The two tasks studied in the paper that introduced this dataset were the retrieval and generation of the
references
that appear
in a given proof (definitions, other cited theorems, and so on); such references are stored as part of the dataset
and hence candidate solutions to both tasks can be mechanically checked for correctness. A subsequent publication
by the same research group did introduce proof-writing tasks, both at the global level of the entire proof and at
the finer-grained level of the next proof step
[
101
]
. However, the issue remains: there is
no known sound and complete algorithm for evaluating an arbitrary piece of natural-language text that purports to prove
a mathematical result. The authors took a pragmatic approach to this by combining
manual evaluation by human experts with the use of computable metrics that can serve as proxies for
proof quality, such as lexical similarity with the gold proof, using metrics like GLEU and Token F1,
and “knowledge grounding” (overlap of the definitions and results cited by the proof attempt with those
of the gold proof). Unfortunately, manual evaluation does not scale, while the heuristics are clearly not sound
(as the authors implicitly acknowledge). A mathematical result can have many different proofs, each relying on
a somewhat different set of previous results and even a different set of definitions.
Nevertheless, the metrics show a strong positive correlation with human judgments of correctness,
suggesting they are useful proxies. Accordingly, this work should be understood as an interesting
pragmatic evaluation methodology, or a framework that can provide a meaningful but imperfect signal about the
ability of models to prove mathematical theorems in natural language.

The 488 data points in MiniF2F are divided between 244 test and 244 validation problems with a stratified
random split ensuring that each half has a balanced set of problems from each category and level of difficulty.
The table marks the maximum reasoning depth of MiniF2F as NA (
—
) for several fundamental reasons that
distinguish it from benchmarks like RuleTaker or ProofWriter, and our own
ProofGrid
.
First, MiniF2F provides only problem statements, not machine-checkable proofs.
This helps to prevent leakage. Although third-party groups have released separate proofs for certain subsets of the corpus,
such as the Lean proof pack for the 14 IMO problems in MiniF2F’s test split, which was recently published by
105
,
these are not part of the benchmark itself.
Without a canonical proof corpus, there are no ground-truth objects that can be used
to measure properties like proof depth.

Even if a comprehensive proof corpus were available, reasoning depth
is a function of the chosen proof system. MiniF2F is a multi-platform benchmark,
with problems formalized in Lean, Metamath, Isabelle, and HOL Light. The structure and length of a proof can vary
dramatically between these systems. For example, a proof in Metamath, which is based on a minimal set of axioms
and a single rule of inference (modus ponens), is typically a long, linear sequence of low-level steps. In contrast,
a proof in a system like Lean or Isabelle can be much more concise by leveraging not only a rich library of
previously proven lemmas but also powerful automation tactics. A single-line proof in Lean might correspond
to thousands of steps in Metamath, undermining any attempt to define a system-independent depth metric.

Tactics add considerable complexity to the notion of reasoning depth in automated proof assistants.
Tactics are high-level abstractions that automate sequences of logical
inferences, typically applied backwards (in an analytic and proof-search-flavored rather than a synthetic proof-building fashion).
A single call to the
nlinarith
tactic in Lean, for instance, can solve complex non-linear real
arithmetic problems by invoking a sophisticated decision procedure that may correspond to many thousands of primitive logical steps.
Is this one step or thousands? The answer depends on the desired level of abstraction. This is analogous to measuring
program complexity by lines of code: a single call to a powerful library function like quicksort represents one
“step” at a high level of abstraction, but it encapsulates a complex algorithm with a non-trivial number of primitive operations.
Because modern theorem proving relies heavily on such abstractions, a simple “proof tree height” metric fails to
capture actual reasoning complexity.

In the case of computation, complexity theory has found a way to talk
about the number of steps in an algorithm independently of specific languages or machine models, even when those steps
involve arbitrarily complex function calls. This is done with the introduction of key abstractions like
Turing machines or RAM programs, which serve as standardized low-level computational models.
Even if we write a program in a high-level language, the analysis assumes it can be compiled down to a sequence of basic steps in the low-level machine.
So when we say that an algorithm runs in
O(n^{2})
time, we’re measuring how many primitive steps in the underlying computation
model are taken, assuming that the high-level constructs are compiled into those. Crucially, this works because the chosen model
(
e.g.
, Turing machines or RAM programs) is universal and robust under simulations, owing to Turing completeness:
any reasonable programming language can be compiled to it with at most a polynomial overhead. There is no widely accepted analogue
for deduction, although in principle one could fix a low-level proof system that does not allow for tactics or proof search,
and then approximate the reasoning depth of a rich high-level proof---one that does
use tactics and performs arbitrary proof search---by measuring the size of the ‘‘expanded’’ certificate in the
bare-bones formalism.
29
29

29

Section 1.8.2 of
4
introduces a rigorous definition of deductive cost
in exactly this way.
Theoretically, this is defensible. It is the approach taken in proof complexity theory, for example, where
the analogue of time complexity is proof length, usually defined as the number of symbols or steps in a derivation
under some fixed low-level system (such a Frege system, resolution, or cutting planes). Different proof systems are
then analyzed on the basis of how efficiently they can prove tautologies (
e.g.
, with some theorems requiring exponentially
long proofs in one system but not in another).

However, in practice proofs introduce complications that do not arise—at least not to the same extent—in the case of pure computation.
In particular, there is often a sizable conceptual gap between the formal depth of a machine proof and the intuitive difficulty that a human perceives.
A problem that a mathematician considers to have a “two-step” solution might require hundreds or thousands of formal steps to rigorously
prove from first principles, including the handling of tedious side conditions and corner cases that a human would dismiss as trivial.
Conversely, a problem with high conceptual difficulty for a human might have a short, brute-force proof that is easily
found by machine. Thus, even though a general and domain-independent notion of formal reasoning depth could be calculated,
it would not necessarily correlate with the depth that is relevant to human mathematical reasoning.

In summary, due to the absence of a canonical proof set, the profound dependency on the chosen formal system, the variable
granularity of tactics, and the disconnect from human-centric problem difficulty, any single number for the
“maximum reasoning depth” of MiniF2F would likely be arbitrary. Similar remarks apply to the range of reasoning
mechanisms exercised by MiniF2F, which we have also marked as NA.

Figure 1:
A radar-chart comparison of
ProofGrid
and 5 logic-based CNL benchmarks.

The radar chart in Fig.
1
provides a visual comparison of
ProofGrid
and 5 reasoning benchmarks along eight evaluation axes
that reflect structural, logical, and operational characteristics. Each axis is normalized to a
[0,1]
scale, where higher values
indicate more favorable properties (e.g., more structured outputs, deeper reasoning, less saturation, and verifiable reasoning
artifacts).
30
30

30

For example, maximum-reasoning-depth values were normalized with min-max scaling, by replacing each value
d

by

\cfrac{d-d_{min}}{d_{max}-d_{min}}

where
d_{min}
and
d_{max}
are the smallest and largest
maximum reasoning depths. For the output axis, we set: Binary: 0, MCQA: 0.2, Literals: 0.4, COT: 0.6, Proof Trees/Entailment Trees: 0.8, Proofs: 1.0.
And likewise for the remaining dimensions.

A small amount of jitter is added to the values of some benchmarks to prevent visual overlap in the plot.
31
31

31

This occurs
when two or more benchmarks have identical scores across all axes—for example, LogicNLI and Folio share nearly the same profile.
Without jitter, the polygon representing one benchmark would be completely obscured by the other, making it invisible in the visualization.
The jitter values are very small (typically around 0.03) and do not materially affect the interpretive significance of the plot;
they only serve as a visual disambiguation aid.

ProofGrid
is the only dataset that scores maximally on all axes except expressiveness and reasoning range, where its values are moderate.
Overall, the plot illustrates how many CNL benchmarks cluster around low to medium values across most axes, while
ProofGrid
occupies a distinctive position as a structurally rich and logically grounded benchmark with a
broad and deep inferential footprint.

Table 2:
Data distribution summaries with inline box-sparklines for the 400 problems in
PL-PW1
.
Box = IQR (Interquartile Range); red line = median; dot = mean; whiskers = 5–95th.

Table
2
summarizes the distributions of several key
features of these problems. The first row, number of premises, pertains only to the 200 arguments that are not
conditionalized. The second row shows the distribution
of problem AST sizes. This is defined, for a given problem, as the sum of the sizes of all
premise ASTs plus the size of the goal AST.
32
32

32

The size of a formula AST (abstract syntax tree)
is simply the number of nodes in the tree.

This is not reflected in the table, but the 200 conditionalized problems tend to be
larger than the other 200 problems. The next five rows show the distributions of the five logical
connectives across all 400 problems. Conjunctions are the most common formulas, followed by
conditionals, negations, disjunctions, and finally biconditionals, which occur very infrequently
(averaging only 0.28 occurrences per problem).

The last feature, the number of atoms (propositional variables) in a problem, is particularly important.
This is a core indicator of logical difficulty. In this task, the median number of
atoms is 3, and in fact 75% of all problems have no more than 3 atoms.
In general, the number of propositional atoms bounds the logical space sharply, since

n
atoms admit exactly
2^{n}
possible truth assignments.
If
n=3
, only 8 valuations need to be distinguished, so no matter how complicated
the premises might appear syntactically, the underlying semantic structure is small.
This is a key reason why
PL1-PW
problems are so tractable—because they
collapse into small finite case analyses.

This is not to say that the structure and size of the premises and the goal are immaterial.
These can pose syntactic and structural challenges: how deeply formulas are
nested, which connectives occur where, how many subproofs must be opened and discharged,
and how effectively the relevant introduction and elimination rules can be coordinated.
All of these factors can affect proof discovery.
However, when the overall number of atoms is small, problems with many or deep premises
often have redundancies, contradictions, or tautologies that reduce to
something simple once the cases are enumerated.
Semantic difficulty, by contrast, correlates strongly with the number of truth assignments
that can be distinguished, and this scales directly with the number of atoms.
A central challenge in proof discovery,
at least in propositional logic, arises from the explosion of the search space
that occurs as the number of atoms increases.

3.1.2
Proof Checker Instrumentation

As an example, consider this relatively large conditionalized problem in
PL1-PW
:

This might look intimidating at first glance, but a more careful inspection of its structure, clarified by
writing the formula in prefix notation, shows that it is not difficult to prove:

⬇

1

(~

(|

E

~C)))

2

(&

~B

3

(|

C

E)))

4

(&

(|

(&

E

~E)

5

(==>

B

F))

6

(&

E

B)))

7

(&

(|

B

C)

8

(==>

B

9

(&

F

~A))))

Assuming the antecedent, which is a conjunction of several formulas, including
E
and
B
, we
need to derive the conjunction of
(B\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}C)
and the conditional
(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}F\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg A)
.
Since we are already given
B
, deriving
(B\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}C)
is trivial (by disjunction introduction). For the second
part, to derive
(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}F\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg A)
, we can assume
B
(which we already have anyway)
and show that both
F
and
\neg A
hold. We are given that
B
implies
F
,
33
33

33

More precisely, we are given (lines 5 and 6)
the disjunction of
(E\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg E)
and
(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}F)
, and this disjunction
is equivalent to
(\mbox{false\/}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}F))
, which, by
\neg\,\mbox{false\/}
and
disjunctive syllogism, yields the conditional
(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}F)

so, since we have
B
, modus ponens gives
F
and the only remaining goal is
\neg A
.
The latter follows by a
reductio
: if we had
A
, we could easily derive a contradiction
by a case analysis of the antecedent’s first conjunct (the disjunction on lines 1–4).
This is, essentially, the proof. Translating it into NDL is a bit tedious but not complicated.

The best models get this reasoning exactly right. Gemini-2.5-Pro, for example, offers the following
lucid account of its proof strategy:

The overall strategy is to prove the main conditional by assuming its antecedent and deriving its consequent.
The antecedent is a large conjunction, which is first deconstructed into its four main components:

1.

((A ==> (
~
(E |
~
C))) | ((
~
B) & (C | E)))
,

2.

((E &
~
E) | (B ==> F))
,

3.

E
, and

4.

B
.

The consequent,
((B | C) & (B ==> (F &
~
A)))
,
is also a conjunction, so its two parts are derived separately.
The first part,
(B | C)
, follows directly from component (4),
B
,
by disjunction introduction. The second part,

(B ==> (F & (
~
A)))
, is proven by a conditional proof,
assuming
B
. Within this subproof,
F

is derived by a case analysis on component (2). The first case,

(E &
~
E)
,
is a contradiction from which
F
follows trivially.
The second case,
(B ==> F)
, yields
F

by modus ponens with the assumed
B
.

Next,
~

A
is derived, also by a case analysis,
on component (1). The first case,
(A ==> (
~
(E |
~
C)))
,
leads to a contradiction if
A
is assumed (given component (3)
E
),
thus proving
~

A
.
The second case,
((
~
B) & (C | E))
,
contradicts component (4)
B
, so
~

A

again follows from the contradiction. With both
F
and
~

A

derived, their conjunction is formed, completing the conditional proof. Finally, the two parts
of the main consequent are joined together to yield the desired conclusion.

Nevertheless, Gemini fails to transcribe this nice piece of reasoning into NDL. Here is (the top part of) its output proof:

⬇

1

#

Assuming

the

antecedent

is

left-associative,

we

decompose

it

into

its

four

main

parts.

2

p1_and_p2_and_E

:=

((((A

==>

(~

(E

|

~C)))

|

((~

B)

&

(C

|

E)))

&

((E

&

~E)

|

(B

==>

F)))

&

E)

3

BY

left-and

on

h1;

4

B_premise

:=

B

BY

right-and

on

h1;

5

...

6

}

The proof goes wrong early, on the step shown on lines 3 and 4. In NDL, conjunction associates to the right
(as is specified in the prompt), so the hypothesis
h1
,
i.e.
,
(
p_{1}\>\&\>p_{2}\>\&\>p_{3}
)
,
should be understood as
(
p_{1}\>\&\>
(
p_{2}\>\&\>p_{3}
))
, not as
((
p_{1}\>\&\>p_{2}
)
\>\&\>p_{3}
)
.
Consequently, the application of
left-and
to
h1
yields
p_{1}
, not

(
p_{1}\>\&\>p_{2}
)
. Here,
p_{1}
is

(((A ==> (
~
(E |
~
C))) | ((
~
B) & (C | E))) ((E &
~
E) | (B ==> F)))

and
p_{2}
is
E
, and the conclusion advertised by Gemini on line 3 is
(
p_{1}\>\&\>p_{2}
)
,
so the NDL interpreter flags this as an error.

Strictly speaking, this is an error, since the prompt explicitly specifies that conjunction is
right-associative in NDL. Yet it is a trivial error in an overall sound proof. Indeed, the model’s
underlying reasoning is impeccable. One could argue that denying credit to it on account of such
peccadillos would be like nitpicking spelling errors in a love letter (though see p.
3.1.5

for a different viewpoint). There are many other examples of similar minor failures, running the gamut from syntax to semantics:
failing to balance parentheses or curly braces properly, forgetting a semicolon or inserting extra semicolons,
using square brackets instead of parentheses, splitting a line of code and putting part of it inside a comment
(following a hash sign), failing to distinguish between
p
and
\neg\neg p
, applying
from-false
to
false
itself
instead of the desired conclusion,
34
34

34

from-false
is a unary inference rule that takes any formula
p

as its argument and delivers
p
back as its conclusion,
provided
that the assumption base contains
false
.
This captures the principle that “anything follows from
false
.” Thus,
e.g.
, if we wanted to derive
(A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}B)

because the assumption base contains
false
, we would write
from-false

on

(A & B)
.
Instead, models often write vaguely plausible but incorrect variants like
(A & B)

BY

from-false

on

false
.

confusing the left and right components of a conjunction, disjunction, or biconditional, and many others.

For that reason, we carefully instrumented the NDL proof checker to systematically ignore
such errors while preserving soundness. The instrumented checker starts by repairing all syntax errors
it can reasonably address.
For example, it might insert a missing pair of curly braces, add or delete semicolons,
or replace square brackets by parentheses.
As an example of a more substantive syntactic intervention, it might change rule applications that use
BY
with
rule applications that name their conclusions and do not use
BY
. Models often generate steps like the following
(this example is taken from
llama-3.1-405b
, but all models make this type of error):

C-or-not-A-to-not-B
BY
mp
on
left, antecedent;

(2)

In NDL, only a formula
p
can appear immediately to the left of
BY
.
In particular, a step of the form
p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{BY}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}\cdots
imposes a constraint
on the inference
\cdots
: its conclusion must be identical to
p
.
It follows that the syntax of (
2
) is
incorrect,
35
35

35

This is putting it mildly. A step of that form suggests deep conceptual
confusion about the role of
BY
.

because
C-or-not-A-to-not-B
is not a formula that can be compared
against the result of the rule application to the right of
BY
. It’s just

a name
that the model wants to give to the result of that application.
Accordingly, our instrumented checker will automatically repair this into:

C-or-not-A-to-not-B := mp
on
left, antecedent;

After syntactic corrections are made, the checker starts evaluating the repaired proof step-by-step while
overlooking a wide class of mostly fiddling semantic and structural errors, such as marginally incorrect applications of inference rules.
For instance, the instrumented checker is insensitive to double negation. If a conclusion ought to be
p
but
the proof derives
(
\neg\neg\,p
)
or vice versa, the checker does not complain.
The checker is likewise insensitive to conjunction and disjunction commutativity,
e.g.
, it will tolerate a step
where an expected conclusion is
(A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}B)
but the model derives
(B\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A)
. It will
even endorse applications of
left-and
that should instead be applications of
right-and
, and vice versa;
and likewise for
left-either
and
right-either
, as well as
left-iff
and
right-iff
. The full list of
interventions is extensive, but essentially, if the model makes a logically sound inference but bungles the
execution, the instrumented checker will attempt to overlook it and continue with the rest of the proof.

Table 3:
Per-model numbers of syntactic and structural or semantic errors detected and repaired by the instrumented proof checker
when evaluating
PL1-PW
proofs, sorted by the total number of errors (from left to right and top to bottom).

The line is drawn at gross logical errors, such as trying to derive a conclusion that does not follow logically
from the current assumption base, or severely ill-formed rule applications, such as applications of conjunction
elimination to a disjunction. To give an idea of the scale of these repairs,
over the course of evaluating the 400 proofs written by o4-mini, the instrumented checker detected
and was able to recover from 649 syntax errors and 363 semantic errors, for an average of 2.53 errors per proof.
For DeepSeek-R1-0528, the total number of corrections exceeded 2500, averaging 6.93 corrections
per proof. Table
3
shows detailed numbers for all models.

Observe that less able models tend to make many more syntax errors than semantic errors. By contrast,
top-tier models tend to make fewer syntax errors, with some of them even making
more semantic errors than syntax errors (
e.g.
, o3, Grok-4, GPT-5.1, Gemini-2.5-Pro, and GPT-5).
Note also that there is a moderate negative correlation between model accuracy and the total
number of error repairs (syntax plus semantics) for a given model: the Pearson correlation coefficient
between these two random variables is -0.52. Nevertheless, we will see in the next section
that even though stronger models generally receive fewer repairs, they tend to benefit from them
more than weaker models. That’s because, for stronger models, these repairs are often enough
to push a proof over the finish line and make it correct, because the proof only needed minor
repairs to begin with. By contrast, proofs by weaker models cannot be salvaged quite
as easily because they typically have additional problems that cut deeper.
Essentially, strong models tend to fail cosmetically (even if the errors seem
flagrant from an instruction-following viewpoint), whereas weaker models tend
to fail structurally.

3.1.3
Results

Table
4
reports two sets of results, one according to the strict NDL proof checker and
the other reported by the instrumented checker. The gap between the two is striking and demonstrates that
naive evaluation by a conventional proof checker would severely underestimate the proof-discovery ability
of these models. A relaxed evaluation algorithm that is able to overlook minor errors reveals
that proof writing in
\textsc{PL}_{1}
is solved, with the top models achieving essentially ceiling accuracy.
Nevertheless, even with the instrumented checker, the task
has sharp discriminating power, with performance falling dramatically among mid-tier and
lower-end models, as R1-0528, o4-mini, and Claude Opus 4 are unable to reach 70% accuracy,
while o3-mini and Claude Sonnet 4 sit well below 50%.

PL1-PW
proof-writing accuracies from the instrumented proof checker and from the regular (strict) proof checker, with Wilson confidence intervals.

This will be a recurring theme in
ProofGrid
: all tasks, even those with strong ceiling effects,
are able to tease apart models of different ability levels quite effectively.
In Section
3.2
we define and study DI (discriminability index),
a simple metric that gauges how well benchmark scores (usually accuracies) manage
to separate a set of models
S
. It is simply the average of score differences between all pairs
of distinct models in
S
.
Expressed as a percentage, the DI for the entire set of
PL1-PW
results is 34, a very high value.

Both the average and the median boost in performance brought about by instrumentation
is 19 absolute percentage points. This means that, on average, if only strict accuracy was reported,
a model’s performance on this task would be underestimated by 19 absolute points.
The following figure displays the accuracy deltas
sorted from biggest to smallest:

The table below provides finer-grained information on the distribution of these deltas:

Mean

Median

Min

Max

Std

@5

@25

@50

@75

@90

@95

Accuracy Delta

19

19

5

39

9

6

12

19

26

31

34

The benefit of instrumentation follows a rougly inverted-U pattern with respect to baseline
accuracy, as shown by the scatterplot of strict accuracy versus instrumentation boost in Fig.
2
.
We explain this pattern below.

Figure 2:
Scatterplot of strict accuracy vs instrumentation deltas across the 24 models.

If one views proof writing as decomposing roughly into two stages, idea discovery
(finding the right proof plan) and rigorous execution (turning that plan into a correct derivation),
instrumentation only helps with the second stage.
The delta is thus minimal for weak models that rarely discover the correct proof strategy, and also small
for the few elite models that already often produce fully valid proofs. In particular,
as confirmed by the preceding histogram, the smallest improvement (5 percentage points)
is realized by GPT-4o, the worst-performing model, while the next smallest improvement (6 points)
is realized by Claude Opus 4.5, the best-performing model.
The largest gains by far occur for mid-tier models that often reach the correct high-level proof
idea but are unable to express it as a formally valid derivation.
These are models that have latent reasoning competence but weak symbolic discipline.
The very best models still receive a decent boost, reflecting occasional slips.
Instrumentation cleans those up but cannot increase accuracy much because the
margins are tight.

While it is tempting to view proof length as a natural axis for analyzing reasoning behavior,
raw proof-length distributions are dominated by differences in which problems a model is able to solve.
A stack of box plots depicting proof-length distributions for the 24 models for the
set of problems that each model is able to solve would naturally invite cross-model comparisons
that would nevertheless be statistically indefensible, since the sets in question vary
across models and are not matched by difficulty, which means
that the resulting distributions are drawn from different populations. It’s possible,
of course, to control for problem identity,
e.g.
, by restricting attention to the intersection
of problems that are correctly solved by a given set of models, but once that is
done we find that proof length is not an informative axis of analysis for this
task.
36
36

36

For instance, we might consider the set of problems
I_{K}
that were correctly solved
by all top
K
models, say for
K=10
. If we pick a particular model
m_{r}
as a reference model (say Claude Opus 4.5,
the highest-performing model), then for each
p\in I_{K}
we can compute the distribution of log ratios

\log L_{m}(p)/L_{m_{r}}(p)
for every model
m
in the set of top
K
models,
where
L_{m}(p)
is the length of the (correct) proof generated by
m
for problem
p
.
The boxplots of these distributions would be directly comparable and would tell
us how much more verbose or exploratory each model’s proofs are by comparison to the reference model.
When we do that, we find that proof lengths are roughly similar, with the median log ratio at 0 and
more pronounced length differences emerging only at the fringes (after the 90th percentile).

This should not be surprising: the simplicity of these problems naturally constrains
verbosity, as proof styles converge and minimal proof lengths become fairly similar.
Nevertheless, to give a concrete sense of the intrinsic length scale of proofs in this task,
the table below provides some statistics on the distribution of the
shortest
correct-proof lengths
for each problem, irrespective of model identity.
37
37

37

Note that every problem was correctly solved by
at least one model, so the quantity “the length of the smallest proof for problem
p
” defines
a total function on this dataset and does not introduce any survivorship biases.

We stress that these are not the shortest or minimal proofs in a formal mathematical sense, only the shortest
ones that were observed.
