3.1.4
Error Analysis

What are the errors that cause proofs to fail even when they are evaluated with the instrumented checker?
That distribution is shown in Fig.
3
.
It distinguishes 6 remaining error types:

Figure 3:
Distribution of first-error types under the instrumented proof checker.

1.

Parsing/Syntax errors
.
Minor syntax transgressions are already fixed by the instrumented checker; what remains are more deviant
parsing errors that cannot be easily detected and repaired. The following examples are illustrative:

•

Writing the entire proof as one single continuous line while also interspersing various comments in it, even though, as noted in the prompt,
a comment in NDL extends to the end of the line on which it appears. Alternatively, the proof is written as a list of comment lines,
where each line starts with a hash and an English sentence (the actual commentary) and is then followed by NDL code, all on the same line.

•

Single or double quotes randomly inserted for no reason.

•

Conditional subproofs with empty bodies, e.g. (example taken from Mistral Magistral):

⬇

1

assume

case2

:=

((~

(A

==>

~B))

|

(B

|

A))

{

2

#

We

will

break

this

down

further

in

the

proof.

3

}

4

...

These are like if-then-else statements in Python whose code blocks for the two branches contain only comments and no code.
Weaker models probably treat these gaps as placeholders whose details remain undetermined.

•

Hallucinating syntax forms that are not part of NDL. Some of these are interesting
because they are coherent
generalizations
of NDL syntax.
Athena, being a superset of NDL, actually accepts quite a few of these hallucinated forms.
For instance, unlike NDL, an argument to a rule application in Athena
need not be a simple formula, but can instead be an entire inlined proof,
e.g.
,
it is possible to write

\vdots

C
BY
cases
on
(A | B),
assume
A {
\,\cdots\,
},
assume
B {
\,\cdots\,
};

\vdots

Here, the second and third arguments to
cases
are arbitrarily large conditional proofs, presumably
deriving the conditionals
(A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C)
and
(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C)
.

Most models wrote at least some proofs in such generalized
supersets of NDL. This was no doubt due to transfer learning from the countless
instances of syntax compositionality that the models encountered during their pretraining.
Because we used Athena as the backbone of our proof checker, we were able to accept
and give credit to many such proofs, even though these generalized constructs
are not valid according to the prompt.

However, many models push this too far and end up being systematically creative in the wrong direction,
by concocting uber-compositional syntax generalizations that are much more extreme and
free-ranging than anything allowed in Athena.
Here is one example of a single inference rule application written by GPT-4.1
(we added the indentation to clarify the model’s intention; the actual output was just one long line):

⬇

1

(B

==>

~A

==>

(~A

BY

mp

on

(B

==>

~A),

B;

2

(B

&

~A)

BY

both

on

B,

~A)),

3

(A

==>

(B

==>

~A),

4

(A

==>

B),

5

A

==>

(B

BY

mp

on

(A

==>

B),

A;

6

~A

BY

mp

on

(B

==>

~A),

B;

7

(B

&

~A)

BY

both

on

B,

~A))

While this fails on multiple fronts, the overall intention is relatively clear: The model was trying
to write a single application of the
cases
rule with nested subproofs as the arguments.
In the process, it helped itself to its own bracketing conventions (such as enclosing a proof sequence
in parentheses instead of NDL’s curly braces) and also embedded the subproofs inside conditional
formulas that could not possibly yield the desired conclusions.
Here is another example by o3-mini:

⬇

1

{false2

:=

false

BY

absurd

on

a,

h3;

2

B1

:=

B

BY

from-false

on

B,

false2};

These pervasive errors indicate a failure of
cognitive control
, as cognitive scientists might put it,
particularly
inhibitory
control—the ability to suppress prepotent or automatic responses, such as
the urge to write in natural language or to use familiar compositional syntax patterns from other languages.
The models find it difficult to inhibit their pretrained tendencies; they overgeneralize
from their training distributions and fail to suppress those generalizations even when
explicitly instructed to do so, finding it hard to conform their output only to
the constraints explicitly provided in their “working memory” (the context
window).

That said, as can be seen from Fig.
3
, parsing errors tend to be the minority for
most models, particularly for better models.
The top performer, Claude Opus 4.5, did not make a single syntax error (the only model to do so).

2.

Not-in-the-assumption-base errors
: These are the most properly logical errors, signifying
the application of an inference rule to a formula that is neither a premise, nor a current hypothesis
(posited by an
assume
that is still in scope), nor a previously derived conclusion (by a preceding
subproof that is still in scope).

3.

Malformed rule applications
: These errors can be viewed as sitting somewhere between syntax and semantics,
rather similar to type errors. A typical example is an application of modus tollens to
(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A)
and
\neg B

(an example from Grok-4) or an application of disjunctive syllogism to
(\neg A\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}B)
and
\neg A
.

4.

Malformed assumptions
: These errors occur in conditional proofs
when a model stipulates a hypothesis that is not a valid formula,
e.g.
,

assume

A_and_B {
\cdots
}
.
The model usually intends the malformed assumption as a mnemonic
name
for the
actual hypothesis
p
(in this case, say, the conjunction
(A & B)
).
But instead of writing
assume

I\,
:=
\,p\;
{
\cdots
}
,
it writes
assume

I\;
{
\cdots
}
. (As an analogy
from functional programming, this is a bit like writing
let val sqrt_of_n in
\cdots
end

instead of writing something like
let val sqrt_of_n = math.sq(n) in
\cdots
end
.)

5.

Wrong conclusions
: This is another type of reasoning error, which happens
when a model manages to derive a conclusion but not the one that was required. This happens in two cases, either:

•

in a single application of an inference rule where the model uses
BY
to advertise the rule’s conclusion as
p

but the application actually produces
q\neq p
; or

•

at the level of the entire proof, where the model writes a proof that successfully
derives
some
conclusion, but not the one that solves the problem.

6.

Unbound identifiers
: This occurs when the proof refers to hallucinated names that have not been defined. For example, in a conditionalized argument with zero premises,
the model might still write
premise-1
in reference to,
e.g.
, the first conjunct of the target conditional’s antecedent.

These 6 failure modes are mutually exclusive only because
they mark
the first error
encountered by the instrumented checker,
analogous to a compiler’s first error report. It’s conceivable that even
if that error were somehow disregarded and evaluation continued, the proof
would make a second error of a different type (and potentially a third one, and so on). This is like a program whose first compiler error
is a syntax error, which is followed by a type error and then
an undeclared variable. In general, failed proofs may contain
multiple errors, and indeed we have anecdotally verified that many proofs,
especially from weaker models, do make both syntactic and logical mistakes.
Nevertheless, the “first-error” view afforded by Fig.
3

is informative, because it identifies the earliest bottleneck that would need
to be addressed to allow a proof attempt to progress further.

The data shows that the first errors of stronger models tend to be more
properly logical or semantic. And although the absolute number of such failures
is too small to support fine-grained statistical analysis, this clearly suggests
a developmental progression, whereby models first learn to imitate proof
structure, then they learn to obey syntax and formatting constraints,
and only later become limited by more abstract reasoning challenges.

3.1.5
Discussion

How should we understand the
PL1-PW
results? This task requires two skills:

1.

The model must possess a high-level understanding of deductive reasoning and a capacity for strategic-level planning
that will enable it to construct an abstract argument, using the inferential machinery of natural deduction, demonstrating
how and why the conclusion follows from the premises.

2.

The model must also be able to render that abstract argument into a rigorous formal proof.

The results show clearly that models are considerably better at the first task than the second. They have largely sound “mental models”
of logical reasoning that they can leverage to solve the straightforward problems in
PL1-PW
. However, they have severe
deficiencies in execution—in the
procedural skill
needed to express their reasoning in the precise syntax of a formal language.
It is likely possible to mitigate these deficiencies,
e.g.
, by fine-tuning on a target formal language and/or by taking an
agentic approach, but the interesting question here is not what could be done with additional engineering effort, but what these results
tell us about the native abilities of these models as general-purpose off-the-shelf intelligent systems.

On one view, the procedural difficulties are not alarming. After all, these models had
virtually no exposure to NDL during their pretraining, so mistakes are
to be expected. These are minor performance errors, not competence errors. Humans, too, when they first see a new language,
need a certain amount of practice before they become fluent in it. Even a brilliant logician would almost certainly
make some errors on their first attempts at writing in a new formal language.

On the other hand, NDL is an extremely simple language that is fully described in a few pages. Moreover, the task was
described in a few-shot setting, with several examples included in the prompt. If a model cannot respect a small number
of clearly defined syntax rules, even when illustrated with multiple examples, that by itself
arguably indicates a sort of failure of general reasoning,
even if it is not, strictly speaking, a failure of logical inference.
And while the models might have never seen this
particular
language before, the language is
quite standard and very similar to many others that the models
have
seen during their pretraining, in enormous quantities.
For instance, NDL uses curly braces
{
\cdots
}
to delineate scope, semicolons to compose different
steps, parenthesized infix syntax for propositional-logic formulas, and so on. None of this is alien to
modern models.

Cognitive science gives us a framework for thinking about this: the concepts
of near-transfer and far-transfer of learning
[
10
]
.
Transfer of learning in general is the application of knowledge
or skills learned in one context to a new
context.
38
38

38

“Transfer learning”
[
96
]

has also been a major research paradigm and driver of progress in deep learning,
by enabling models trained on one task to perform
well on others with limited additional data. While the two traditions overlap,
AI has focused on data-efficient reuse of representations, whereas cognitive science is more
concerned with the extent to which structural insights can leap across different domains.

Near transfer occurs when we apply knowledge to a new situation
that is very similar to what we have seen before (a person who has only driven a Toyota Camry before can
almost certainly drive a Honda Accord without any instruction). Far transfer occurs when we apply
knowledge to a new situation that is structurally different and seemingly unrelated, say, using principles of chess
to inform a business merger strategy. (This is notoriously difficult for humans and is considered a hallmark of
deep, flexible intelligence.) Many of the errors made by these models suggest near-transfer failures.
Inserting multiple semicolons in a row, writing code to the right of a comment marker and on the same line,
or stopping a formula in midstream with unbalanced parentheses are not trivial slip-ups. They indicate that the models
are failing to apply core known principles to the current specific case
and are not attending to the stated rules.

Finally, as we have already stressed, all our tasks are intended as tests of fluid reasoning.
We are deliberately using a novel language to prevent the models from relying on crystallized knowledge.
The very first step in any fluid-intelligence task is to understand the “rules of the game.” If a system cannot
reliably internalize and apply the given rules, it has failed at the most basic level of such a test.
Logical deduction is a subsequent step that depends on the first.

In conclusion, while several models at present exhibit a significant capacity for
the abstract logical planning required by this relatively straightforward task
(as shown by post-correction accuracy rates near ceiling), most of them show a
deficit in cognitive control and procedural grounding, failing to autonomously
generate fully valid formal proofs, given that after the top 3 models, pre-correction accuracies
dip below 80%. Even the very best models currently show a gap between abstract logical competence
and rigorous execution. This suggests that the reasoning abilities of language models
are not yet fully integrated, instead requiring external “scaffolds” to translate their
abstract plans into formally correct proofs. Further evidence for this will appear in subsequent sections.

We close with a few additional observations:

•

The instrumented checker catches a very large number of issues, but it is not an oracle. It’s still possible that
a proof might fail due to a relatively innocuous error, so even the instrumented accuracies might underestimate
abstract proof-writing competence in

PL1-PW
.
39
39

39

As a concrete example (taken from
deepseek-reasoner
),
consider a case analysis over a disjunction
(p_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}p_{2})
intended to derive a conclusion
q
.
The model shows that
p_{1}
implies
q
, but for the second case it shows instead that
p_{2}
implies
false
;
it then purports to derive
q
by applying
cases
to the 3 arguments
(p_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}p_{2})
,
(p_{1}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
, and
(p_{2}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\mbox{false\/})
.
This application is ill-formed because the two conditionals don’t share the same consequent, so the current
implementation rejects it. One could argue that this is a “good enough” case analysis, since
(p_{2}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\mbox{false\/})

could readily be transformed to
(p_{2}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
. But these are the sort of idiosyncratic (and
relatively uncommon) errors whose automated detection would need much more sophisticated intent inference
and whose repair would cross the line from proof checking to proof synthesis.
The current instrumented checker focuses on very common patterns of minor errors that
can be easily identified and overlooked.

That said, it should not be assumed that models always come up with the right
high-level proof plan and fail only in execution. The reasoning strategies themselves, expressed in English, are sometimes
misguided.
40
40

40

Quantifying this would require a rigorous analysis of every proof plan, which would involve manual
inspection (since the plans are unstructured text) or the use of other models as judges; we leave this to future work.

As an example, consider this problem:

⬇

1

assert

premise-2

:=

(B

==>

A)

2

assert

premise-3

:=

(A

==>

(~

(B

==>

~C)))

3

assert

premise-4

:=

((A

==>

B)

&

(~C

|

A))

4

#

Derive:

(A

&

C)

A human with some experience would realize that the key is to derive
A
, which can be done by contradiction. If we had
\neg A
then modus tollens on
premise-2

would yield
\neg B
. Moreover, disjunctive syllogism on the right conjunct of
premise-4
would give
\neg C
. Since we have
\neg B
and
\neg C
,
we have
(B\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}C)
(by the definition of a biconditional), which, by
premise-1
, leads to
C
—a contradiction, since we’ve already derived
\neg C
.
Now that we have
A
,
C
follows easily from
premise-3
: modus ponens on that and
A
gives
\neg(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg C)
, which, by the definition
of a negated conditional, gives
\neg\neg C
, and hence
C
.

Here is how GPT-5 describes its reasoning for this problem:

We first extract
(A

==>

B)
from
premise-4
and use
premise-2

(B

==>

A)
. Using these, we prove
(B

==>

B

via
(B

A)
,

(B

==>

~

, then under a temporary assumption

~

build
(B

==>

~

C)
, reaching a contradiction and hence
C
.
This yields
(B

==>

C)
. From

we then derive
C
by chaining
A

==>

B
and

B

==>

C

A

==>

C

To obtain

gives

, and finally we conjoin

and

to derive
(A

&

.

This strategy places the emphasis on
(A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C)
and treats the derivation of
A
itself as an afterthought,
when the opposite is required: Deriving
(A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C)
is straightforward (though GPT-5 does it via a circuitous route);
it’s the derivation of
A
that is (relatively) more demanding. GPT-5’s plan erroneously claims that
“To obtain
A
outright, we appeal to the disjunction
(
~

|

A)
(from

)
and set up a
cases

.” But

does not follow from
(
~

|

A)
,
especially from a simple case analysis of it. The concrete proof written by GPT-5 has no procedural flaw; it follows

complaining that

is not in the assumption base.

•

The more common problem with proof strategies is not the presence of outright logical errors but rather
the presence of gaps that leave crucial details unspecified. Even the best models do this to a certain degree,
but mid-tier and third-tier models often take it to an extreme, with strategies that are
so broad and underspecified as to be vacuous. Here is an example from
deepseek-v3
:

The proof strategy involves deriving a contradiction from the given premises to conclude the disjunction of negations of various propositions.
This is achieved by systematically applying inference rules to the premises to derive intermediate conclusions that eventually lead to a contradiction,
thereby allowing us to conclude the desired disjunction.

This could be shortened to “The strategy is proof by contradiction.”

•

Some models use the proof text itself as a thought scratchpad for exploring the proof search space.
Mistral Magistral is particularly fond of doing this, as illustrated in the following fragment:

Magistral repeatedly makes incorrect inferences and recognizes them as such (see the comment on line 12),
but continues unfazed to explore many alternative reasoning paths. The model is clearly performing a search,
but it fails to separate its internal monologue and search process from the final polished output.
This is another failure of cognitive control.
41
41

41

Mistral Magistral does this more
than other models,
but it is not alone. More than 10% of the proofs written by Claude Opus 4 include comments
with phrases like
Wait
;
Hmm
;
This is getting too complex, let me try yet another approach
;
Let me restart with a clearer strategy
;
and so on. Claude Sonnet does this as well (
This won’t work, let me reconsider
;
Wait, that’s wrong. Let me fix this.
;

Wait, that’s not right either. let me reconsider the biconditional
;
etc.
), as do GPT-4.1 (
\cdots\>
but that’s
not helpful. instead, let’s use the other disjunct of premise3
) and others, occasionally even Grok-4.
The only exceptions are the OpenAI models starting with GPT-5 and above, the o-series models, and the Gemini models.

In conclusion, despite the very impressive progress reflected in these results
and the near-ceiling performance of a few models (aided by instrumentation),
this simple task suggests that models still operate in a semantic soup of sorts.
They have learned
that the purpose of
left-and
and
right-and
is to “get a piece of a conjunction”
but they have not mastered the precise operational semantics of how those tools work.
They treat them like interchangeable magic wands for pulling things
out of a conjunction, rather than as specific, deterministic functions with preconditions
and postconditions. This is an abstraction failure. The models have correctly abstracted
the goal of the rules but failed to abstract their mechanics. In the semantic soup in which
these models operate, a conjunction logically entails each conjunct. For the models, this
semantic entailment is the most important signal. The precise syntactic path required to
demonstrate that entailment is a secondary, lower-importance detail that they often get wrong.
Here is a typical example, from the
deepseek-r1-0528
proof for problem 325, which contains the following step:

C
BY
left-and
on
(A & C)
. Even without the precise specification
of
left-and
given in the prompt, it should be apparent to the model that
C
is the right
component of
(A & C)
, not the left one.
Even the best models at this time continue to be susceptible to these errors. Here’s an example from GPT-5.2 on
an extremely simple problem (item 119) that seeks to derive
(A & B)
from
(B <==> A)

(given as
premise-1
) and
B
(
premise-2
):

⬇

1

##

From

the

biconditional,

get

the

direction

B

==>

A

2

B-implies-A

:=

right-iff

on

premise-1;

3

...

4

}

But
right-iff
detaches the conditional in the opposite direction, as explained in the prompt (which also contains examples
of
right-iff
in action):

Rule 12: Name: right-iff (biconditional elimination)
Description: Applied to a sentence of the form (p <==> q) that holds,
it produces the conclusion (q ==> p).

Our instrumented proof checker largely validates the models’ semantic reasoning while forgiving
their procedural sloppiness (and overlooks this particular error), but mathematics and logic
demand precision.

3.2
Proof Checking

Dataset and Task Formulation

PL1-PC
contains proofs for 300
\textsc{PL}_{1}
arguments, only 26 of which are conditionalized.
The core task here is to examine each of these proofs and determine if it is correct.
If the model deems a proof incorrect, it must also report the first error made by
the proof (“first” while reading the proof from top to bottom and from left to right).

38 of the proofs were generated by a theorem prover written in Athena, while the rest were produced
by Gemini-2.5-Pro, o3, GPT-4o, and
Claude Sonnet 3.5.
42
42

42

LLM-written proofs were included in order to broaden the dataset beyond
a narrow range of synthetically generated proofs and reasoning errors.
A potential concern is that asking models to check proofs
that they wrote themselves might introduce an evaluation bias. However,
PL1-PC
evaluates 24 models
while the model-generated proofs included in this task were written by only 3 of them (one of the four,
Claude 3.5, is not among the models we evaluated). This means that for the large majority of
evaluated models (21/24), the “LLM proofs” are never their own outputs.
Even for the contributing models, the risk of direct self-evaluation is heavily diluted by
the mix with synthetically generated proofs and proofs written by other models.
Thus, even in principle, cross-model evaluation ensures that there is no systematic “grading your own homework” effect.
We also see no empirical evidence of self-evaluation bias.
For instance, Gemini-2.5-Pro performs nearly identically on its own proofs (0.91 accuracy)
and on o3-written proofs (0.89), while o3 does worse on its own proofs (0.81) than on Gemini-written proofs (0.92).
That said, accuracy deltas might not be the most informative indicator of a self-evaluation bias, so
we also computed
\Delta\,
FPR, that is, the change in false-positive rate when an evaluator judges
its own incorrect proofs versus those of others
(
\Delta\,\mbox{FPR}=P(\mbox{accept\/}\;|\;\mbox{incorrect\/},\mbox{own\/})-P(\mbox{accept\/}\>|\>\mbox{incorrect\/},\mbox{other\/})
).
This targets the specific leniency mechanism that accuracy can mask due to prevalence effects.
For Gemini-2.5-Pro vs o3 we find
\Delta\,\mbox{FPR}=-0.10
(no leniency;
p\approx 0.65
),
and for o3 vs Gemini-2.5-Pro we find
\Delta\,\mbox{FPR}=+0.33
(suggestive in magnitude but
not statistically significant;
p\approx 0.066
with small
n
).
Taken together, these results, alongside the accuracy comparisons above, provide no compelling
evidence of self-evaluation bias in this setting.
The real risk of including model-written proofs is the unpredictability of their structure.
Synthetic proofs allow fine-grained control over which proof constructs are used, and this
greatly simplifies evaluation. Model-written proofs, by contrast, often rely on creative
corner-case features, resulting in proofs that are technically invalid under the prompt’s
constraints yet arguably correct in spirit. Accommodating such cases typically
requires painstaking modifications of the evaluation pipeline and/or prompt revisions.

The proofs written by the theorem prover were correct by construction, so synthetic errors
were randomly injected in some of them. The model-written proofs were not corrupted because
they contain organic errors of their own (which is why they were included).
The dataset is balanced, with an even split between correct and incorrect proofs.
Correctness judgments and ground truth in general come from Athena but undergo extra scrutiny to account for NDL’s simplicity,
as explained below. The following table presents summary statistics for the distribution of proof lengths
across these 300 samples.

Mean

Median

Min

Max

Std

@5

@25

@50

@75

@90

@95

Proof length

30.69

5

125

13.85

12.0

21.0

29.0

38.0

49.0

55.05

As the numbers show, these proofs are generally short, with 90% having fewer than 50 steps. Here is an example of a correct proof:

⬇

1

assert

premise-2

:=

(C

==>

&

2

assert

premise-3

:=

B

3

4

#

Goal:

A

5

6

{

7

#

We

proceed

by

contradiction

to

derive

We

will

~A

and

derive

false.

8

(~A)

{

9

We’ll

use

premise-1

to

perform

a

analysis.

(~B)

{

false

BY

absurd

on

premise-3,

(~B)

12

};

13

case2

:=

assume

(~A

==>

C)

{

14

C

BY

mp

on

(~A

==>

C),

(~A);

15

(A

&

~B)

BY

mp

on

premise-2,

C;

16

A

BY

left-and

on

(A

&

~B);

17

false

BY

absurd

on

(~A),

A

18

};

19

false

BY

cases

on

premise-1,

case1,

case2

20

};

A

BY

by-contradiction

on

A,

(~A

==>

false)

22

}

The input prompt consists of a detailed description of the proof-checking task and of NDL,
followed by a particular NDL argument (zero or more premises, labeled
premise-1
,

premise-2
,
\ldots
, plus the target conclusion) along with an ostensible proof for that argument.
The model is instructed to output a JSON dictionary with two keys,
correct

and
errorDetails
. If the proof is correct, the value of
errorDetails
should be the empty
dictionary,
{}
. If the proof is incorrect, then
errorDetails
should have 4 keys whose
values collectively specify the first (topmost) error found in the proof:

1.

offendingLine
: the line of the proof containing the error (if such a line exists
43
43

43

For
an example where no such line exists, consider a proof that goes through to completion but derives a conclusion
other than the expected target. That’s an error, but it is not caused by any specific line in the proof.
).

2.

offendingLineNumber
: the number of the offending line (needed for disambiguation purposes,
since the same line might have multiple occurrences in the proof), where counting starts at 1. If there
is no specific offending line,
offendingLineNumber
should be set to 0.

3.

errorType
: one of three values:
syntax
,
type
, or
logic
.

4.

errorDescription
: a detailed description of the error.

Regarding the three error types:

1.

A syntax error can be relatively minor (
e.g.
, an extra or missing semicolon, an unclosed curly brace,
an unparenthesized formula, a misspelled rule name, and so on), or something more substantive,
e.g.
, a stretch
of text that doesn’t parse under the described grammar rules of NDL and whose intended meaning is unclear.
The models are instructed to disregard minor syntactic errors.

2.

A type error occurs when an inference rule is applied to the wrong number of arguments or to arguments
of the wrong form,
e.g.
, an application of modus ponens to 3 arguments or negation elimination to a conditional.

3.

Logic errors occur when an inference rule is applied to formulas that do not currently hold, or when a proof
step derives a conclusion other than the one specified in the corresponding proof line.

The following is the algorithm for evaluating a model’s output:

•

If the model’s verdict is positive, award it a point as long as the instrumented checker finds the proof correct modulo syntax repairs.
No point is given if the instrumented checker finds the proof incorrect or if it performs structural/semantic repairs.

•

If the model’s verdict is negative, award it a point as long as:

1.

the instrumented checker finds the proof incorrect and reports the same error as the model; or

2.

the instrumented checker finds the proof correct but only after performing structural/semantic repairs,
and the first such error it repairs coincides with the one reported by the model.

No point is given if the instrumented checker finds the proof correct without performing any structural/semantic
repairs, or if the first such error it repairs is different from the one reported by the model.

We see that the top 7 models achieve very high accuracies on the full task and nearly perfect accuracies on the
simplified (‘‘guessing only’’) version. Unsurprisingly, when the task is simplified to giving a plain
yes/no answer, the biggest beneficiaries are the worst-performing models.
We see the accuracy of GPT-4o more than doubling, jumping from 0.23 to 0.51. Huge gains are also seen by DeepSeek-V3, Grok-3,
DeepSeek-V3.1, and Hermes 4.

44

Note, however, that even in this setting these models perform roughly on par
with random guessing.

But even very strong models benefit from having to simply guess instead of having to explain themselves
(
e.g.
, o3’s performance jumps up by 10 absolute points), underscoring the point that
the surface-level classification required by binary or multiple-choice questions is too easy
and severely overestimates model ability.

The deltas are much smaller for the top 5-6 models that already perform very well on the full task,
partly because these are already starting to bump up against the ceiling and don’t have
much room for improvement.
While there is indeed top-end compression and a moderate ceiling effect among the elite models,
which are clustered within a few percentage points of one another, performance on the full task
shows wide variance across the entire set of models, with accuracies gently declining from the low 90s to the low 20s,
as can be seen in Fig.
4
, showing that even this simple task has significant discriminating power.

Quantifying Benchmark Discrimination

Appendix
B
goes beyond simple variance or pairwise-difference
analysis to derive a more theoretically grounded estimate of the discrimination power of
this and other
ProofGrid
tasks through IRT (Item Response Theory). It also includes detailed
item-level information
for several
of the benchmark’s tasks. A lighter but still informative alternative to IRT-based discrimination power
is a
discriminability index
(DI) that quantifies how well a benchmark separates models.
This is a simple measure based on Gini mean difference rather than standard deviation.
Intuitively, DI measures how much model accuracies differ from one another.
If all models score about the same, DI will be close to 0. If some models are much better
than others, DI will be larger.

Specifically, suppose there are
n
models with accuracies
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{n}
,
each between 0 and 1.
We look at all possible pairs of models and compute their score differences:

DI=\frac{1}{\tfrac{1}{2}n(n-1)}\sum_{i<j}|A_{i}-A_{j}|

This is simply the average absolute difference in accuracies between every pair
of distinct models.
45
45

45

As mentioned earlier, this measure, the mean pairwise absolute deviation
of model accuracies, is commonly known in statistics as the (sample)
Gini mean difference

(where the random variables are the accuracies of the various models).
We refer to it here as a “Discriminability Index” (DI) because in the present context
it quantifies how effectively a task differentiates between models.

Because the maximum possible difference between any two accuracies is 1.0,
the DI value itself lies between 0 and 1.

DI=0
means every model got exactly the same score, so the benchmark cannot tell them apart.

DI
near 1 means that model performances are spread widely apart and the benchmark is extremely discriminative.
Although DI is theoretically bounded between 0 and 1, such extreme values would require some models to
be nearly perfect while others fail entirely. In practice, accuracies across contemporary models
occupy a narrower range, leading to DI values typically below 0.3, which we can
interpret qualitatively as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}0.00\text{--}0.05:&\text{Benchmark strongly saturated (models nearly identical)}\\
0.05\text{--}0.10:&\text{Early signs of compression}\\
0.10\text{--}0.20:&\text{Healthy discrimination}\\
0.20\text{--}0.30\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{or above}:&\text{Strong discrimination (large spread)}\end{array}

These categories are not based on strict mathematical criteria;
they are practical guidelines reflecting the operating range of DI
observed in realistic evaluation settings.

DI naturally
depends on the composition of the model set. For instance, restricting attention to a set of models
with a narrow range of abilities will yield smaller pairwise differences
and thus a lower DI value. Thus, ideally, for rigorous comparisons
across benchmarks or tasks, the set of models should be held fixed. But this is a control
ideal that may not always be feasible. In practice,
when two benchmarks have overlapping and comparably diverse model sets, large differences
in observed dispersion remain informative.

In our case, the accuracies of the models evaluated on the full
PL1-PC
task range
from about
0.23
(lowest) to
0.94
(highest). Plugging these numbers into
the formula above yields
DI\approx 0.27
.
This means that, on average, any two models differ by about

\,|A_{i}-A_{j}|=0.27\,
in accuracy, a 27-percentage-point gap.
This is a high DI value that lies in the upper region of the strong-discrimination
range. It shows that the task clearly separates models of different ability:
mid-tier and weaker models score significantly lower than the strongest ones.
Only the very best models (those near
A_{i}\approx 0.9
) are tightly clustered
together, hinting at a ceiling effect at the top.
But the task still provides meaningful information about model ability,
and differences in score genuinely reflect differences in ability.

Figure 5:
Lorenz-style curve of model accuracies on the full
PL1-PC
task.

Finally, unlike standard deviation, which measures distances from the mean, DI directly measures
pairwise model differences and is thus more intuitively interpretable.
It also tends to be more robust to outliers, and it is in the same family of dispersion
measures as the Gini index, which means it connects directly to the geometry of Lorenz curves
and inequality coefficients. For instance, Fig.
5
visualizes the
information conveyed by the
PL2-PC
DI in a more intuitive way using a Lorenz-style curve. The horizontal axis shows
the cumulative share of models, starting from the weakest on the left and moving to the strongest on the right.
Every point along the unit interval on the
x
-axis
represents a fraction of our model population, sorted from the lowest-scoring models on
the left to the highest-scoring models on the right. For example, the point
x=0.4
represents
the bottom 40% of the models in the task, while
x=1.0
represents the totality of all
24 models. At
x=0
, none of the models are included yet. So the
x
-axis can be thought of as a
kind of “percentile rank” axis for the models being tested. It tells us what fraction of that
population we have counted so far, starting from the weakest. The vertical axis shows the cumulative
share of the total accuracy mass contributed by these models (literally the sum of all models’ accuracies),
i.e.,
y=0.23
represents 23% of the task’s total accuracy mass. In our case the total accuracy mass is 16.1,
so the point
y=0.23
represents 3.7 units of accuracy. As we move from
y=0
to
y=1

we consume more and more of that accuracy mass, with
y=1
accounting for all accuracy.
The dashed diagonal represents a state of perfect equality: if the bottom
x
% of models contributed
exactly
x
% of the total accuracy mass, then the Lorenz curve would coincide with this diagonal.
It’s not hard to show that this could only happen if all models have identical accuracies,
in which case the task would have zero discriminating power.

Any bowing below the diagonal therefore
indicates performance inequality, meaning that some models account for smaller shares of the total accuracy.
In our case we see that the bottom 20% of the models contribute only 10% of the total accuracy, while the
bottom 40% contribute 25%. By the time we hit 60% we have accounted for 47% of the total accuracy.
So we have a modest amount of ‘‘inequality’’ (a Gini index of 0.19
46
46

46

The Gini index (or “coefficient”)
G
is the ratio of the area between the diagonal and the Lorenz curve divided by the total
area below the diagonal, which is always 1/2. It measures how far the observed curve lies below
the “line of equality.” In general,
G=0
means perfect equality and
G=1

means maximal inequality (one model has perfect accuracy and all others fail completely). Like DI,
G
quantifies
spread but does so geometrically. In our case, we constructed the Lorenz curve for
PL1-PC
from the task data
using interpolation and we computed
G
by numerical integration (using the trapezoidal rule). The “Gini from DI”
value shown in the plot is derived from the theoretical approximation of
G
as
\mbox{DI}/2\mu
, where

\mu
is the mean accuracy. This becomes an identity in the limit (with larger numbers of models
n
). Even with
only
n=24
models in
PL1-PC
, this approximation (0.199) is quite close to the actual value found by
integration (0.191), which indicates internal consistency between these two ways of quantifying task
discrimination and strengthens the conceptual link between DI (which we introduced for intuition) and the Gini coefficient
(borrowed from inequality theory).
) and a gently sloping Lorenz curve,
which is precisely what we should want in a benchmark task. Consider three versions
of a Lorenz curve for such a task: (a) a straight line along the diagonal, indicating perfect equality and a complete
lack of discriminative power; (b) a highly concave curve that is almost flat for most models and only rises
sharply towards the end, indicating a state of extreme inequality where only the very best models contribute
meaningfully to the total accuracy mass and the task is out of reach for the rest (a kind of
model plutocracy); and (c) a moderately concave curve, indicating that stronger models contribute more but weaker ones still
contribute significantly. Lorenz curves of the third type are preferable.

Figure 6:
Types of root errors made by models on
PL1-PC
.

Error Analysis

We partition all possible root errors into 5 types:
47
47

47

By a “root error” we mean the

first
error that ought to be reported according to the task instructions.

1.

Type-1 errors
: The model mistakenly claims that the input proof is correct. If we view
PL1-PC
as a debugging task whose objective is
the detection of reasoning failures, then type-1 errors can be regarded as false negatives.

2.

Type-2 errors
: The model mistakenly claims that the proof is wrong (false positives).

3.

Type-3 errors
: These represent formatting issues (such as the model’s response missing an expected field).

4.

Type-4 errors
: The model correctly claims that the proof is wrong but fails to pinpoint the first (topmost) error.

5.

Type-5 errors
: The model correctly claims that the proof is wrong
and
manages to pinpoint the first error, but it mischaracterizes
the error’s type (syntax/type/logic).

Formatting errors represent instruction-following failures and are rare.
The rest of the error types are arranged in order of seriousness:
Type-1 errors are perhaps the most serious: failing to detect an incorrect proof.
Type-2 errors (mistakenly claiming that a proof is incorrect) are also fundamental.
These are the green and blue errors in the plot, and together they make up the majority of
errors for most models, especially the weaker ones. Type-4 and type-5 errors can only occur if the model
has already correctly classified the proof as wrong.
Accordingly, these errors (brown and gray) expose more subtle metacognitive weaknesses: failures
in error localization or error characterization. Type-4 errors, in particular, occur when the model
fails to identify the root problem in an incorrect proof. Instead, the model singles out another error (since the proof
may contain multiple errors) or it hallucinates a spurious one. Type-5 errors are the most innocuous.
Here the model properly calls out an incorrect proof and successfully identifies the first error
but classifies it incorrectly (e.g., miscategorizing a syntax error as a logic error).

Figure
6
plots the distribution of these error
types across the set of all root failures by all models.
Among the top-performing models, false negatives (type-1 errors) tend to be more common than false positives (type-2 errors).
Gemini 3.1 and Claude Opus 4.5 are the only models to make zero type-4 errors,
highlighting their strong debugging skills. That said, in this task the number of errors
among the top models is so small that detailed analyses are unlikely to yield statistically
robust conclusions.

Among the rest of the models, we see clear behavioral
similarities between DeepSeek-V3, Grok-3, and GPT-4o.
All three are strongly inclined to find fault with correct proofs,
with type-2 errors for these models exceeding all other error types combined.
DeepSeek-V3, in particular, rejects 291 out of the 300 proofs, consistent with the overly cautious profile
it manifested in other
ProofGrid
tasks. This tendency was tapered
somewhat in DeepSeek 3.1, which rejects 200 out of the 300 proofs,
but that is still a lot of false positives.
The situation is largely reversed for
llama-3.1-405b
, which accepts 84% of all proofs
as correct. Mistral Magistral strikes a balance between false positives and false negatives.

Performance vs Input Size

Figure 7:
PL1-PC
accuracies for the top 10 models as a function of proof length.

Most proofs in
PL1-PC
are short, so there is insufficient data for a conclusive analysis
of proof-checking performance as a function of proof length. Nevertheless, for the better
models anyway, and in this particular evaluation regime of short proofs
without intricate logical structure, the data suggests that
performance is not significantly
affected by proof length. This is in contrast to proof writing and other generative reasoning
tasks, where performance is more sensitive to input size, even for smaller problems.
Fig.
7
plots proof-checking accuracy as a function of proof length
for the top 10 models. Proof length stretches to no more than 50, excluding comments and blank lines.

To clarify the relationship between accuracy and proof length, the proofs have been divided into
variable-width bins along the horizontal axis. Each bin covers a range of proof lengths
(for example, 5–20, 29–40, and so on), and the points show the average accuracy for all proofs in that bin.
The bins are variable-width because the distribution of proof lengths is uneven: there are many short proofs
but far fewer long ones. Using equal-width bins (
e.g.
, every 10 units of proof length) would have produced
some bins with too few samples to estimate accuracy reliably, particularly at the long-proof end.
Instead, we have automatically adjusted the bin widths so that each bin includes roughly a minimum
number of
n
samples (here
n\geq 30
). Extremely sparse tail bins (where proof lengths exceeded 100
and only one or two examples were available) were dropped entirely to avoid misleading fluctuations.
Thus, the final range reflects all bins that passed this minimum sample threshold.
Beneath each bin label on the
x
-axis, the number of proofs (
n
) used in that bin is printed, showing the data
density across the proof-length spectrum.
This adaptive binning, which we use for all similar plots in this paper,
gives a clearer and fairer picture of performance across the range,
instead of overemphasizing regions with plenty of data or misrepresenting sparse regions.

All of these models start out strongly and maintain high performance throughout, experiencing only gentle declines
as proof length increases. The best of them maintain accuracy strictly above 80% for proofs of all shown lengths.
This is unsurprising, as these models would not be able to attain overall accuracies in the 90s without exhibiting
stable performance across proofs of all sizes. Fig.
8
plots similar
curves for the bottom 14 models. Here we see some steeper declines on longer proofs,
but the overall effect remains relatively modest even in this group.

These plots suggest that the core challenge in checking these proofs is not their size.
This aligns with our results from the harder task
PL3-PC
, where proofs are considerably
longer yet performance remains largely insensitive to proof length, especially for top-tier models.
And it is consistent with what we know
about the computational complexity of proof checking, which is much lower—essentially
O(n)

for a proof of size
n
—than that of reasoning problems involving search.
One might therefore expect that these verification tasks could be scaled
to longer proofs (with several hundreds or thousands of lines) without immediately plateauing
the best models at a failure point, which could be useful for probing
logical dependency management when the relevant information
is spread out across far more tokens. However, the challenge version of this task that we introduce
in Section
3
,
PL1-PC-c
, will show that even the strongest models at present
struggle in this task when the input proofs become significantly longer (in the hundreds of lines)
and more structurally complex (
e.g.
, with more heavily nested assumptions).

Figure 8:
PL1-PC
accuracies as a function of proof length for the bottom 14 models.

Qualitative Observations

PL1-PC
outputs are richly structured and provide a wealth of data for
analyzing performance and gaining deeper insights into how models reason.
For instance, it is an interesting
question whether specific types of model errors exhibit systematic correlations with structural proof
characteristics such as the level of assumption nesting or recall span (how far back a cited lemma was derived).
While we defer such finer-grained analyses to future work, in the rest of this section we make some
high-level qualitative observations informed by a close inspection of the
PL1-PC
results.

•

In this task, models that claim to find reasoning errors must justify their verdicts with detailed information
about the alleged error—its exact location and characteristics. As mentioned earlier, the required documentation
includes an
errorDescription
field whose value must describe the
error in natural language. When less able models make a type-2 error, wrongly
rejecting a proof as incorrect, their error descriptions not only tend to be vague, but they also tend to ramble on,
often devolving into self-refuting statements. Instead of being concise error summaries,
they end up serving as records of extended deliberations and self-analyses in which models eventually
talk themselves out of their own error reports.
This is a distinct linguistic signature of a recurring failure of cognitive control
that manifests itself under different guises. For instance, recall the
proof-writing pattern mentioned on p.
41
about comments
being used as proof-search scratchpads, where the model contemplates different tactics and changes course midstream
instead of commenting on the particular proof it has written, “failing to separate its internal monologue and search process
from the final polished output.” Here is an example
from GPT-4.1 on
PL1-PC
item 7, where the model claims to find an error in a proof that
is in fact correct, objecting to this particular line:

B_or_A := (B | A)
BY
right-either
on
B, A_from_case1;

Here,
A_from_case1
is the atom
A
, which was previously derived and holds at this particular point in the proof.
Recall also that
right-either
is a binary rule that takes any two formulas
p
and
q
as arguments, and provided
that the second argument
q
holds, it returns the disjunction
(
p\;|\;q
)
.
GPT-4.1 explains its rationale for why the above application of
right-either
is flawed
as follows (we reprint its response here verbatim, including the use of caps):

right-either
requires its right argument to be a formula that currently holds, but at this point in
the proof,
B
does not hold. The only formulas being assumed or derived inside this scope are

(A & (A | B))
,
A
(from
left-and
on
case1
), and
A_from_case1
itself.

B
has not been established as true in this scope. The correct application would be
right-either

on any formula for the left, and the right (
A_from_case1
), since
right-either

produces
(B | A_from_case1)
, but requires only
A_from_case1
to hold. However,
using
B
as the left argument is only permitted syntactically, not semantically, if
B

does not hold; but the right argument (
A_from_case1
) must hold, which it does.
However,
right-either
on
B, A_from_case1
produces

(B | A_from_case1)
, so as written, it is correct; but if the order were reversed,
and the right argument did not hold, it would be an error. Thus, looking further, while the
BY

annotation and the use here is correct, it does not actually lead to a logic error at this specific step.
Therefore, continuing to the surrounding context, the derivation in the second disjunct
((B | A)

&

(B

==>

~

B))

proceeds using
B_implies_notB
, which does hold. The next possible error is the application of
from-false

on
((((
~

B)

|

A)

&

(
~

(B

|

(
~

A))))

|

((B

|

A)

&

(B

==>

(
~

B))))

in the scope where false was just derived. However,
from-false
requires that
false
holds,
which it does, and it can derive any formula, so this is also correct. Moving back to the
BY
annotation in the step:

⬇

1

((((~

B)

|

A)

&

(~

(B

|

(~

A))))

|

((B

|

A)

&

(B

==>

(~

B))))

2

BY

right-either

on

(((~

B)

|

A)

&

(~

(B

|

(~

A)))),

D2

here,
right-either
requires only its right argument,
D2
, to hold, not the left;
D2
is

((B

|

A)

&

(B

==>

~

B))
, just constructed above. That is correct.

After fully checking, THERE IS NO LOGIC, TYPE, or SYNTAX ERROR in the annotated proof.

Nevertheless, the FINAL DERIVED CONCLUSION of the proof is the result of the last step:

cases on premise-1, case1_proof, case2_proof
. That produces the conclusion

\mbox{\tt((((\raisebox{3.01385pt}{\texttildelow}\mbox{$\,$}{}B)\mbox{$\,$}|\mbox{$\,$}{}A)\mbox{$\,$}\&\mbox{$\,$}(\raisebox{3.01385pt}{\texttildelow}\mbox{$\,$}(B\mbox{$\,$}|\mbox{$\,$}(\raisebox{3.01385pt}{\texttildelow}\mbox{$\,$}{}A))))\mbox{$\,$}|\mbox{$\,$}((B\mbox{$\,$}|\mbox{$\,$}{}A)\mbox{$\,$}{}\&\mbox{$\,$}(B\mbox{$\,$}{}==>\mbox{$\,$}(\raisebox{3.01385pt}{\texttildelow}\mbox{$\,$}{}B))))},

which matches exactly the required conclusion!
Thus, the proof is correct.
Therefore,
errorDetails
should be empty and
correct
should be true.

Of course, it is incoherent that “
errorDetails
should be empty” when the model itself
inserted this text into
errorDetails
.

This happens even with more recent and powerful models, though much less frequently.
On problem 150, Grok-4 emits a page-long
errorDescription
that contains 10 occurrences of “wait”, 32 “yes” answers
to questions that it posed to itself, 9 occurrences of “fine”, and ends with “Yes. Fine. I think the proof is correct.”
Here is another one from Grok-4 on item 276:

The step derives
(
~

A)

inside the assumption of
B
, but the use of
from-false
is valid only if
false
holds at that point.
However, the previous contradiction is derived, yes, but the intention is to derive
~

A
, which is fine, but wait, no error here.
Wait, actually, upon reexamination, the error is later.

The meandering of these descriptions means that they’re often longer than their correct counterparts.
For example, the mean string length of correct error descriptions by DeepSeek-V3 is 281, but when the model makes type-2 errors this
jumps to 424, a 51% increase. For some models this increase is statistically
significant
48
48

48

We performed statistical comparisons between the mean character lengths of

errorDescription
strings for correct judgments and for type-2 errors (false rejections).
For each model, we applied a one-sided permutation test
(
H_{1}
: type-2
>
correct) together with Welch’s unequal-variance

t
-test and two effect-size estimates—Cliff’s
\delta
and Hedges’
g
.
A difference was considered reliable when the permutation
p\leq 0.05

and the 95% confidence interval of
\delta
lay strictly above zero.
By these criteria, several models show statistically significant increases in description length:
DeepSeek-V3 (+143 characters,
\delta
= 0.41),
claude-sonnet-420250514
(+86 characters,
\delta
= 0.38),

grok-3
(+73 characters,
\delta
= 0.29),
deepseek-r1-0528
(+71 characters,
\delta
= 0.52),
and
o3-mini
(+102 characters,
\delta
= 0.54). For stronger models with very few type-2 cases
(e.g.,
grok-4
and
claude-sonnet-4.5-ext-thinking
), the apparently large jumps are statistically unreliable
due to small-sample variance.

and could be used as a cheap signal to gauge the correctness of their outputs,
essentially by treating the length of error descriptions as a lightweight proxy for uncertainty
or reasoning instability, since longer outputs tend to reflect internal conflict in the reasoning trace,
with the model alternating between competing internal judgments.

The fact that this is a cross-task behavioral motif suggests a broader hypothesis: LLMs exhibit
cognitive leakage between what philosophers call the context of discovery and the context of justification
(see Section 5 of Schickore’s article on scientific discovery
[
77
]
).
When models are uncertain, explanations that are supposed to justify the reasoning behind their decisions
sometimes collapse into stream-of-consciousness attempts to second-guess and revise those decisions, typically
ending in a state of cognitive dissonance.

•

Some error explanations are incoherent not because the model contradicts itself on the
existence of the error, but for other reasons, such as gross pattern-matching mistakes or
random unintelligible claims.
Here is an example from
deepseek-reasoner
(on item 163):

dn
must be applied to a double negation of the form

(
~

~

p
)
, but here it is applied to

(
~

(
~

(
~

C & A)))
,
which is a triple negation and does not match the required syntactic form.

Brushing aside the fact that every triple negation is also trivially a double negation,
the formula in question here,
(
~

(
~

(
~

C & A)))
, is not a triple
negation; the innermost negation sign applies only to
C
, unlike the two previous negation signs.

•

All models, including the best ones available today, seem to have a hard time grasping rudimentary concepts involving negation.
A notable example is a widespread failure to understand complementation. This is a pervasive notion in logic, especially
in the context of SAT solving, where it is usually defined for CNF literals: the complement of an atom
\alpha
is
\neg\alpha
, while
the complement of a negated atom
\neg\alpha
is
\alpha
. Thus, unlike conventional syntactic negation, which always prepends
a negation sign, complementation is an involutive operation that systematically flips polarity. If we write
c(l)
for
the complement of a literal
l
, we always have
c(c(l))=l
.

The notion is naturally extended to all formulas: if a formula
p

is a negation
\neg q
, then its complement is
q
; and if
p
is not a negation, its complement is
\neg p
.
The concept is introduced in the first paragraph of the
PL1-PC
input prompt as a relation, emphasizing
that it is a symmetric relation:

We say that two formulas are “complementary” (or “complements”, or that they “complement” each other) when one is
the syntactic negation of the other. This is a symmetric relation:
p
complements
q
iff
q
complements
p
.

Accordingly,
A
complements
\neg A
just as well as
\neg A
complements
A
.
There is no directionality or order involved; it’s not the first argument that
always needs to be the negation of the second, or the other way around.
The only requirement is that one of the two (either one) is the negation of the other.

Certain inference rules of NDL require some of their arguments to be complementary. For instance,

mt
(modus tollens) must be applied to two formulas of the form
(p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
and
r
where
r
and
q
are complements.
Assuming that both
(p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
and
r
hold, the result (conclusion) is
\neg p
. Likewise,
dsyl
(disjunctive syllogism)
is applied to two formulas of the form
(p_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}p_{2})
and
q
, both of which must hold and
where
q
complements one of the disjuncts, either
p_{1}
or
p_{2}
.
If it complements
p_{1}
then the result is
p_{2}
, and if it complements
p_{2}
the result is
p_{1}
.
This is not a subtle notion, yet somehow all models end up getting it wrong at some point
(some more often than others), frequently confusing it with negation, which is intrinsically asymmetric.
Here is an example from Grok-4 on item 56:

dsyl
requires the second argument to complement one of the disjuncts in the first argument syntactically, but
D

does not complement
(
~

D)
or
(C

==>

A)
.
The syntactic complement of
(
~

D)
is

(
~

(
~

D))
, which is not identical to
D
.

But if “the syntactic complement of
(
~

D)
is

(
~

(
~

D))
,” then the syntactic complement of

D
must be
(
~

D)
. And if complementation is symmetric,
as stated in the prompt, then
D
also complements
(
~

D)
.
The rudimentary error here is to view complementation as identical to negation.

As another example, here is part of the error description that GPT-5 gives for item 184:

dsyl
requires arguments of the form
(
p

|

q
)
and
r
where
r
is complementary to either
p
or
q
.
Here

is

(A

|
~

and the second argument is
B
, which is not complementary to either
A
or
~

B
.
Therefore the application of
dsyl
is invalid.

Similar mistakes are common,
e.g.
, GPT-5.2 says the following regarding item 36:

is invalid. The disjunction

is

Disjunctive syllogism requires the second argument to complement (i.e.,
be the syntactic negation of) one of the disjuncts. But

not_B
is
(
~

B)
,
which does not complement either
(
~

or
(
~

~

(the complement of
(

(
~

would be
(
~

(
~

(
~

).
Therefore
dsyl
cannot be applied here, and the conclusion

(
~

is not derivable by this step.

Likewise, on problem 69 it states that
(
~

does not complement
(
~

~

B)
,
and on problem 198 it stresses that
(
~

is not complementary to
(
~

~

A)
.

•

The most noteworthy and concerning theme that emerges from a close inspection of the data
is a recurring failure of models to exhibit judgment consistency. A model might flag something as an error in one proof and
disregard the exact same issue in another proof. We refer to this broad phenomenon as

epistemic instability
. It has many manifestations, with one of the most striking instances emerging from the interaction between
proof writing and proof checking. For example, a model might write a correct proof
D
for a given problem,
but when we later ask it independently to verify
D
, it incorrectly rejects it as wrong. This is
clearly problematic, suggesting a sort of split-brain syndrome whereby the model writes proofs with one brain and checks
them with another.

For a human reasoner, it would be odd
if a person who is both rational and truthful would write a correct
proof and then reject that same proof, especially if this behavioral
cycle repeated itself. After writing their proof for the first time,
they might perhaps discover what they (incorrectly) believe to be a bug.
But if so, they would not then go on to rewrite the same proof with
the same alleged bug.
In other words, even if a human reasoner exhibited an initial
inconsistency between the two tasks, they would
quickly reach an equilibrium. Crucially, this equilibrium is independent
of the proof’s objective correctness; it is instead determined
by the human reasoner’s subjective cognitive state.
Unless they were trying to be deceptive, their proof-writing and proof-checking stances
would align. Indeed, it would be a sign of incoherence if they did not.
But we found such unstable cross-task behavior to be a common motif with AI models.

We plan to revisit model behavior at the interplay between proof writing and proof checking
in forthcoming work. However, the phenomenon is not limited to the interaction between those two tasks.
A careful analysis shows that it is frequently seen even within
PL1-PC
in isolation.
An example is given by the aforementioned issue with complementation. For every time that a model
flags a spurious error by demanding syntactic negation instead of complementation, there are more times
where it does
not
flag instances of the exact same pattern, seemingly happy to accept complementation
instead of strict syntactic negation. This indicates that the model has no stable epistemic commitment
on the issue and no coherent internal principles governing the distinction between the two.

For concrete examples, recall that on multiple occasions GPT-5.2 insists on conflating complementation with syntactic negation.
Yet many other times it does the opposite. For instance, on item 24, it condones an application of

dsyl
to
(\neg A\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg C)
and
C
, even though, according to its own logic,
“disjunctive syllogism requires the second argument to complement (i.e., be the syntactic negation of)
one of the disjuncts” and
C
is obviously not complementary to
\neg C
in that sense. Likewise, on item 37,
GPT-5.2 happily accepts an application of
dsyl
to
(\neg\neg C\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg B)
and
B
.
On item 49 it accepts an application of
dsyl
to
((\neg A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C)\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg(D\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}E))

and
(D\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}E)
. On item 15 it accepts an application of
dsyl
to
((A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg B)\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg(C\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}B))

and
(C\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}B)
. There are other examples. On item 299 it delivers a lecture on how
B

does
complement
\neg B
, even though
B
is clearly not the syntactic negation of
\neg B
.

As another example, GPT-5 rightly complains several times about applications of
dm
(De Morgan)
that skip a double negation step. For instance, on item 171 it writes:

Applying
dm
to

(
~

(
~

A

&

C))
should yield

(
~

~

|

~

,
not
(A

|

~

C)
. The step’s

annotation claims a conclusion that is not
the direct result of the
dm
rule (it eliminates the double negation without a separate

GPT-5 flags the same error elsewhere (
e.g.
, items 143 and 210). Yet on item 298 it happily accepts the following
fragment:

⬇

1

(~

3

...

4

5

...

even though this

Here is another example: As we pointed out earlier (p.
4
),
LLM-written proofs often write conditional proofs with syntactically malformed assumptions,
along the lines of

assume

arbitrary-identifier

{
\cdots
}
. In
PL1-PC
, strong models often
detect this error and explain it quite well. For instance, here is what GPT-5 says about the proof of item 140:

Malformed
assume
statement. A conditional proof must have the form

\mbox{\tt assume}\mbox{$\;\>$}p\mbox{$\;\>$}\{\mbox{$\;\>$}\cdots\mbox{$\;\>$}\}

or

\mbox{\tt assume}\mbox{$\;\>$}I\mbox{\tt:=}\;p\mbox{$\;\>$}\{\mbox{$\;\>$}\cdots\mbox{$\;\>$}\}

where
p
is a formula. Here,
assume

B_hyp

{

lacks a hypothesis formula (and
B_hyp
is not defined as a formula),
so the step cannot be parsed and the intended conditional
(B

==>

C)
is not formed.

But on item 281 GPT-5 says nothing about the conditional subproof

even though the assumption is malformed in exactly the same way as the one that GPT-5 complained about in item 140.
The same pattern can be observed across all systems; we use examples from GPT-5 and GPT-5.2 here for illustration purposes.

These inconsistencies are akin to a teacher grading student assignments who sometimes marks a particular step
as an error (“you can’t use double negation here”) and other times gives full credit for the very same step.
The problem isn’t the strictness (or even the fairness) of the grading, but the
judgment instability
:
the teacher’s evaluative criteria drift from one paper to the next, suggesting they have no stable conception
of what the rules actually mean.

It is possible that these issues will be resolved in the near future, although the
pervasive judgment inconsistencies point to deeper problems of epistemic coherence;
we will return to this topic in Section
7.5
.
Our main aim in this section is not to catalog a particular set of failure modes or quirks of these specific model versions,
which are likely to be transient, but to illustrate the sort of reasoning pathologies that tasks like
PL1-PC

can expose. These clearly demonstrate the need for richer, non-scalar evaluation signals for assessing reasoning competence.

A challenge version of
PL1-PC

In this section we present results for
PL1-PC-c
, a more challenging proof-checking dataset consisting of 50 longer proofs.
We only computed results for the 4 currently strongest models, as the rest are very unlikely to perform better than
random guessing. Even among these 4 top-tier models, only two of them manage to perform better than flipping a coin on the
guess-only version of the task. These 50 proofs have lengths ranging from 720 to 987, with average and median lengths
of 839 and 823, respectively. The proofs were generated by our own hand-rolled theorem prover and were verifiably correct.
We then randomly and minimally corrupted a small number of them (by injecting exactly one error in 9 of the 50 proofs).
The proofs are challenging to verify because they have a lot
of conditional subproofs (215.5 per proof on average), with a high degree of nesting.
The distribution of nesting depths is shown below.

The figure shows that the mass of conditional subproofs rises steadily and peaks around depth 6,
then falls off fairly quickly after 7. So a typical subproof in this challenge corpus is
nested fairly deeply (at an average depth of 5.3).

Results for the 4 models are shown below.

Table 6:

All models perform somewhat better in the guessing-only version of the task, but the deltas are marginal.
This implies that the main difficulty for these models is deciding correctness in the first place,
not localizing and explaining errors.
Restricting attention to the guessing-only task variant, even the top-performing model, Claude Opus 4.5,
performs only 38% better (69-50/50) than random guessing.
49
49

49

Flipping a coin still guarantees 50% accuracy, even
though the set is imbalanced with 41 correct proofs and 9 incorrect ones.
It does worse compared
to the majority-guess baseline: because 41 out of the 50 proofs are correct, always guessing “correct”
guarantees an accuracy of 82%, considerably higher than 69%, which suggests that the model is not exploiting
the class imbalance. Indeed, all four models are below the majority baseline. If they were even
weakly exploiting signal in the proofs, they should at least conclude that most proofs are correct.
The fact that they don’t suggests that they are not reliably extracting the right structural cues.
Remarkably, both Gemini 3.1 and GPT-5.4 fail to beat random guessing.

Perhaps the clearest takeaway from these results is that proof-checking/debugging ability is an orthogonal
capability axis. While Claude Opus 4.5 places last among these 4 elite models in generative reasoning tasks like
proof unmasking and gap filling (see Sections
4
and
5
),
it takes first place in proof checking. Likewise, even though GPT-5.4 outperforms GPT-5.2 in those tasks,
here it trails behind by 20 absolute points, reinforcing the point that better generation does not translate
to better verification.

4
Proof Masking

In this section we introduce a novel proof-completion task. Given a proof, we mask any of the following four types of items:

1.

conclusions (formulas immediately preceding the
BY
keyword);

2.

inference rules (immediately preceding
on
);

3.

assumptions (immediately following
assume
); and

4.

one or more arguments to an inference rule application (immediately following
on
).

Masks are unique identifiers:
MASK1
,
MASK2
, and so on (though the indices need not be consecutive).
We only mask formulas at the top level, not any proper subformulas (
e.g.
, we are never left with something like
(
~

MASK8 | C)
).

The objective is to determine if there is a way to unmask the proof so that it correctly derives the target conclusion from the given premises;
and if so, to provide a detailed assignment of appropriate values to the masks. As an example, consider again this proof:

With the original proof in sight, it is of course straightforward to generate a correct mask assignment:

MASK1
:
A
;
MASK2
:
B
;
MASK3
:
mp
;
MASK4
:
A
; and so on.
But without the benefit of having seen the proof before, this is not trivial.
It can be loosely likened to a constraint satisfaction problem (CSP), where the variables
are the masks and the constraints are given by the syntax rules as well as the operational
semantics of the proof language, including scope rules (for names as well as assumptions and intermediate conclusions).
For instance:

•

We can infer that
MASK1
must be
A
from the specification of the excluded-middle
rule and the fact that we see the conclusion
(A |
~

A)
to the left of
BY
.

•

On the last line, because the goal is
(B |
~

D)
and the
pivot disjunction is
(A |
~

A)
, we can infer from the specification of
cases
that
MASK15
and
MASK16
must be conditionals of the form

(A ==> (B | D))
and
(
~

A ==> (B | D))
, respectively.

•

Knowing that the high-level proof is a case analysis on
(A |
~

A)
, we can infer that the conditional subproof on lines 12–16 must
postulate
(
~

A)
as an assumption,
i.e.
, that
MASK8
must be
(
~

A)
.

When a proof is heavily obscured, the problem generally becomes more complex. In this dataset, we randomly
masked anywhere from 30% to 90% of all candidates (of all 4 types listed above) in a given proof.

Although this task is superficially similar to a CSP, it diverges in two crucial respects.
In a CSP, constraints are explicitly given as part of the problem: each constraint
pertains to a specific list of variables and allows a specific set of assignments (tuples of values).
By contrast, the constraints governing valid mask assignments are implicit, heterogeneous,
and distributed across the proof’s syntactic and semantic structure. Indeed, identifying
these constraints is a key part of the problem. The main source of difficulty is not
combinatorial solution multiplicity but delayed constraint revelation and the
need to maintain a long sequence of correct commitments.
The task thus occupies a regime where symbolic reasoning is unavoidable but
the relevant symbolic structure is not explicitly given. This distinguishes it
from puzzles where the main difficulty lies in combinatorial search rather than
in extracting the relevant constraints from context and the operational semantics of proofs.
When models fail to unmask a proof, it is not simply because the
search space is large, but because the task requires jointly inferring structure,
constraints, and solutions under global consistency requirements. This reflects
deeper limitations than simply “models can’t do backtracking.”
The second major difference is that the set of values is not explicitly given either.
It is not even clear that this set is finite. We know that mask
values are either formulas or rules, and that the former are likely to be
subformulas of the premises and/or the goal, of which there is only a finite number.
But the subformula property is not guaranteed, since we know nothing about the original
proof, which could have arbitrary detours above and beyond the usual and
relatively innocuous detours introduced by classical logic (double negations and
the use of
false
). A fortunate corollary of these differences is that

PL1-PM
instances cannot be straightforwardly encoded as CSP or SAT problems and
outsourced to an external solver. Nor is it easy for a model to solve the task
just by writing bespoke code for it. Even if the model could write flawless
infrastructure for the problem (e.g., a parser for masked NDL proofs), an unmasking
algorithm would still be far from obvious.

However, even with a large number of masks sprinkled over a large proof, this is a relatively easy task
as long as mask
density
is not excessive (see below for a more precise definition of density).
First, because NDL is strongly structured,
the expected category (or “type”) of a mask is fixed: it is either
formula
or
rule
, and it is determined
by the position of the mask variable: if the mask occurs immediately before a
BY
or immediately after an
assume
,
it must be a formula; if it occurs immediately before an
on
, it must be a
rule
; and so on.
Second, all masks are unique, so there are no cross-references and unification across multiple proof lines is not required.
Finally, most masks are functionally determined and have unique/canonical values that can be locally inferred.
Accordingly, putting aside pathological cases such as proof with several steps that are completely masked,
there is no combinatorial explosion of solutions, no need to keep track of large equivalence classes of assignments,
and no true symmetry-breaking issues (aside from rare commutations like example
3
below).
This means that values for most masks can be inferred incrementally, on a line-by-line basis.
Some choices are, of course, semantically coupled across the entire proof tree. For example,
choosing the conclusion of an excluded middle application constrains the shape
of the subsequent case analysis and thus the assumptions and conclusions of the relevant conditional subproofs and the arguments of the corresponding
cases
application.
But these tend to be light inter-dependencies that become obvious after a single pass over the proof tree,
which typically suffices because the constraints are acyclic (determined by the linear order of the proof)
50
50

50

A
proof step depends only on information established in earlier steps (such as previously derived conclusions, introduced premises, or assumptions)
and never on content that appears later. This means that the constraint graph over masks is a DAG (directed acyclic graph),
allowing the proof to be reconstructed or verified in a single forward pass without backtracking. Once a line has been correctly
unmasked, it can be referenced but need not be altered. In that sense, the task
is topologically sorted by proof order: solving or checking constraints proceeds deterministically from top to bottom.

and nearly deterministic.

Figure 9:
PL1-PM
problem statistics

The
PL1-PM
task slice contains 300 proofs that have been masked as described above. All 300 proofs are correct, containing no errors of any kind.
80 of these proofs are conditionalized, meaning that the relevant premises are explicitly assumed as hypotheses (instead of taken as global premises).
Statistics about the corresponding problems are shown in Fig.
9
. Note that these are “easy” problems along pretty much
all dimensions: the total number of premises is small (less than 3 on average) and, viewed as an AST, each premise has about 6 nodes on average.
Conclusions have a median size of 3 nodes but the average is considerably higher (slightly over 6). The median number of propositional atoms in a problem is 3,
and negations are the most rare logical connective save for biconditionals.

Statistics about the length of these proofs are shown in the following table. Note that these proofs are even shorter than those in
PL1-PC
,
whose median length is 16% greater. 90% of the
PL1-PM
proofs have fewer than 50 lines.

Mean
