More granular statistics about the distribution of inference rules and conditional reasoning in these proofs can be found in
Fig.
10
. Conditional (hypothetical) reasoning is very common in this corpus, largely
because about half of these proofs are conditionalized and all such proofs start by explicitly making hypotheses.

Figure 10:
Inferential profile of
PL1-PM
proofs.

Given a masked proof and the underlying argument (premises and target conclusion), the model is asked to output a dictionary
M
that maps
every mask in the input to an appropriate value:

{MASK1:
\>\cdots
,
\ldots
}

in such a way that when the proof is unmasked in accordance with
M
(i.e., by plugging in
M[m]
for every mask
m
that appears in the input),
the result is a complete proof that correctly derives the goal from the premises. The model is told to output the empty dictionary
M=\mbox{\tt\lx@text@lbrace\lx@text@rbrace}

if it believes that there is no valid assignment of values to masks that can successfully unmask the proof. This effectively acts as a distractor, since we know
that every masked proof can be unmasked.

The key question addressed by this task is whether a model can infer missing symbolic structure consistent with the rules that
govern natural deduction.
Whereas proof writing and proof checking test a model’s ability to construct or evaluate a line of reasoning,
proof masking asks it to reconstruct elided reasoning details from partial information, inferring what intermediate conclusions,
assumptions, or rule applications could make the surrounding proof context coherent.
This task is superficially reminiscent of code infilling, where a model must recover a missing span of source code,
but the resemblance is limited. In code infilling the missing span typically forms a single contiguous “hole”—a bulk
fragment whose boundaries are arbitrary and syntactically defined, and whose recovery in practice depends mainly on
local lexical and type constraints. By contrast, in proof masking the missing content is dispersed across dozens
of micro-holes, each corresponding to a distinct atomic element of the proof (an individual conclusion, rule name,
assumption, or argument to a rule application). Each mask encodes only a tiny unit of inferential information, yet all
of these units must be filled jointly and coherently, because the value assigned to one mask might constrain
the permissible values of subsequent masks. The model must therefore maintain a global, internally consistent hypothesis about
the entire proof structure while simultaneously resolving many fine-grained local uncertainties.
Essentially, it must treat the proof itself as an object of reasoning, reasoning about reasoning rather than merely executing it.
This gives proof masking a distinct meta-reasoning flavor. A system that can perform well in this task
demonstrates not only procedural knowledge of inferential mechanisms, but also a representational grasp of
how those mechanisms fit together and a capacity for metalogical comprehension.

As we already pointed out, most mask values are uniquely determined.
In general, however, masking problems are underdetermined,
i.e.
, there
may be multiple mask assignments that yield a correct proof. Accordingly,
it is not expected that a mask assignment produced by a model recovers the original proof. For instance, the original proof might contain a chain of two consecutive
applications of left and right conjunction elimination:

\cdots

left-and
on
(A & B);

right-and
on
(A & B);

\cdots
.

(3)

But if we mask both rules,
e.g.
, by replacing
left-and
by
MASK-3
and
right-and
by
MASK-4
,
then
MASK3
:
left-and
;
MASK4
:
right-and
and
MASK3
:
right-and
;
MASK4
:
left-and
are both valid solutions,
because the two inference steps can be swapped without affecting the correctness of the subsequent reasoning.
For that reason, LLM responses must be evaluated semantically rather than syntactically. That is, LLM responses are evaluated
not by matching the specified mask values against a fixed ground truth, but by unmasking the proof according to those values and then
using Athena to check that the resulting proof correctly derives the target.
If a model claims that the problem is unsolvable, that response is always counted as incorrect, since we know by construction that every
masked proof can be unmasked.

It may be interesting to note that while the reasoning required for this task appears to be purely deductive,
it actually leans heavily on pragmatics and on inductively learned regularities about proof construction.
In particular, many proof steps are not strictly (deductively) determined by the local context, but
they
are
determined when one takes into account how proofs tend to be written in practice.
As a concrete example (from item 5 of the challenge dataset), suppose the proof has a biconditional
premise of the form
(p_{1}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}p_{2})
, say
((D\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}B)\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}(B\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A))
.
The first three steps of the masked proof are as follows:

⬇

1

p0

:=

((B

<==>

A)

==>

(D

==>

B))

BY

MASK3

on

((D

==>

B)

<==>

(B

<==>

A));

2

((~

(D

==>

B))

|

(B

<==>

A))

BY

MASK5

on

MASK4;

It is clear from line 2 that
MASK3
must be
right-iff
, as that is the only inference rule that could produce the given conclusion.
Now, in theory, line 1 remains underconstrained, in that
MASK1
could still be either
left-iff
or
right-iff
.
This is because, in principle, the proof could contain a redundancy and go on to repeat the same inference
on line 2; nothing in the logic forbids that.
Pragmatically
,
however, this would be highly unorthodox. It’s much more likely that line 1 detaches the left-to-right part of the biconditional premise and line 2
detaches the right-to-left part. That’s not a quirk; it’s an inductive regularity about proofs as social artifacts,
not just as logical objects.
So a human solver would naturally commit to
MASK1
being
left-iff
after deducing from line 2 that
MASK3

must be
right-iff
.
51
51

51

Deductively, there is yet another possibility for the first line: line 1 could be
an application of “biconditional definition” that derives
((p_{1}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}p_{2})\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}(\neg p_{1}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg p_{2}))
from
(p_{1}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}p_{2})
.
But this would also be dispreferred by human subjects, because we implicitly know, for example,
that proofs typically use a connective before expanding its definition, and that canonical pairs of eliminations
(e.g., a
left-iff
immediately followed by a
right-iff
) are an extremely common inference pattern
that is stylistically tight.

This marks another point of divergence from CSP/SAT problems, which do not have any notion of pragmatics.
The upshot is that proof reconstruction in this task, at least for humans, relies not just on pure deduction but on proof-writing conventions, stylistic regularities,
and general inductively derived pragmatic expectations about proof structure.

Table 7:
PL1-PM
accuracies, using both the instrumented NDL checker and the regular proof checker.

Results are shown in Table
7
, both under the instrumented proof checker
and the conventional one. Without the benefit of instrumentation,
performance tends to drop (significantly in many cases), though the vast majority of these
errors are uninteresting and rather innocuous, such as omitting an outer pair of parentheses from formulas.

On the basis of the instrumented accuracies of Table
7
, the discriminability index (DI) of
PL1-PM
is 0.38.
As discussed in Section
3.2
, this means that, on average, any two models differ in accuracy
by about 38 percentage points—a very large spread showing that the task teases apart models of different ability levels
extraordinarily well.

While the leading 3 models attain impressively high scores on this task,
it should be kept in mind that the proofs in this dataset are very short
(the median length is 25 lines, while 95% of the proofs have no more than 50 lines).
Even with these very short proofs, Fig.
11

shows that the performance for most of the top 10 models drops as proof length
increases even modestly, although the drop is very gentle for the top 4 models
(and the performance of Gemini 3.1, in particular, is essentially flat).
As shown in Fig.
12
, the decline is much more dramatic for the
remaining 14 models, whose accuracies effectively drop to zero by the time we get to
proofs with a mere 30-35 steps, with only a couple of exceptions.

Figure 11:
PL1-PM
accuracy vs proof length for the top 10 models; variable-width bins with
n\geq 25
.

Figure 12:
PL1-PM
accuracy vs proof length for the bottom 14 models; variable-width bins with
n\geq 25
.

This performance profile should be expected when correctness requires all masked positions to be
filled simultaneously.
52
52

52

The word “simultaneously” is referring to the evaluation criterion, not to the
generation process. The models of course generate mask values autoregressively, but the correctness of their outputs
is evaluated over the entire set of masks jointly, with an output counting as correct only if all masks
are given valid values that collectively yield a correct proof. So even though generation is incremental,
the reward landscape is globally conjunctive, with success requiring a perfect joint configuration of
all

micro-decisions made by a model.

If each individual prediction has probability
p
of being correct, then the probability
that the entire proof is correctly unmasked scales approximately as
p^{M}
, where
M
is the number of masks.
Thus, even small per-mask error rates compound multiplicatively into rapid instance-level degradation.
In addition, longer proofs impose heavier burdens on transformer attention and retrieval, with the model having to track
more assumptions, rule dependencies, and line references, while maintaining perfect syntactic fidelity.
These factors make the task more fragile under sequence generation.

A similar multiplicative-error-compounding argument could be made for proof checking, especially for
correct proofs: on the assumption that a proof step has an independent fixed probability
p
of being correctly checked by a model,
proof-checking accuracy should decay exponentially with longer proofs even if the task is locally easy (has a high
p
).
However, what we saw in
PL1-PC
is that accuracy either held steady or declined more gracefully as proof length increased.
So why does proof-checking seem to scale better than proof masking?
While the two tasks share some similarities (both are “locally easy”),
they also place different demands on a generative model.
Given an incorrect proof in
PL1-PC
,
a model must report the first erroneous step, which can be viewed as a sequential test
over steps
1,\dots,N
with success probability

P(\mbox{success\/}\mid j^{*})=(1-f)^{\,j^{*}-1}(1-m),

where we write
j^{*}
for the location (line number) of the first error, and
where
f
is the false-positive rate on correct steps above
j^{*}
(i.e., the probability
that the model will incorrectly mark such a correct step as erroneous)
and
m
is the miss rate on that first error. For strong models, and assuming we are
dealing with average incorrect proofs that have not been adversarially selected,

f
and
m
are small
and approximately length-invariant, so the probability of success will be relatively high even for
a long proof.
53
53

53

For instance, with
f=0.001
and
m=0.01
we get

P(\mbox{success\/}\mid j^{*})=0.9
even for
j^{*}=100
.

For
correct
proofs, on the other hand, the proof-checking situation is mathematically similar
to that in proof masking, with both tasks exhibiting multiplicative structure. In particular,
writing
P_{c}
and
P_{m}
for the
PL1-PC
and
PL1-PM
probabilities, respectively,
we have
P_{c}(\mbox{success\/})=p^{N}
,
where
p=1-f
is the probability that a step is correctly checked and
N
is the size of the proof (number of steps).
Likewise,
P_{m}(\mbox{success\/})=q^{M}
,
where
q
is the probability of correctly decoding a single mask and
M
is the total number of masks in the proof.
While both expressions have the same form, exponential decay is highly sensitive to base rates, so small differences in those rates
can translate into big differences in performance. For instance, if
p=0.999
(i.e., the error rate is
f=0.1\%
per step) and
q=0.99
(with a
1\%
error rate per mask),
then for
N=M=60
we have
P_{c}(\mbox{success\/})=0.94
, whereas
P_{m}(\mbox{success\/})=0.54
.
Why might the proof-masking base rate be an order of magnitude smaller than the proof-checking base rate (for correct proofs)?
Because one task requires generation while the other only requires recognition: in
PL1-PM
, a model must produce
syntactically correct formulas, variables, and rule names, whereas for
PL1-PC
it only needs to judge
correctness, without any risk of generation errors.
54
54

54

This applies to classification-only
formulations of proof checking, where the model does not need to generate any structured output.

Error Analysis

There is no natural error typology for
PL1-PM
that partitions all possible errors into a set of mutually exclusive
and jointly exhaustive types that can be readily counted, so we instead compute statistics on a number of revealing
error types that are analytically meaningful but neither exclusive nor exhaustive. These classes of errors are described
below. It will be useful to define
\mu
as a shorthand for the following quantity:
Writing
\#(D)
for the number of masks that occur in a masked proof
D
, and if
D_{1},\ldots,D_{300}
are the proofs in
PL1-PM
,
we set:

\mu={\sum}_{i=1}^{300}\#(D_{i}).

1.

illFormattedAnswers
: These occur when the output generated by a model is not a JSON dictionary of the schema specified by the
prompt (a mapping from masks to appropriate values). Min value: 0, max value: 300.

2.

emptyAnswers
: These errors occur when a model returns the empty dictionary
{}
as its answer, indicating
that there is no way to unmask the input proof. (Recall that the possibility of an empty answer was included as a distractor, since we know by construction
that all masked proofs can be unmasked.) Min value: 0, max value: 300.

3.

bogusMasks
: These occur when a non-empty answer includes values for hallucinated masks that do not appear anywhere in the given input.
Min value: 0, max value:
\infty
.

4.

missingMasks
: These occur when a non-empty answer is incomplete because the model fails to specify values for some of the masks in the input.
Min value: 0, max value:
\mu
.

5.

rulesToFormulas
: These occur when the model mistakenly lists a formula as the value of a mask that could only possibly assume a rule
as its value. For instance, if the input proof contains a line such as

\mbox{\tt MASK8 \mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{BY}}} MASK12 \mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{on}}}}\>\cdots

(4)

and a model assigns
MASK12:(
~

A)
, this constitutes a
rulesToFormulas
error, as it is patently obvious (by a purely
syntactic/positional cue, the fact that
MASK12
appears immediately before the
on
keyword) that
MASK12
can only range over rules,
not formulas. Min value: 0, max value:
\approx\mu
.

6.

formulasToRules
: These are similar to
rulesToFormulas
errors but err in the opposite direction: They occur when a model
assigns a rule to a mask that can only receive formulas as values. Considering (
4
) again, an example
would be a model that generated
MASK8:left-and
. Min value: 0, max value:
\approx\mu
.

Figure 13:
PL1-PM
errors

These 6 categories represent
severe
errors. It is one thing to mix up
one rule for another, but listing a rule as the value of a mask that can only range
over formulas is a much more serious mistake. Likewise for the rest of the above error
types, with the possible exception of
emptyAnswers
, a relatively less
flagrant type of failure. There are two main types of errors that do not fall
into any of these categories:
rulesToRules
errors (e.g., assigning
mt

to a mask whose correct value is
mp
), and
formulasToFormulas
errors,
which occur when the wrong formula is listed as the value of a mask. These are
more subtle errors, or at least less egregious, and round out our error typology
for this task.

Fig.
13
plots the raw counts for these error types across all models.
Note that we capped GPT-4.1’s
formulasToRules
errors
at 500 (its true value is 1,363) because using the full value would stretch the error-count
axis so far that the lower counts of other error types would become difficult to read.
We make the following observations:

•

Empty answers very likely signal uncertainty (
I can’t do this
) rather than random failures.
As usual, these errors are common among OpenAI’s mini models and earlier DeepSeek models, though we also
see a remarkable spike for GPT-4o (222 empty answers out of a total of 300).
These models are essentially using empty answers to broadcast uncertainty aversion:
when an input crosses some internal difficulty threshold (long proof or many masks),
they emit an empty dictionary instead of venturing a guess.

•

It is striking that even elite models make hard typing errors, of
type
rulesToFormulas
or
formulasToRules
(
e.g.
,
GPT-5 makes 17
formulasToRules
errors; the only model that makes zero such errors is Gemini 3.1).
It’s reasonable to conjecture that humans would very rarely make such errors, for the same reason they are
unlikely to put a verb where an adjective is expected.
Positional typing is a hard constraint for humans: after
assume
and before
BY
only formulas fit;
immediately before
on
only rules fit; and so on. For LLMs, by contrast, these constraints appear to be “soft” or fluid.
The fact that virtually all models make these mistakes, albeit to different degrees, means that models
find it easier to verify typing constraints (as in
PL1-PC
) than to produce outputs that respect them.
When generating, their decoders aren’t gated by the relevant grammar/typing lattice and end up
relying on token-level heuristics that occasionally slip across categories. This is the classic
failure signature of generative brittleness (lack of a hard “type checker” in the loop).
However, the frequency of these errors falls drastically among the top 4 models.

•

missingMasks
errors represent planning failures, or deficits in
executive function. These are numerous even for top models (e.g., 109
for GPT-5, 53 for Gemini-3, 31 for GPT-5.2, 35 for GPT-5.1, and 34 for Gemini 3.1).
Models here start filling and end up omitting keys. That’s not an issue of misunderstanding NDL
or making reasoning errors, it’s an output orchestration issue—counting
and enumerating all masks and persisting that plan through potentially long JSON outputs.
It’s the same phenomenon behind tool-call omissions in long chains.

•

bogusMasks
errors are relatively few but telling: when the list of required answers
is long, some models hallucinate masks.

•

Formatting errors are even rarer, but they are occasionally made, even by top models (2 such
errors by o3). These errors obviously track prompt adherence and JSON discipline.

A challenge version of
PL1-PM

In the remainder of this section we introduce
PL1-PM-c
, a challenge dataset
containing 50 masked proofs having hundreds of lines (anywhere from 323 to 946 lines;
the median is 400). As with the challenge version of
PL1-PC
, we only evaluate
a small number of the most capable models. Each problem is evaluated with high reasoning
settings, a high allowance reasoning tokens (100K), and a maximum wall-clock time
of 30 minutes . If no output is produced within this time, the instance is marked as failed.

While this dataset involves longer proofs, it is less challenging than

PL1-PM
in a precise technical sense.
Define the
mask density
of a masked proof
D
as the ratio
M(D)/L(D)
, where
M(D)
is the
number of masks
55
55

55

This is the same as the number of mask occurrences, since masks are unique.

in
D
and
L(D)
is the number of lines in
D
that may contain a mask, namely,
all inference steps (applications of an inference rule) and all openings of conditional deductions
(lines of the form
assume

\cdots
). This simple statistic is the cleanest indication of how
heavily masked a proof is. Low density means that most inference steps have
clearly visible structure (
i.e.
, their rule, arguments, and/or conclusion remain largely unobscured)
and their masks are therefore locally forced with monotonic assignments. With high mask density,
by contrast, some masks must be assigned values speculatively, forcing constraints only appear later, and
incorrect assignments may survive for several steps (potentially dozens or even hundreds of steps).
While the overall number of valid mask assignments might remain low
(since mask values tend to be uniquely determined), the number of plausible partial assignments explored
along the way can be large. In other words, high mask density increases entropy. The extreme case
is fully masked inference steps like
MASK51
BY
MASK72
on
MASK8, MASK12
.
Such steps have zero locally forced information and thus no way to commit without global reasoning.
Again, this doesn’t increase the number of solutions, only the depth of blind search
required to discover one of the (relatively few) solutions.

The table below shows the distribution of mask density across both datasets.

Mean

Median

Min

Max

Std

@5

@25

@50

@75

@90

@95

PL1-PM

1.72

1.64

0.32

4.0

0.49

1.11

1.4

1.64

1.95

2.24

2.67

\mbox{\bf PL1-PM}_{C}

1.65

1.64

1.53

1.78

0.05

1.57

1.62

1.64

1.67

1.70

1.72

While the mean and median densities are very similar,
PL1-PM
has high variance (0.49) and quite a few outliers, e.g., a minimum
density of only 0.32, which is essentially an unmasked proof. More importantly, it has a heavy right tail,
with a significant proportion of heavily masked proofs (everything above the
75^{\mbox{th}}
percentile).
In the challenge dataset, by contrast, the distribution of mask density is tightly concentrated, with
a very small standard deviation (0.05). There are no heavily masked proofs on the right side of the median,
and no outliers in either direction. The new dataset is thus near-constant density, uniformly enforcing consistent,
moderate local difficulty.

Moreover, 16% of all inference steps across
PL1-PM
proofs are
completely
masked, whereas
less than 1% of the inference steps in the challenge dataset are fully masked (0.1%, to be exact).
At the macro level, 85% of proofs in
PL1-PM
have at least one inference step that is fully masked,
whereas only 8% of the proofs in the challenge dataset have at least one such step.
Recall that a fully masked inference step has no local syntactic anchor (no visible rule, conclusion, or arguments)
and thus requires global constraint propagation to resolve; it cannot be solved incrementally.
This means that
PL1-PM
problems regularly contain steps with zero local information, whereas
challenge problems have them very rarely, marking an enormous reduction in local ambiguity.

A final notable difference is the masking of assumptions. Masking a hypothesis is one of the highest-entropy
perturbations that can be made in a proof.
The introduction of a hypothesis
p
in a line of the form
assume

p
adds
p
to the assumption base,
introduces a new logical scope for it, and determines the shape of the resulting conditional
(p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
.
If
p
is masked, the entire body of the conditional proof becomes underconstrained, as every inference inside
that body may well depend on
p
, but we don’t know what that is. And unless we manage to infer
p
with certainty,
we cannot be sure of the conditional proof’s conclusion either. In the challenge dataset, only 10% of conditional proofs
have masked hypotheses, whereas in
PL1-PM
, 43% of conditional proofs (almost 1 in 2) have masked hypotheses.

These numbers show that the challenge dataset is not harder because its proofs are more masked or because they have fewer local hints.
The opposite is true: the new dataset is technically easier because its proofs are less heavily masked and provide more local cues.
The authors have tried solving a number of these longer problems by hand and have found it relatively straightforward to make
consistent incremental progress; with sufficient patience and time, a human familiar with this proof style
could finish such a problem. Yet this entropy reduction does not necessarily make the problem tractable at scale for models,
as shown in the following results:

Model

Instrumented Accuracy

Strict Accuracy

Model

Strict Accuracy

Table 8:

instrumented and strict accuracies.

The difficulty has shifted from unmasking particular steps to maintaining a consistent global state
across hundreds of steps.

Observe that while GPT-5.4 outperforms GPT-5.2 in terms of instrumental accuracy, its strict accuracy is much worse,
half the strict accuracy of GPT-5.2 (though the Wilson confidence intervals are obviously wider here, since there are only 50 problems in
the challenge dataset).

The reasoning traces of the models provide interesting insights.
Here is part of what GPT-5.4 wrote while tackling item 9:

\cdots

In analyzing the subblock, I see that
MASK188
must be biconditional
(D<=>B)
since the first line uses

left-iff
on
MASK189
. This means my assumption is set to
(D<=>B)
.

\cdots
I need to explore how this structure influences the rest
of the proof and what implications arise from these assumptions. It’s fascinating how these logical connections can shape the outcome!

\cdots
The challenge is that
B
doesn’t hold as expected. Could I assume
MASK79 = B
and label it? However,
the line uses literal
B
and
MASK80
. Yet if I assume
B
, it holds in that context, even without naming it.
If I set
MASK79
to
B
, then it becomes an active assumption and holds. I see I overlooked that!

\cdots

Such traces must not be over-interpreted, since they may be narrative artifacts rather than genuine
reflections of the model’s internal computations. Nevertheless, they can still shed light on a number of fronts,

e.g.
, on the style of reasoning that the model believes is appropriate or where it seems to experience higher amounts
of uncertainty. The traces strongly indicate that these elite models are not simply pattern-matching; they are
explicitly reasoning (or at any rate simulating a deliberation process) about the logical availability
of conclusions, assumption scope, rule semantics, and possible mask assignments.

5
Proof Gaps

This dataset was obtained by randomly inserting holes (gaps) in a set of 200 correct proofs, written either by the Athena theorem prover or by a model.
Unlike masks, which are locally focused on individual formulas and rules, gaps apply at the subproof level,
i.e.
, a gap replaces an entire subproof, which could be either a single
step (inference rule application), or a conditional subproof, or a
proof chain
: a sequence of consecutive subproofs. A proof usually receives more than one gap,
in randomly selected subtrees. The total fractional amount of information elided by the gaps ranges from 0.3 to 0.99. In the latter extreme case, the entire proof
is essentially replaced by a single gap, and the task then reduces to proof writing. The mean fractional total size of the gaps inserted in a proof is 0.67 of the original proof’s size.

The objective is to fill the gaps by generating appropriate subproofs for the
gap identifiers
:
GAP-1
,
GAP-2
,
\ldots
.
There are no distractors for this task, which we refer to as
PL1-GF
. The following is a simple example:

⬇

1

assert

premise-2

:=

(C

==>

D)

2

assert

premise-3

:=

(A

<==>

(E

==>

D))

3

#

Goal:

(E

==>

D)

4

5

{

6

left-iff

on

premise-3;

7

GAP-1;

8

assume

E

{

9

case1

:=

assume

C

{

10

mp

on

premise-2,

C

11

};

12

case2

:=

assume

(A

&

E)

{

13

left-and

on

(A

&

E);

14

mp

on

(A

==>

(E

==>

D)),

A;

15

GAP-2

16

};

17

GAP-3

18

}

19

}

Bearing the goal
(E\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}D)
in mind, it is clear from the structure of the overall proof skeleton, and particularly from the structure of the top-level conditional proof
(the
assume
that opens on line 9 and closes on line 19), that this conditional proof establishes
(E\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}D)
. The body
proceeds by a case analysis of
premise-1
, the disjunction
(C\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}(A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}E))
.
The
case1
subproof establishes that
C
implies
D
, thus
case2
must establish that

(A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}E)
implies
D
. Therefore,
GAP-2
must derive
D

from the assumption base at the end of line 15, which includes the conditional
(E\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}D)
(derived on line 15) and the
case-2

hypothesis
(A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}E)
(as
GAP-2
is inside the scope of that hypothesis). So this gap is easy to fill: first detach
E
from the
assumption
(A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}E)
and then use modus ponens on
(E\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}D)
and
E
to infer
D
:

GAP-2:

E
BY
right-and
on
(A & E); D
BY
mp
on
(E ==> D), E

GAP-3
is a simple rule application:
cases
on
premise-1, (C ==> D), ((A & E) ==> D)
.

Note that
GAP-1
does not make any contribution to the proof. It is there only because
the LLM that generated the initial proof included a redundant step at that point, namely, the application of
right-iff
on
premise-3
. Any replacement for that gap
is given full credit as long as the entire proof obtained by filling the gaps as specified by the model correctly derives the goal from the premises.
This is the only criterion we use for correctness.

Figure 14:
PL1-GF
problem statistics

. The problems are quite simple
and roughly similar in their complexity to masking problems, except that conclusions are notably smaller (the median number of nodes is 3).

as those in
PL1-PM
, but on average these proofs are about 25% longer (34.5 lines vs 27).

The discriminability index (DI) of
PL1-GF
is 37.5%, meaning (roughly) that any two of these models differ in accuracy
by about 37.5 percentage points—again a very large spread. This task strongly separates some elite models as well,

e.g.
, the delta between o3 and Gemini 3 Pro Preview (as well as the GPT-5-series models, Opus 4.5 and Gemini 3.1) is highly
statistically significant, while the delta between, say, Grok-4 and Claude Sonnet 4.5 with extended thinking
is statistically significant.
56
56

56

We used McNemar’s test to check whether these pairwise deltas
could be due to random variation. We call the
p
-value of a particular test result “statistically significant” if it’s below
the usual 0.05 threshold and “highly statistically significant” if it’s below the 0.01 threshold.
In the case of Gemini 3 Pro Preview and o3, for example, out of 38 problems where the models disagreed,
Gemini was correct on 31 and o3 on only 7, which corresponds to a
p
-value of 0.000116. This means
the likelihood that such a large advantage occurred by chance is less than 0.1%, and thus
the difference in performance between these two models is very statistically robust.

We treat blank (empty-string) responses after a timeout budget of at least 30 minutes as genuine task failures (non-responses) that
count as incorrect outcomes in our accuracy computation. By contrast, if there is a genuine API call error we do not include
that data point in the accuracy denominator, and in such cases we try to repeat the call from scratch;
57
57

57

In the final
results there is only one API call error left, for Mistral Magistral on item 135.

see Appendix
C
for additional details.
For transparency, we also report non-response rates: Grok-4 abstained on 11/200 problems (5.5%),
Gemini 3 Pro Preview on 7 problems (3%), Mistral Magistral on 7 problems (3%),
and o3 on 1 problem (0.5%). All other models returned non-empty responses for all 200 problems.

Formatting issues were rife for some models, particularly for the two Claude 4 models (Opus and Sonnet),
which frequently fail to return a valid gap assignment, or indeed any gap assignment,
instead emitting a random fragment of NDL code.
58
58

58

Claude 4 Opus did this for 135 out of 200 problems
(67.5% of all inputs) and Claude 4 Sonnet for 119 problems (59.5%).

This was a stable behavioral pattern, confirmed by recomputing results multiple times.
Other models made formatting errors of different types, some more exotic and blatant than others.
Unlike other
ProofGrid
tasks, the
PL1-GF
prompt does not require JSON output,
instead instructing the models to simply think through the problem
and then write their solutions at the very end of their output, in the following simple form:

GAP-i:
<subproof for GAP-i>
GAP-j:
<subproof for GAP-j>
...

Only the latest generation of frontier models adhered faithfully to these instructions.
All other models deviated, typically in predictable ways
that could be programmatically handled.
59
59

59

For example, many models insisted on writing the
GAP-i
headers in boldfaced
markdown, writing, for example,
**GAP-8**:
or
***GAP-8***:
instead of
GAP-8:
;
or in different (and seemingly arbitrary) markdown heading levels; sometimes they would insert the colon
before the asterisks,
e.g.
writing
**GAP-8:**
; and so on. And many models enclosed the generated NDL fragments
inside triple backticks (another common markdown convention, for marking code blocks), e.g. writing something like:

But some less common formatting issues resulted
in outputs that could not be easily salvaged and had to be counted as incorrect. For example, in a few cases
(most notably involving o3-mini), the NDL proof fragments assigned to some gaps contained references to other gaps.
Another example: occasionally the model’s response would only contain a COT trace in natural language,
without ever culminating in a gap assignment. Other times there would be additional commentary inserted after
the supposedly final gap assignment; other times the model would insert a gratuitous delimiter
(such as a long dashed line) in between the different proof fragments; and so on.

A more common error was incomplete gap assignments: properly formatted outputs that nevertheless
neglected to assign values to some of the input gaps. Here Claude 4 Opus and Sonnet lead the pack
with 144 and 130 respectively (due to the malformed responses mentioned earlier), followed by
by GPT-4o (30), Mistral Magistral (21), Grok-4 (12), o4-mini (9), DeepSeek-V3 (8), Gemini-
2.5-Pro (5), GPT-4.1 (4), Grok-3 (3), the Nous-tuned version of Llama-400b (1),
the extended-thinking version of Claude Sonnet 4.5 (1),
DeepSeek R1-0528 (1), and o3 (1). The remaining models never generated incomplete answers.

Table 10:
Error repairs made by the instrumented checker per 100 lines of generated proof code.

Note that Claude Sonnet 4 and Opus 4 have very few repairs for a pathological reason: as noted earlier,
their outputs are often so ill-formatted that a fatal error often halts
evaluation on the very first step, thereby robbing the instrumented checker of the opportunity
to repair minor errors.

As in proof writing, an interesting question here concerns the extent to which a model’s propensity
for minor formal slipups correlates with its overall reasoning ability. In theory, the two could be orthogonal.
We can imagine a brilliant mathematician who can do the heavy lifting of a proof by connecting
all the right dots at a high level of abstraction, but is less adept at technical details and
would benefit from help in converting a proof blueprint into a rigorous derivation. Likewise, it’s possible
that a model could make a lot of minor errors but nevertheless attain a fairly high accuracy with the
aid of the instrumented checker, which would come to its rescue by detecting and overlooking those errors.

Figure 15:
PL1-GF
instrumented model accuracies vs total error repairs per 100 lines.

The data shows that the two are negatively correlated: in general, the more minor/repairable errors
a model makes, the worse its overall performance. The correlation is not ironclad but it is strong nevertheless.
Fig.
15
shows the relevant scatterplot, which indicates a clear
inverse relationship (downward slope) between gap-filling accuracy and repair density.
Specifically, the Pearson correlation coefficient between accuracy and normalized error-repair density
(per 100 lines) is -0.82. DeepSeek R1-0528 is interesting. It has a lot of error repairs
but manages to come close to 50%, slightly outperforming o4-mini. However, the vast
majority of these errors are syntactic, and most of them are due to dropping outer parentheses
from complex formulas. In other words, most of the errors that R1-0528 makes and the instrumented checker fixes
are ultra-local and low-impact systematic deviations that violate strict
formalism without breaking logical coherence. These are exactly the sort of
lapses that the instrumented checker was designed to tolerate.

As shown in Table
9
, and as was also the case for
PL1-PW
,
the instrumented checker raises all model accuracies significantly, except for
the bottom tier of the 8 worst-performing models, where improvements are marginal.
As explained earlier, that was the intention behind the instrumented proof checker:
to try to decouple formal precision from logical competence by overlooking minor errors.
Nevertheless, the overall correlation between repair rate and accuracy remains
strongly negative, as shown by the scatterplot and the correlation coefficient.
This is not contradictory. At an intra-model level of analysis, we see that
instrumentation unlocks latent reasoning ability that would otherwise be obscured by procedural slips.
At an inter-model or global level of analysis, across models, a high rate of procedural error repairs
indicates weaker executive control, so models that generate formally correct proofs also
attain higher reasoning accuracy.
In other words, formal discipline and semantic insight appear to co-develop
in LLMs.
This suggests that reasoning and instruction-following share an
underlying architectural or representational substrate.
And it also indicates that as models scale
and align better, they improve not just in raw inference ability but also in
procedural compliance,
a skill that is essential both in logic and in coding.

Models tend to do better on proof writing than on gap filling (compare Tables
4

and
9
). Sometimes the difference is dramatic, e.g., a 19-point gap in the
case of Gemini 2.5 Pro. This might seem counterintuitive. Gap filling introduces
a proof-synthesis spectrum, where on one end we have a tiny number of small gaps that do not
make any significant synthesis demands, while on the other end we have very large gaps
that essentially require models to write the entire proof. It would seem that the problem
would get harder as we move along this continuum.

However, gap-filling introduces constraints that are not present in proof writing.
In
PL1-PW
, a model has the freedom to choose any proof strategy it likes,
to use “favorite” inference patterns, discharge assumptions
in whatever order it deems appropriate, and so on. There is no need to respect and follow
a pre-existing plan. In
PL1-GF
, by contrast, the input proof skeleton forces a
specific problem decomposition with a particular sequence of subgoals and hardwired local plans,
which the model has to reverse-engineer,
e.g.
, by deducing why certain surrounding lines are there.
It is tackling a planning-under-constraints problem rather than a plain proof-writing problem.
While plain proof writing in propositional logic can be viewed as a classic forward
planning problem, gap filling imposes a hidden inverse search problem, in the
technical sense of inverse Bayesian planning, where the goal is
to abductively infer the intentions of the agent who wrote the initial proof
by “inverting a model” of how intentions cause proof-writing
behavior
[
8
]
.
60
60

60

In classical
planning this is related to
plan recognition
, also known as “inverse planning,”
where one observes a sequence of actions and must infer the goals that gave rise to them
[
71
]
.

Depending on its exact formulation, inverse planning is at least as computationally
hard as forward planning.
Gap-filling also demands stronger meta-reasoning skills, since it requires explicit
deliberation about the given proof skeleton and constructs like assumption scope.
(All inverse tasks generally require reasoning about another
agent’s reasoning processes, thus exercising a sort of theory-of-mind skillset.)
These considerations could help to explain why, for example,
Grok-4 goes from 85% accuracy in
PL1-PW
to 69% in
PL1-GF
.
In general, the diversity of tasks in
ProofGrid
allows for richer profiling
that separates models along different axes of ability.

Next, we analyze the first gap-filling errors made by models, i.e., those errors that the instrumented
interpreter does not fix. These are divided into the same six groups
introduced in the
PL1-PW
error analysis.
Their distribution is shown in Fig.
16
.

Figure 16:
PL1-GF
distribution of first errors.

As in other tasks, three main cohorts emerge from this picture:

•

The surface-fragility regime of weaker models like GPT-4o and Llama-400b, whose
error profiles are dominated by parsing/syntax (blue) failures. These models fail before
their reasoning can be scrutinized. Instrumentation can’t help because
the emitted fragments deviate from proper syntax in highly irregular ways that cannot
be programmatically detected.
As two simple examples, instead of writing
A
BY
left-and
on

\cdots
, a model in this
cohort might write
A INFERRED BY left-and on
\cdots
,
or instead of
(A

&

B)
they
might write
A AND ALSO B
. We have anecdotally observed that even
if these grammar issues were somehow resolved, the generated fragments would still fail
due to downstream structural and logical errors. In other words, parsing errors and
logical failures are entangled phenomena for weaker models, not independent pathologies.

•

The mixed-failure regime of mid-tier models, where parsing errors range
from
\approx 30\%
to 60% of the total, the rest coming from logical errors
(citing formulas that are not in the assumption base), misapplied inference rules,
and references to unbound identifiers.

•

The top-tier models, whose errors are predominantly semantic/logical or structural.
GPT-5.2 stands out with
zero
parsing errors,
an indication of its exceptional instruction-following ability.

Figure 17:
PL1-GF
accuracy vs gapped proof length for the top 12 models

We close by examining how gap-filling performance scales with input size.
Fig.
17
plots the accuracy of the top 12 models as a function of
the input proof length, while Fig.
18
does the same for the bottom
12 models. These curves show that the accuracy of all models
falls as the number of proof lines in the input increases.
However, while weaker models exhibit a sharp and steadily monotonic performance collapse,
with many hitting near-zero accuracy around 40 lines, the declines of the stronger models
are much more gradual and less catastrophic.
61
61

61

Some models either plateau or show a small uptick at the tail end.
This is likely due to a gap-fraction confound
(by “gap fraction” we mean the proportion of the original proof that was replaced by gaps).
Generally, once we control for proof length,
the problem gets harder as the gap fraction increases.
Likewise, if the gap fraction is fixed, the problem gets harder as proof length increases.
However, because
PL1-GF
is a small dataset (200 data points) and not perfectly stratified across both features,
longer proofs tend to have smaller gap fractions, while shorter proofs have
higher gap fractions. This imbalance explains why
a plot of accuracy vs gap fraction would likewise show a declining sensitivity curve
with a small rebound at the tail end: because of a confounding effect, this time
(symmetrically) due to proof length. In both cases, the apparent slight
improvement at the tail happens because the other variable is easier in the corresponding region.

Figure 18:
PL1-GF
accuracy vs gapped proof length for the bottom 12 models.

A challenge version of
PL1-GF

In this section we discuss a more challenging version of this task,
PL1-GF-c
, with 50 problems featuring longer proofs.
The median proof length is 399, and 90% of the proofs have fewer than 510 steps. The maximum length is 946.
The median number of gaps per proof is 45, and the maximum is 100.

Although these proofs are considerably longer, the gaps are easier to fill for a number of reasons.
The original proofs for this dataset were generated by a simple automated theorem prover, and as a result they have
a great deal of structure and a number of recurring patterns. In addition, gap completions tend to be locally short
and semantically anchored. (For instance, among the set of correct answers generated by GPT-5.4, the median gap completion
is 1, and the average is 2.6; 90% of all gap completions have fewer than 6 lines.)
As an example, below is the top portion of a proof from
PL1-GF-c
.

⬇

1

assert

premise-2

:=

((~

((~

(A

|

C))

==>

D))

Table 11:

instrumented and strict accuracies.

The score spread across these 4 powerful models is remarkably wide. The mean numbers of proof lines written across all 50 problems
are 132 for GPT-5.4, 145 for GPT-5.2, 134 for Gemini, and 46 for Claude Opus, which fails to return meaningful gap completions for about half
of the problems.

The point-biserial correlation coefficients between original proof lengths and outcome success indicate that, for these models,
proof length is not correlated with task success. These coefficients are -0.15 for Gemini 3.1, -0.04 for GPT-5.4, and 0.09 for
GPT-5.2 (the correlation is meaningless for Claude). This suggests that, for elite models, raw proof length is not the operative difficulty
variable in this task. We conjecture that the dominant source of difficulty
is average gap size (or, more generally, the amount of omitted proof material per gap), but leave further analysis to future work.

6
\textsc{PL}_{2}

As we pointed out earlier,
\textsc{PL}_{1}
problems are easy because they involve few atoms and randomly constructed formulas of low structural complexity.
The
\textsc{PL}_{2}
division contains richer and more complex problems with more atoms. Nevertheless, problems are still relatively
short, with about 10 premises on average, and premises also tend to be short, with fewer than 10 nodes on average. We start
by illustrating the generation of
\textsc{PL}_{2}
problems with a concrete example, after which we describe a general algorithm that can take
an arbitrary formula as input and construct a
\textsc{PL}_{2}
problem from it. For reasons that will become clear shortly,
\textsc{PL}_{2}
problems
have a strong abductive flavor, despite their deductive formulation. Indeed, the algorithm we describe below could also
be used to generate proper abductive-inference problems, where the desired output is a minimal hypothesis that explains a set of
observations.

Consider the formula
p=((\neg A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}B)\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}(\neg B\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A))
. We can view
p
as the following circuit:
G_{1}
G_{2}
G_{3}
G_{4}
G_{5}
A
B

The atoms
A
and
B
represent the inputs to the circuit, while
G_{1},\ldots,G_{5}
represent the outputs of the five gates.
The output of the whole circuit is
G_{5}
, produced by the fifth gate. We introduce 5 fresh atoms
N_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,5
,
where
N_{i}
means that the
i^{\mbox{th}}
gate,
G_{i}
, is functioning normally:

\displaystyle N_{1}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(G_{1}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}\neg A)

(5)

\displaystyle N_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(G_{2}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}\neg B)

(6)

\displaystyle N_{3}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(G_{3}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}(G_{1}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}B))

(7)

\displaystyle N_{4}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(G_{4}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}(G_{2}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A))

(8)

\displaystyle N_{5}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(G_{5}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}(G_{3}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}G_{4}))

(9)

We’ll use the letter
\mathcal{T}
to refer to the set of formulas (
5
)—(
9
).
Using these formulas as premises, we can prove that if all gates are functioning normally, then
G_{5}
computes
the exclusive-or function over
A
and
B
. More precisely:

\mbox{$\mathcal{T}$}\cup\{N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},N_{4},N_{5}\}\models(G_{5}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}((\neg A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}B)\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}(\neg B\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A))),

and we could write a proof that derives the conclusion on the right-hand side of the turnstile by taking the
formulas on the left-hand side as premises. Perhaps more interestingly, assuming again
\mathcal{T}
,
and assuming
p
, we can prove that if
G_{5}
does
not
hold then at least one of the gates must be malfunctioning:

\mbox{$\mathcal{T}$}\cup\{p,\neg\,G_{5}\}\models\neg N_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg N_{2}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg N_{3}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg N_{4}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg N_{5}.

(10)

This says that, given
\mathcal{T}
, if the inputs to the circuit (the atoms
A
and
B
) satisfy
p
and yet the output bit
G_{5}
is 0, then at least one of the gates
is not operating normally.
62
62

62

Note that, given
\mathcal{T}
, the formulas
p
and
\neg G_{5}
by themselves are not inconsistent. This is because the “theory”

\mathcal{T}
does not allow us to conclude
(G_{5}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}p)
unconditionally;
p
and
G_{5}
become equivalent (and hence
p
and
\neg G_{5}
become inconsistent)
only if we also assume that all gates are operating normally.

While this discussion is couched in deductive terminology, the underlying reasoning is inherently abductive, as this is a classical
model-based diagnosis

problem. The normal-operation definitions form our
theory

\mathcal{T}
, while
p
and
\neg\,G_{5}
serve as our
observations

O
, reporting that the output bit

G_{5}
is 0 and that the inputs
A
and
B
satisfy
p
.
63
63

63

Typically, “observations” in a particular test case
are
concrete
,
e.g.
, individual sensor readings for inputs and outputs, such as
A=0
,
B=1
,
G_{5}=0
. These are naturally
represented by literals (atoms or their negations). This makes our use of a complex formula like
p
as an observation somewhat unorthodox, as
p
represents
not just one particular input state but multiple states. Essentially,
p
serves as a constraint over the atoms of the input formula. Its use as a premise means
that the atoms are in some (any) assignment where their XOR is 1, but we don’t bother to specify a particular assignment.

It should be clear that
\mbox{$\mathcal{T}$}\cup O\cup\{N_{1},\ldots,N_{5}\}
is inconsistent, and the obvious way to restore consistency is to reject at least one
normality hypothesis, which is precisely what (
10
) does.

We now describe a general algorithm
Ab
that takes any propositional formula
p
(whose AST is denoted by
\mbox{ast\/}(p)
) and produces a sequent of the form

\mathcal{T}\cup\{p,\;\lnot\,G_{\mathit{root}}\}\;\vdash\;\bigvee_{v\in\text{\mbox{nodes\/}}(\text{\mbox{ast\/}}(p))}\lnot N_{v},

(11)

where
\mathcal{T}
is a set of guarded definitional clauses.
The intuition is that if all gates are normal, then the circuit output
G_{\mathit{root}}
must equal
p
. Therefore, if
p
holds but
G_{\mathit{root}}
is observed false, at least one gate must be abnormal.

Step 1

Parse the input formula
p
into
\mbox{ast\/}(p)
. Each internal node contains a propositional connective, while leaves are atoms.

Step 2

For every AST node
v
, introduce two fresh atoms:

•

G_{v}
: represents the output wire of the gate computing the subformula at
v
.

•

N_{v}
: represents the statement that the gate at
v
is normal.

Step 3

Add guarded definitions.
For each internal AST node
v
with operator
\mbox{op\/}_{v}
and children
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
, add the guarded equivalence

N_{v}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\bigl(G_{v}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}\mbox{op\/}_{v}(G_{u_{1}},\dots,G_{u_{k}})\bigr).

Collect all such guarded definitions into the set
\mathcal{T}
.

Step 4

Transform the guarded definitions. With probability 0.5, each conditional
p_{1}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}p_{2}
in
\mathcal{T}
becomes either the disjunction
\neg p_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}p_{2}
or the
contrapositive
\neg p_{2}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg p_{1}
.

Step 5

Identify the root. Let
\mathit{root}
denote the root of
\mbox{ast\/}(p)
.
Its gate variable is
G_{\mathit{root}}
.

Step 6

Add the observations.
Specifically, we add two observation formulas:

•

p
(the specification is satisfied for the current input assignment).

•

\lnot\,G_{\mathit{root}}
(the actual output wire is observed false).

Step 7

Formulate the conclusion and produce the output.
Define the disjunction

C\;=\;\bigvee_{v\,\in\,\text{nodes}(p)}\lnot N_{v}.

The target output sequent is then:

\mathcal{T}\cup\{p,\;\lnot\,G_{\mathit{root}}\}\;\vdash\;C.

(12)

If all
N_{v}
are true, every clause in
\mathcal{T}
reduces to a Tseitin-style definitional equality.
64
64

64

The atoms
G_{v}
are exactly the
Tseitin variables one would introduce in a Tseitin CNF encoding of
p
. The difference here is that each definitional equivalence is guarded by a normality atom
N_{v}
.
Dropping the guards yields the usual Tseitin encoding.
By induction on the AST, this yields
G_{\mathit{root}}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}p
, which is inconsistent with
the observations
\{p,\neg G_{\mathit{root}}\}
. Therefore, not all
N_{v}
can be true,
i.e.
, at least one
\lnot N_{v}
holds. This is the output entailment problem.

PL2-PW
contains 200 problems that were obtained from
\textsc{PL}_{1}
problems as follows. Given a problem with
n
premises
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
and goal
q
,
we sort the list of
n+1
formulas
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},q
in order of decreasing length and choose a random initial segment
S
of the sorted list that
has at least 2 elements. We then negate each element of
S
with probability 0.5 and join the results into a single complex formula
p

via random propositional connectives. For example, if
S
initially contains 3 formulas
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
, then these formulas might be joined into

p=((p_{1}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg p_{2})\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}p_{3})
. The proof problem is then
\mbox{Ab\/}(p)
,
i.e.
, the result of applying the algorithm

Ab
described above to the formula
p
.

Table 12:
Data distribution summaries for the 200 problems in
PW-3
.
Box = IQR (Interquartile Range); red line = median; dot = mean; whiskers = 5–95th.

All
PL2-PW
problems thus share a common logical template, namely (
12
).
While the input
p
varies widely, the overall construction still imposes
a great deal of schematic uniformity on the resulting problems.
This homogeneity limits diversity and makes
PL2-PW
less suitable
for gauging general proof-writing ability. Rather, this task measures
generative performance on a family of structurally related, abductively
flavored reasoning problems. Consequently, it is plausible that once a model
internalizes the strategy induced by the problem generator (in our case,
how to reason backward from the disjunction over the
\neg N_{v}

literals to the structure of
p
’s AST), it becomes capable of transfer
across instances. Essentially, the concern is that if a model figures out how
to solve one problem of this form, it may become able to solve all of them, modulo size
constraints.

That said, template-driven problem generators are a common theme in
evaluation. Many classic benchmarks (in maze and puzzle solving in general,
in circuit evaluation, in SAT families, and elsewhere) rely on uniform or parametric
generators—and for good reason, since these allow for controlled scaling,
ablations, and clean attribution of failure modes.
More importantly, structure discovery is not free; figuring
out the underlying algorithm is itself the core difficulty. If a system reliably
discovers and executes the correct reasoning chain, that’s a real capability, not a loophole.
In the case of
PL2-PW
, most models fail to do so despite the shared
structure of the problem instances, indicating that the task is far from trivialized
by the underlying semantic homogeneity. This is consistent with other benchmark tasks,
both within
ProofGrid
and elsewhere. In
\textsc{PL}_{3}
, for instance, all problems from the same family
(
e.g.
, all graph coloring or subset cardinality problems) are structurally similar, but several families
remain challenging nevertheless. Even if a model somehow learns “the general idea” behind
a problem, this still doesn’t ensure that it will be able to solve any given instance,
since its performance will still be limited by its planning horizon, its working memory,
error accumulation, and combinatorial explosion.

Table
12
summarizes some key characteristics of these 200 problems.

\textsc{PL}_{2}
problems have more premises (the median is 10), but premises tend to be short.
This is to be expected, given the algorithm we just described. We have more premises because
a new formula is introduced for each internal node of the starting formula
p
. And because
these new formulas are guarded conditional clauses that are frequently of the form
(N_{u}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(G_{v}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}G_{r}\>\mbox{op\/}\>G_{t}))
,
their size is often exactly 7, so premises tend to be short (the inclusion of the starting formula
p

nudges the average size slightly higher). The number of atoms per
PL2-PW
problem is considerably higher, with a median of 18,
since two new atoms (
N_{v}
and
G_{v}
) are introduced for each node
v
in
\mbox{ast\/}(p)
.
Goals here are also more complex, since they are disjunctions of
negated normality atoms. In addition, recall that the
Ab
algorithm randomly negates
each formula in the initial segment
S
described above with probability 0.5; and every initial guarded
conditional in
\mathcal{T}
is turned into a formula with at least one negation sign (Step 4 of the algorithm).
As a result, these problems are negation-heavy, with the median number of negations at 24. Note that each
premise has fewer than 2 negation signs on average (1.66, to be exact), but these add up over the
entire problem simply because there are more premises. The main drivers of complexity in this task slice
are the higher numbers of atoms and negations.

We close this section with a brief description of how the
Ab
algorithm could be modified to generate proper
abductive-inference problems. The target conclusion of problem (
11
) can be viewed as an explanatory hypothesis or a diagnosis,
but it is a rather uninformative one insofar as it essentially says that “something is wrong,”
i.e.
, that at least
one gate is malfunctioning. This is because
our chief observation pertains to the top-level (“root”) node, which clearly depends for its function on every other node,
and we have no information about the outputs of any other nodes.
But the algorithm could be easily tweaked to generate proper abductive-inference problems
targeting much more informative explanations, e.g., by randomly choosing a concrete interpretation
I

(assignment of truth values to
p
’s atoms) as a concrete test case and an internal node (“gate”)
v
in
\mbox{ast\/}(p)
.
We would then compute the expected value of
G_{v}
under
I
(1 or 0) and take
as a premise the literal that asserts the opposite of that value.
The target hypothesis will then be that one of
v
’s predecessor nodes must be malfunctioning.
For example, choosing
I=\{A\mapsto 1,B\mapsto 0\}
and
v
as the node corresponding to
G_{4}
, we see that if everything is working properly
then
G_{4}
should hold (its value should be 1). We thus take the opposite,
\neg G_{4}
, as a premise, along with
(A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg B)
(the formula representing
I
)
and
\mathcal{T}
. With this information as background, the ground-truth hypothesis would be
(\neg N_{2}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg N_{4})
.
The target diagnosis could be sharpened further if we provide additional information about specific gates,
e.g.
,
N_{2}
or
G_{2}
.

6.1
Results

Statistics on the lengths of the proofs written by all models for the
PL2-PW
proofs are shown in Fig.
19
.

Figure 19:
PL2-PW
proof-size distributions per model: all proofs (blue) vs correct proofs (green);
min–max line, 5–95% band, IQR box, median tick, mean dot.

For each model
M
, the figure shows the distribution of length over the set of all proofs written by
M
(blue line) as well as the length distribution
over the set of correct proofs written by
M
. Note that average proof sizes for both sets are about an order of magnitude higher than those in
PL1-PW
.

The green (correct-proofs-only) distributions are narrower and shifted left compared to the blue (all-proofs) distributions,
showing that correct proofs tend to be shorter than the full set of proofs that a model generates. Incorrect proofs are
thus generally longer and contribute outliers that stretch the blue ranges significantly.
This is particularly so for the more “compulsively generative” models,
most notably
llama-3.1-405b
and
mistral-magistral
, which tend to ramble on even when they are unable to solve a problem.
They are essentially performing an unguided and verbose random walk through the proof space, hoping to stumble upon a solution.
Models that keep proof sizes stable (tight bands, less variance) tend to perform better, as we will see shortly.

Figure 20:
PL2-PW
proof-size median ratios by each model (a higher value means that the all-proofs median is longer than the correct-proofs-only median size).

Figure
20
shows the ratio of the median proof size for all proofs versus correct proofs across models.
A value above 1 means that the median proof length is longer when considering all proofs than when considering only
correct proofs, indicating that incorrect proofs inflate length. The dashed line marks the
average ratio across models (
\approx 1.5
).
65
65

65

There is no bar for GPT-4o because that model
doesn’t write even one correct proof, so the ratio in question is mathematically undefined (zero denominator).

Several patterns stand out. First, models like Grok-3, Llama-3.1-405b, and o3-mini have the
largest ratios (
>2
), suggesting that their incorrect proofs are often
much
longer than their correct ones.
This indicates their tendency to overproduce when they fail to find a correct reasoning path.
In contrast, o4-mini and GPT-5.2 sit very close to 1, meaning their incorrect proofs
are more or less the same size as their correct ones—they produce much more uniform proof
lengths regardless of correctness. That tends to be the case for the strongest models,
though there are top performers in between with modest ratios (say Gemini-3 and GPT-5
with 1.2 and 1.25, respectively), showing that while they do occasionally generate longer incorrect proofs,
they generally keep output sizes more stable. The plot suggests that proof size could be used as
a correctness signal for several models, especially those with high ratios, whose shorter proofs are much more
likely to be correct. Models with lower ratios, on the other hand, seem to control verbosity better, which
could reflect better internal calibration. This simple metric could therefore provide a diagnostic for evaluating
a model’s reasoning. Finally, it is noteworthy that models from the same family can have widely different
behavior profiles along this axis (e.g., compare
claude-opus-4-20250514
and
claude-sonnet-4-20250514
, or
o3-mini
and
o4-mini
).

The instrumented and non-instrumented accuracies of all models on
PL2-PW
are shown in Table
13
.
Only 4 models manage to exceed 60% instrumented accuracy, and only 3 models do better than 80%,
Gemini 3.1, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2. Strict accuracy is bleak, with almost all models scoring near 0, with the exception of
Gemini 3.1 and GPT-5.2, which reach 44% and 12%, respectively.

Table 13:
PL2-PW
accuracies from the instrumented proof checker and from the regular (strict) proof checker.

As in
\textsc{PL}_{1}
, the gaps between strict and instrumented accuracies are very large for elite models but negligible
for the others. This is because strong models already operate very close to the boundary of correctness,
where most failures are procedural slips rather than crude errors in reasoning and planning.
So repairs here may have low density but they unlock large chunks of latent correctness and yield
high marginal benefit. By contrast, weak models make many errors, both minor and fatal.
Although it may be possible to detect and fix a lot of the minor errors, these repairs
amount to surface-level adjustments that cannot compensate for the absence of a sound proof core.
To put it simply, they don’t help much because there’s no coherent reasoning structure underneath.

The discriminability index (DI) of
PL2-PW
is 0.3, which means that this task separates models very sharply.
This is one of a small number of tasks that separates elite models even more sharply than the entire model population: the DI for
the top 10 models, for example, is 0.35. It is 0.31 for the top 8 models and 0.29 for the top 6 models. Note the stunning
deltas between the top 3 models on the one hand and, for instance, GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.5, Claude Sonnet 4.5 with extended thinking (or even Gemini 3)
on the other.

Corrections automatically made by the instrumented checker are shown in Fig.
21
.

Figure 21:
PL2-PW
error corrections made by the instrumented checker per model (sorted by total).

In the course of writing 200 proofs,
deepseek-v3.1
made a total of 6640 errors that were detected and overlooked by the instrumented checker,
the vast majority of them syntactic; this comes to about 33 errors per proof. Observe that performance correlates with the proportion
of syntax errors. Weaker models tend to make a lot of syntactic errors, many more than semantic errors, while the situation is reversed
for the strongest models, particularly for Gemini 3.1 and GPT-5.2, whose error bars are mostly orange (semantic).

The figure shows absolute numbers of
error corrections, which does not take into account the total volume of proof code generated by a model (models that have written a
lot of proofs will naturally make more errors). Dividing, for each model
M
, the total numbers of errors made by
M
by the
total number of proof lines (excluding comments) written by
M
gives a cleaner “probability of error per proof step” signal,
and here Claude Opus 4.5 emerges as the model that is least likely to make an error on any given proof step
(3.9% probability), followed by Gemini 3.1 (5.7%), GPT-5.2 (7.6%) and Grok-4 (8.5%).
DeepSeek-3.1 comes last with a 52% probability of making an error on any given step.
Keep in mind, however, that these are the
fixable
errors that the instrumented checker detects
and overlooks, so they do not correlate strongly with instrumented accuracies.

The errors that are made by the models even with instrumentation are partitioned into the same 6 categories introduced for
PL1-PW
;
their distribution is shown in Figure
22
.
Here too, syntax errors are in the minority for stronger models (particularly for Gemini 3.1),
suggesting greater procedural control and mastery of a novel formal language.
Gemini 3.1 also makes virtually zero
notInAB
errors, which are the most serious type of logical error
(appeals to results that do not hold). It does, however, misapply inference rules (
malformedRuleApp
).

Figure 22:
PL2-PW
error types flagged by the instrumented checker.

Interestingly, most models prefer to write incomplete proofs with gaps in them than to not write anything. The best models keep this to a minimum, and when they do it they tend to fill out most of the details and leave only small gaps. Less able models do it more often. A search for comment keywords like
skip
,
omit
,
brevity

and
skeleton
reveals that 100 proofs written by DeepSeek-V3 (
i.e.
