This is a key reason why the incorrect proofs by DeepSeek-V3 and o4-mini tend to be much shorter than the incorrect proofs by other models—because when they cannot write a complete proof, they
write an extremely short and high-level skeleton only, instead of producing noise. This is consistent with their “fail-fast” profiles,
as opposed to models like Mistral Magistral and Llama-405b, which
consistently over-generate. In 20 of the 200 problems, o3 returns an empty string as the proof,
with explanations such as:
I am sorry—after a long analysis I could not construct a completely correct proof that satisfies all the syntactic and semantic constraints
while deriving the requested (extremely long) disjunction from the nine intricate premises.
\cdots
Because an incomplete proof would be useless, and because a formally incorrect proof
would only raise parsing errors in the grader, I must for now return no formal proof.
66
66

66

A rather entertaining qualitative finding is that some models
tend to exaggerate the difficulty of the task and dramatize the challenge in a tone that borders on petulant.
In the above quote, o3 goes on to grumble that “the time allotted here is not sufficient to build
and check such a large derivation step-by-step
\cdots
A full solution would be several hundred lines
long and would need multiple levels of case–analysis
\cdots
”
In fact, full solutions for that problem (item 116) are written by many models in significantly
fewer than 100 lines (
e.g.
, R1’s solution is only 62 lines, while the proof by Gemini 2.5 Pro has 74 lines).

But there is a lack of consistency (something we discuss extensively later in this paper), as many other times o3
happily generates incomplete proofs.
67
67

67

If not abused, the generation of incomplete proofs could be helpful in an agentic approach.
All interactive proof systems have special mechanisms for postponing proof obligations
by marking certain subproof efforts as incomplete. (In Athena this is done with the primitive method
force
, in Lean with the keyword
sorry
, in Coq with
Admitted
, and so on.)
Models could be instructed to formally mark gaps with such keywords, which could then be outsourced to other agents in subsequent iterations.

Figure 23:
PL2-PW
accuracies for the top 12 models as a function of problem size.

Finally, performance drops
as problem size increases. Fig.
23
depicts accuracy vs. problem size
for the top 12 models, obtained by binning the 200 problems into variable-width bins
with
\geq 30
items per bin results.
The size of a problem is defined as the sum of the sizes of all formula ASTs (premises and goal).
While the performance of all models declines with problem size, the degradation curve of Gemini 3.1
is very gentle, with the model maintaining near perfect instrumented accuracy even on the longer problems
in
PL2-PW
; likewise for GPT-5.2 and GPT-5.1, though to a lesser extent.
This demonstrates a robust capability on moderately complex problems, where all other models have
already started to fail catastrophically.
These are the only 3 models demonstrating a meaningful ability to solve the larger problems in
PL2-PW
,
suggesting a qualitatively more scalable reasoning architecture.

The next-best models (Gemini-3, GPT-5, o3, and Gemini-2.5 Pro) start lower but also
experience more rapid and catastrophic collapse, with their accuracies eventually
falling off a cliff (less than 40% accuracy on the longest problems for all except Gemini 3).
These models appear to have mastered the patterns required for the simplest versions of the task
but are not able to handle even a modest increase in combinatorial complexity.
All others, including cutting-edge models like Claude Opus 4.5, Grok-4, and the extended-thinking version
of Claude Sonnet 4.5, are clustered at the bottom, with most of them already performing poorly even
on very small
PL2-PW
problems. These models fundamentally lack the core capabilities required for this task.

Despite the impressive accuracies of Gemini 3.1 and GPT-5.2, this task is not saturated.
First, the gaps between even the 3 top models is significant, leaving substantial headroom for improvement.
As already mentioned, the task remains very strongly discriminative at the top end in general, with a DI of 31
percentage points among the top 8 models. As discussed in the introduction, ceiling saturation means that
all strong models are clustered near the ceiling and the benchmark is no longer able to distinguish them.
Second, the high accuracies represent instrumented accuracy and are therefore conditional on a checker
that measures reasoning success modulo surface noise, by disregarding minor syntactic and semantic failures,
therefore answering the weaker question “Can the model usually find a roughly correct latent proof plan?”
So even if the top few models often recover the right underlying proof idea, they still frequently
fail to realize it as a fully correct formal proof. If we demand full correctness, the accuracies
of the top 3 systems drop to 44%, 12%, and 3%, respectively.
Even for instrumented accuracy, the numbers indicate that
PL2-PW
is probing a real underlying
capability that only 2 models currently have in large quantities.

7
\textsc{PL}_{3}

7.1
Overview and Introduction to
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}

The
\textsc{PL}_{3}
division of
ProofGrid
has two tasks,
PL3-PW
(proof writing) and
PL3-PC
(proof checking).
Both feature propositional-logic problems that are structurally richer than those in
\textsc{PL}_{1}
and
\textsc{PL}_{2}
.

In particular,
PL3-PW
contains 300 problems that are grouped as follows:

(a)

10 pebbling problems on DAG pyramids;

(b)

5 counting-principle (partition) problems;

(c)

30 relativized pigeonhole-principle problems;

(d)

60 Tseitin-formula problems;

(e)

70 subset-cardinality problems;

(f)

70 graph-coloring problems;

(g)

15
De Bruijn
problems; and

(h)

40 simple DAG pebbling problems encoding Horn-clause inference.

The composition of
PL3-PC
is similar and is described in detail in Section
7.6
.
Section
7.2
discusses all 8 problem families in detail.

The general form of these problems is identical to that of
\textsc{PL}_{1}
and
\textsc{PL}_{2}
problems: each
consists of a set of premises and a conclusion to be
derived from the premises. In all 300 cases, the conclusion is indeed derivable
from the premises.

Both tasks in
\textsc{PL}_{3}
involve
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
, a language that is even more stripped-down than NDL, doing away with all
inference rules in favor of one uniform syntax form for inference:

p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{from}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}

where
n\leq 5
. The semantics of
this syntax form are simple: conclude
p
on the basis of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, where each
p_{i}
is
in the current assumption base.
68
68

68

This boils down to
p_{i}
being
either a premise or an active assumption or a previously derived result that remains in scope.

The step succeeds only if
p
follows logically from
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
.

The proof checker for
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
operationalizes this as follows.
First, it checks to make sure that
n\leq 5
and that each
p_{i}
is in the current
assumption base. If not, it reports an error. Otherwise it calls
a SAT solver to determine whether
p
follows from
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}

(by checking whether the set
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},\neg p\}
is unsatisfiable).
The step is accepted and produces
p
as its conclusion if and only if that check succeeds.

The
from
mechanism is a powerful tool.
With it, models are able to make much larger inferences in a single step (larger
than any possible inference in conventional NDL), without having to specify
technical minutiae or particular inference rules.
As long as it is not abused, this ability to suppress inferential details
and derive a helpful conclusion in one fell swoop is critical given the increased
complexity of
\textsc{PL}_{3}
problems, especially for the
PL3-PW
task.
Demanding fully detailed proofs for these problems in vanilla NDL would
not be realistic (or fair), as such proofs would be too long and onerous.

The use of
from
is also a more accurate reflection
of how mathematicians actually reason. A typical inference step in a mathematical proof is
model- rather than proof-theoretic: it’s an assertion that a claim follows from earlier results
and/or assumptions, not an application of a particular inference rule. It is up to the reader to verify
the claim. In that sense,
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
and
\textsc{PL}_{3}
shift the focus from rule-based and
proof-theoretic reasoning to
semantic reasoning
and generally to entailment-based inference.

The restriction to
n\leq 5
arguments is intended to ensure that
from

is only used to make reasonably sized inferences, not as a magic wand that compresses
the proof to the point of degeneracy.
If no limit is placed on
n
, a model could easily avoid
honest toil by generating a trivial one-line “proof”:
goal

from

P
, where
P
is a list of

all
premises given for the argument and
goal
is the target
conclusion.
69
69

69

Indeed, OpenAI models tried just that before we imposed this requirement.

Such gamed proofs are not acceptable, and setting
n\leq 5
is an
effective way to preclude them.
70
70

70

In principle, a model
could work around this restriction by gradually merging premises into a single conjunction,
using no more than five at a time, and then finally deriving the goal in one step
from the conjunction of all premises. We have not observed any such attempts.
In any case, simple extensions of the
n\leq 5
rule could block such contrived strategies as well
(
e.g.
, by also enforcing
m\leq 5
where
m
is the total number of conjuncts across all arguments
that represent premises).
This restriction resembles the sort of pragmatically
controlled abstraction that humans use in mathematical argumentation,
where the granularity of a proof step is left to the judgment
of the proof writer, but only up to a certain point.

\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
remains sound and complete for propositional logic. Soundness is ensured by definition, while completeness
follows because
from
steps are strictly more powerful that any primitive inference rule of vanilla
NDL, meaning that any step of the form
R\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{on}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
that produces a conclusion
p
,
where
R
is one of the 24 primitive inference rules of NDL, can be simulated by the
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
step

p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{from}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
.
71
71

71

There are only 3 exceptions
to this simple technique:
left-either
,
right-either
,
and
by-contradiction
.
Applications of the form
\mbox{\tt left-either}\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{on}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}p_{1},p_{2}

can be replaced simply by
(p_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}p_{2})\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{from}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}p_{1}
, and symmetrically for
right-either
,
while steps of the form
\mbox{\tt by-contradiction}\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{on}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p,\,(p^{\prime}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\mbox{\tt false})
can be replaced by

p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{from}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}(p^{\prime}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\mbox{\tt false})
.

While
\textsc{PL}_{3}
problems are complex, they may be viewed as “easy-hard” problems.
They are hard insofar as their general problem families are known to be challenging,
but they are easy in that we only use instances whose size and difficulty are strictly bounded,
to keep the tasks tractable. For instance, most
PL3-PW
problems were initially generated
by producing an unsatisfiable set of CNF (conjunctive normal form) clauses.
First, we ensured that the CNF encodings for the vast majority of these problems
(over 95% of them) contain no more than 100 clauses each.
We then translated these clauses into a set of regular formulas
S=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
,
which we finally converted to a forward inference problem, typically by taking
p_{2},\ldots,p_{n}

as the premises and
\neg p_{1}
as the goal, or, in a small minority of cases, by taking
S
as the premises
and
false
as the conclusion. We ensured that the target conclusion can always
be derived from the premises using a resolution proof with fewer than 300 steps.
Indeed, for most problems that resolution proof has fewer than 150
steps.
72
72

72

To avoid relying on a single theorem prover, we used multiple ATPs
to solve each problem and verified that at least one of them produced a proof
with fewer than 300 steps. We did this because the same result can be proven
by different ATPs with proofs of widely different lengths, depending on
ability, configuration settings for the underlying search algorithm, and
whether the resulting proof is restricted to binary inference rules or instead
uses rules like hyperresolution, which may combine more than two inputs, like
from
.
We used Prover9, Vampire, and Spass. Prover9 usually finds the shortest proofs,
while Spass tends to produce the longest ones.

Note that because the starting set of formulas
S=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}

is unsatisfiable, in some cases the premises
p_{2},\ldots,p_{n}
are jointly
inconsistent, while in other cases they only become inconsistent when
p_{1}
is added.
Even when the premises are inconsistent, the contradiction is deeply hidden and
not syntactically obvious; it cannot be detected without reconstructing
the underlying combinatorial structure.
Moreover, unsatisfiable cores are typically proper subsets of
S
, which means that

S
may contain several distractors—
p_{i}
that are not logically necessary,
in that they could be removed without making
S
satisfiable.
This makes the proof-writing problem harder because it effectively increases the
search space by making premise selection noisier and diluting relevance signals.

The generation of
PL3-PC
problems is discussed in Section
7.6
.
The rest of this section describes the different problem types in
\textsc{PL}_{3}
in detail.

7.2
\textsc{PL}_{3}
Problem Families

7.2.1
DAG Pebbling Problems

Pebbling problems on DAGs (directed acyclic graphs) are fundamentally about dependencies, resource constraints, and sequential execution;
several AI planning problems can be translated into SAT using pebbling-like encodings. Our pebbling problems on DAG
pyramids
are particularly
interesting because a naive approach to them would require exponentially long proofs. Only systems capable of structured reasoning with
abstraction and lemma reuse can reason successfully about these problems as the height of the pyramid increases.

In particular, consider a DAG pyramid, say, of height 2, as shown below.
Nodes
A
–
F
represent tasks. Each task can be completed in two ways:
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
for
A
,
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
for
B
, and so on.
We indicate this by attaching these disjunctions to every node.
The DAG’s source nodes are
D
,
E
, and
F
; they have no dependencies on other tasks. Arrows indicate task dependencies:
B
depends on
D

and
E
(cannot be completed before they are),
C
depends on
E
and
F
, and so on.
A
is the
target node
, the task that can only be completed
(or
pebbled
) last.

A
B
C
D
E
F
a_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}a_{2}
b_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}b_{2}
c_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}c_{2}
d_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}d_{2}
e_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}e_{2}
f_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}f_{2}

A pebbling problem on a DAG pyramid of this sort is defined by 3 classes of formulas.
The
source formulas
assert that every source node can be pebbled,
i.e.
, we have

d_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}d_{2}
,
e_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}e_{2}
, and
f_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}f_{2}
. The
precedence formulas
encode dependencies:
For every non-source node, we assert 4 conditional precedence formulas encoding 4 distinct
completion possibilities. For instance, the 4 precedence conditionals for
B
are:
d_{i}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}e_{j}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}b_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}b_{2}
,
for all
i,j\in\{1,2\}
. This says that
B
can be pebbled if either
D
has been pebbled by
d_{1}

and
E
by
e_{1}
, or
D
by
d_{1}
and
E
by
e_{2}
, and so on. (Precedence formulas
are typically represented by clauses in the literature
[
19
]
,
but we find conditionals more intuitive.) The last class of formulas consists of
the negations of all target disjunctions, in our case simply
\neg(a_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}a_{2})
since we only have one
target (
A
). The task now is to show that the set of all formulas is unsatisfiable.
However, in our forward-inference formulation we simply prove that target
A
must be pebbled:

a_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}a_{2}
. This follows logically from the source and precedence formulas.

A naive approach to proving the target disjunction performs an intricate case analysis of all
source completion combinations, which requires exponentially
long proofs as pyramids grow taller. A smarter approach proceeds inductively in a bottom-up
direction, by incrementally proving lemmas for the nodes of each level. The source nodes provide the
induction basis, and the precedence formulas act as inductive hypotheses.
Continuing this way finally derives the target disjunction.
We include 10 problems for DAG pyramids up to height 10. Our ground-truth
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proofs
for these problems are written programmatically (using the outlined algorithm) as an
Athena method that emits
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
certificates.

The “simple” DAG pebbling problems (family (h) in the list of Section
7
)
don’t have disjunctions associated with every node and the DAGs are not pyramids.
Rather, they have long linear dependency chains and can thus be used to gauge
how well a system can perform forward inference with deep implication chains,
encoded as simple Horn clauses. As an example, here are the premises of such a problem:

A_{1}

A_{1}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{2}

A_{2}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{3}

A_{2}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A_{3}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{4}

A_{3}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A_{4}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{5}

A_{5}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{6}

A_{2}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A_{5}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A_{6}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{7}

A_{4}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A_{7}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{8}

A_{6}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A_{8}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{9}

The target conclusion for this problem is
A_{9}
.
Here’s the corresponding DAG:
A_{1}
A_{2}
A_{3}
A_{4}
A_{5}
A_{6}
A_{7}
A_{8}
A_{9}

To make these problems nontrivial, we rewrite some randomly chosen Horn clauses using standard transformations,
most notably the definition of conditionals
p_{1}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\cdots\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}p_{n}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}p
as

\neg p_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\cdots\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg p_{n}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}p
. The premises of this particular problem are equivalently rewritten as follows:

A_{1}

\neg A_{2}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg A_{1}

A_{2}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{3}

A_{2}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg A_{3}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}A_{4}

\neg A_{5}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A_{3}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg A_{4}

A_{5}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{6}

A_{6}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg A_{7}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg A_{2}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg A_{5}

\neg A_{8}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A_{7}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg A_{4}

A_{6}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg A_{9}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg A_{8}

Note that the 10 DAG-pyramid problems are completely negation-free,
while the forward-inference pebbling problems also don’t have any
essential negations
or
disjunctions, only those introduced by the aforementioned
definitional transformations, which produce formulas in NNF
(negation normal form, where negation signs only appear in from of atoms).
This simplifies both classes of problems considerably, especially in comparison with the
remaining problem families.

7.2.2
Graph Coloring Problems

For the 70 graph-coloring problems we restrict attention to
3
colors. We
use the
networkx
library to construct random undirected graphs with relatively few nodes
(typically
\leq 10
) and edge probability randomly chosen from the uniform distribution [0.6,0.9],
which tends to be above the threshold for the 3-color phase transition (meaning that we typically
sample from the unsatisfiable side of the phase transition). Using a greedy coloring heuristic,
we filter out those few graphs that can be colored with
K\leq 3
colors. We then run an
exact backtracking algorithm to exhaustively search for a coloring assignment and use the number
of recursive calls as a proxy for the difficulty of the problem.
We encode the problems in SAT using a standard encoding (see Chapter 4 of
3
), and
we finally retain only those problems that satisfy the additional constraint that their
unsatisfiability—the impossibility of coloring the graph with 3 colors—can be proved with
<150
resolution steps.

7.2.3
Relativized Pigeonhole Principle Problems

Consider
m
pigeons,
t

resting places
, and
n
pigeonholes. We introduce two families of Boolean variables:

p_{i,k}\quad(1\leq i\leq m,\;1\leq k\leq t)\quad\text{and}\quad q_{k,j}\quad(1\leq k\leq t,\;1\leq j\leq n),

where, informally,

•

p_{i,k}
means “pigeon
i
comes to rest at place
k
.”

•

q_{k,j}
means “the (unique) pigeon at resting place
k
eventually flies into hole
j
.”

We then conjoin formulas enforcing the following constraints:

1.

Coverage of pigeons by resting places:

\bigl(p_{i,1}\lor p_{i,2}\lor\cdots\lor p_{i,t}\bigr)\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{$\;\>$}(\forall\>i\in\{1,\ldots,m\})

so every pigeon rests somewhere.

2.

Mutual exclusion at resting places:

p_{i,k}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg p_{j,k}\quad\text{for all }1\leq i<j\leq m,\;1\leq k\leq t,

so no two pigeons share the same resting place.

3.

Coverage of occupied places by holes:

p_{i,k}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\bigl(q_{k,1}\lor q_{k,2}\lor\cdots\lor q_{k,n}\bigr)\quad\text{for all }1\leq i\leq m,\;1\leq k\leq t,

so if a pigeon rests at
k
, that place is assigned to some hole.

4.

Mutual exclusion at holes:

q_{k,j}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg q_{k^{\prime},j}\quad\text{for all }1\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq t,\;1\leq j\leq n,

so no hole receives more than one pigeon.

A simple combinatorial argument shows that the conjunction of these clauses is satisfiable if and only if

m\;\leq\;t\;\leq\;n
.
In other words, there must be at least as many resting places as pigeons, and at least as many holes as resting places.
Accordingly, unsatisfiable instances can be generated by choosing
m
,
t
, and
n
that violate this inequality.

As an example, take
m=2
pigeons,
t=2
resting places, and
n=1
hole. Then

\{p_{1,1},p_{1,2},p_{2,1},p_{2,2},\;q_{1,1},q_{2,1}\}

are our variables, and the above four sets of constraints become

\displaystyle p_{1,1}\lor p_{1,2};\quad p_{2,1}\lor p_{2,2};

\displaystyle p_{1,k}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg\,p_{2,k}\quad(k=1,2);

\displaystyle p_{i,k}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q_{k,1}\quad(i=1,2,\;k=1,2);\mbox{and}

\displaystyle q_{1,1}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg\,q_{2,1}.

Here
m=2\leq t=2
but
t=2>n=1
, so these formulas are unsatisfiable, as there is no way to route two pigeons, via two resting places, into a single hole.

7.2.4
Subset Cardinality Problems

Let
B=(L,R,E)
be a bipartite graph with left part
L
, right part
R
, and edge set
E\subseteq L\times R
, where the vertices in
L
and
R

are represented as integers.
We introduce one Boolean variable

x_{i,j}
for each edge
(i,j)\in E
, where
x_{i,j}
means we “select” the edge
(i,j)
. For any vertex
v\in L\cup R
, we write
N(v)
for
the set of
v
’s neighbors:

N(v)\;=\;\{\,u:(u,v)\in E\text{ or }(v,u)\in E\},

and we define
d(v)=|N(v)|
. Abusing notation to let
x_{i,j}
stand for the number 1 (or 0) if
the propositional variable is true (or false, respectively), we can express our
two sets of cardinality constraints with the following formulas:

\bullet

Left-side constraint:

For each
i\in L
, at least half of its incident edges must be selected:

\sum_{j\in N(i)}x_{i,j}\;\geq\;\Bigl\lceil\tfrac{d(i)}{2}\Bigr\rceil.

\bullet

Right-side constraint:

For each
j\in R
, at most half of its incident edges may be selected:

\sum_{i\in N(j)}x_{i,j}\;\leq\;\Bigl\lfloor\tfrac{d(j)}{2}\Bigr\rfloor.

Each of these is then translated into conjunctive normal form by a standard cardinality-constraint
encoding,
e.g.
, a sorting-network encoding or a sequential counter
[
78
]
. We used the
cnfgen

library
[
51
]
to generate 70 unsatisfiable formulas of this sort in DIMACS format, and we then
reversed the CNF transformation in most cases and converted the formulas into the uniform
NDL/Athena notation used throughout
ProofGrid
.

Subset-cardinality problems were first introduced in 2014 by
63
.
The source of the unsatisfiability in these problems is the tension between the left and
right constraints: on the left we are saying that at least half of the edges must be selected
but on the right we are saying that at least half of the edges must
not
be selected
(at most half may be selected). The intuitive contradiction here is that the left wants a lot of
selected edges while the right wants the opposite, not too many selected edges.

7.2.5
Tseitin Formula Problems

Consider the following undirected graph
G
, where each node (
A
,
B
,
C
,
D
) is also viewed as a propositional atom, and where additional propositional atoms
x_{i}
are attached to the edges:

A
B
C
D
x_{1}
x_{2}
x_{3}
x_{4}
x_{5}\;

The core Tseitin formula for
G
is the conjunction of the following
definitions
, one for each node:

A\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}x_{1}\oplus x_{2}\oplus x_{5}
,
B\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}x_{1}\oplus x_{3}
,
C\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}x_{2}\oplus x_{4}
,
D\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}x_{3}\oplus x_{4}\oplus x_{5}
, where
\oplus
denotes the exclusive-or (“XOR”) operation:
x_{i}\oplus x_{j}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}(x_{i}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg x_{j})\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}(x_{j}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg x_{i})
.
For arbitrary formulas
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, an easy induction on
n
, with
n=2
as the base case,
shows that
p_{1}\oplus p_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus p_{n}
holds iff exactly an odd number of
p_{i}
hold (we omit parentheses since
\oplus
is commutative and associative).
Thus, these definitions assert that each node atom holds iff an odd number of edges incident to that node hold.
So far all we have is a set of definitions, one for each node, that are perfectly jointly consistent. But now
suppose we go on to assert that an odd number
k
of node atoms hold, say by specifying
A
,
\neg B
,

\neg C
,
\neg D
(so that only
k=1
atom holds,
A
in this case). By what we just said about the exclusive
or, it would follow that
A\oplus B\oplus C\oplus D
holds. However, by the given definitions we have:

\displaystyle A\oplus B\oplus C\oplus D

\>\Leftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle(x_{1}\oplus x_{2}\oplus x_{5})\oplus(x_{1}\oplus x_{3})\oplus\,(x_{2}\oplus x_{4})\oplus(x_{3}\oplus x_{4}\oplus x_{5})

\>\Leftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle(x_{1}\oplus x_{1})\oplus(x_{2}\oplus x_{2})\oplus(x_{3}\oplus x_{3})\oplus\,(x_{4}\oplus x_{4})\oplus(x_{5}\oplus x_{5})

\>\Leftrightarrow\,

\displaystyle\mbox{false\/}.

The first step follows just by rearranging the variables on the right-hand side of the first equivalence, while the second step follows
because
p\oplus p\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}\mbox{false\/}

for any formula
p
. Thus we have a contradiction:
A\oplus B\oplus C\oplus D
holds because we have stipulated that only
A
holds, but at the same time we have
shown that
A\oplus B\oplus C\oplus D
cannot hold because it leads to
false
. The key intuition is that because each edge variable appears twice on the right-hand
side of the first equivalence (once for each node to which the edge is incident), we can regroup all variables as shown above to derive
false
.

While the conventional formulation of a Tseitin problem is as a jointly unsatisfiable set of formulas (typically clauses, but again, we work with full propositional logic),
we can easily pose it as a forward inference problem: Given the
n
definitions for the
n
node atoms of a graph
G
, prove the negation of any conjunction of an odd number of
those atoms. All Tseitin problems in
\textsc{PL}_{3}
are of this form.

7.2.6
Counting Principle Problems

The 5 problems in this group encode statements of the form
There is a way to partition a set of
M
elements into subsets of size
p
each
.
Clearly, this statement is satisfiable iff
p
divides
M
. Set
n=M/p
. For each element
e\in\{1,2,\dots,M\}
and each part (or “block”)
b\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
,
introduce a Boolean variable
x_{e,b}
that holds iff element
e
is assigned to block
b
.
We conjoin the following sets of constraints:

1.

Exactly one block per element.
Every element
e
must appear in exactly one block:

\bigl(x_{e,1}\lor x_{e,2}\lor\cdots\lor x_{e,n}\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad x_{e,b_{1}}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg x_{e,b_{2}}\quad\mbox{for all}\mbox{$\;\>$}b_{1}\neq b_{2},\quad e=1,\ldots,M.

2.

Exactly
p
elements per block.
Each block
b
must contain exactly
p
elements:

\sum_{e=1}^{M}x_{e,b}\;=\;p,\quad b=1,\ldots,n.

In SAT, such an equality is typically encoded by a pair of cardinality constraints
\geq p
and
\leq p
,
each represented by a standard cardinality encoding.

Simple counting shows that these constraints admit a satisfying assignment
precisely when
p
divides
M
.

7.2.7
De Bruijn Problems

For any integer
n>0
, the
n^{\mbox{th}}

De Bruijn
formula, which we denote by
\mathcal{D}_{n}
, is defined as the conditional
(p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
, where
q
is the conjunction
(A_{1}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\cdots\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A_{n})
and

p
is the conjunction of the
n
conditionals
((A_{1}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{2})\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
,
((A_{2}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{3})\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
,
\ldots
,
((A_{n}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{1})\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
.
It is easy to show (see below) that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is a tautology—and hence provable—iff
n
is odd.
Thus,
\mathcal{D}_{n}
does not hold for any even
n
.
Odd-indexed formulas are often used as stress tests for theorem provers. However, the corresponding proofs are also good tests of a system’s
proof-checking ability. The proof sizes grow linearly as a function of
n
, but what makes the proofs more interesting
is that they include highly nested conditional subproofs introducing long staircases of assumption scopes,
which are ideal for evaluating a model’s ability to track hypotheses.

To make things concrete, consider
\mathcal{D}_{5}
, depicted diagrammatically as follows:
q=A_{1}\land A_{2}\land A_{3}\land A_{4}\land A_{5}
A_{1}
A_{2}
A_{3}
A_{4}
A_{5}

The slanted arrows coming in from each side represent the
n=5
conditionals

((A_{1}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{2})\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q),\ldots,((A_{5}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{1})\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
, which are taken for granted (
i.e.
, essentially
taken as premises, by virtue of their conjunction
p
serving as a hypothesis of
\mathcal{D}_{5}
).

The key observation is that, because
n=5
is odd, at least one of the 5 biconditionals
A_{1}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{2},\ldots,A_{5}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{1}

must be true, because at least one pair of adjacent atoms (
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, or
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
,
\ldots
, or
A_{5}
and
A_{1}
)
must be assigned the same truth value (either true or false, respectively represented here as green and red). And, of course,
if two adjacent atoms
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
are assigned the same truth value (the same color), then
A_{i}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{j}
becomes true,
because that’s what a biconditional
A_{i}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{j}
means: that
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
are both true or both false.

Now, to see why some pair of adjacent atoms must be assigned the same color, approach the matter adversarially and try
to assign colors (truth values) to the atoms so that this is
not
the case,
i.e.
, so that every two adjacent atoms
receive distinct colors. Say we start with
A_{1}
true (green) as in the above diagram. We then must make
A_{2}
false (red),
and thus
A_{3}
green and
A_{4}
red. But then
A_{5}
must be green, which makes
A_{5}
and
A_{1}
equivalent, thereby allowing us
to conclude
q
by the hypothesis
(A_{5}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{1})\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q
. This is exactly the situation shown in the diagram, but any other
assignment of truth values to atoms would have the same upshot; and the same argument would work for any
other odd
n
.
73
73

73

In Athena it is easy to express the above procedure as a parameterized
method

(a proof algorithm) that takes any odd integer
n
and derives
D_{n}
by assuming the conjunction
p

of the
n
conditionals of the form
(A_{i}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{j})\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q
and then supposing, by way of contradiction,
that
\neg q
holds. The method proceeds by a case analysis of
A_{1}
: either
A_{1}
holds or not.
If it holds, then
A_{2}
must not hold (for if it did, we would have
A_{1}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{2}
and
p
would allows us to conclude
q
,
contradicting the supposition
\neg q
). Likewise,
A_{3}
must hold (if it did not, we’d have
A_{2}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{3}
and again

p
would let us derive
q
),
A_{4}
must not hold, and so on, all the way back to
A_{1}
, which would be shown
to be false, contradicting the assumption that it holds. We likewise alternate through all the atoms under the second
assumption of the case analysis, where
A_{1}
does not hold, ultimately concluding that it does hold and thus reaching
a contradiction.

When
n
is even, an adversarial assignment of truth values to atoms
along the aforementioned lines provides a counterexample to
\mathcal{D}_{n}
that makes all

n
conditionals
(A_{i}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{j})\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q
vacuously true (since each
A_{i}\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}A_{j}
is false),
while making the conjunction
q
false because at least one atom is assigned false (colored red).

7.3
A Sample Proof and Multi-Hop Reasoning Depth in
\textsc{PL}_{3}
Proofs

This section illustrates a
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proof from the family of simple DAG-pebbling
problems.
74
74

74

The proof appears in
PL3-PC
as a proof-checking problem,
while the underlying argument also appears in
PL3-PW
as a proof-writing problem.

We discuss the generation of the problem itself (its premises and conclusion),
the proof and its logical structure as a graph, and define the general
notion of reasoning depth for
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proofs.

For this particular problem family (by far the easiest in
\textsc{PL}_{3}
),
our data-generation process starts by stochastically building
a simple DAG of a given size, one layer at a time, up to a maximum depth of 6.
By construction, there is always one source node and one target node. At the end, we
randomly add some extra cross-layer edges for redundancy and symmetry, which further
simplifies the reasoning problem. Here is a sample DAG generated by our code
(corresponding to item 233 in both
PL3-PW
and
PL3-PC

The sole source node here is 1, while the target node is 8.
This DAG is given as an argument to the

function of the

library
[
51
]
, which generates a random CNF for it.
We then declausify that output by converting the CNF clauses to a collection
of formulas that make heavy use of conditionals. Every numeric DIMACS variable
i
is consistently
mapped to the atomic formula
A
i
. Finally, we pick the atom corresponding to the DAG’s

target node as the goal of the proof. In this example the goal is
A8
, while the premises are
shown below. The proof (for
PL3-PC
) is obtained by a call to Vampire
[
49
]
. The resolution proof output by Vampire,
expressed in TPTP notation
[
86
]
, is then translated into
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
as a proof by contradiction.

Premises 2 to 8 assert a chain of implications leading from
A1
to
A8
. For example,
premise-2

asserts that
A1
implies
A2
,
75
75

75

Strictly speaking, it asserts the contrapositive of that implication.
As we pointed out earlier (p.
7.2.1
), the Horn clauses of these problems are sometimes
rewritten using basic logical equivalences.

premise-6
states that
A2
and
A5
imply
A6
, and so on.
The very first premise sets the dominoes toppling by asserting
A1
.
The goal is to derive
A8
. This is done by the
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proof on lines 11–31. The reasoning
proceeds by contradiction, reflecting Vampire’s resolution proof. On the assumption that
A8
does not hold, it is easy to derive
the absurdity
false
, simply by deriving
A8
and contradicting the hypothesis
(
~

A8)
.
Observe that reasoning by contradiction here is quite
unnecessary. The proof uses it only because it is mechanically translated from Vampire’s
resolution proof, which is inherently refutational. In fact, lemmas 1 through 15 form a perfectly adequate
direct
proof of
A8

from the premises, which is easily verified by noting that
negated-conjecture
does not appear anywhere in lines 13--27.
The negated conjecture only makes a perfunctory appearance in the very final step, on line 28.
76
76

76

The proof-optimization algorithm
of
5
would automatically simplify this proof by hoisting lines 13–27 outside of the
assume
and then eliminating the
subsequent decimated conditional subproof along with the final application of
by-contradiction
, because at that point that pair of deductions
would be a detour, re-deriving what the hoisted inference block has already derived (namely,
A8
).
Nevertheless, in the interest of
simplicity and uniformity, we retain the same refutational proof template throughout.
These proofs form the raw data of the proof-checking task of
\textsc{PL}_{3}
(
PL3-PC
).

The linear flavor of the above proof belies the rich two-dimensional logical structure of the underlying reasoning.
The graph below depicts that structure diagrammatically. Nodes are either premises or lemmas, and a directed edge from a node

u
to a node
v
representing a lemma indicates that the derivation of that lemma relies on the formula represented by
u
.

In particular, for every derivation of the form
p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{from}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, there is a directed edge from every node
corresponding to an argument
p_{i}
to the node corresponding to the conclusion
p
.
(To save space, the diagram uses “l” and “p” as abbreviations for “lemma” and “premise,” respectively, thus, e.g., writing

l-8
as shorthand for
lemma-8
,
p-2
for
premise-2
, and so on.)
Accordingly, for line 14 of the proof,
lemma-2 := A3
from
premise-3, lemma-1
, we see that the graph has two edges
leading to
l-2
, one from
p-3
and the other from
l-1
. By convention, we add an extra mock arrow from the very last
lemma that derives
false
to the literal
false
itself, in this case with an edge leading from
l-16
to
false
.
Such a reasoning graph is also a DAG (since there cannot be any cycles, which would indicate circular reasoning),
whose sources are the premises and whose sink node is the constant
false
. We define a
maximal branch
of such a graph as a
maximum-length path from a source (premise) to the conclusion
false
. In the diagram above, the nodes and edges along such a path
are colored in red. We define the
reasoning depth
of a proof as the length of such a maximal branch.
77
77

77

In theory,
there may be multiple maximal branches in such a graph, but, by definition, both must have the same length.

For this simple example, the reasoning depth is 12, but for more complex problem families the reasoning of a proof can be much deeper.
The DAG of a proof for a medium-complexity Tseitin-formula problem appears in Figure
24
.
The general distribution of reasoning depth across all
PL3-PC
proofs is shown in Figure
25
.

Figure 24:
The directed acyclic reasoning graph of a proof for a medium-complexity Tseitin-formula problem.

We stress that this reasoning-depth distribution applies only to
PL3-PC
,
which uses the
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proofs obtained through Vampire.
The
PL3-PW
task has no a priori depth distribution, as an AI model is free to come up with its own proofs,
which, in theory, could be simpler or more complicated—and thus more or less shallow—than those found by Vampire.
We have already shown that the Vampire proof shown above could be simplified by converting it
into a direct proof that does not use reasoning by contradiction. But the proof could be simplified
even further, while still adhering to the proviso that each
from
step operates on no more than five arguments.
Here is a much simpler direct proof of the goal
A8

⬇

A competent language model should be able to compose this chain of reasoning without much difficulty.
Indeed, for this particular problem (item 233 in
PL3-PW

in that sense they do better than Vampire. Neither of the other two Gemini models (2.5 or 3.0) does quite as well,
while GPT-5.1 performs worse: it produces a correct proof, but it’s much longer and more convoluted than the

We discuss the proof-writing task in more detail in the next section.

7.4
PL3-PW

The
PL3-PW
task is simply to write an
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proof for a given
\textsc{PL}_{3}
problem.
As explained in Section
7.1
,
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proofs have exceptionally simple syntax and operational semantics,
such a proof being one of three things:

1.

an application of
from
to a number of formulas;

2.

a composite proof (
i.e.
, a proof sequence), built by stringing together a number of
other proofs and separating them by semicolons, exactly as in regular NDL;
or

3.

a hypothetical (
assume
) proof, also exactly as in regular NDL.

Applications of
from
are primitive proofs with no internal structure,
while composite and hypothetical proofs are compound, having recursive structure.

This parsimony is a significant advantage, as it strips reasoning tasks to
bare semantic essentials: entailment (
from
), assumption scope (
assume
),
and sequential composition or lemma chaining (the semicolon operator). This provides a minimal
universal proof grammar that any model can learn from a short prompt,
which should isolate pure logical competence more cleanly than vanilla NDL.

Figure 26:
Mean values of key numeric features of
PL3-PW
problems, organized by problem type.

Fig.
26
summarizes key statistics for
PL3-PW
problems by type.
For each of the 8 problem families, it reports the mean number of premises, atoms, and occurrences
of each logical connective in a problem of that family (across the premises and target conclusion),
along with the average premise size, conclusion size, and total problem size (the sum of the
sizes of the premises plus the size of the conclusion).
Some of these attributes have very large average values for a given problem type,
e.g.
,
De Bruijn
problems are conditionalized (have no premises) and hence their conclusion sizes are much larger
than the conclusions in any other problem family, so histogram bars for these other problem families
are not visible in the conclusion-size chart. In such cases we simply print the corresponding
numeric values where the histogram bars would normally appear, using the color scheme of the legend
(so that,
e.g.
, mean conclusion size is 8.9 for relativized-pigeonhole problems, 3.8 for subset-cardinality problems, and so on).
For clarity, we use the same convention in every histogram that has some disproportionately short bars.
Observe that biconditionals appear only in the
De Bruijn
family, hence their average number
in problems of every other type is zero. By contrast,
De Bruijn
problems have no
explicit disjunctions or negations. DAG-pyramid problems likewise do not contain any negations.

Overall accuracies for all models are shown in Table
14
.
Instrumentation for
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proofs
is less involved than in the case of regular NDL proofs, because there are no structural errors to repair
(simply because there are no inference rules). Thus,
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
instrumentation mostly fixes
superficial syntax errors, and the gaps between instrumented and strict (non-instrumented) scores
tend to be much smaller than in
PL1-PW
and
PL2-PW
.

Table 14:

Taking the instrumented accuracies as the official results, the discriminability index
(DI) of
PL3-PW
is 23.0%, which, as discussed in Section
3.2
,
is a strong degree of separating power. In addition, (a) there are no ceiling effects in this task
at present, since the best-performing model (Gemini 3.1) does not reach 75% accuracy; and (b) even
the best models are well-discriminated. In particular, restricting the computation of DI to the top 10 models (up to and including
Claude Sonnet 4.5 with extended thinking) yields a value of 17.2%, meaning that, on average, the accuracies of any
2 of these top 10 models differ by about 17 percentage points.
Even among the top 4 models, the DI is 0.14, reflecting substantial
performance variation in this elite group (most notably, the large gap between Gemini 3.1 and the other models).
As we show below in the breakdown of performance based on problem type,
PL3-PW
reveals that top
models have markedly different reasoning profiles.

The fact that the top models achieve accuracies on these problems ranging from the mid-40s to the low 70s
suggests that they have acquired latent model-theoretic reasoning schemas, not just surface inference templates.
That said, a closer analysis shows that model performance is very sensitive to problem type, with accuracies varying
widely across different problem families. The heatmap in Fig.
27

makes this variance clear by offering a much finer picture of model performance across problem types.
It shows that the top few models attain relatively high overall accuracies mostly because they
do very well on the easy problem types, which pushes up their average scores. In particular,
most elite models ace simple-DAG pebbling problems and DAG-pyramid problems, and perform
strongly on graph-coloring problems as well. But accuracy drops sharply as we move towards other problem families.
Note also that even the “simple” problem families are quite discriminating,
e.g.
,
Claude Sonnet 4.5 with extended thinking, a top-tier model that sometimes performs on par with GPT-5,
gets only 55% accuracy on the easiest problem family (simple-DAG pebbling), while o3 gets 68%.
Grok-4 gets only 60% on DAG-pyramid problems, while o3 aces those problems. And performance on graph coloring
drops below 50% once we move beyond the top 8 models, while even the performance of those 8 models
shows a good deal of variance. On
De Bruijn
problems, Gemini 3.1 and GPT-5.2 get perfect scores,
Grok-4 follows with 87%, Claude Opus 4.5 with 80%, while Gemini-3 clocks in at 20% and GPT-5/GPT-5.1 at 53%.
And as we move farther to the right, we see increasingly challenging
problem families,
e.g.
, relativized-pigeonhole, Tseitin-formula, and subset-cardinality problems
are harder, with no model reaching even 40% accuracy on subset-cardinality problems.
Moreover, these families are not hard simply because their problems are larger or demand longer proofs.
If anything, as Fig.
29
shows, some of the easier problem
categories (such as DAG pyramids, simple pebbling problems, and
De Bruijn
problems) tend to have
larger

problem instances. The harder problem families are challenging due to the combinatorial
and structural characteristics of their problems, not due to sheer size.

Figure 27:
PL3-PW
model accuracy vs. problem type heatmap.

These results reveal a rich complexity hierarchy within
PL3-PW
that generates a ladder of logical hardness:

1.

DAG problems are structurally transparent. They feature low fan-in and uniform dependencies,
inviting linear forward inference. Nevertheless, even these straightforward problems are tractable only for models
with precise multi-hop control. Mid-tier and older models still lose track of dependency order and lemma
management, despite the shallow problem structure.

2.

Graph-coloring and
De Bruijn
problems require non-local combinatorial coordination, reasoning over
mutual exclusions or cyclic dependencies.

3.

The remaining 4 families are the most demanding, requiring reasoning about global
constraints, nonconstructive parity, or meta-counting, not just local entailment. We discuss
the reasoning challenges posed by some of these problem types in more detail below.

The performance plateau observed in the hardest four families could mark the transition
between symbolic compositionality, where the best models now largely succeed, and abstract
combinatorial reasoning, where inference must coordinate quantitative rather than qualitative relationships,
and which appears to remain unsolved.

Figure 28:
PL3-PW
distribution of first errors.

Following the instrumented checker’s automatic detection and repair of syntax errors, the
remaining model errors can be divided into 4 groups:

•

parsing errors (that could not be auto-repaired);

•

genuine logic/reasoning errors (typically a
from
step whose conclusion does
not follow from the listed arguments, or with an argument that does not hold at that point in the proof);

•

a reference to an unbound identifier (usually because the identifier is out of scope at that point in the proof,
and sometimes because the identifier is hallucinated);

•

a wrong-conclusion error, whereby the proof (or a subproof) does manage to derive
some
conclusion, but not
the expected one.

Fig.
28
shows the distribution of these errors.
Logical errors dominate overall, especially
among the elite models, but there are notable exceptions,
e.g.
, most of the
errors made by Grok-4 are parsing errors.
However, these are the very first errors (the ones that cause proofs to fail),
not all errors made by a proof. It is possible, and actually common in our
experience, that a proof might contain multiple errors of different types, meaning that even if
the very first error were somehow repaired or ignored, the proof would still fail later on with
a different error. For example, a manual analysis of the first 30 GPT-5 proofs with parsing errors
showed that in most cases the proofs also had subsequent logical errors. For
instance, GPT-5’s proof for item 25 (a Tseitin-formula problem) fails due to
a parsing error, but even if that were overlooked, the proof would fail later due to
a logical error in this step:
A8
from
(A8 |
~

A6), (
~

A6)
.

So why are subset-cardinality problems challenging for LLMs? After all, these problems encode simple quantitative
constraints of the form “at least half of these variables must be true” or “at most half of these variables may be true.”
However, after translation to propositional logic, all explicit counting disappears. What remains is a web of small
formulas whose collective effect implicitly enforces global numerical invariants. No single formula,
or even any small neighborhood of formulas, is contradictory. Instead, unsatisfiability emerges only when many
such formulas are aggregated. Essentially, the solver must rediscover the original counting argument
from a distributed set of local logical fragments.

As an example, consider this bipartite graph:
1
2
3
4
A
B
C

The left and right subset-cardinality constraints for this graph can be encoded as follows:

Left-side constraints:

\{(x_{1A}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}x_{1C}),(x_{2A}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}x_{2B}),x_{3B},(x_{4B}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}x_{4C})\}

Right-side constraints:

\{(x_{1A}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg x_{2A}),(x_{2B}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg x_{3B}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg x_{4B}),

(x_{3B}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg x_{2B}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg x_{4B}),(x_{4B}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg x_{2B}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg x_{3B}),(x_{1C}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg x_{4C})\}

This is already an unsatisfiable set of
N=9
formulas. (In the
ProofGrid
formulation,
the task would be to prove that the negation of one of these 9 formulas follows from the other
N-1=8

formulas.) In particular, the left-side constraints force
x_{3B}
, which
yields
\neg x_{2B}
and
\neg x_{4B}
by virtue of the right-side constraint
(x_{3B}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg x_{2B}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg x_{4B})
.
Resolving these two atoms with the left-side constraints
(x_{2A}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}x_{2B})
and
(x_{4B}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}x_{4C})

gives
x_{2A}
and
x_{4C}
. But
x_{2A}
gives
\neg x_{1A}
(by the right-side constraint
(x_{1A}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg x_{2A})
),
while
x_{4C}
gives
\neg x_{1C}
(by the right-side constraint
(x_{1C}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg x_{4C})
). Finally,

\neg x_{1A}
and
\neg x_{1C}
contradict the left-side constraint
(x_{1A}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}x_{1C})
.

It is the requirement to extract latent global arithmetic structure that makes these problems
difficult for both LLMs and classical
resolution-based SAT solvers. Resolution is inherently a local
inference mechanism: it combines pairs of clauses by eliminating variables, which is effective
for local inconsistency but not very suitable for computing sums, majorities, or other global invariants.
Simulating counting via resolution produces wide intermediate clauses, which leads to
provably long proofs for such problems. And although LLMs obviously differ radically from SAT solvers in
their internal mechanics, they share a similar weakness: they are very good at detecting local
pattern-based inconsistencies and short causal chains, but they struggle to infer global
budget constraints (for example, that the left side of the bipartite graph collectively
forces at least
k
variables to be true while the right side collectively limits the total
to at most
k-1
). From the model’s perspective, the necessary contradiction is not “nearby”
in the clause space; it only appears once the entire system is integrated.
In short, subset-cardinality benchmarks expose a key limitation
of present LLM-based reasoning systems: they must perform non-local arithmetic reasoning while
operating on representations that offer only highly local symbolic cues.

Tseitin-formula problems present similar challenges.
Like subset-cardinality instances, they encode a global numerical invariant (a parity condition in this case)
using only local propositional constraints. In a Tseitin encoding, each graph node contributes an equation
stating that the XOR of the incident edge variables must equal (be logically equivalent to)
a node label. Each such constraint is
locally satisfiable, and nothing in any single equation suggests inconsistency. The contradiction
arises only when all constraints are combined: summing the node equations modulo 2 counts each edge
twice and therefore yields an even total, whereas the stipulated node labels sum to an odd total.
The system is therefore globally inconsistent even though every local piece of it
appears perfectly coherent.

It is this global character of the contradiction that makes Tseitin formulas difficult for
both classical SAT solvers and for LLMs. In the resolution setting, simulating XOR reasoning
inside CNF requires building large intermediate clauses, which leads to the well-known
exponential lower bounds for resolution on Tseitin formulas over expander graphs.
In other words, resolution has no built-in ability to compute mod-2 sums and
must instead simulate parity reasoning indirectly and expensively.
Despite their obvious differences, LLMs share an analogous limitation.
As already mentioned in connection with subset-cardinality problems, LLMs
are good at identifying small, localized inconsistency patterns, but they struggle
with algebraic invariants that emerge only after aggregating many constraints.
When given a Tseitin instance, a model sees a lot of small formulas with no explicit
indication that they collectively impose XOR constraints. To detect unsatisfiability,
the model must effectively reconstruct an argument asserting that the parity of
all node labels must equal the parity of all edge constraints, a global property
that is invisible at the level of individual formulas.
Thus, Tseitin formulas expose a second core weakness of current LLM reasoning abilities:
the lack of robust mechanisms for global algebraic aggregation, here in the form of parity
rather than counting. Subset-cardinality formulas require discovering hidden integer bounds,
while Tseitin formulas require discovering hidden mod-2 invariants. In both cases,
the contradiction is not “nearby,” appearing only after combining many constraints
in a way that neither resolution nor contemporary LLMs perform efficiently.

We close the section with a few qualitative observations about the
PL3-PW
results.

•

Given the simplicity of
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
’s syntax, one might wonder what sort of nontrivial
parsing errors models still manage to make.
Here is a small but representative sample:

–

Hallucinated compositionality, such as compound
from
steps or formulas with subformulas that are named inline. Here’s an example
from Claude Opus 4.5 (item 11):

and one from GPT-5.2 (item 80):

–

e.g.
, here is a step from Grok-4’s proof for problem 10:

Wait, in this branch, we are under assm13 = A3
,
and one from Grok-4’s proof for item 83:

–

Conditional proofs with empty bodies, e.g. (from Grok-4’s proof for problem 22):

–

Atomic inference steps with empty bodies,
e.g.
(from Claude Opus 4.5 on item 17):

–

Incorrectly formatted comments,
e.g.
, here is a proof step by o3-mini (item 41):

Here o3-mini used a C-style comment block instead of hash signs (it did this in 10 proofs overall).

•

Fig.
28
shows that some top-tier models
make a lot of syntax errors—more than logic errors.
This is especially pronounced for o3, which makes over 3 times as many parsing errors
as logic errors. This is odd behavior for a top model, since these usually have better
cognitive control.
A closer look reveals that many of these are not genuine parsing failures.
They typically occur either because:

(a)

the model gives up (
e.g.
, o3 explicitly writes in its proof description
for item 15:
Proof deliberately omitted: problem too complex to resolve within given constraints
)
and returns an empty proof, which of course fails to parse; or because

(b)

the model incorrectly concludes that the theorem it was given to prove is not a theorem and (falsely) claims to discover a counterexample.
This also results either in a completely empty proof or one that only has comments explaining the alleged counterexample,
thus also resulting in a parsing error.

As an example of (b), GPT-5.2 gives the following proof for problem 12:

⬇

1

#

No

proof

can

be

given

in

NDL

for

this

sequent,

because

it

is

not

valid.

2

#

Countermodel

(satisfies

all

premises,

falsifies

the

target):

3

#

A3=true,

A1=false,

A2=false,

A4=false,

A5=false,

4

#

A6=false,

A10=false,

A8=false,

A11=true,

A12=false,

A13=false,

5

#

A9=true,

A7=false.

6

#

Under

this

assignment,

(~A3)

is

false,

hence

((~A3)

==>

(A5

|

A2

|

A4

|

A1))

is

true,

7

#

and

the

desired

negation

(~

((~A3)

==>

(A5

|

A2

|

A4

|

A1)))

is

false.

8

}

When the comments are stripped out, this too is an empty proof that doesn’t parse.

Response patterns (a) and (b) are very different. The former reflects deliberate abstention,
where the model explicitly acknowledges its inability to solve a problem
and refrains from generating an output that looks plausible but is incorrect.
If anything, this suggests better calibration and some degree of epistemic awareness.
While
PL3-PW
was not designed to measure abstention, it is nevertheless possible to gauge it
indirectly by inspecting proof descriptions and lengths. A cursory analysis suggests
that o3 is the model that most often realizes its inability
to produce a working solution and instead returns a minimal output
consistent with the formatting instructions.
78
78

78

Although here too, as in

PL2-PW
, o3 often exaggerates the difficulty of these
problems. For example, on problem 18 it writes that a short proof is impossible
due to “the extremely intricate web of the 24 premises,” adding that a fully
explicit derivation “would easily require several hundred carefully checked proof-lines,”
even though Gemini 3.1 solves the same problem in fewer than 50 steps.
This tendency suggests deficits in o3’s metacognition that often make it unable
to distinguish “this is very hard for me” from “this is objectively very difficult.”

That said, many syntax errors by other strong models are genuine. This is the case,
for instance, for almost all syntax errors by Grok-4 and most by Claude Opus 4.5.
Gemini 3.1 stands out for its nearly impeccable syntax control, registering
only 3 minor parsing errors across all 300 proofs and zero grammar hallucinations.

•

It is noteworthy that in over a dozen out of the 70 relevant problems, Gemini 3.1
was able to explicitly recognize that the input formulas were encodings of graph-coloring problems, and proceeded to
reason about them as such. For instance, here is what the model had to say about problem 144:

The provided premises describe a graph-coloring problem where each triplet of variables (e.g.,
A13
,
A14
,
A15
)
represents a single node taking one of three colors. We can extract a subset of four nodes—specifically the ones corresponding to

premise-1

(A13|A14|A15)
,
premise-2

(A16|A17|A18)
,

premise-4

(A4|A5|A6)
, and
premise-5

(A7|A8|A9)
—which form
a complete graph of size 4 (
K_{4}
). Because each node must take one of exactly 3 colors, and no
two connected nodes can share a color, these four nodes cannot be successfully colored.
This implies the premises are inherently logically inconsistent.

We derive this contradiction rigorously through exhaustive case analysis on
premise-2
,
(A16|A17|A18)
.
For each assumption (
A16
,
A17
, and
A18
), we analyze the options for
premise-1

(A13|A14|A15)
.
In every nested case, the possible colors for the remaining two nodes are restricted, leading to a chain of forced
implications that systematically rules out all three options for the final triplet, allowing us to derive
false
.
We then use disjunction elimination to bubble up
false
through all the assumption blocks until we prove
false

unconditionally from the four premises.

This is a compelling demonstration that the model has built powerful internal
representations that allow it to recover the latent
combinatorial structure of an unknown (previously unseen) problem even after it has been
compiled away into entirely abstract propositional logic formulas.
In this case, Gemini 3.1 managed
to infer the underlying graph, reconstruct its coloring constraints, and reason about it at the level of graph theory rather than
uninterpreted symbolic logic. That kind of structural reverse-engineering is precisely what makes
symbolic reasoning difficult for conventional solvers, yet at times these language models appear capable of performing
it robustly. Nevertheless, the contrast with their performance on other problem families (such as
subset-cardinality and Tseitin-formula instances) shows that this ability is limited at present.
It works well when the hidden structure is relational and pattern-based, but not when it requires detecting
global numeric or parity invariants that leave no local syntactic traces.

This observation also brings into question the aim of testing fluid reasoning through abstract symbolic problems
that are ostensibly free of content. If the task items are drawn from well-known problem families, such
as graph coloring, it is evidently possible that strong models will be able to decode them and solve them
by relying on crystallized knowledge.

That’s a fair point. If a model can look at a compiled symbolic problem instance and infer that it encodes
a Tseitin formula or a pigeonhole structure, then the problem is no longer testing
reasoning in a vacuum. So
PL3-PW
should certainly not be viewed as a psychometrically pure measure
of fluid reasoning (although in any event we do not make that claim for any
ProofGrid
tasks).
And this does raise questions for future benchmarks. Abstract symbolic formulations of task items
can still carry structural fingerprints of their source problem families, and over time models will
become better at recognizing such fingerprints and solving problems in schema-conditioned ways.

At the same time, this does not necessarily create a loophole. De-engineering hidden structure is
itself a hard reasoning act. A loophole would be direct retrieval of a memorized proof, superficial
matching to a canned template, or exploiting accidental formatting cues. By contrast, to
successfully decode a compiled logical instance back into an underlying combinatorial object, a model has to
detect subtle regularities; hypothesize a latent generating structure; map the symbolic encoding
back to that structure; and then solve the problem by exploiting that reconstruction. This is a
deep and substantive capability, not surface pattern completion. In general, there is an important difference between

retrieval
of explicit memorized content versus
reconstruction
of latent structure from the
problem instance itself.
PL3-PW
blocks the first but does not fully block the second, and arguably
it shouldn’t. If a model recognizes the infinity-of-primes proof because it saw it in training,
that tells us little. But if a model reconstructs an implicit graph from a compiled
logic formula and then reasons over that graph, that tells us a lot. That is not just crystallized
knowledge; it is crystallized knowledge being productively brought to bear through a demanding
inference process. Humans do this all the time (“this is really a parity argument” or “this is a disguised
pigeonhole principle,” etc.), and we don’t call it cheating—we call it insight.

7.5
Introduction to Epistemic Instability: Remembering Locally and Forgetting Globally

We previously discussed, in the context of checking
\textsc{PL}_{1}
proofs (p.
•
‣
3.2
),
“a recurring failure of models to exhibit judgment consistency,” a broad phenomenon that we
refer to as
epistemic instability
. Here we give another illustration, this time
more rigorously, in the context of proof writing, which provides an ideal testbed
for this kind of analysis.
The starting point is a theme that emerges upon inspection of incorrect proofs: these proofs often
fail due to steps with trivial logical errors—so trivial that one would expect the models
that wrote these steps, especially the more sophisticated ones, to be able to easily recognize
such steps as invalid.

Here is a concrete example from the GPT-5.2 proof for
PL3-PW
problem 96.
At some point in that proof, GPT-5.2 writes:

where
a16-or-a15
denotes the previously derived conclusion
(A16 | A15)
. In other words, the model
is claiming that
A15
follows from
(A16 | A15)
.
This is an elementary mistake:
A15
does not follow from
(A16 | A15)
, and in general,

q
does not follow from
(p\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}q)
.
Indeed, if we separately ask GPT-5.2 to judge the validity of this inference,
it readily gives the right answer along with the obvious counterexample:

⬇

1

A15:

false

That part is unsurprising, as this is a trivial satisfiability question
that any model should be able to get right with minimal effort.
And GPT-5.2 does get it right, consistently: if we ask it this
question multiple times, it will reliably (and quickly) tell us that
A15
does
not follow from
(A16 | A15)
. And yet, in the context of writing a proof,
the model somehow forgets this elementary fact and claims
that
A15
does follow from
(A16 | A15)
.

An example from Gemini 3.1 comes from problem 11, where the model writes:

⬇

1

A4

from

premise-16,

A10;

2

...

where
premise-16
is
(A4 ==> A10)
.
In words, Gemini 3.1 here claims that
A_{4}
follows from
A_{4}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A_{10}
and
A_{10}
,
which is clearly wrong (it’s the classic fallacy of affirming the consequent).
And again, when we later ask the model to evaluate
the validity of this step, it rejects it as incorrect and judges it to be a trivial
error (on a scale of 1 to 5, with 1 being trivial and 5 being very difficult, it
rates this inference at 1). It might be that the model confused
premise-16
with
premise-15
,
which is
\neg A_{4}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg A_{10}
and which does imply
A_{4}
in conjunction with
A_{10}
.
Similar lexical confusions could be behind Gemini 3.1 errors on steps like

A11_1
from
(
~

A11 ==> false)
(from item 13); and likewise
on
false
from
A11_1, (A11 ==> false)
(again from item 13).
But fuzzy lexical proximity does not explain errors like

false
from
A6,
~

A7
(item 67), unless
one holds that
A6
and
A7
are easy to confuse;
and likewise for steps like
A10
from
(A8

|

A10), A8
(item 71)
or
false
from
A4,
~

A15
(item 105), or many other similar errors.
When we later ask Gemini 3.1 to evaluate the validity of
these inferences, it of course recognizes them as wrong and generates the obvious
counterexamples. It also rates the complexity of these entailment steps at level 1 or 2
(very simple), with remarks like “It’s a trivial problem.”

This is strikingly dissimilar to how a human mathematician—and indeed any stable rational agent—would
operate. A competent human writing proofs would not make such elementary logical
blunders. A beginner who hasn’t yet mastered these concepts
might make such blunders, but they would typically make them consistently and
regardless of the overarching task context, simply because they don’t
have the level of competence needed to recognize them as blunders. But
these giant models
do
have that level of competence, because when
they are given these simple entailment problems, they invariably solve them correctly
and demonstrate a robust understanding of what does and doesn’t follow and why,
and they even rate the problems as trivial; and yet somehow they continue to
make these errors when writing proofs.

To quantify this more precisely, we did the following for each of the top 9 models
M
:
we took all proofs written by
M
that had at least one logical error on a step of
the form
p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{from}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, selected one such erroneous step from each proof, and asked

M
whether
p
follows from
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
. Invariably, all models correctly responded
that
p
does
not
follow and provided appropriate counterexamples. Table
15

summarizes the results.
79
79

79

As the caption of Table
15
notes, the numbers in the
column
# of Logical Errors
are undercounts because only one error is chosen
from each logically incorrect proof. Estimates of the total counts of logical errors
across all 300
PL3-PW
problems are as follows: Claude Opus 4.5: 1659, Gemini-3.1: 1046, Gemini-2.5-Pro: 925,
Gemini 3: 905, GPT-5: 737, Grok-4: 441, o3: 353, GPT-5.1: 340, GPT-5.2: 138.
These are conservative estimates that ignore all proof steps with syntax errors,
so they might also be undercounts.
(In particular, models that make many syntax errors may end up
with underestimated logical-error counts because many of their steps are
garbled and cannot be analyzed algorithmically; if they were analyzed manually, additional
logical errors would likely be discovered, errors that are currently
absorbed by syntax noise. This also means, ironically, that models that
write cleaner proofs, such as Gemini 3.1, may end up with higher logical-error counts
simply because we’re able to scrutinize more of their proof steps.)
Of course, absolute error numbers don’t take into account the volume
of proof code written by each model. Once we normalize accordingly, we get the following ratios:
GPT-5: 0.0401, Gemini 2.5: 0.0355, Claude Opus 4.5: 0.0324, o3: 0.0312, Gemini 3.1: 0.0252,
Gemini 3: 0.025, GPT-5.1: 0.0204, Grok 4: 0.0139, and GPT-5.2: 0.0092. These can be viewed
as conservative empirical probabilities of logical error per proof line.

We see that all models were able to flag 100% of their logical errors,
and moreover judged those errors to be elementary (with 1 being trivial, 2 easy, 3 average, 4 hard, and 5 very hard,
most of these inference errors were rated 1; the mean rating did not reach 2 for any of these models).
The results make it clear that proof-generation competence and local-entailment competence
come apart in this setting. In Section
8
we sketch the beginnings of
a quantitative theory of epistemic stability, which we then apply to this data
to obtain concrete epistemic-stability scores for the models
in Table
15
, normalized to the unit interval.

In conclusion, the best models fail in this task not because they cannot recognize
elementary logical relations, but because that knowledge is not stably preserved
under the cognitive load of proof generation; the models “know locally” but “forget
globally.” In the conclusions, we attribute this partly to a failure of computational
cognitive modularity.

Here

is proof-level: one per incorrect proof; we are not counting all erroneous steps.

7.6
PL3-PC

This task is similar to
PL1-PC
, except that it
works with
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
rather than NDL: given an
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proof for a problem,
determine if the proof is correct (successfully derives the target
conclusion from the premises); and if it is not, specify the first
line in the proof that has an error and describe the details of that error.
In addition, if the error is in a step of the form
p

from

p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}

and amounts to
p
not following from
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, the model must produce
a counterexample,
i.e.
, an interpretation which satisfies every
p_{i}
but falsifies
p
.

All
PL3-PC
problems were solved with ATPs and the resolution proofs were then translated
into
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
. All 300
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proofs obtained in this way were correct by construction.
We postprocessed every proof as follows: for every step
p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{from}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}

of every proof, we randomly decided whether to (a) inline some argument(s)
p_{i}
; and/or to
(b) append extra arguments
p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{k}^{\prime}
, randomly chosen, for some
k>0
.
Inlining in this context means replacing a variable occurrence like
lemma-8
by
the corresponding formula.
This plainly preserves correctness, but it may make verification more difficult
since the model must focus more on the formulas themselves rather than their names.
Because classical logic is monotonic, adding extra arguments also preserves correctness; these
simply serve as distractors. Finally, to avoid a severely imbalanced dataset, we corrupted
a relatively small number of proofs (53),
e.g.
, by negating the conclusion of a
from

step. We used a SAT solver to ensure that every corrupted step is indeed incorrect and should
therefore receive a negative verdict from a proof checker.

The 300 problems in
PL3-PC
are largely the same as the 300 problems of
PL3-PW
,
with two main differences:

1.

The premises of each
PL3-PC
problem form a subset of the premises of the corresponding
PL3-PW
problem.
That’s because in a
PL3-PC
problem we typically retain only those premises that are actually used in that
problem’s proof, whereas a
PL3-PW
problem may have additional, redundant premises.
Also, each premise in a proof-checking problem may be a trivial semantics-preserving
transformation of its counterpart in the proof-writing problem,
e.g.
, a premise
like
(A_{3}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg A_{5}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg A_{8})
might become the contrapositive

(A_{5}\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}A_{8}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg A_{3})
in the corresponding proof-checking problem.

2.

The
De Bruijn
problems (items 280 through 294) are larger, starting
with the
De Bruijn
formula
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{n}
for
n=11
(instead of
n=1
, as is the case in
PL3-PW
)
and ending with
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{39}
. This shift was made because the first few odd-numbered
proofs (for
n=1,3,5,7,9
) are too short and easy to check.
Some of the proofs for the new
De Bruijn
problems are much longer than
average, with several hundred steps (although none are larger than 1K steps).
These are the only proofs that are longer than the maximum of 150.
The following table summarizes the precise distribution of
proof lengths in
PL3-PC
, showing that 95% of the proofs have no more than 150 steps.

Figure
29
gives finer-grained information per problem type.
As noted earlier,
premise sets in this task are shorter. Except for DAG-pyramid problems, the average number of
premises is below 40, while most problem families have fewer than 20 atoms per instance on average. Premises themselves
are small, typically having fewer than 8 nodes on average; the sole exception is simple DAG pebbling problems,
whose premises average around 10 nodes. Target conclusions are also quite small, with the exception of
De Bruijn
problems.
(These are conditionalized, incorporating the premises into the conclusion.) Also, because
PL3-PC

includes
\mathcal{D}_{n}
formulas for larger
n
(up to
n=39
), these conclusions become quite large, as reflected in
the tall orange bars in the “conclusion size” and “problem size” plots. The remaining plots report average counts
of the various logical connectives across entire problems (summed over all premises and conclusion). Note that
De Bruijn
problems are the only ones with biconditionals.

Figure 29:
Mean values of key numeric features of
PL3-PC
problems, organized by problem type.

Because most of the proofs in
PL3-PC
are flat, having been originally derived from
resolution-based ATPs, they test long-context dependency tracking but not hierarchical
context management (the latter is tested by
PL1-PC
and especially by the challenge
set
PL1-PC-c
). Specifically, here are the core capabilities that are stress-tested by
PL3-PC
:

1.

Reference resolution in linearly growing lexical environments: in a step like

can the model reliably retrieve the definitions of
lemma_5
,
lemma_2
, and
premise_12

from earlier parts of its context, and flag an error if one of them is not found?

2.

Logical availability verification (assumption-base tracking), also in monotonically
growing logical contexts . Any argument to
from
may be inlined, e.g., a step might be:

So the model must only resolve the names
lemma_3
and
premise4
,
but must also make sure that the inline formulas
(
~

C7

|

~

C8

|

A2)
and
B4

are in the assumption base at that particular point in the proof,
i.e.
, that
they are logically available and can be used as ammunition to derive further conclusions.

3.

Micro-reasoning under long-context pressure: Can the model verify that
p
follows
from
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
(for
n\leq 5
)? The model has to solve dozens or hundreds of
these mini-SAT problems in the course of checking a single
\textsc{PL}_{3}
proof, and it must
do so without compromising any of the other subproblems it must solve. This is the main
capability tested by
PL3-PC
(and one that
PL1-PC
doesn’t already test).

The main results for
PL3-PC
are reported in Table
16
. The evaluation protocol for this task
is lenient in the following sense: true positives (cases where a model correctly determines that a proof
is invalid
80
80

80

Recall (p.
1
) that we view proof checking as a debugging task (finding reasoning errors),
so a “positive” verdict is one that flags a proof as incorrect.
)
are counted as such when the model identifies the erroneous step, even if it’s wrong about the exact
nature of the error or adduces an incorrect counterexample. However, manual inspection of
a random sample suggests that reports that manage to pinpoint the location of the
error also tend to describe it accurately and to generate valid counterexamples,
so this leniency is unlikely to affect the results materially. We also list guessing-only accuracies.
