Table 16:
PL3-PC
accuracies and guessing-only accuracies.

Based on the accuracies for the full task, the DI (discriminability index) of this task is 0.42, indicating exceptionally high discriminating power.

Note that guessing-only mode is of marginal help to strong models.
However, as we move down the table and the ability scale, guessing-only mode becomes increasingly beneficial, with accuracy
boosts ranging from 2X to 10X for lower-end models.

Because a verdict that finds the input proof correct does not require supporting evidence,
a model can easily inflate its score by disproportionately favoring that outcome.
This is especially true for an imbalanced dataset like
PL3-PC
, which only has 53 incorrect
proofs out of 300, meaning that a trivial policy of always answering “correct”
can already achieve an accuracy of 82%. Verifying incorrect proofs is more demanding, because
it requires precise error localization. Accordingly, we also report performance when evaluation
is restricted to the set of incorrect proofs, in Table
17
.
We see that the top two Gemini models are barely affected by this challenge, but the rest of the elite
models are impacted more significantly (e.g., Claude Opus 4.5 goes from 93% to 75% and GPT-5.2
falls to 64%), while performance at the middle and lower tiers plunges (
e.g.
, the accuracy of
DeepSeek Reasoner drops from 72% to 17%). We also see some pathological perfect accuracies on the
guessing-only variant here from models like DeepSeek 3 and Grok 3, which always output negative
verdicts.

Fig.
30
gives a finer-grained picture of proof-checking performance,
indexed by problem type.
It shows that most of these problem families are easy for top-tier models, with

De Bruijn
proofs being the most notable exception. As we have already pointed out,
these are considerably longer than the other proofs, and they also feature
very large inlined formulas that are hard to process without a reliable
algorithm. However, mid-tier and third-tier models exhibit a good
deal of performance variation across these problem families. For example,
dag-pyramid problems are almost universally accessible (e.g., Llama-405b by Nous Research
and Mistral Magistral reach 70% and 40% there, respectively), despite being the longest proofs
in the dataset (apart from
De Bruijn
proofs). By contrast, proofs from the relativized-pigeonhole
problem family, for example, are much more challenging for such models (
e.g.
, the accuracies of
Llama-405b and Mistral Magistral fall to 21% and 3% there, respectively).

Figure 30:
PL3-PC
accuracy per problem type.

Figure 31:
Error distribution across the 4 error types for
PL3-PC
.

Fig.
31
shows the distribution of the various error types made by the models.
We use the same error taxonomy we used for
PL1-PC
, with the exception of type-5 errors, which
are not relevant in this setting:

1.

Type-1 errors
: The model incorrectly claims that the proof is right (false negatives).

2.

Type-2 errors
: The model incorrectly claims that the proof is wrong (false positives).

3.

Type-3 errors
: Formatting issues (
e.g.
, responses missing expected fields).

4.

Type-4 errors
: The model correctly claims that the proof is wrong but fails to localize the error.

We see that type-2 errors dominate across all models and ability levels.
Deepseek-V3, in particular, makes a stunning 247/247 type-2 errors—a false-positive
error rate of 100%. The model invariably hallucinates non-existent errors.
Type-1 errors, where a buggy proof is wrongly pronounced correct, are much less
common, with Deepseek-Reasoner taking the lead with 28 such errors (out a total possible
of 53, for an error rate of 53%), followed by Llama 405b and o4-mini (41% and 40%, respectively).
Gemini 3.1 is the only elite model that did not make even a single type-1 error,
while the two top Gemini models also made very few type-4 errors,
a testament to their strong debugging skills, and specifically their
ability to reliably identify subtle errors in complex reasoning chains.
GPT-5.2 underperforms on this task, with a false-positive rate of 12%.

Fig.
32
plots accuracy as a function of proof length for the top 12 models. We exclude
De Bruijn
problems
because those 15 proofs are significantly longer than the others and there are not enough of them for
a robust plot. The figure shows that proof size is not a strong degrader of
PL3-PC

performance, at least in this particular evaluation regime of flat
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
proofs with no more than a few hundred steps,
which translates to inputs with considerably fewer than 10K tokens.
81
81

81

Excluding

De Bruijn
problems, the average number of BPE tokens in a
PL3-PC
input, counting both
the detailed instructions and the input proof, is 6.9K (computed with

tiktoken
using the
cl100k_base
encoding).
The median is 6.6K tokens. With
De Bruijn
proofs, these become 7.7K and 6.6K, respectively.

Modern attention mechanisms show robust retrieval for context windows of that size (and even for
substantially larger ones).

In conclusion, even though a few models already attain very high absolute accuracy on
PL3-PC
,
the task remains sharply discriminative overall and exposes substantial qualitative differences
in error calibration, localization, and robustness. Moreover, the observed ceiling effects are contingent
on conservative corruption and scoring choices (only 53 problems were noised, in a straightforward way), and could be pushed back simply
by increasing corruption density or requiring stronger justifications. (As the results show, accuracy drops for all models
when evaluation is restricted to the set of incorrect proofs.) Accordingly,
PL3-PC
is not quite solved at present
and should instead be viewed as a scalable diagnostic test of verifier-side semantic reasoning (based on entailment rather
than inference rules).

Figure 32:
PL3-PC
accuracy, excluding
De Bruijn
problems, as a function of proof length for the top 12 models.

8
A Quantitative Measure of Epistemic Stability

When a rational agent
\alpha
asserts a proposition, they make themselves accountable
for what others can infer from it. These inferences may be made by pure deduction, but
more often they are made by commonplace inferential practices in the shared space of norms
that govern the use of the relevant concepts.
We might say that, by asserting
p
,
\alpha
becomes committed to additional propositions
q_{1},q_{2},\ldots

that represent
epistemic obligations
, meaning that
\alpha
cannot deny any
q_{i}
on pain of
epistemic incoherence. One of the
q_{i}
is
p
itself, and in that case the
epistemic obligation reduces to familiar self-consistency: If
\alpha
asserts
p
, then
\alpha

cannot also deny
p
(or assert
\neg p
).
82
82

82

Non-monotonic reasoning, of course, is
commonplace—people often change their minds. But typically they do so upon receiving new information,
at which point they might retract
p
. They do not maintain both
p
and
\neg p
at
the same time.

But there are many other epistemic commitments.
If we claim
p=\mbox{Paris is the capital of France}
, we commit ourselves,
among other things, to the propositions that France is a sovereign country and that
Paris is a city in France. Unless we willfully aimed to be uncooperative
communicators, it would be incoherent to deny these facts while maintaining
p
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be any problem that may be meaningfully posed
to a model
M
. We distinguish between the general problem
\mathcal{P}

and particular
instances
of
\mathcal{P}
.
83
83

83

The general problem can be understood
as the overall specification of the task (“Given the name of a country, respond with its population and area in square kilometers.”),
while an instance of the problem is a specific input to the task (“France”).

We assume there is a prompt associated with
\mathcal{P}
and we write
\pi(\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$})
for the
core part of that prompt, namely, the input-insensitive part that gives the general specification of
\mathcal{P}
.
Even if two problems
\mathcal{P}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
involve the same task, they may
be formulated in incompatible ways. For instance,
\pi(\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$})
and
\pi(\mbox{\mbox{$\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}})
may assume different epistemic postures
or operate within different epistemic contexts,
e.g.
,
\pi(\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$})
may be aimed towards truth or factual coherence whereas

\pi(\mbox{\mbox{$\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}})
may be sarcastic or adversarial, or both may be aimed towards truth but

\pi(\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$})
may be operating within the context of Euclidean geometry whereas

\pi(\mbox{\mbox{$\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}})
may adopt hyperbolic geometry.
We say that
\mathcal{P}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
are
compatible
iff no presupposition or instruction of
\pi(\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$})

contradicts one of
\pi(\mbox{\mbox{$\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}})
, and neither prompt provides new information to
M

that is not also provided by the other.
84
84

84

By “new” information
we mean information that did not appear in
M
’s pretraining corpus.

This is a symmetric relation.

We write
M(x,y)
to mean that given
x
as input, model
M
has generated
y
as the output;
and we refer to
M(x,y)
as a
judgment

j
. Now let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
be two compatible problems and
let
x
and
x^{\prime}
be two instances of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
, respectively.
We write
M(x,y)\mbox{$\>\mbox{$\vdash$}_{{N}}\>$}M(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

and say that
M(x,y)
epistemically
constrains

M(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

iff
M
’s generation of
y
as the answer to
x
creates an epistemic obligation for

M
to generate
y^{\prime}
as the answer to
x^{\prime}
, because not doing so
would be epistemically incoherent. We call this a
positive
epistemic obligation.
Suppose
\mathcal{P}
poses geography questions under a truthful system prompt
\pi(\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$})
,
instance
x
asks for the capital of France, and
M
has generated the expected answer, Paris,
as the output
y
. Suppose further that
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
asks multiple-choice trivia questions,
also under a truthful system prompt, and that
x^{\prime}
asks whether Paris is a city in
France, Portugal, or Venezuela. Then
M
would be normatively bound to
generate “France” as the answer
y^{\prime}
. We take
\mbox{$\vdash$}_{{N}}
as a primitive relation
for present purposes.

A
negative
epistemic obligation, written as
M(x,y)\>\not{\hskip-0.50615pt\mbox{$\mbox{$\vdash$}_{{N}}$}}\>M(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
, obtains iff

M
’s generation of
y
as the answer to
x
precludes it from generating
y^{\prime}
as the answer to
x^{\prime}
.
Typically, a positive epistemic obligation
creates a number of trivial negative epistemic obligations,
e.g.
, if
M

generates “Paris” as the answer to question
x
above, then it is
negatively bound from generating Portugal or Venezuela or any other country as the answer
to
x^{\prime}
. But the converse may not be true: a negative constraint from
\mathcal{P}
to

\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
need not impose positive epistemic constraints on
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
. For instance,
let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
be the proof-checking and proof-writing problems, respectively.
If
M
claims that a proof
D
is incorrect, it is negatively bound from generating
D
as the answer
to the corresponding instance of
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
, but this does not impose any positive
obligations on
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
, as
M
is free to generate other proofs for the relevant problem.
In the converse direction, however, we have positive epistemic obligations: If
M
generates
D

as the answer to an instance of
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
,
then
M
is epistemically obliged to answer
yes
to the instance of
\mathcal{P}
that asks whether

D
is a correct deduction.
85
85

85

Modulo answer variation due to stochasticity.
We assume here that all judgments are robust, i.e.
M(x,y)
means that
y
is a stable mode
of
M
’s conditional distribution over outputs given
x
. Note, however, that in
mathematical tasks, persistent answer variation when given one and the same problem instance
is itself a sign of weak reasoning, indicating that the model doesn’t have a stable internal
representation of the solution space.

Now fix an empirical distribution
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
of
n
observed input-output pairs
\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{m},y_{m})\}
,
where each
x_{i}
is an instance of
\mathcal{P}
and
M(x_{i},y_{i})
;
and a corresponding distribution
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}=\{(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(x_{n}^{\prime},y_{n}^{\prime})\}

where each
x_{j}^{\prime}
is an instance of a compatible problem
\mbox{$\mathcal{P}$}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
and
M(x_{j}^{\prime},y_{j}^{\prime})
.
It is notationally convenient to view
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
as a distribution of judgments

M(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,M(x_{m},y_{m})
, and likewise for
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
. Define the set

\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]
as follows:

\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]=\{j^{\prime}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}\mbox{$\;|\;$}\exists\>j\in\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}\mbox{$\ .\ $}j\,\not{\hskip-0.50615pt\mbox{$\mbox{$\vdash$}_{{N}}$}}\>\mbox{\mbox{$j^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}\}.

(13)

In words, this set contains all judgments in
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
that are precluded (negatively constrained) by, or are
incompatible with
,
some judgment in
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
.
We can quantify the degree of
M
’s epistemic stability relative to these two distributions
simply by computing the size of this set, dividing it by
n
(the size of
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
),
and subtracting the result from 1. This gives us a measure of
M
’s epistemic stability
wrt to
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
, written as
\mbox{ESI\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]
.
We drop the subscripts
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1},\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
when they are immaterial or
obvious from the context.
We refer to this quantity,
1-(\mbox{$|\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]|$}/n)
,
as the
epistemic stability index
of
M
relative to
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
.
Thus,
ESI
ranges smoothly from 0 to 1, with higher values obviously being preferable.

The ratio
\mbox{$|\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]|$}/n
has a natural probabilistic interpretation,
as the empirical probability that a randomly chosen judgment from
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
is incompatible with some
judgment in
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
. Accordingly, 1 minus this ratio is the probability of the complementary
event, namely, that a randomly chosen judgment from
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
is compatible with

all
judgments in
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
. We want this number to be as close to 1 as possible.

We augment this intuition with a soft log-based transformation that adds curvature reflecting
nonlinear penalties for judgment inconsistencies, as well as diminishing returns from such penalties:

\mbox{ESI\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]=1-\cfrac{\log\biggl[1+\gamma\cdot\frac{\mbox{$|\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]|$}}{\mbox{$|\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}|$}}\biggr]}{\log\bigl[1+\gamma\bigr]}

(14)

We still have
\mbox{ESI\/}=1
when
\mbox{$|\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]|$}=0
(perfect epistemic stability) and

\mbox{ESI\/}=0
when
\mbox{$|\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]|$}=\mbox{$|\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}|$}
(complete incoherence),
but the penalty for relatively small
|\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]|

grows faster than in the linear case, accentuating even small amounts of instability,
while saturating smoothly towards 0 as
\mbox{$|\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]|$}/\mbox{$|\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}|$}\to 1
.
The parameter
\gamma
controls the curvature and can be tuned depending
on how stringent we want to be; we use
\gamma=5
as a reasonable default.
Figure
33
shows different ESI curves for
\gamma\in\{2,4,6,10\}
.

Figure 33:
ESI curves for different values of the curvature parameter (
\gamma
). The
x
-axis is
the fraction
|\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]|
/
n
.

We assume that
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
is not chosen adversarially, with prior knowledge of the constraints in
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
.
Otherwise it would be possible to cherry-pick data so as to compute any desired ESI value. To avoid
metric gaming, the selection of
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
must be
independent
of
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
,
and in particular must not occur after inspecting the judgments in
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
.
Methodologically, this requirement is somewhat analogous to the familiar principle that test data
must not be tuned to a model’s performance on the training set. While this independence condition
can be made fully precise (for example, by fixing the generation process for
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
in advance,
or by requiring
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
to be sampled from a distribution specified independently of
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
),
we do not need to formalize it here. For present purposes, it suffices to note that allowing
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}

to be selected in response to observed judgments in
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
would undermine the intended interpretation
of this stability measure.

As a concrete application of
ESI
, consider the results of Section
7.5
and let
M

be any of the models in Table
15
. We may take
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
to be the set
of 300 proofs written by
M
in response to the
PL3-PW
prompt, or more precisely, the set
(x,y)

where
x
is an input prompt from the
PL3-PW
dataset and
y
is the corresponding output
proof generated by
M
. Now consider the set
S
of all individual steps
p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{from}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}

that appear in any of those 300 proofs written by
M
(
S
will have thousands of elements),
and let
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
be the set of all entailment/SAT judgments made by
M
for each of those steps.
In other words,
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
consists of all pairs
(x,y)
, one for every individual step
p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{from}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
in
S
,
where
x
is an input prompt asking whether
p
follows from
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, along perhaps with an estimate
of the difficulty of making that judgment, and
y
is
M
’s response.

We can then use the data in Table
15
to compute
\mbox{ESI\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]
.
The number of self-recognized errors becomes the size of the set

\mbox{Inc\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]
,
i.e.
, the set of all
M
-written proofs that are precluded
by some
M
-made entailment judgment in
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
. Plugging in the numbers from Table
15
into
the defining formula (
14
) yields the values shown in Table
18
.
The results show that all top-tier models suffer from severe epistemic instability.
Even for the most stable model, GPT-5.2, there is a nearly 40% chance that a randomly chosen
PL3-PW

proof written by it will be inconsistent with its own relevant entailment judgments. More than half of these elite models
have ESI values less than 0.5, meaning that any proof written by such a model is more likely than not to
disagree with the model’s corresponding entailment judgments.

Model

ESI

Model

ESI

gemini-2.5-pro

0.27

gpt-5

0.27

gpt-5.1

0.38

gemini-3.0-pro-preview

0.41

claude-opus-4-5-20251101

0.48

grok-4

0.52

o3

0.53

gemini-3.1-pro-preview

0.61

gpt-5.2

0.62

Table 18:
Epistemic stability values for
PL3-PW
proofs relative to individual entailment judgments for the 9 models of Table
15
.
A model’s
ESI
value can be understood as the probability that a randomly chosen
PL3-PW
proof written by the model is consistent with
the model’s individual entailment judgments. Ideally, all of these values would be 1.

At its core,
ESI
is a fraction, and therefore it is inherently asymmetric.
Disregarding the log transformation, it is essentially the probability that a random draw from
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
is not precluded
by any draw from
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
. We are thus normalizing by
|\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}|
, not by anything symmetric
across both distributions. This is to be expected, because
\mbox{ESI\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]

and
\mbox{ESI\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}]
measure different qualitative phenomena; each has a different
unit of analysis (the objects in
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
, respectively).
For instance, with
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
as defined in this example,

\mbox{ESI\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}]
is a
proof-level
score, whereby a proof in

\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
is incompatible with
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
iff it contains at least one step that diverges in
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
.
So in this case the
ESI
metric serves as an “all-steps-must-pass” aggregator.
If we flip
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
, then
\mbox{ESI\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}]
would
become a
step-level
score that would address a very different question: what fraction
of the model’s entailment judgments survive contact with the corresponding proofs?
Both quantities may be of interest, but no matter what
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}
are,

any
low values for
\mbox{ESI\/}_{\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}}[\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}]
are a source of concern.

In general, epistemic stability is not an absolute scalar property. It is defined with respect
to a reference distribution, for which there is no universally correct choice.
Definition (
14
) is general, but genuinely different stability concepts can be
singled out by instantiating the distributions in different ways. For example, above we took
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{2}

to be the set of all 300
PL3-PW
outputs by
M
, but we could instead consider the subset comprising
only the
incorrect
outputs generated by
M
(while leaving
\mbox{$\mathcal{D}$}_{1}
unchanged).
Doing so would pick a different measure of epistemic stability, one that we might call

error-triggered

ESI
, or
\mbox{ESI\/}^{\,\mbox{ET\/}}
for short.
(We do not report
\mbox{ESI\/}^{\,\mbox{ET\/}}
values here for
PL3-PW
, but
it suffices to note that they are very low,
e.g.
, 0.14 for Gemini 3.1 and 0.38 for GPT-5.2.)
The first version of
ESI
can be viewed as a global or generic measure of epistemic stability, while the second
is conditioned on errors. The difference between the two is roughly analogous to the difference between
blood pressure at rest vs blood pressure under stress. They measure two different aspects of epistemic
stability. An agent with high error-triggered stability makes errors that are part of a stable
internal theory, even if that theory is wrong. In other words, the agent may be wrong
but they are wrong in a unified
and stable way. This is common for humans (disregarding trivial performance errors).
By contrast, low error-triggered stability means that the agent’s mistakes are not stably
believed, arising instead from breakdowns in deployment, control, or integration.
This appears to be common for language models, which often have the competence to make the right local
judgments (such as micro-SAT entailments) but fail to recruit that competence during more complex
and global tasks (such as proof writing).

It may be noted that epistemic stability is not unequivocally desirable.
In particular, the capacity for self-correction and epistemic stability appear to pull in opposite directions.
Low stability values indicate poor integration of different types of competence,
but they also suggest that the model possesses latent knowledge that can be recruited by external scaffolding
or iterative self-checking and verification. High stability values, by contrast, indicate
that the model’s mistakes are part of a stable internal reasoning pattern and are thus less
amenable to agentic self-correction. So there seems to be a tradeoff: low epistemic stability is bad for unified
reasoning but good for self-aided corrigibility, while high epistemic stability is good for unified
and internally stable reasoning but not for self-repair. While this tension is generally real,
in practice epistemic stability is the more valuable primitive; we need systems that are stable,
self-consistent, and predictable across contexts. At any rate, the tension is only with self-correction, not
with correction in general. From an engineering perspective, one does not need a single model to,
e.g.
,
both generate and verify. Corrections can be made by external verifiers or critic agents
rather than by relying on the same model to repair its own inconsistencies.

9
\textsc{PL}_{4}

9.1
Proof Writing in Hilbert Systems

This division contains 250 proof-writing problems in the Hilbert calculus for propositional logic.
Because writing proofs in a Hilbert system is challenging, we have split this division into two tasks:

1.

the main
PL4-PW
task, consisting of 200 problems that are shorter and easier on average than
PL1-PW
items;

2.

a challenge set,
PL4-PW-c
, consisting of 50 problems randomly sampled from longer
PL1-PW
problems.

The Hilbert calculus is a flat proof system that does not admit conditional subproofs: a
proof is just a list of formulas, each of which is an instance of an axiom schema or
else follows from previous steps by a single inference rule, modus ponens.
This flat linear structure makes Hilbert systems primarily of metatheoretical
interest (e.g., for studying soundness and completeness),
because the lack of assumption scope and the pared-down rule set
make it easier to prove theorems about these systems
than within them. But for benchmarking purposes,
these same factors make Hilbert proofs substantially harder
to write than proofs in natural deduction.

Formulas here are defined exactly as in NDL, built up inductively from
propositional atoms and logical connectives. In the variant we use, which is the most
common formulation of the Hilbert calculus, the only two primitive connectives are
negation and implication. Disjunctions, conditionals, and biconditionals are desugared
using only these two connectives.
In particular,
p\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}q
is written as
\neg p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q
,
p\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}q
is written

\neg(\neg p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg q)
, and
p\mbox{$\>\Leftrightarrow\,$}q
as
\neg((p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg(q\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}p))
.

Conditional elimination (modus ponens, also known as “detachment”) is the only inference rule;
it derives
q
from
p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q
and
p
. In addition, any proof step may infer any instance
of an axiom schema (a logical formula template). For example, Axiom 1 of the calculus
is
(p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(q\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}p))
, where
p
and
q
are metavariables ranging over arbitrary formulas.
Any formula that
matches
this template is an axiom, such as
(A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A))
or

(\neg A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}((B\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}C)\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg A))
.

The standard version of the Hilbert calculus that we use formalizes a classical logic of implication
and negation with only three propositional axiom schemas:
86
86

86

The first two axiom schemas are
known as
K
and
S
, respectively, as they correspond precisely to the
K
and
S
combinators
of combinatory logic
[
45
]
, namely
\lambda\,x\mbox{$\ .\ $}\lambda\,y\mbox{$\ .\ $}x

and
\lambda\,f\mbox{$\ .\ $}\lambda\,g\mbox{$\ .\ $}\lambda\,x\mbox{$\ .\ $}(f\>x)\,(g\>x)
. To see the correspondence
of the latter with Axiom 2, read
f
as a function of type
p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(q\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}r)
,
g
as a function of type

p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q
, and
x
as a variable of type
p
.
We then we have
(f\>x)\hskip-2.168pt:\hskip-0.72229ptq\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}r
and
(g\>x)\hskip-2.168pt:\hskip-0.72229ptq
, so that
(f\>x)\,(g\>x)
is of type

r
, corresponding to the tail occurrence of
r
in Axiom 2.
Dropping Axiom 3 and keeping only K and S gives rise to the classical logic of minimal implication.

•

Axiom 1:
(p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(q\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}p))

•

Axiom 2:
(p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(q\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}r))\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}((p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}r))

•

Axiom 3:
(\neg p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg q)\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}((\neg p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}p)

A derivation of a target tautology
p

is a sequence of formulas
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}=p
, where each
p_{i}
is
an axiom instance or else follows by modus ponens from two earlier formulas.
This format is sufficient in principle, but, for convenience, we name each line
and provide a justification either by citing an axiom or by listing the arguments to
modus ponens. For example, the following is a proof produced by o4-mini for the
tautology
((\neg A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A)\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}A)
. Each axiom is implemented as a primitive method
in Athena
[
6
, p. 103]
, which matches the input sentence to the respective
axiom schema or reports that no match is possible. In this way the proof becomes
fully verifiable using Athena.

We also permit the use of premises, treating them essentially as axioms.
As a result, we can pose problems of the form “prove that
p
follows from
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
”
in addition to pure-tautology problems of the form “prove
p_{1}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\cdots\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}p_{n}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}p
.”
This is an important change that allows models to perform suppositional reasoning and
emulate natural deduction, yielding a flexible knob controlling problem
difficulty. As we explain later, it also enables some interesting experiments
gauging the ability of models to simplify proof construction in this system
by intelligently exploiting the famous deduction
theorem
[
62
]
[p. 28].
87
87

87

The deduction theorem,
independently discovered by
89
and
44
,
states that if there is a Hilbert proof of
p

from premises
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},p_{n+1}\}
, then there is a Hilbert proof of
(p_{n+1}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}p)
from

\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
.

PL4-PW
, the (easy) 200-problem task, was constructed as follows:
(a) 20 problems were hand-picked simple tautologies that appear as standard
textbook exercises, such as
(A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(\neg A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}B))
,

(A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg\neg A)
, and
(A\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg A)
; and (b) 180 randomly selected
problems from the same distribution from which
PL1-PW
problems were sampled.
We targeted short problems (mean length 46 characters) to keep the task feasible.
Any problem using logical connectives other than negation and implication was
converted to an equivalent formula using only negation and implication, as described above.
Only 15 out of the 200 problems are pure tautologies; the rest have at least one premise.

The size distributions for problems in
PL4-PW
and
PL4-PW-c
, measured as the combined
AST sizes of the original premises and goals, are shown in Figure
34
.
Table
19
summarizes the mean and max numbers
of atoms, negations, conjunctions, disjunctions, conditionals, and biconditionals
in these datasets.

9.2
PL4-PW
Results

We evaluated model performance under two settings: (1) a strict setting in which the model is
required to produce a proof that can be transformed into an Athena-verifiable
proof without any post-processing;
and (2) a lenient setting in which we apply the following
limited programmatic corrections to superficial syntax issues and certain formatting errors
in applications of modus ponens:

•

We use a parsing algorithm that can repair certain formulas with unbalanced parentheses
by adding or removing parentheses at the right end of the formula to restore balance;
this recovers some formulas that would otherwise be rejected by the Athena parser.

•

We allow different parenthesization styles, including minimal parenthesization
where connective application is inferred according to Athena’s precedence and associativity rules.

•

We repair missing or superfluous semicolons and other simple syntax errors that
are readily fixable by programmatic search-and-replace.

•

We correct flipped argument order in applications of detachment when the swap
yields a valid rule application.

The above can be seen as a limited amount of proof-checking instrumentation (compared to the
more extensive interventions in NDL).

PL4-PW
results for both modes are shown
in Table
20
. Gemini 3.1 is in a class of its own in this task, far ahead of all other models.

9.2.1
Error Analysis

Errors in this task are grouped into the following categories:

•

Failed MP
: The model applied detachment incorrectly.

•

Failed Axiom
: The model used an axiom improperly. These errors
are essentially axiom hallucinations, where the model falsely claims that a sentence is an instance of an axiom.

•

Undefined/Hallucinated Intermediate Step name
: This occurs when a intermediate-step name
p_{i}
is quoted
in an application of modus ponens without being introduced in a previous step.

•

Wrong Conclusion
: The proof reaches a conclusion other than the goal.

•

No Proof
: The model did not generate a proof. This occurs in two ways: either the
model fails to generate any response after multiple API calls
for a given problem, or the model returns a response that does not contain a proof.
(This is especially common for o3, occurring in 102 out of the 200 problems.)

•

Syntax Error
: The model returned a proof with a syntax error that our automated repairs could not fix.

Figure 35:
The distribution of error types in
PL4-PW
under lenient proof checking, for the first error that caused a proof failure (or the failure to return a proof).

The distribution of these errors in
PL4-PW
(under
lenient proof checking) is shown in Figure
35
.

Incorrect applications of modus ponens in
PL4-PW
fall into one of three types:

1.

the first argument is not a conditional formula (a type error of sorts);

2.

the rule is applied to two formulas
p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q
and
r
where
r\neq p
(also a type error); and

3.

at least one of the two arguments is not in the assumption base (a strictly logical error).

Most failed-mp errors made by the strongest models are of type 2.

9.2.2
Proof Length Analysis

Success rates in this task correlate strongly both with problem size and with the length of the
generated proofs. Figure
36
shows model accuracies as a function of problem size,
defined as the total AST size of the premises and goal. Performance degrades rapidly as problem size increases,
even though problems in this task are quite shorter than those in
PL1-PW
.

Successful proofs for this task are generally short.
The shortest proofs for the vast majority of solved problems have fewer than 30 lines.

Figure 36:
PL4-PW
accuracy as a function of problem size.

Statistics on the lengths of all proofs written by all models for
PL4-PW
,
similar to those in tasks like
PL2-PW
, are shown in Figure
37
.
For each model
M
, the figure shows the distribution of length over the
set of all proofs written by
M
(blue), as well as the distribution of length over the set of
all correct proofs written by
M
(green). Again, for most models the green distributions (correct proofs only)
are narrower and shifted left compared to the blue distributions (all proofs),
again showing that correct proofs tend to be shorter.
However, some strong models (
gpt-5.2
,
gpt-5.1
,
gpt-5
, and even
grok-4
,
deepseek-reasoner
, and
o3
)
generate correct proofs of higher mean length than that of all their proofs.
Several models write extremely long incorrect proofs (Gemini 2.5 produced the longest, a proof
with 2045 lines), suggesting that these models may generate long, verbose proof attempts when
they are unable to solve a problem.

Figure 37:
PL4-PW
proof-size distributions per model: all proofs (blue) vs correct proofs (green); min–max line, 5–95% band, IQR box, median tick, mean dot.

We also compared a set of strong models in terms of the length of their successful proofs,
by calculating the average proof length across the problems that all of these models managed to solve (under lenient settings):

gpt-5.2
,
grok-4
,
gpt-5.1
,
gemini-3.0-pro-preview
,
deepseek-reasoner
,
gemini-2.5-pro
,
claude-opus-4-5-20251101
, and
claude-sonnet-4.5-ext-thinking
.
We found that
gpt-5
produced the shortest proofs across the 17 problems that all of these models solved,
with an average proof length of 8.4 steps, while
gemini-2.5-pro
produced the longest proofs,
with an average of 9.4 steps. Remarkably, most models found a proof
of the same length for each problem, suggesting that the models converged on the same proof.

Proof length ratio analysis on
\textsc{PL}_{4}
-PW problems of moderate difficulty
(problems with
\geq 3

For each model we show mean
\ell/\ell_{\min}
ratios, where

and

To further investigate the relationship between proof length and correctness, we computed the average proof length ratio of correct vs. incorrect proofs
for each model as follows. We first identified all problems for which at least 3 models succeeded and at least 3 models failed, where the failures
were required to be due to errors in the proof rather than failures to return a proof. For each of these problems, we computed the shortest correct
proof length and ranked models according to their average ratio of correct proof lengths to shortest correct proof lengths. We then
computed the average ratio of incorrect proof lengths to shortest correct proof lengths for each model across these problems.
The results are shown in Table
23
. Note that neither
gemini-3.1-pro-preview
nor
gpt-5.2
is included because they did not have
at least 3 incorrect proofs among these problems, and a number of weaker models are not included because they did not have at least 3 correct proofs
among these problems. As a general trend, most models tend to write correct proofs that are similar in length
to the shortest correct proof, with the exception of
gpt-5.1
and
grok-4
, which tend to write longer correct proofs.
Among incorrect proofs, some models tend to write much shorter proofs.
Other models tend to write much longer proofs, with
gemini-3.0-pro-preview
having the largest ratios;
it could be said that these models tend to persist in proof writing even
when they “know” that the proof is not succeeding.

As first noted in connection with Table
22
, we observed that models
usually return proofs of identical length for the same problem.
This is often an indication that the models have converged on the same proof.
To further investigate this, we computed the pairwise similarity between model proofs
as follows. First, we defined the “fingerprint” of a proof as the counts of each
axiom and applications of detachment in the proof.
According to this measure, two proofs are identical iff they have the same length,
the same counts of each axiom application, and the same number of detachment applications.
Second, we defined the “sequence” of a proof as the order in which the axioms
and detachment applications appear in the proof. According to this measure,
the proofs must have identical fingerprints and, additionally, the same precise
order of application of the axioms and detachment.

Figure
38
shows pairwise similarity
heatmaps according to the fingerprint and sequence measures for
PL4-PW
.
For each pair of models,
fingerprint similarity
is the percentage of commonly
solved problems for which the two successful proofs have the same fingerprint (counts
of each axiom and detachment applications), while
sequence similarity
additionally
requires the same order of axiom and detachment applications.
We include a model in the heatmap only if it shares at least 10 commonly solved problems
with every other included model (models are ordered by their performance for inclusion,
although this ordering does not affect the final inclusion list).
As the heatmaps show, the models demonstrate a remarkable degree of convergence to essentially the same proofs.
Proofs that match in fingerprint are often identical up to some reordering of the applications of different axioms and MP.

Figure 38:
Pairwise proof similarity between models on
\textsc{PL}_{4}
-PW problems.
Models are grouped by hierarchical clustering based on fingerprint similarity.
Yellow indicates higher similarity; dark blue indicates lower similarity.

The results of this section show that even when the underlying problems are unchanged
from
PL1-PW
(and are, in fact, drawn from the easier end of that distribution),
changing the proof formalism to one that does not mirror human reasoning
increases the level of proof-writing difficulty by an order of magnitude.

PL4-PW
thus stands apart from other propositional-logic tasks in
ProofGrid
,
all of which are rooted in natural deduction. Writing Hilbert proofs does not test human-style reasoning.
Instead, it probes the ability to compile proofs into a very sparse calculus that is essentially
a machine language for deduction. It places much higher pressure on symbolic manipulation discipline
than on high-level semantic reasoning about the problem.
As such,
\textsc{PL}_{4}
is complementary to the other
ProofGrid
divisions, providing a compelling additional
axis: proof-writing skill under a deliberately unforgiving and syntactically brittle formalism.

However, there is an interesting direction for future work that would go beyond raw symbolic muscle
to test human-like meta-reasoning: study whether models are able to exploit the deduction theorem
in Hilbert systems, instead of attempting every proof directly from the 3 axioms. For example,
to prove implication transitivity,
(A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}B)\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C)\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}(A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C)
,
a human would typically not search for a pure (premise-free) Hilbert derivation from first principles.
Instead, they would first derive
C
from the temporary assumptions
A
,
A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}B
, and
B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C
,
and then repeatedly apply the deduction theorem to discharge these assumptions and obtain a pure Hilbert
proof of the conditionalized tautology. Since this discharge process is constructive (and can be carried
out by a simple algorithm), it suggests a natural controlled experiment: compare model performance on
the premise-based Hilbert proofs in
PL4-PW
with their performance on the corresponding
conditionalized (premise-free) versions. This would address the question of whether a model can solve
a conditionalized Hilbert problem by first solving the easier premise-based version and then mechanically
compiling the solution into a pure Hilbert proof. A model that can operationalize the deduction
theorem in roughly the same way a human reasoner would is expected to degrade only moderately between
the two settings, whereas a sharp drop would suggest that it is not leveraging a natural meta-level
proof compilation strategy. Although we do not pursue this experiment here, it points to a promising
way in which Hilbert systems may probe not just low-level proof synthesis but also strategic reasoning about proofs.

9.3
Equational Logic

Equational reasoning sits at the heart of mathematics, and by extension, of science
and engineering. Work in all of these fields routinely involves equations between apparently
different expressions that refer to the same entity. Engineers use equational
reasoning to show that two circuit designs are functionally identical,
physicists to track conserved quantities, and compiler engineers to verify that
back-end optimizations yield identical program semantics.
In computer science, the use of rewrite rules to reason about equations constitutes
a Turing-complete model of computation, forming the foundation of functional programming
and playing a key role in program verification. Any model aspiring to master these domains
must internalize equational reasoning as a core competency.

Equational reasoning offers a particularly attractive testing ground for AI models
because it is almost entirely language-independent. Proofs do not need to rely on the specialized
syntactic rules of any particular formal system, but can instead be written as simple sequences of steps
where each step is just a term annotated with the equational axioms used to derive it from
the preceding term.
88
88

88

Of course, the terms must still be written in formal notation, such as

\mbox{plus\/}(\mbox{succ\/}(X),\mbox{times\/}(a,\mbox{zero\/}))
. But this requires nothing more than
the standard convention of a function symbol followed by parentheses enclosing comma-separated arguments.
This notation is both universal and simple, and is already extensively represented in the generic
corpora on which language models are pretrained.

Such representations are both rigorous and machine-checkable, while avoiding the burden of ensuring adherence
to the idiosyncrasies of a formal language. Despite this structural simplicity, equational reasoning
is remarkably rich and powerful, capable of encoding subtle and sophisticated arguments. Indeed,
in the general case equational reasoning is undecidable, which underscores that the expressive reach
of this style of reasoning is more than adequate for probing the limits of AI models.

Reasoning about chains of identities requires both domain-independent syntactic skill (carrying out manipulations correctly)
and domain-specific semantic insight (grasping the meaning of equivalence in a particular theory).
In this paper we focus on the former. Using an uninterpreted signature, we introduce
three tasks that evaluate a model’s general ability to recognize and apply identity transformations.
Because complex terms are inherently structured as recursive syntax trees, these tasks also
probe a model’s ability to process hierarchically organized data with rigor and precision.

Specifically, we introduce
\textsc{EQ}_{1}
, a dataset containing
1K purely equational (unconditional) proofs. Each example
can be viewed as a pair that includes two main components:

•

a set of equational axioms
\{E_{1},\ldots,E_{m}\}
, where, for easy reference, all
m
equational axioms are
given unique names:
E_{1},\ldots,E_{m}
; and

•

a derivation of an identity
s=t
from those axioms. The structure of derivations is described below.

Each axiom is of the form

\forall\>x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\mbox{$\ .\ $}l=r
,
where the
x_{i}
are variables and
l
,
r
are standard first-order terms built from variables, constants,
and function symbols of nonzero arity,
e.g.
,
g(a,f(Y))
.
Variable names start with upper-case letters, while function symbols and constants start with lower-case letters.
We use
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
and
e
as constants.
Unary function symbols are
f,f_{1},\ldots
; binary symbols are
g,g_{1},g_{2},\ldots
;
ternary symbols are
h,h_{1},\ldots
; and quaternary function symbols (of arity 4) are
r,r1,\ldots
.
We don’t use any function symbols with arity greater than 4.

If
op
is a function symbol of arity
n
, the abstract syntactic structure of a term of the
form
\mbox{op\/}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
is captured by a
tree
with the function symbol
op
at the
root and with the trees corresponding to
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}
as its children, ordered from left to right.
Constants and variables always appear at the leaves. Viewing terms as trees in this way,
positions

within a term are represented using Dewey notation. Thus, if
t=g(f(a),g(X,f_{2}(Y)))
, position

[]
corresponds to the subterm of
t
at the root (
i.e.
,
t
itself), position
[1]
corresponds
to
f(a)
,
[1,1]
corresponds to the constant (leaf)
a
,
[2,1]
corresponds to
X
,
[2,2]
to
f_{2}(Y)
,
and so on. For a term
t
and valid position
p
in
t
,
\mbox{sub\/}(t,p)
denotes the subterm of
t
at
p
.
We write
\mbox{Dom}\hskip 0.43338pt(t)
for the set of all valid positions in
t
.

A substitution
\theta
is a function from variables to terms,
e.g.
,
\theta=\{X\mapsto f(a,Y),Z\mapsto b\}
.
We write
\theta(t)
to denote the result of
applying

\theta
to a term
t
, namely, the term obtained
from
t
by replacing every variable
v
in the domain of
\theta
with the term
\theta(v)
. Thus, for the given
example,
\theta(g(Y,g(X,Z)))=g(Y,g(f(a,Y),b))
. We say that a term

t

matches
a term
s
, or that
t
is an
instance
of
s
, iff there exists a substitution
\theta
such that
t=\theta(s)
, in which case we say
that
t
matches
s
“under”
\theta
(or wrt
\theta
).

A proof of an identity
s=t
from a set of equational axioms
\mathcal{E}

is a sequence of
n>0
steps of the following form:

s

=

s_{0}

=

s_{1}

by

\cdots

=

s_{2}

by

\cdots

\vdots

\vdots

\vdots

=

s_{n}

by

\cdots

where
s_{n}
is syntactically identical to
t
. Such a proof can be understood as a chain of identities
leading from
s
to
t
. Each step is justified by citing one or more of the axioms in
\mathcal{E}
, listed after the
by
keyword.
The following is a sample equational proof of 5 steps that derives the identity
g(h(c,a,c),h(a,c,a))=g(g_{1}(g_{4}(f_{1}(f_{5}(f_{3}(a))),d),e),g_{2}(a,c))

from a set of 6 equational axioms. (Universal quantifiers are omitted when listing the equational axioms; all variables are assumed to be universally quantified.)

⬇

1

E1:

h(c,V1170,c)

=

h(V1170,a,f4(f1(c)))

2

E2:

h(V1173,V1174,V1173)

=

g2(V1173,V1174)

3

E3:

h(a,V1181,f4(f1(c)))

=

g1(f2(f4(V1181)),f4(e))

4

E4:

f2(f4(V1184))

=

g(f3(V1184),f2(e))

5

E5:

g(V1187,f2(e))

=

g4(f1(f5(V1187)),d)

6

E6:

f4(V1188)

=

V1188

-

Proof:

s

=

g(h(c,a,c),h(a,c,a))

#

step

0

11

g(h(a,a,f4(f1(c))),h(a,c,a))

by

E1

#

step

1

12

g(h(a,a,f4(f1(c))),g2(a,c))

by

E2

#

3

13

g(g1(f2(f4(a)),f4(e)),g2(a,c))

by

E3

#

step

g(g1(g(f3(a),f2(e)),f4(e)),g2(a,c))

#

A proof is correct (or
sound
) iff for every step
s_{i}=s_{i+1}\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{by}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}E_{i_{1}},\ldots,E_{i_{k}}
, the identity
s_{i}=s_{i+1}

follows logically from
E_{i_{1}},\ldots,E_{i_{k}}
. By Birkhoff’s seminal result
[
13
]
, this amounts to the identity
s_{i}=s_{i+1}
being
derivable from the cited equations using the inference rules of equational
logic.
89
89

89

There are various options for which equational inference rules to take as primitive,
but a standard choice consists of reflexivity, symmetry, transitivity, and congruence (also known as substitution of equals).

A more algorithmic characterization of this derivability relation is based on the notion of
rewriting
. Given an equation
e
of the form
l=r
,
we write
s\longrightarrow_{e}t
to mean that
s
has a subterm at some position
p
such that
\mbox{sub\/}(s,p)
matches
l
wrt to a substitution
\theta

and
t
is identical to
s
except that
\mbox{sub\/}(t,p)=\theta(r)
. In words,
t
is obtained from
s
by replacing the subterm
\mbox{sub\/}(s,p)

that matches the left-hand side of
e
under
\theta
with the corresponding instance of the right-hand side
r
,
i.e.
,
\theta(r)
.
We refer to
\mbox{sub\/}(s,p)
as a
redex
for
e
(or an “
e
-redex”) and to
\mbox{sub\/}(t,p)=\theta(r)
as the
corresponding
contractum
. If
s\longrightarrow_{e}t
, we say that
s

rewrites
or
reduces
to
t
in one step.
For a set of equations
\mathcal{E}
, we write
s\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}t
to mean that
s\longrightarrow_{e}t
for some
e\in\mathcal{E}
.
We write
\longleftrightarrow_{e}
and
\longleftrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}
for the symmetric closures of
\longrightarrow_{e}
and
\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}
,
respectively, and
\longleftrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{E}}
for the reflexive, symmetric, and transitive closure of
\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{E}}
.
As Birkhoff first noted
[
13
]
,
s=t
is provable from a set of equations
\mathcal{S}
using the inference rules of equational logic
(and thus, by Birkhoff’s completeness theorem,
s=t
is a logical consequence of
\mathcal{S}
) iff
s\;\mbox{$\longleftrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\;t
.
In our case, a proof step
s_{i}=s_{i+1}\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{by}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}E_{i_{1}},\ldots,E_{i_{k}}
is correct iff the identity
s_{i}=s_{i+1}
follows from
the set of equations
\mathcal{S}=\{E_{i_{1}},\ldots,E_{i_{k}}\}
, which is to say, iff
s_{i}\;\mbox{$\longleftrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{S}}$}\;s_{i+1}
;
and a proof as a whole is correct iff every such step is correct.

The above definition of equational proofs does not impose any requirements on the granularity of the individual steps.
This has the advantage of allowing for equational proofs with widely different levels of detail, depending on one’s
preferences (e.g., depending on the intended audience). On the flip side, it allows for proofs that are very hard—potentially
impossible—to check by algorithm. In the extreme case, one could have a correct “proof” of a very complicated
identity
s=t
with just one inference step
s=s_{0}=t\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{by}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}\mathcal{E}
that cites all given axioms.
To rectify this and to simplify the equational tasks considerably, we stipulate that
in a step
s_{i}=s_{i+1}\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{by}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}E_{i_{1}},\ldots,E_{i_{k}}
, the term
s_{i+1}

must be obtainable from
s_{i}
by
k
one-step rewrites using the
k
cited equations, and moreover, that the relevant

k
redexes occur in mutually disjoint positions of
s_{i}
, and thus, in principle, all
k
rewrites could
take place concurrently in one single step. (Two positions are disjoint iff neither is a prefix of the other.)
All proofs in
\textsc{EQ}_{1}
adhere to this structure and have extremely simple
steps that cite only one or at most two equations, which are only used for one-step reductions in the sense just
described.
More precisely, if
\mathcal{S}=\{E_{1},...,E_{k}\}
is a set of
k>0
equations, we write
s\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}t
iff
s
contains
k
distinct redexes,
one for each
E_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
, in
k
disjoint positions; and
t
is obtained from
s
by replacing each of these

k
redexes by the corresponding contractum. By definition, if
s\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}t
, then
\mathcal{S}

does not have any redundant equations; each of the
k
equations in
\mathcal{S}
is used to reduce a unique redex in
s
.
The reducibility relation
\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}
is a proper subset of the usual
\longleftrightarrow^{*}_{\mathcal{S}}
; the latter is implied
by the former but not conversely.

Consider the proof given in the preceding example and take the first step,
s_{0}=s_{1}
:

g(\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mbox{$h(c,a,c)$}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},h(a,c,a))=g(\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mbox{$h(a,a,f_{4}(f_{1}(c)))$}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},h(a,c,a))\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{by}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}E_{1}.

The underlined redex
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mbox{$h(c,a,c)$}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
of
s_{0}
matches the left-hand side of
E_{1}
under the substitution
\theta=\{V_{1170}\mapsto a\}
, and
s_{1}
is obtained
from
s_{0}
by replacing this redex by the corresponding instance of
E_{1}
’s right-hand side, namely the contractum
\theta(h(V_{1170},a,f_{4}(f_{1}(c))))=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mbox{$h(a,a,f_{4}(f_{1}(c)))$}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.
The entire proof with underlined subterms, using red for redexes and blue for contractums, is shown below:

s

=

g(\mbox{$\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h(c,a,c)}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$},h(a,c,a))

=

g(\mbox{$\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h(a,a,f_{4}(f_{1}(c)))}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$},\mbox{$\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h(a,c,a)}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$})

by

E_{1}

=

g(\mbox{$\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h(a,a,f_{4}(f_{1}(c)))}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$},\mbox{$\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{2}(a,c)}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$})

by

E_{2}

=

g(\mbox{$\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{1}(\mbox{\mbox{$\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{2}(f_{4}(a))}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$}},f_{4}(e))}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$},g_{2}(a,c))

by

E_{3}

=

g(g_{1}(\mbox{$\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mbox{$\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g(f_{3}(a),f_{2}(e))}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$},\mbox{$\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f_{4}(e)}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$}),g_{2}(a,c))

by

E_{4}

=

g(g_{1}(\mbox{$\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g_{4}(f_{1}(f_{5}(f_{3}(a))),d)}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$},\mbox{$\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\underline{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$}),g_{2}(a,c))

by

E_{5},E_{6}

Here, only the penultimate term contains multiple (two) redexes; all other steps follow by replacing a single redex by a single contractum.
The two redexes occur in disjoint positions, as is always the case when more than one equation is cited.

We started with 1K randomly generated examples, where each example included its own set of equational axioms and proof. All 1K proofs
were correct by construction. To balance the dataset with incorrect examples, we randomly corrupted about half of the proofs by introducing errors
of two types:

1.

Incorrect contractums
: On a proof step from
s_{i}
to
s_{i+1}
, we randomly corrupted a contractum in
s_{i+1}

so that
s_{i+1}
is no longer derivable from
s_{i}
by the equations cited by the step.

2.

Incorrect equations
: We randomly changed one of the justifying equations for a given proof step. Thus,
e.g.
, while
the original—and correct—version of the step cited equations
E_{2}
and
E_{5}
, we might change that to
E_{9}
and
E_{5}
, ensuring
that the step no longer goes through.

Only one error was inserted in each corrupted proof, at a randomly chosen step.

We introduce and study three
\textsc{EQ}_{1}
tasks that fall along a spectrum of increasing difficulty,
forming a natural progression that probes different reasoning skills:

1.

Proof checking
(
EQ-PC
, easiest): This is largely a local and low-level matching and substitution task,
although we will see that even here there are different gradations of difficulty depending
on how much explanatory detail we demand.

2.

Equation recovery
(
EQ-ER
, intermediate): This requires more search and matching than simple checking,
but it’s still constrained to a single step at a time.

3.

Gap filling
(
EQ-GF
, hardest): This is a synthesis task. The model is given a start and end point
separated by multiple elided steps and must generate a valid intermediate path. This requires multi-step lookahead,
global planning, and search through a combinatorial space of possible rewrites. It’s considerably more complex than the other two tasks.

9.3.1
Equational Proof Checking

This task contains 200 randomly chosen examples from the set of 1K equational proofs.
The slice is balanced so that about half of the chosen proofs are correct, while the others
are corrupted as described above. The model is asked to determine whether the proof is
correct, and more importantly, to
justify
its answer. Accordingly, the requested output
has two components: a binary correctness verdict and an explanation/justification for the verdict.

Checking an equational proof in detail means verifying that each correct step goes through
owing to the cited identities, and not only identifying incorrect steps but pinpointing
the exact source of error. In particular, if a model claims that a proof with
n
steps is correct,
then its full explanation must be a sequence of
n
structured objects, one for each proof step

s_{i}=s_{i+1}\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{by}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}E_{i_{1}},\ldots,E_{i_{k}}

for
i=0,\ldots,n-1
, elaborating
why
each step is correct. The structured object
consists of the relevant proof step index (the number
i+1
) along with a list of rewrites corresponding
to the
k
redexes in
s_{i}
. Each rewrite is a dictionary with the following keys:

•

redex
: the redex in question;

•

position
: the exact position of the redex in
s_{i}
;

•

equation
: the identity whose left-hand side matches the redex;

•

contractum
: the corresponding contractum in
s_{i+1}
.

More concretely, the model’s output must be a dictionary with two keys,
correct
and
explanation
. The value of
correct
is
true
or
false
,
depending on whether the model determines that the input proof is correct.
When
correct
is
true
, the value of
explanation
must be
a list of dictionaries, each with two keys,
step
(the step number, one for each step of the proof) and
rewrites
,
a list of rewrites of the form described above. Thus, for the example in the preceding section, an appropriate output would be:

⬇

{
"correct"
:

true,

"explanation"
:

[{
"step"
:

1,

"rewrites"
:

[{
"redex"
:

"h(c,a,c)"
,

"position"
:

[1],

"equation"
:

"E1"
,

"contractum"
:

"h(a,a,f4(f1(c)))"
}]},

{
"step"
:

2,

"rewrites"
:

[{
"redex"
:

"h(a,c,a)"
,

"position"
:

:

"E2"
,

"contractum"
:

"g2(a,c)"
}]},

{
"step"
:

"rewrites"
:

"redex"
:

,

"position"
:

:

,

By contrast, if
correct
is
false
, then the value of a complete
explanation
must be
a single dictionary corresponding to the first incorrect step in the proof, and should be:

1.

either of the form

⬇

{
"step"
:

...,

"equation"
:

...,

"position"
:

...,

"expectedContractum"
:

...,

"actualContractum"
:

...}

indicating that the contractum for the equation and redex in question (at the given position) is not what was expected by the cited equation;

2.

or of the form

⬇

{
"step"
:

...,

"givenEquations"
:

[...],

"correctEquations:[...]},"

indicating that the cited identities do not succeed in justifying that step, though the identities listed under
correctEquations
do.

Outputs of the first form capture errors introduced by corrupting contractums; outputs of the second form capture errors introduced by noising cited identities.

The error here is on step 4:
E_{2}
does not justify the passage from
h(f(f_{1}(b)),f_{5}(b),h(e,f_{4}(a),d))

to

h(f(f_{1}(b)),f_{5}(b),h(e,g_{2}(a,f_{5}(b)),d)).

There are no redexes in
h(f(f_{1}(b)),f_{5}(b),h(e,f_{4}(a),d))
that match the left-hand size of
E_{2}
.
However, the step
is
justified by
E_{6}
, which rewrites the redex

f_{4}(a)
to
g_{2}(a,f_{5}(b))
. Thus, the correct output in this case is:

⬇

{
"correct"
:

false,

"explanation"
:

{
"step"
:

4,

"givenEquations"
:

[
"E2"
],

"correctEquations"
:

[
"E6"
]}}

Our few-shot prompt for this task contains several ICL examples of both correct and incorrect proofs, along with appropriate corresponding outputs.

What was described above is a task that requires thorough and detailed checking of equational proofs. We refer to this as
level-3 proof checking
.
This is what our proof-checking prompt for equational logic specifies. Nevertheless, to paint a richer performance picture, we distinguish two
additional evaluation modes that require less detail. In total, we have:

•

Level 1
: The model is given full credit as long as it can guess whether the proof is correct,
i.e.
, as long as it makes
the right binary judgment. Accuracy at this level measures a model’s ability to get the “vibe” of the proof. Is it roughly correct or does something seem off?

•

Level 2
: If the model rightly claims that the proof is correct, then it must provide detailed evidence for
its claim,
i.e.
, the
explanation
it outputs in that case must be fully correct.
This level makes no demands on the model’s output for incorrect proofs. As long as the model correctly guesses that the proof has an error,
it is given full credit even if it gets the error details wrong. Thus, level-2 accuracy measures a model’s ability to perform the
computational work of positive verification: identifying redexes, matching them to equations, and confirming the contractums.

•

Level 3
: As already mentioned, level-3 proof checking is the most thorough: credit is given only
if the verdict is correct and the model’s
explanation
gets all the details right, both for
correct and for incorrect proofs. Thus, this level requires everything required by the previous levels,
as well as debugging skills, or the ability to perform error analysis by precisely identifying and explaining
the first point of failure in an incorrect proof. This is the highest level of proof-checking scrutiny. The model must
keep track of the “expected” state (what the term should look like after the rewrite); compare it against
the actual state; and then articulate the discrepancy. This level also requires
a degree of error-repair ability, insofar as the model must single out the correct (or “expected”) contractum
in the case of a contractum corruption, or the correct equations in the case of an equation corruption.

These levels form a ladder of proof-checking rigor: proof-checking success at L3 (level 3) entails L2 success,
which entails L1 success, but the converses do not hold.

Table 26:
Equational proof-checking accuracies at 3 levels of detail.

Results for all three levels are shown in Table
26
.
For L1, the random-guessing accuracy baseline is already
50\%
. Thus, above-chance accuracy for the top-performing model,
Gemini 3.1, is 98%-50% = 48%. If we normalize, we can say that Gemini’s accuracy
is
(0.98-0.5)/(1-0.5)=0.96
,
i.e.
, that Gemini 3.1 is achieving 96% of the possible
improvement over random guessing. This is near-perfect performance @L1.
However, beyond the top 13 models, performance drops to below 70% even at L1.
So top-tier and some mid-tier models have very strong “intuition” that can
reliably distinguish correct equational proofs from broken ones, but the rest
have a lot of room for improvement. The worst-performing models do about as well as random guessing.

Performance across the board degrades sharply when we move to L2 and require
models to not simply guess correctness but explain on a step-by-step
basis
why
the proof is correct, namely, which are the relevant redexes matching the cited equations,
where exactly they are located in the preceding step’s term, and what are the corresponding contractums.
Opus 4.5 experiences a 25-absolute-point drop in going from L1 to L2,
while for Gemini 3 and GPT-5.2 the drop is 22 and 15 absolute points, respectively.
This shows that even for elite models, there is a large difference between simply guessing
that a proof is correct and properly articulating its correctness in a structured explanation.
Gemini 3.1 is the only top model whose decline is relatively contained (5 points only).

Performance drops further at L3, which extends the detailed-explanation requirement to
incorrect proofs.
At this level, a model must do everything it must do for L2 but also document where and
how an incorrect proof fails. For some top models (especially for Gemini 3.1 and Gemini 3),
the
L2
\,\rightarrow\>
L3
drop is basically zero, and considerably smaller than
their
L1
\,\rightarrow\>
L2
drops. This suggests that these models are
competent debuggers, almost as good at diagnosing errors as they are at verifying correctness.
By contrast, most mid-tier models (like DeepSeek-R1-0528, o4-mini, and o3-mini),
experience catastrophic
L2
\,\rightarrow\>
L3
drops.
These models are much better at verifying a correct stretch of equational
reasoning than diagnosing a flawed one.

Taking the L3 numbers as the main set of results, the DI of this task
is an exceptionally high 0.33.

The L2 error distribution is shown in Fig.
39
.
We distinguish 6 error types at this level.
Recall that, at L2, if a model declares a proof correct then
its explanation must include a list of rewrites for every proof step, where every rewrite is a dictionary with four keys:
position
,
redex
,
contractum
, and
equation
.
Error types 3–6 correspond precisely to one of these 4 values being wrong.

1.

Type 1
: Ill-formatted responses, such as a JSON-schema violation (
e.g.
, a response dictionary that does not include one of the expected keys)
or an explanation for a proof that is declared correct but is missing some of the proof steps.
90
90

90

We are lenient in these judgments, e.g., instead of checking for strict equality between the set of expected
dictionary keys and the set of generated keys, we check only for a subset relation, as some models unilaterally insert extra key-value entries, such as a
note
that provides additional commentary about something.

2.

Type 2
: Wrong correctness verdict.

3.

Type 3
: Wrong position in a rewrite of an explanation step.

4.

Type 4
: Wrong redex in a rewrite of an explanation step.

5.

Type 5
: Wrong contractum in a rewrite of an explanation step.

6.

Type 6
: Wrong equation in a rewrite of an explanation step.

Error types 1 and 2 are mutually exclusive with types 3–6, but the latter can co-occur in
an explanation (and even on a single explanation step), so Fig.
39

reports raw error counts summed over all proof steps. For that reason,
the absolute values of these counts cannot be meaningfully compared
across models with widely different accuracies on the set of correct proofs.
For example,
claude-sonnet-420250514
makes far fewer errors
than
gemini-2.5-pro
, but that’s because it only recognizes 27 out of the
103 correct proofs, whereas
gemini-2.5-pro
correctly recognizes 91, so naturally it will
have more opportunities to make errors of types 3–6.

Figure 39:
Level-2 error distribution for equational proof checking.

The plot shows that misidentifying redex positions (type-3 errors) is the most common
failure mode at L2. Models usually output positions that are close to the correct position
but do not actually match it. We quantify this closeness relation
by defining a similarity metric that considers how much of the path is shared
between the correct position and the model-generated position
before the two diverge,
i.e.
, their longest common prefix. The more steps they share from the root, the more similar they are.
The metric is normalized so the score always lies between 0 and 1. If the two positions are identical,
the score is 1; if they diverge
immediately at the root, the score is 0. For intermediate cases, it strikes a balance between the amount of common structure and the
total lengths of the paths. For instance,
[3,3,1]
and
[3,3]
share a prefix of length 2 and one is a prefix of the other, so their
similarity is high (0.8), while paths that diverge early get much lower scores.
The mean similarities for all models across their
type-3 errors are as follows:

gemini-3.1-pro-preview
: 0.83,
gpt-5.2
: 0.77,
gpt-5.1

gpt-5
: 0.76,

: 0.75,

claude-sonnet-4-20250514

These numbers track only modestly with overall performance, featuring some notable exceptions such as
claude-sonnet-4-20250514
,
which is better at locating precise positions than both
gemini-2.5-pro
and
grok-4
,
suggesting that this might be a fairly distinct skill, not just a corollary of general reasoning ability.

The results indicate that most models are fairly good at “what” and “why” but bad at “where.” They can often correctly
identify the string being changed (redex), what it changes into (contractum), and even the rule that governs
the change (equation); but they fail at precisely locating that change within the larger tree structure.
This should not be surprising, given that models do not explicitly build or traverse syntax trees.
Tree structure is inherently two-dimensional, so a transformer-based model must infer it
from syntactic cues in the 1D token sequence that it actually sees, primarily from parentheses and commas,
which tends to be fuzzy and imprecise for the purposes of exact hierarchical localization.

GPT-5.2 stands out here as having the strongest localization skills.
As mentioned earlier, raw counts of type-3 errors cannot be compared across models
that accept widely different numbers of correct proofs.
However, for models that have similar acceptance rates on correct proofs, comparing
the counts of error types 3—6 can reveal strikingly different failure regimes.
Focusing specifically on type-3 errors, we see that GPT-5.2 accepts 98 out of the 103 correct
proofs and makes a total of only 8 type-3 errors. By contrast, Gemini 3 Pro Preview accepts
102 out of the 103 correct proofs and makes 175 such errors. Normalizing, we get
a type-3 error rate or 0.08 for GPT-5.2 and 1.7 for Gemini 3, a difference of about 1.32
orders of magnitude. Even Gemini 3.1, the top-performing model, makes 26 type-3 errors, for
an error rate of 0.25, about 3 times larger than GPT-5.2.

i.e.
, any position for a term
t

that is not a member of
\mbox{Dom}\hskip 0.43338pt(t)
), summed over all explanation steps.

How well models identify the precise positions of subterms of interest, such as redexes,
is likely indicative of their general ability to navigate deeply nested tree-structured data.

Among exclusively L3 errors, the most common type for most models
is misidentifying the step where the proof first goes wrong.
Further, from the two types of possible proof
errors, one due to contractum corruption and one due to corruption of the justifying equations, models have a harder time
pinpointing the details of the former. They
struggle roughly equally in identifying the expected contractum and the actual contractum.
The discrepancy makes sense. An equation corruption is an easier diagnosis: “I see a change from
u
to
v
.
The cited rule is
E_{i}
, but this rule doesn’t apply at all.” A contractum corruption, by contrast, is much harder: “I see a change
from
u
to
v
. The cited rule is
E_{i}
. The redex
u
matches the LHS of
E_{i}
, so the rule does apply. Now, I must
compute the expected contractum
v^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
and compare it to the actual
v
.” This requires the model to perform a forward
inference step itself, hold the result in memory, and then perform a comparison, so it’s more computationally demanding.

9.3.2
Recovery of Elided Equations

This task also contains 200 samples drawn from the set of 1K examples, evenly split between correct and incorrect proofs.
The input given to the model is the same as in the previous task, a set of named equations
\mathcal{E}
and a proof
D
,
except that all justifying equations are now omitted from every step in
D
and the model has
to discover them on its own. Specifically, for every step of the proof that derives a term
s_{i+1}
from a term
s_{i}
,
the model has to output a set
\mathcal{S}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
of equations that justify
the step in the sense that
s_{i}\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}s_{i+1}
, assuming that such a set
\mathcal{S}_{i}
exists.
If it does not,
e.g.
, because the step from
s_{i}
to
s_{i+1}
has an error,
then the correct output is the empty set of equations.
For example, given the input:

⬇

1

2

E1:

h(c,V1170,c)

=

h(V1170,a,f4(f1(c)))

3

E2:

h(V1173,V1174,V1173)

=

g2(V1173,V1174)

4

E3:

h(a,V1181,f4(f1(c)))

=

g1(f2(f4(V1181)),f4(e))

5

E4:

f2(f4(V1184))

=

g(f3(V1184),f2(e))

6

E5:

g(V1187,f2(e))

=

g4(f1(f5(V1187)),d)

7

E6:

f4(V1188)

=

V1188

8

9

-

Proof:

10

11

s

=

g(h(c,a,c),h(a,c,a))

#

step

0

12

g(h(a,a,f4(f1(c))),h(a,c,a))

#

step

1

13

#

#

the following list would be a correct output:

⬇

[{

:

:

{
