Note that this task has no unique correct output for a given step from
s_{i}
to
s_{i+1}
, as there may be multiple sets of equations
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{E}

such that
s_{i}\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}s_{i+1}
(although typically the number of possibilities is small). We thus verify each answer independently. If
an answer
\mathcal{S}
is a non-empty set of equations, we check that
s_{i}\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}s_{i+1}
. If an answer is the empty set of equations, we check
that there is no
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
such that
s_{i}\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}s_{i+1}
. This is easily decidable, first by filtering those
candidate equations that match some subterm of
s_{i}
, and then checking whether every disagreement between
s_{i}
and
s_{i+1}
can be resolved by one of these
candidates.
91
91

91

The set
D
of “disagreements” between two terms
s
and
t
consists of those minimal positions
p\in\mbox{\rm$\mbox{Dom}\hskip 0.43338pt(s)$}\cap\mbox{\rm$\mbox{Dom}\hskip 0.43338pt(t)$}
such that
the subterms
\mbox{sub\/}(s,p)
and
\mbox{sub\/}(t,p)
have different roots. By “minimal” we mean that if
p\in D
and
q

is a strict suffix of
p
then
q\not\in D
. Thus, this set is simply the set of earliest
or highest positions where the two trees have a mismatch.

This task has a strong abductive-verification flavor. For every step of the proof, the model is faced with three pieces of “evidence”:
the starting state
s_{i}
, the ending state
s_{i+1}
, and the equational axioms in
\mathcal{E}
as the permissible “physical laws.”
The claim to be investigated is that
s_{i+1}
is easily derivable from
s_{i}
by a set of equations
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
(where easy derivability means
no deduction chains, just single-step reductions as defined by
\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}
).
To decide the claim, the model must work backward from the evidence by looking at the “effect” (the difference between
s_{i}
and
s_{i+1}
) and trying
to find a “cause” (a minimal set of axioms) that would explain it. This can be performed by difference analysis followed by hypothesis generation.
Difference analysis aims to identify the subterm(s) that changed in the transition from
s_{i}
to
s_{i+1}
; this is the locus of the analysis.
Each subterm change consists of a redex candidate in
s_{i}
and a transformed version in
s_{i+1}
, for which the model generates hypotheses:
“Could axiom
E_{1}
explain this transformation? Could
E_{2}
? Could
E_{3}
?
\cdots
”

While the task requires processing a good deal of highly structured data, it lends itself to pattern recognition
and does not require fine-grained analysis. In the running example, for instance, the first step can only be justified
by
E_{1}
, since that’s the only equation whose right-hand side can introduce a subterm of the form
f_{4}(f_{1}(c))
.
Likewise, the second step is easily seen to follow by
E_{2}
.

We report two metrics on this task. The main one is
proof-level accuracy
(or PLA for short): the fraction of proofs for
which the model generates correct outputs at every step. The secondary metric is
step-level accuracy
(SLA):
the proportion of individual proof steps for which the model produces correct outputs.
The former is clearly more demanding, as a model is rewarded only if it handles correctly
every

step of the proof. The step-level metric is much more forgiving, as it rewards partial success
on individual steps. Proof-level and step-level accuracies are shown in Table
27
.

EQ-ER
performance;
PLA
= Proof-Level Accuracy,
SLA
= Step-Level Accuracy.

The SLA metric gauges raw ER capability. Top models have basically perfect
internal mechanisms for solving this problem. PLA, by contrast, measures reliability
and consistency. Here only two models manage to score higher than 80%: GPT-5.2 and
GPT-5.1, with the former getting a nearly perfect score.
The rest of the models, even elite models like Gemini 3, are not reliable enough
to get every step right consistently across an entire multi-step proof.
A single mistake in a 20-step proof is enough to fail the PLA metric. This shows
that moving from high capability to high reliability in sequential reasoning tasks
remains a critical frontier for most LLMs.

PLA’s DI (discriminability index) is exceptionally high at 0.36.

9.3.3
Error Analysis

We partition the set of all possible step-level errors into 4 types:

1.

The model’s output is ill-formed.

2.

The model gives the empty list of supporting equations for a step from
s_{i}
to
s_{i+1}
, indicating that the step
cannot be
\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}
-justified by any
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, when in fact it can.

3.

The model gives a non-empty list of supporting equations
\mathcal{S}
for a step when in fact the step cannot be

\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}
-justified by any
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
.

4.

The model gives a non-empty list of supporting equations
\mathcal{S}
for a step and the step
is
justifiable, but
the particular equations given in
\mathcal{S}
do not justify the step.

A proof-level error is this: Every element of the list given by the model is properly formatted, but instead of containing
an element for every step of the proof, the list is cut off prematurely or only contains answers for some of the proof steps, but not all of them.
We refer to this as a type-5 error.

Step-level rates for the 4 possible error types are shown in Figure
40
.

Figure 40:
Normalized distribution of step-level ER errors.

Note that the SLAs of Table
27
are not equal to 1 minus these error rates. That is because the error rates of Fig.
40

are normalized with respect to the total number of steps that the model has attempted to explain (these are the values
n=
\,\cdots
shown above each bar in the figure).
By contrast, the SLAs of Table
27
are
normalized with respect to the total number of all steps across all proofs, regardless of whether the model has provided a response for a step.
As an example, consider
gpt-4o
and
llama-3.1-405b
. These models only provided answers for a small fraction of all steps, particularly
gpt-4o
, which only handled
200 out of a total of 3904 steps, a mere 5%.
In fact, only 10 models engaged with all possible steps;
the rest omitted at least a few. The number of steps omitted (the difference between
3904 and the value of
n
shown in the figure) is a direct absolute
measure of a model’s type-5 errors.

No model makes a formatting error. The large majority of ER errors are of type 4. Models correctly identify that the step is valid but they provide the wrong set of justifying equations.
This is not a failure of pattern recognition in the simple sense. The model is usually “in the right ballpark” but lacks precision. It suggests a failure of meticulous verification.
Models seem to rely on heuristics like “Axiom
E_{5}
looks very similar to the change I see” and then confidently output
E_{5}
without performing the rigorous computational
step of applying the substitution and ensuring that it produces exactly the target term.

Type-2 errors (where the model misclassifies the step as unjustifiable by outputting
[]
) are
a different failure mode that cannot be explained simply by lower reasoning competence,
since there are several models with poor performance but no
type-2 errors.
The rate of type-2 errors likely depends on
whether a model’s post-training alignment prioritizes
helpfulness and engagement versus abstention in the presence of uncertainty.
Competence still determines how often the model is uncertain,
but whether the model tries to engage despite the uncertainty is
largely shaped by its alignment regimen.

Another finding involves bidirectional rewriting. Our formalization explicitly defines
\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}
as left-to-right only, which suffices for this dataset.
Yet several models repeatedly choose sets of equations that can only justify a step if reduction is performed from right to left.
Strictly speaking, these answers are incorrect because they do not adhere to the prompt instructions, which only allow for left-to-right rewriting. We give full credit to such answers anyway, because they are logically correct, even if not consistent with the prompt.
While this happens relative rarely,
92
92

92

o3-mini leads the pack, with bidirectional rewrites in 32 proofs (out of the total 200); followed by GPT-4.1 (17 proofs). Many others use bidirectional rewriting more sporadically.

it is curious that a model would choose equations that require
right-to-left rewriting when equally simple equations that work from left to right
(and are thus fully consistent with the prompt) are available.
This suggests that many models are following the spirit of equational reasoning (proving two things are the same) while violating the letter
of the specific task instructions (only rewriting from left to right). In other words, their
deeply ingrained semantic understanding of equality
as a symmetric relation is overriding the specific procedural constraints of the task.
This underscores how deeply learned priors from pretraining (such as the symmetry of equality) impact a model’s operation
and points to an alignment challenge in getting models to follow instructions that might feel “unnatural.”

9.3.4
Equational Gap Filling

This task is also a subset of
\textsc{EQ}_{1}
with 200 proofs, but each proof has a gap of a randomly chosen size
inserted at a randomly chosen location. The model is asked to fill that gap with an appropriate sequence of steps.
The output must be a dictionary with two main keys,
explanation
and
missingSteps
.
The value of
explanation
is a plain text string describing the model’s reasoning strategy.
The value of
missingSteps
is the main payload: the subproof filling the gap.
This is expressed as a list of dictionaries representing proof steps, each with
two keys,
term
and
supportingEquations
. Thus, such a list
can be viewed as a sequence of pairs
(t_{i},N_{i})
,
1\leq i\leq k

for some
k\geq 1
, where each
t_{i}
is a term (the value of the key
term
) and
N_{i}

is a list of equation names (the value of the key
supportingEquations
).
The specified steps correctly fill the gap iff the proof

\displaystyle\mbox{\mbox{$s^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}

\displaystyle=t_{1}

\displaystyle\mbox{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{by}}} $\>N_{1}$}

(15)

\displaystyle=t_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{by}}} $\>N_{2}$}

\displaystyle=t_{k}

\displaystyle\mbox{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{by}}} $\>N_{k}$}

\displaystyle=\mbox{\mbox{$t^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}

\displaystyle\mbox{\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{by}}} $\>\cdots$}

correctly derives the equation
\mbox{\mbox{$s^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}=\mbox{\mbox{$t^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}
,
where
s^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
is the term that appears on the step immediately before the gap and
t^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
by
\cdots

is the step immediately after the gap.
More precisely, we must have
s^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow_{N_{1}}t_{1}
;
t_{k}\Longleftrightarrow_{\cdots}t^{\prime}
; and

t_{i-1}\Longleftrightarrow_{N_{i}}t_{i}
for every
i=2,\ldots,k
, where
\Longleftrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}
is
the symmetric closure of
\Longrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}
. (Thus, unlike ER, this task explicitly allows for
bidirectional rewriting.) If the model determines that there is no way to fill the gap, it must output the
empty list as the value of
missingSteps
. We write
??
to indicate the presence of a gap.
We explicitly require
\mbox{$|N_{i}|$}\leq 2
,
i.e.
, to avoid trivializing the problem, a model may not cite
more than 2 equations per step.

For example, given the input:

since this one step is sufficient to fill the gap.

Only a small minority of examples (7 out of 200) have gaps that cannot be filled.

In addition to
explanation
and
missingSteps
, the top-level JSON output for this task must
contain a third key,
confidence
, whose value is an integer from 1 to 5 representing the model’s
confidence in its answer (in the value of
missingSteps
). Here 5 indicates perfect confidence,
1 is zero confidence, 2 is some (low) confidence, 3 is medium confidence, and 4 is strong but not full
confidence.
93

In earlier versions we had requested confidence levels to be continuous numbers from 0 to 1,
but in practice models would only output numbers in a small fixed set, such as
\{0.2,0.4,0.6,0.5,0.80.9,0.95\}
,
so we moved to an inherently discrete formulation.

Table
28
shows some elementary statistics about the gap-filling data along 5 dimensions:
the original length of each of the 200 proofs (before the insertion of gaps); the size of each gap (the number of steps removed from the original proof);
the size of each equational theory (the number of equations in each of the 200 problems); the number of equations
cited per proof step; and the sizes of the equations themselves, viewed as ASTs. As the table shows,
the average gap is slightly longer than half the size of the proof, so these are quite substantial reasoning holes.

The main metric we report for this task is accuracy, the ratio #(correct answers)
\,/\,
#(all answers).
When is a gap-filling answer considered correct? If the value of
missingSteps
is the empty list of steps,
then this is correct iff the gap cannot be filled.
94
94

94

This is determined
with the help of an ATP (automated theorem prover).

If the value of
missingSteps
is a non-empty list of steps specifying a subproof,
it is considered correct iff the subproof closes the gap correctly
in the sense specified by (
15
). We use our own code to decide, for any given
s
,
t
,
and subset
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, whether
s\Longleftrightarrow_{\mathcal{S}}t
.

Fig.
42
shows a classification of all possible errors, assuming structurally correct responses by the models.
There are five main error types:

1.

Type 1 errors
: the gap is fillable but the model outputs an empty completion, signaling that the gap cannot be filled.

2.

Type 2 errors
: the gap is fillable and the model outputs an non-empty completion, but (at least) one of the terms in the proof has unbalanced parentheses
(a “syntax error”).

3.

Type 3 errors
: the gap is fillable and the model outputs an non-empty completion, but (at least) one of the terms in the proof has a “type error,” meaning
that a function symbol is applied to the wrong number of arguments.

4.

Type 4 errors
: the gap is fillable and the model outputs an non-empty completion, but the proof does not go through because a step is incorrect, in that the
cited equations do not justify the derivation of the relevant term.

5.

Type 5 errors
: the gap is not fillable, but the model outputs a non-empty completion that claims to fill it.

{forest}

Figure 42:
Error typology for the gap-filling task.

These can be partitioned into 3 disjoint blocks,
\{1\}
,
\{2,3,4\}
, and
\{5\}
.
Types 2, 3, 4, are not mutually exclusive because a proof can have syntax errors as well as type errors and/or semantic errors.

Almost all responses by all models were structurally sound (with a sole exception from
llama-3.1-405b
, one of whose proofs had a step without the required
supportingEquations
key).
Fig.
43
shows raw counts for the five error types for all models.

Figure 43:
Prevalence of errors of different types in the gap-filling task.

Some high-level takeaways from these findings:

•

The large majority of gaps (193 out of 200) are fillable, so type-5 errors are rare.

•

As seen in other tasks, when failure-averse models cannot solve a problem, they prefer to fail succinctly rather than generate longer answers,
which means they tend to produce the smallest possible output that adheres to user specifications. DeepSeek models
lead the pack here. DeepSeek-V3 and DeepSeek-3.1, for example, refuse to fill 156 and 157 out of the 200 gaps,
respectively, claiming that they cannot be filled. However, this should not be interpreted as the models
being aware of their limitations. If the models had such awareness, they would generate
these answers with low confidence. By contrast, both DeepSeek-V3 and DeepSeek-3.1 (for
example) wrongly claim that more than 150 fillable gaps are unfillable with a very strong
median confidence of 4. (DeepSeek-V3 does not even once output a “very low” confidence of 1.)
In their explanations, these models often admit that they arrived at a negative verdict
due to the problem’s complexity (a favorite phrase is “Given the time constraints and complexity,
I concluded that the gap cannot be filled validly”) and yet they claim
a high confidence in the verdict nevertheless, as if problem complexity by itself was
evidence of unsolvability. Likewise for R1 and OpenAI’s mini models,
although o4-mini is much more humble in its confidence.

•

By contrast, the “compulsively generative” models with the tall red bars on the right side of the figure
have few type-1 errors because they tend to plunge ahead and fill
the gaps even if they’re not in a position to do so successfully. As a result, the vast majority of errors for these models are of
type 4, genuinely failing proof steps.

•

Stronger models also tend to provide better-calibrated confidence levels.
Here is an explanation provided by Gemini-2.5-Pro:
The transformations required,
particularly for the first and fourth arguments of the main term, are exceptionally complex. Despite significant effort, I could not construct a complete
and verifiable path from start_term to t_k. The search space of possible rewrite sequences is vast due to the large number of equations.
Given the difficulty in finding a valid path and the high chance of error in such a complex derivation, I am unable to provide a sequence of steps
with high confidence. It is possible that a key insight or a simpler path was missed, but it is also possible the gap is invalid as stated.
Therefore, I will indicate that the gap is invalid by providing an empty list, but with low confidence, reflecting the possibility that a
solution exists but is beyond my current analysis capabilities.
That said, our ECE analysis below shows that
all

models are consistently over-confident, and Gemini-2.5-Pro is the worst-calibrated of the elite models.

•

Syntax and type errors (types 2 and 3, respectively) are less common, but they do occur in non-negligible
numbers across all models, including the very best. Their presence indicates that the models’ internal representation
of these symbolic structures is not quite robust. They can still lose a parenthesis or misapply a function.
The high rate of syntax errors by o3-mini is particularly noteworthy—it makes such errors in
a remarkable 50 proof attempts, or 25% of all inputs.

•

Regarding instruction following, recall that the prompt explicitly requires all steps to cite no more than two equations. While virtually all models adhere to that constraint
at the
95^{\mbox{th}}
percentile (the only exception is DeepSeek-V3, which
cites 2.3 equations per step even at the
90^{\mbox{th}}
percentile), only a minority of the models (10 out of 24) achieved perfect adherence
to the constraint, without ever citing more than 2 equations: GPT-5.2, GPT-5.1, GPT-5, Gemini-3.1, Claude Opus 4.5, Grok-3, o4-mini, o3-mini, DeepSeek-3.1, and DeepSeek-Reasoner. For all other models, the maximum number of cited equations on a given step ranges from 3
(for Grok-4) to 10 (for GPT-4.1).

•

The best models clearly show an ability to compose nontrivial reasoning chains.
For a problem with 46 equational axioms and a gap size of 23, Gemini 3 Pro Preview
managed to fill the gap with a subproof of 35 steps, each one citing a single equation.
However, this is was the longest correct proof written by Gemini 3 and an outlier.
As we will see shortly, performance drops sharply across the board as the gap
size increases.

We now examine how accuracy depends on gap size as well as the size of the equational theory for the top 10 models.
These two relationships are depicted in Fig.
44
and
Fig.
45
, respectively.
The plots show that as the problems become more challenging, performance drops drastically
even for the strongest models.

Figure 44:
Gap-filling accuracy for the top 10 models as a function of gap size.

Figure 45:
Gap-filling accuracy for the top 10 models as a function of equational theory size.

In a sense, gap size captures the depth of the required search while theory size captures its breadth.
The size of the gap is a measure of the number of needed reasoning steps, which taxes the model’s ability to maintain a goal,
avoid getting lost, and chain inferences correctly. By contrast, the number of equational axioms taxes the model’s ability to
select the
correct
tool at each step from a large set of distractors. It’s a measure of its ability to handle a large context
of rules.

The heat map in Fig.
46
shows how model accuracy (across all models) depends jointly on both parameters: gap size on the
x
-axis
and equational theory size on the
y
-axis.
Each cell’s color represents the fraction of correct proofs among all attempts falling into that combination of gap size and theory size,
with lighter colors indicating higher accuracy. The pattern reveals a clear gradient: accuracy
is highest in the bottom-left corner, where gaps are small and theories are simple, and drops sharply as either dimension increases.
Moving to the right, accuracy decreases with larger gaps; moving upward, it decreases with larger theory sizes, even as gap sizes stay small.
In the top-right region, where both the gaps and the theories are large, accuracy is near zero, showing that these two sources of difficulty
combine to severely degrade model performance. Overall, the heat map highlights that while both factors independently hurt success rates,
their effects compound. Models that can handle a relatively large gap with a small theory, or conversely, break down almost
completely when both are large. This shows that current models lack the robust search and planning abilities
needed to navigate complex combinatorial search spaces effectively.

Figure 46:
Consolidated accuracy heat map for gap filling, as a function of gap length and equational theory size.

The plot in Fig.
47
compares the calibration of the models on the same dataset of 200 proofs.
For each model, 6 thin bars summarize calibration performance. The first five bars (L1–L5) correspond to absolute
calibration gaps in each of the model’s discrete confidence levels. Recall that we allow for 5 possible confidence levels,
1 (very low confidence) through 5 (near certainty). We map these levels to probabilities as follows:

\{1\mapsto 0.05,2\mapsto 0.25,3\mapsto 0.5,4\mapsto 0.75,5\mapsto 0.95\}.

(16)

The gap for a given bin is the absolute value of the difference between the empirical accuracy observed in that bin
and that predicted by the probability assigned to that bin. For instance, the probability assigned to bin 2 (for a confidence
level of 2) is 0.25, so if a model were perfectly calibrated we would expect its outputs to be correct 25% of the time. If they
are only accurate 10% of the time, we have an absolute gap of 15%—a significant marker of overconfidence.
In the plot, the height of each bar shows how far the model’s confidence in that bin differs from its
actual empirical accuracy:
a higher bar means worse calibration. The final, black bar shows the overall Expected Calibration Error (ECE), a weighted average across bins,
with the following standard definition:

\mbox{ECE\/}={\sum}_{m=1}^{M}\cfrac{\left|B_{m}\right|}{n}\cdot\left|\mbox{acc\/}(B_{m})-\mbox{conf\/}(B_{m})\right|,

where
M
is the number of bins (5 in our case);
B_{m}
is the set of predictions that fall into bin
m
;
n
is the
total number of predictions (200 in our case);
\mbox{acc\/}(B_{m})
is the fraction of correct predictions in
B_{m}
;
and
\mbox{conf\/}(B_{m})
is the average confidence score of the predictions in the bin.
96
96

96

This is the general definition
of ECE. In our setting, because we don’t have continuously varying probabilities,
\mbox{conf\/}(B_{m})
is fixed for each bin by the mapping (
16
).

MCE
is defined as the maximum calibration error across all bins:

\mbox{MCE\/}=\max_{m\in\{1,\ldots,M\}}\left|\mbox{acc\/}(B_{m})-\mbox{conf\/}(B_{m})\right|.

These are the two canonical calibration metrics that are most commonly reported.

Figure 47:
Gap-filling calibration errors across all models (bin-level and overall ECE).

Looking across all models, none of them are well-calibrated. Only GPT-5.2, Gemini-3.1,
and Claude Opus 4.5 (with average errors of 0.12, 0.12, and 0.13)
land in a range that most calibration researchers would consider
respectable. While not ideal, it is small enough that the models’
confidence estimates could plausibly carry some actionable signal.
All other ECE scores are above 0.2, which is generally considered high miscalibration.
Everything above 0.4 (this includes Gemini 2.5 Pro, o3-mini, and DeepSeek-R1-0528) is
basically noise, while some models (e.g.,
mistral-magistral
,
llama-3.1-405b
, and
grok-3
) are
essentially completely uncalibrated, with extremely large per-bin errors ranging
from 0.7 to 0.9. The stated confidence of these models is usually off by 70-90
percentage points, which is not mere miscalibration but complete epistemic collapse.
The worst bins across
almost all models are L4 and L5, which correspond to predictions made with high or very high confidence.
This is classic overconfidence: models severely overestimate their reliability when they are confident.
In other words, the models are most wrong precisely when they think they’re most right,
which is the opposite of what is needed in mathematical reasoning.
If confidence levels cannot be trusted specifically when they are high,
they are of little value as guardrails,
e.g.
, thresholding on “confidence
\geq
4’’
would be unsafe for all of these models.
97
97

97

To put it in terms of odds, an ECE error of 0.25 in bin 5 means
that when the model says
I’m
\approx
95% sure I’m right
, it’s actually right only
about 70% of the time. Taking the model at its word implies betting odds of 19:1, i.e.,
you expect to win (the model to be right) about 19 times out of 20. But the real
odds are 7:3, so you’d win only about 7 times out of 10.
If you are willing to risk $19 to win $1 because you believe the 19:1 odds
(you take the model at its word), then the expected value of your betting strategy per decision
would be
0.7\cdot 1+0.3\cdot(-19)=-5
,
i.e.
, on average you would lose $5 per decision.

The exception is GPT-5.2, which has an exceptionally small error in L5, 0.008. Claude 4.5 Opus
also has a small calibration error in L5, 0.05.
This suggests that when these two models assign near-maximum confidence, their predictions
are very likely correct. Gemini-3.1 has a more sizable L5 error, 0.12.
Note, however, that both GPT-5.2 and Gemini 3.1 still exhibit severe miscalibration in other bins,
with very high
MCE
values (0.5 and 0.75, respectively), leaving significant room for improvement.
At 0.22, the MCE of Opus 4.5 is lower but still large.

These results are interesting for a couple of reasons. First, they show that miscalibration is
not inevitable; it can be dramatically reduced.
Second, combined with the accuracy results, they show that calibration is not a corollary
of correctness. While accuracy and calibration do tend to increase together, the correlation
is far from perfect. For example, Gemini 3 is the second best-performing model but it is miscalibrated,
featuring a significant ECE of 0.27—more than twice that of GPT-5.2.
Conversely, Claude Opus 4.5 is much better calibrated but its accuracy is only 41%.
Therefore, calibration is not an emergent property of scale and reasoning competence alone.
It very likely requires explicit uncertainty supervision and/or architectural or training changes that link
reasoning depth to confidence. The three best calibrated models almost certainly had an additional
training signal or mechanism binding confidence to correctness. That could have been
explicit calibration loss, confidence supervision, rejection-aware training, or tighter
coupling between reasoning depth and confidence output.

10
Conclusions

This paper introduced
ProofGrid
, a new benchmark designed to assess the reasoning abilities
of LLMs through tasks that demand rigorous and machine-checkable proof construction and analysis
under minimal formalism.
Unlike benchmarks that focus solely on answer accuracy,
ProofGrid
emphasizes
the integrity of a model’s inferences and offers a more precise and fine-grained assessment
of reasoning competence.

A core theme emerging from our results is one of extraordinary progress in a short time.
We started this work in late 2024. At that point, the state of the art was GPT-4 and Claude
Sonnet 3.5, which were essentially incapable of sustained reasoning. We were concerned at the time
that what are now the easiest tasks in the benchmark,
PL1-PW
and
PL1-PC
,
might be too challenging; performance was well below ceiling and overrun with
elementary errors. In less than a year, these tasks had
become easy for the best models and accessible even to mid-tier models.
Our current results provide many examples of superb fluid reasoning
on highly challenging abstract problems.
The debate as to whether these systems can reason at all should
be considered settled at this point: they can.
The evidence is decisive.
ProofGrid
should provide
a robust platform for tracking and accelerating these
developments, underscoring the importance of proof-based evaluation as
models move toward deeper integration into scientific and mathematical
workflows.

Nevertheless, major challenges remain. Even the best models continue to
make rudimentary reasoning errors (albeit at a much lower rate than before),
and all models quickly run up against hard limitations as the difficulty
of the reasoning increases, especially on structurally rich problems drawn from
classical families of hard problems. More concerningly, we see high levels of
what we call
epistemic instability
: the tendency of models to “forget”
what they seem to know well when they are working on a hard problem,
and to frequently contradict themselves.
We formalized this phenomenon by introducing a new quantity called ESI
(epistemic stability index) and we presented the first set of empirical results
on that front.

In particular, while our results strongly suggest that models possess at least
partial reasoning competence, they still fall short on procedural grounding and
cognitive control, which often prevents them from
rigorously
executing
reasoning tasks without external scaffolding. By and large, they reason in a semantic
space, not a formal one. They often identify which conclusions follow from which assumptions and
construct globally coherent proof strategies. However, they fail to reliably
execute these strategies within a formally constrained system, even a very simple one.
They struggle to suppress invalid but tempting operations, to adhere
strictly to syntactic constraints, and to treat inference rules as deterministic
operators rather than semantic heuristics. In that sense, current models exhibit
semantic reasoning without procedural grounding.

The issue can perhaps be illuminated through the lens of the manifold hypothesis
[
12
]
.
Neural models perform well when the data distribution lies on a low-dimensional manifold embedded
in a high-dimensional representation space, as is the case for images or natural language.
In such domains, nearby points in representation space typically correspond to semantically
similar objects, and interpolation is meaningful. Formal logical reasoning appears to violate this assumption.
The space of formal proofs is extremely sparse, and small perturbations to a formula or an inference
rule often result in radical and disruptive semantic changes rather than smooth variation. Unlike natural
language, where many paraphrases express essentially the same meaning,
formal proofs do not allow for much redundancy: a single misplaced connective,
rule application, or even a stray parenthesis can invalidate the entire derivation.

From this perspective, the behavior we observe is unsurprising. Models trained to operate
in smooth semantic spaces attempt to solve proof tasks by interpolating within a semantic
manifold that, in this domain, does not meaningfully exist. This results in “semantic”
reasoning that often captures the intended structure of an argument but fails to satisfy
the discrete, brittle constraints imposed by formal proof systems.
In this sense, current models exhibit reasoning that is well-adapted to continuous
semantic domains but poorly matched to the sharply discontinuous geometry of rigorous,
formal proofs. Their failures may therefore reflect a deeper mismatch between
the inductive biases of deep learning and the structure of symbolic reasoning.
This is not to say that formal reasoning is inherently non-manifold or that deep learning cannot
in principle approximate it well enough; only that, at least in their current training
and architectural incarnations, the continued symbolic brittleness of models
may be structurally related to the apparent disconnect between their inductive
bias and the geometry of formal reasoning spaces.

Of course, the rapid improvement of frontier systems suggests that this
geometric tension is not insurmountable. But it is worth noting that contemporary
high-performing models are no longer simple next-token predictors operating in isolation;
many incorporate inference-time search, reranking, verifier guidance, or other forms of structured deliberation,
and often distill the results of such search back into their parameters.
These mechanisms effectively introduce discrete branching, pruning, and constraint
enforcement steps that differ qualitatively from token-by-token generation
and partially compensate for the smooth, interpolation-driven
inductive biases of the underlying neural representations. In this sense, the
gains we observe may not so much reflect the disappearance of symbolic brittleness,
but the increasing use of architectural guardrails that approximate modular,
search-based reasoning within an otherwise continuous semantic substrate.

A related recurrent pattern has been failures of cognitive isolation or
computational modularity. To put it simplistically,
with transformer-based architectures it is not the case that “if a model can reliably solve task
S

and task
T
is composed of many
S
subtasks, the model can also reliably solve
T
."
Reliable problem solving needs reliable context-switching into a cognitively isolated “system-2 mode” that can load
a particular subproblem’s details into working memory while shutting out everything else as irrelevant noise.
This is what both humans and conventional symbolic computers do: they use function calling
to encapsulate complexity. When a function is called, its internal logic is
independent of and isolated from the global state of the program, which can be arbitrarily
complicated without affecting the execution of the function.
In a transformer model, by contrast, every token attends to every other token in
the entire context window.
In checking
\textsc{PL}_{3}
proofs, for example, the embeddings for the tokens
in step 42 are mathematically influenced by the attention patterns from all
previous steps and the system prompt. So the model’s mini-SAT circuits that
are used to solve the local entailment problems in such a proof are not running
in isolation; they are running in a big noisy room that subjects the output
to interference from irrelevant sources. Our results underscore that language models
do not draw upon modular internal reasoning capabilities;
if they did, their performance would be much more epistemically stable and invariant to context.
Instead, they perform a context-dependent simulation of reasoning,
essentially approximating system-2 behavior using mechanisms designed for system-1 tasks.
That simulation degrades when the model’s architectural and representational resources (such as effective
attention bandwidth and feature superposition limits, respectively) are stretched thin
by maintaining the global reasoning state.

These findings suggest that proof-based evaluation is not merely a stricter grading scheme,
but a lens into the structure of current reasoning systems. The gap between semantic fluency
and formal control reveals where architectural biases still dominate. In that sense,
ProofGrid
is less
a leaderboard and more a diagnostic instrument for the evolving nature of machine reasoning.

We close with a brief
FAQ
that we hope will help to clarify and reinforce some of this paper’s key points.

•

Q1
: Why use formal notation?
Forcing models to express their reasoning in a formal language like NDL
introduces confounds, such as artificial syntax and formatting errors.

A
: From first principles, there are only two main options for a reasoning benchmark:
models can express their reasoning either in a formal language or in natural language.

For testing purposes, the core foundational problem with
natural language is that it doesn’t scale.
There is no algorithm for determining the
correctness of an arbitrary stretch of text that purports
to reason its way to a conclusion.
As a result, natural-language reasoning cannot be reliably and

automatically
evaluated, and must instead be manually inspected.
This difficulty explains why most existing “reasoning benchmarks” do not
actually evaluate the reasoning itself (ignoring, for example, COT traces
during scoring), because there is no procedure that can take an arbitrary
natural-language reasoning trace and reliably output
correct
or
incorrect
.
This limitation alone is reason enough for AI benchmarks to move away from natural language
and toward formal languages, which make reasoning amenable to computational analysis and evaluation.

Moreover, natural language doesn’t remove confounds; it simply
obscures them. In plain English, we can only determine whether a reasoning trace looks plausible.
Assumption scope is a simple case in point. In a formal language, this is explicitly
indicated with opening-closing braces (or
begin
-
end
pairs, Python-style indentation, etc.),
leaving zero doubt about what hypotheses are active and when. In English, this has to be
guessed from the text by taking into account a wide range of signals,
including formatting details like the way in which the text is broken into paragraphs.
Experienced authors write in a way that minimizes guesswork,
but ambiguity often remains: “Suppose that
p
holds. Then
\cdots
. Next, consider
q
. We have
\cdots
”
Is
p
still active inside the second ellipses? What if there is a new paragraph start
right before “Next, consider
q
.”?

It might be argued that natural-language reasoning
can
be mechanically evaluated, using “LLMs as judges”

[
109
]
. But there is an air of circularity in that approach. Who has judged the judges, and
where can we look for independent ground truth? Using a system of unknown or dubious
reasoning reliability to judge reasoning correctness seems questionable.
An NDL proof can be independently evaluated by a proof checker that has a perfectly precise specification,
is written in a few pages of code that can be audited or even formally verified, and does not change
across runs, resulting in an exceptionally small and stable TCB (trusted computing base). An LLM judge, by
contrast, has billions of parameters, cannot be easily audited,
has no formal semantics or specification, and its behavior may depend on temperature, prompt,
and version changes, among other factors.

Finally, the use of a formal language like NDL provides much higher
analytical resolution, allowing us to
measure
cognitive control.
We are able to precisely distinguish parsing errors from
malformed rule applications, wrong conclusions, purely logical errors,
unbound identifiers, and so on. Such fine-grained analysis is simply
not possible in natural language, where most issues collapse
into “the model’s answer seems wrong.”
Formal languages, in other words, make deviations from correct reasoning explicit
and mechanically detectable, whereas natural-language reasoning often obscures
such failures behind linguistic ambiguity. Models can “sound right” while being wrong,
and logical gaps can easily be hidden in rhetorically compelling prose.
In this sense, the errors we observe
when models use NDL are not confounds but diagnostic signals, revealing limitations
in models’ ability to reliably execute rigorous reasoning. A natural-language formulation
would avoid some of these surface errors, but at the cost of making correctness
judgments substantially less precise and more subjective, and error analysis much
more brittle and qualitative.

•

Q2
: There is a third, middle-ground alternative: controlled natural language (CNL).
Why not require models to express their reasoning in a controlled fragment of English that can then
be formally translated into logic and computationally analyzed?

A
: CNL sounds like the best of both worlds (natural and formal languages), but in
this setting it would cause greater difficulties. Crucially, CNL here would not be
given as
input
to the models (as is widely done, for example, in benchmarks like
RuleTaker
[
28
]
, see Section
2
), but would instead be required
as the
output
of the models. This immediately presents two problems.

First, the CNL must be formally defined and its entire spec must be given to the model, so that
the model knows exactly what it is allowed to say and what sort of sentences are off limits.
The definition would need to fully specify allowable sentence structures, anaphoras, and tense and voice;
the interpretation of conjunctions; the expression of implications; scope rules; the introduction and discharge
of assumptions; what counts as a valid reference; how to disambiguate ambiguous constructions; and so on.
This would be orders of magnitude harder than specifying NDL.
The issue is that, for all practical purposes, any CNL is itself a formal language. It must commit to
a grammar, a vocabulary, scoping and disambiguation rules, an interpretation function, and so on.
The main difference is that its syntax is less precise, its semantics are harder to specify,
and its failure modes are less transparent. By contrast, a language like NDL has a tiny grammar (it can be written
in BNF with a handful of productions) and its operational semantics
can be fully described in a page or two of text.

Second, CNL would not eliminate issues of procedural control.
There are no guarantees that models would respect the formal spec
and reliably remain within the bounds of the prescribed fragment,
just as they may fail to respect the rules of NDL.
Models would likely be “almost compliant” while drifting outside the allowed fragment,
as a single stray clause, pronoun, or tense change can break parsability.
With NDL, we can detect and repair or overlook minor deviations, as we have done with
our instrumented proof checker. Achieving the same level of robustness for a CNL would be much more challenging.

•

Q3
: Why NDL? NDL has hardly ever been seen by models during their pretraining.
Isn’t it unfair to make them reason in what is essentially a foreign language to them,
and aren’t a lot of the model errors you’ve encountered due to this unfamiliarity?
Why not use a language like Lean or Isabelle, which are much more extensively represented
in a typical pretraining corpus?

A
: A reasoning benchmark should be universally applicable to any model,
without requiring prior exposure to a specific formal system.
In fact, if we are serious about testing fluid reasoning, we should minimize reliance
on the sort of crystallized knowledge and skills that accrue when a model ingests large
amounts of pretraining material that it can directly leverage at test time.
We want to measure reasoning ability, not tool familiarity. If a model succeeds in Lean because
it saw a lot of it in training and has therefore memorized common patterns and tactic heuristics, then
we are testing recall and imitation just as much as we are testing reasoning.
We should be testing whether a model can internalize and follow a system
of rigorous rules from specification, not whether it has memorized one.
For testing purposes, the absence of strong NDL priors is an advantage, not a liability.

Another point (already made in Section
2
, but it bears repeating here)
is that Lean proofs are procedural and tightly coupled with proof search in a sequent calculus.
This is unsurprising, given that Lean is an interactive theorem-proving system and thus naturally
more focused on tactic orchestration and automation steering. NDL, by contrast, is more focused
on describing the proofs themselves, rather than the search for them. It
mirrors natural deduction, expresses proofs entirely declaratively, makes assumption and logical
scopes explicit, and separates logical structure from proof search.

Further, as we also already pointed out in Section
2
, languages
like Lean are intentionally powerful and thus correspondingly complex, featuring esoteric syntax forms, heavy type theory,
opaque automation, and a large ecosystem of libraries, modules, and built-in theories.
This makes Lean very expressive and versatile, but also unsuitable as a fruit fly for rigorous reasoning.
NDL, by contrast, is intentionally minimal. It fits fully in a short prompt, has no hidden machinery or
inference automation, no meta-level tactics, and no library dependencies.
This makes it cleaner, simpler, entirely self-contained, tooling-independent,
much more diagnostic (because its simplicity exposes real inferential limitations),
and much more appropriate as a controlled testbed for reasoning.

Finally, with respect to the fairness concern, we restate our earlier position: the main role
of a benchmark is to enable meaningful comparison across models, not to extract their maximum achievable performance.
A benchmark is a diagnostic instrument, not an exercise in system optimization.
Fairness is ensured by clearly specifying the evaluation criteria and applying them
consistently to all models.

•

Q4
: Why instrument the proof checker? Doesn’t that help the models to cheat?

A
: Instrumentation has been a sharp analytical tool.
While NDL may be exceptionally simple, it is still a formal language
and strict checking of NDL proofs would flatline the accuracy of
many models at exceedingly low levels, often due to minor procedural slips.
This would collapse multiple failure modes into a single low score,
resulting in poor discrimination and little information about latent reasoning competence.
By contrast, our instrumented proof checker reveals
a graded competence spectrum, large separation between models, and internally coherent
error profiles that correlate with other behavioral indicators.
Far from helping models cheat, instrumentation restores measurement resolution
and disentangles executive brittleness from higher-level reasoning.

•

Q5
:
You claim that using abstract symbolic problems allows you to test fluid reasoning
because the problems don’t have any content (other than pure logical content),
and so the models cannot leverage their huge knowledge banks to retrieve memorized answers.
However,
the pretraining of modern models exposes them to a vast range of proof templates,
patterns of symbolic manipulation, typical lemma structures, and so forth.
This means that even abstract tasks can partially reduce to pattern reuse.
Doesn’t it follow then that
ProofGrid
-style benchmarks test the ability to generalize reasoning
strategies absorbed during pre- (and post-) training, rather than tabula-rasa reasoning?

A
: That’s a valid point, and we agree with it in part. Our claim is not that

ProofGrid
measures tabula-rasa or psychometrically pure fluid reasoning in any strong sense.
Contemporary models plainly rely on pretraining to acquire the concepts of proof,
logical entailment,
symbolic manipulation, and deductive
structure. In that respect,
ProofGrid
tasks (like essentially
every benchmark for advanced cognition) tests the ability to deploy and generalize previously
learned reasoning strategies, rather than reasoning ex nihilo. The point of
ProofGrid
is narrower and
more concrete: it probes a model’s native analytical competence in symbolic reasoning,
particularly deductive reasoning, and does so in a small, simple, and fully specified
formalism, on abstract problems whose solutions are extremely unlikely to be directly memorized.
Because the problems are content-free and procedurally constrained, the model cannot rely
on world knowledge, standard textbook proofs, or domain-specific theorem memories.
It must instead instantiate general proof skills in a new setting. So the benchmark is best
understood not as a measure of pure fluid intelligence, but as a measure of a model’s
ability to compose, verify, analyze, and manipulate proofs under minimal
semantic scaffolding.

It is also worth reiterating that all nontrivial cognitive benchmarks test the reuse of
learned strategies. Human IQ tests do too. Nobody reasons from nowhere. Humans bring years
of schooling, language, symbol use, and learned problem schemas to Raven matrices,
analogies, and logic puzzles. The relevant question is not whether prior learning matters,
but what kind of prior learning is useful and how directly task success can be reduced to retrieval.
It may be useful to distinguish three different phenomena: (a) memorization of specific proofs;
(b) reuse of learned proof methods; and (c) the ability to coordinate these methods
correctly during reasoning when tackling a novel problem.
The symbolic nature of
ProofGrid
helps to rule out (a): simple regurgitation of famous contentful
proofs. This is what we would get if we were just asking for proofs of the infinity of primes or
the irrationality of the square root of 2.

ProofGrid
is not robust against (b), nor should it be.
If a model has learned useful proof techniques, such as proof by contradiction
and case analysis, that is part of what mathematical competence consists in.
And what
ProofGrid
probes quite effectively is (c): whether a model can compose
these techniques correctly in a rigorous setting.

•

Q6
: Many of the problems in
ProofGrid
, especially in tasks like
PL3-PW
, look harder
than what most humans could realistically solve by hand. If humans themselves would struggle to solve
these problems, why should we regard such tasks as a fair test for models rather than an
artificially superhuman bar? And if the strongest models are already scoring fairly well on these
difficult problems (some of which have 50-100 premises), doesn’t that suggest that they already
surpass humans in sustained, hard reasoning?

A
: A benchmark is not unfair simply because humans would do poorly on it.
As already mentioned, fairness in benchmarking requires that the rules are explicit
and evenly enforced, the scoring is consistent, and the tasks avoid obscure content knowledge,
highly exotic/specialized skills, or long-context artifacts. A more relevant question is not
whether a graduate student can solve a task, but whether the task is a clean, interpretable, and discriminating
probe of the capability we want to measure—rigorous proof-based reasoning in our case.

There are, broadly speaking, two strands of benchmarking work in AI. The first and older tradition
has a more psychometric flavor and uses human ability as its reference level,
typically for the purpose of comparing human performance against that of computer programs.
This can make benchmarks conservative and anthropocentric.
Additionally, insisting on human-level difficulty as a fairness criterion risks conflating psychological
ease with problem-solving essence. Arithmetic with 100-digit numbers is unfair to humans but can be perfectly
legitimate for models as a measure of their capacity for numerical computation.

More importantly, once models catch up to human baselines, these benchmarks quickly become saturated.
And if human solvability is treated as a prerequisite, they cannot be expanded as needed
to keep up with the technology. This explains why benchmarking in games like chess and Go
continues but has now shifted from questions like “Can a computer beat the best human?” to
engine-vs-engine rating lists and tournaments. More generally, beyond board games,
this is also why the last two years have seen the emergence of a new class of benchmarks
that are not anchored on ordinary human ability. Instead, they target either expert-human
ability, as in Humanity’s Last Exam; superhuman ability, as in FrontierMath’s open-problem track; or
operational capabilities that we care about regardless of human performance, as in SWE-bench, where
the key question is whether the issue gets resolved correctly in a reproducible environment.

ProofGrid
falls mainly into this last category.

Instead of taking humans as the baseline, benchmarks like
ProofGrid
can start to locate language
models with respect to a different and in some ways more relevant and demanding reference class: symbolic
inference techniques, like theorem provers and automated reasoning procedures in general. On that scale, current language models remain far
from robust “hard” reasoners; the problems in
ProofGrid
are still trivial by the standards of mature
methods such as resolution or Knuth-Bendix completion. But part of what makes
ProofGrid
useful is precisely
that it begins to quantify that gap. This makes the benchmark relevant not only to abstract questions
about reasoning, but also to practical domains such as high-assurance software engineering, where
the long-term goal is not merely automated generation of code, but automated generation of

reliable
code, accompanied by robust justification artifacts, perhaps even including,
eventually and when appropriate, machine-generated proofs of correctness.

This brings us to the other relevant question, whether the benchmark is measuring a capability that we should
care about. The ability to carry out deep chains of inference is an important component of
highly intelligent systems; it is crucial in science and (especially) mathematics, but also, as just mentioned,
in software and other types of engineering. In these domains, correctness is often non-negotiable, and
small errors can propagate into large failures if not rigorously controlled. Systems capable
of sustained, internally consistent reasoning could play a transformative role.

Finally, on the question of whether the current performance of leading models shows that they are
already better reasoners than humans, the answer depends on how broadly we understand reasoning.
If we construe it as the general ability to solve inference-intensive problems, then the answer is no.
Humans can often solve such problems with ease by offloading parts of the work to external tools
of their own making, such as notes and proof assistants, and by drawing on higher-level abstractions and insight.
Models are just now approaching the point where they can generate long and rigorously valid
inference chains under strict rules, chains that are deeper and more exacting than what an unaided
human could manage.
98
98

98

They are not quite there yet, as shown by the various reasoning
pathologies documented in this article.

On that important but narrow front, humans were already surpassed decades ago by
automated theorem provers. But humans remain far better at decomposing problems and at socially scaffolded
forms of problem solving, including the use of tools and abstractions built up by other humans,
through a process that cultural psychologists call
cumulative cultural evolution

[
92
]
. Language models can ingest the total stock of human knowledge
up to a fixed snapshot in time, but it remains to be seen whether agentic collections of models
will eventually be able to simulate this evolutionary process computationally, and achieve on their
own the same cumulative ratchet effect that powers human problem solving.

11
Limitations

While
ProofGrid
offers a wide-ranging and deep evaluation of
logical reasoning capabilities in language models, a number of limitations
should be acknowledged.

First,
ProofGrid
intentionally tests logical reasoning in (minimally) formal languages,
not natural-language reasoning.
Thus,
ProofGrid
’s results may not fully
generalize to models’ reasoning abilities in natural language
contexts. However, as we argue in the paper, tests of natural-language
reasoning are outcome-based and thus only gauge reasoning competence indirectly.

Second, our evaluation metrics focus on
accuracy rather than efficiency. Different models may achieve similar
accuracy scores while varying significantly in test-time compute
or other resource requirements. A more comprehensive evaluation would include these dimensions.

Third, while propositional and equational logic are rich and fundamental domains,
they do not exhaust the broader landscape of reasoning abilities that a
comprehensive benchmark should measure. In particular, the present tasks in
ProofGrid
do not evaluate phenomena that arise in settings with quantification, variable binding,
or more expressive semantic structures. An important direction for future work is
to broaden the benchmark’s coverage.

Finally, given the rapid advancement in AI capabilities, particularly
in reasoning-focused models, the relative performance of various model
families may change significantly in the not-too-distant future.
In particular, the empirical rankings we report here should be understood
as a snapshot tied to the models available during the time period of our investigation,
rather than a stable characterization of model capabilities.
This kind of temporal fragility is increasingly unavoidable in contemporary
benchmark studies, especially in fast-moving areas of model development.
We therefore expect that some of the comparative conclusions in this paper
will need to be revisited as newer systems become available.

Despite these limitations,
ProofGrid
represents a significant advancement in the
evaluation of logical reasoning in AI systems, offering insights into
both the impressive progress and the remaining challenges in this crucial domain.

Acknowledgments

We thank Theo Evgeniou for the fruitful discussions that helped to spark this project and contributed to shaping its initial direction.

Author Contributions

The first author conceived the benchmark, designed the tasks, generated the datasets,
developed the prompts, led the analysis of the results, and wrote the manuscript (with the exception
of Section
9
and Appendix
C
, which were written jointly).
Both authors contributed to the experimental infrastructure.
The second author led the execution of large-scale experiments across multiple models, including
parallelized API-based evaluation pipelines, and contributed to debugging, validation, analysis,
experimental reliability, and the provisioning of substantial computational resources.
The first author additionally defined and implemented NDL and
\mbox{\rm NDL}_{0}
,
introduced the Epistemic Stability Index (ESI), proposed the use of IRT and Fisher information,
and carried out the implementation and analysis of the 2PL models.

Appendix A
NDL

NDL is a minimalist but rigorously defined formal language for writing natural-deduction proofs.
It is:

1.

expressive enough to represent standard natural-deduction proofs;

2.

simple enough to admit a precise interpreter-based semantics;

3.

unforgiving enough to expose reasoning errors rather than hide them behind informal prose.

Below is a sample proof that was already seen earlier, deriving the conclusion
(B\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}D)
from the three
premises
(A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}B)
,
(\neg A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C)
, and
(C\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}D)
. Comments in NDL start with the hash sign
#
.

premise-2

:=

C)

(C

by

a

case

on

(A

which

holds

by

the

of
