Checking an NDL proof
D
amounts to evaluating
D
by running it through the NDL
interpreter
\mathcal{I}
.
99
99

99

We use the letter
D
as a variable ranging
over NDL proofs (
D
stands for “deduction,” which we use interchangeably with “proof”).

The evaluation of a proof can only result in one of two outcomes: either the proof works and it produces
a unique
conclusion

p
as its result, or the proof fails and its evaluation
generates a relevant error message.

An NDL proof is always evaluated in the context of—or
relative to—an
assumption base

\beta
. More precisely, the NDL interpreter
\mathcal{I}
is a
function of two arguments: a proof
D
and an assumption base
\beta
. Thus, we may only write
\mathcal{I}(D,\text{$\beta$})
,
not
\mathcal{I}(D)
.
100
100

100

Unless we explicitly
curry

\mathcal{I}
and give it the signature:

\mathcal{I}:\mbox{\bf Ded}\mbox{$\,\rightarrow\>$}\mbox{\bf AB}\mbox{$\,\rightarrow\>$}\mbox{\bf P}\cup\{\mbox{error\/}\}
,
so that it takes a proof
D\in\mbox{\bf Ded}
followed by an assumption base
\text{$\beta$}\in\mbox{\bf AB}
and outputs
either a formula
p\in\mbox{\bf P}
or the distinguished constant
error
. In that case,
\mathcal{I}(D)

would be a unary function of an assumption base. Here we stick with the rectangular signature

\mathcal{I}:\mbox{\bf Ded}\times\mbox{\bf AB}\mbox{$\,\rightarrow\>$}\mbox{\bf P}\cup\{\mbox{error\/}\}
.

An assumption base is just a finite set of formulas that are taken for granted for the purposes of a proof.
Initially, the assumption base is empty:
\text{$\beta$}=\mbox{$\emptyset$}
. The keyword
assert
is then used
to insert—and name—as many premises as we like into the top-level (global) assumption base.
By convention, premises in
ProofGrid
are
always sequentially named:
premise-1
,
premise-2
,
etc.
These names can then be used to refer to the
corresponding premises without having to write out the formulas inline. In this example we have three
assert
directives on lines
1–3, which insert the corresponding premises into the assumption base. Thus, by
the time we get to line 4, the top-level
\beta
contains exactly 3 formulas.

Following the assertions, we have the main proof (lines 6–26). This is a
composite
proof of the form
\mbox{\tt\lx@text@lbrace}D_{1};D_{2};\cdots;D_{m}\mbox{\tt\lx@text@rbrace}
,
where
D_{1},\ldots,D_{m}
are the
subproofs
being composed. The evaluation of this proof starts on line 6,
in the assumption base that contains the 3 premises that were asserted earlier. As control flows through the proof,
the assumption base grows and shrinks to reflect the conclusions derived by the various rule applications, such as
the application of
ex-middle
on line 7 or
mp
(modus ponens) on line 12; or the hypotheses
p
that
are conditionally postulated when we enter a subproof of the form
\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{assume}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p\mbox{$\;\>$}{}\mbox{\mbox{$D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}
and then automatically
discharged after
D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
.
In this example, the first element of the composition is the application
of
ex-middle
to
A
,
which produces the conclusion

(A\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg A)
. The proof then proceeds
by a case analysis of this disjunction. It first shows, on lines 10–15, that
if
we assume
A
,
we can derive the target conclusion
(B\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}D)
; and then it shows (lines 18-22) that we can do the same
if we assume the negation of
A
. Since we already know from line 7 that either
A

or its negation holds, case analysis (captured by the inference rule
cases
) allows us to infer the
desired
(B\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}D)
(line 25).

In general, a core NDL proof
D
is one of three forms:

1.

A
rule application
, namely, an application of an inference rule
R
to a number of arguments
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
, written as

R\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{on}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}

(17)

where
R
ranges over 24 built-in inference rules, from introduction and elimination rules for the 5 logical connectives to higher-level
rules that are included for convenience, such as
dsyl
(disjunctive syllogism)
and
dm
(De Morgan’s).
101
101

101

A complete list of NDL’s inference rules and their operational semantics
can be found online in the prompts for the tasks that involve NDL, such as
PL1-PW
.

Typically, a rule application of the form (
17
) works as follows: if the arguments
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
are in the assumption base, then
R
derives
some appropriate conclusion
p
, which becomes the result of (
17
). But if some
p_{i}
is not in the assumption base,
the application fails with an appropriate error message.

For instance, consider the application of
mp

to
premise-1
and
A
, on line 12. At that point in the proof, both of these arguments are in the assumption base:
premise-1

is in the assumption base because it was put there by the
assert
on line 1, while
A
is in the assumption base because
it was postulated as a hypothesis by the
assume
on line 10, and at line 12 we are still squarely inside the scope of that hypothesis (meaning
that
A
is still in the assumption base), as that scope does not close until line 15.
Thus, because both of its arguments are in the assumption base,
mp
happily produces the
conclusion
B
, which becomes the output or result of the step on line 12.

If one of these arguments were
not
in the assumption base, the application of
mp

would cause an error. For instance, suppose that line 12 was instead “
D
BY
mp
on
(A

==>

D), A
”. In that case the first argument,

(A

==>

D)
, would not be in the assumption base, and the application would be rejected as unsound.

While this is the typical mode of operation, it is not always the case that every single argument
p_{i}
in a rule application (
17
)
needs to be in the assumption base at the time of the application. This depends on the rule
R
. For example, the unary rule
ex-middle
takes
an arbitrary formula
p
as its sole argument and always produces the conclusion
(p\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg p)
, without caring about whether
p

is in the assumption base. (Intuitively, this is because the conclusion

(p\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}\neg p)
is always valid; whether
p
is in the assumption base is immaterial.)

2.

A
conditional proof
of the form

\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{assume}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{\mbox{$D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}},

(18)

where
p
is the
hypothesis
and
D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
is the
body
of the conditional proof, representing the
scope
of the hypothesis
p
.
The evaluation of such a proof in a given
\beta

is straightforward: we add the hypothesis
p
to
\beta

and evaluate the body
D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
in
\text{$\beta$}\cup\{p\}
;
if and when that evaluation produces a conclusion
q
, we produce the conditional

(p\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}q)
as the result of (
18
).
Otherwise, if the evaluation of
D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
in
\text{$\beta$}\cup\{p\}
generates an error, that error is returned as the result of (
18
).

3.

A
composite proof
(or simply “composition”) of the form
\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@lbrace}D_{1};D_{2}\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@rbrace}
, or more generally,

\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@lbrace}D_{1};D_{2};\cdots;D_{m}\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@rbrace}
for
m>1
.
102
102

102

The composition operator
;
associates to the right, so

\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@lbrace}D_{1};D_{2};\cdots;D_{m}\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@rbrace}
is syntax sugar for
\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@lbrace}D_{1};\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@lbrace}D_{2};\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@lbrace}\cdots D_{m}\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@rbrace}\cdots\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@rbrace}\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@rbrace}\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@rbrace}
.

Composition is the lemma-formation
mechanism in NDL. To evaluate a proof of this form in a given
\beta
, we start out by evaluating
D_{1}
in
\beta
. If that yields a conclusion
p_{1}
,
we proceed to evaluate
D_{2}
in
\text{$\beta$}\cup\{p_{1}\}
. Thus,
the conclusion of
D_{1}
is now available in
D_{2}
(and indeed in every subsequent
D_{i}
),
essentially becoming a lemma that downstream proofs can use as needed. If the evaluation of
D_{2}
in
\text{$\beta$}\cup\{p_{1}\}
produces a conclusion
p_{2}
,
we go on to evaluate
D_{3}
in
\text{$\beta$}\cup\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
, and so on. Continuing this way, the conclusion of the rightmost subproof
D_{m}
becomes the conclusion
of the entire composition.
If any
D_{i}
generates an error, then the entire composition
produces that error as its output.

Note that rule applications are primitive or atomic proofs,
i.e.
, they do not contains any subproofs. Conditional
proofs and compositions are complex, meaning that they (recursively) contain subproofs.

Any NDL proof
D
can also be written in conclusion-annotated style, as
p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{BY}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}D
. This can be viewed as an annotation or a promise of sorts:
It says that the evaluation of proof
D
(in some appropriate
\beta
) must produce the conclusion
p
. Accordingly,
it is an error if we evaluate
D
and obtain a conclusion
\mbox{\mbox{$p^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}\neq p
.
This construct does not introduce any new inferential power, it only enforces alignment between proof intent and result.

The above was a description of both the concrete and abstract syntax of NDL, and indeed of its formal semantics as well. We essentially
defined the interpreter
\mathcal{I}
as shown in Figure
48
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(R\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{on}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},\text{$\beta$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mbox{\mbox{$q^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}&\mbox{if $R\equiv\mbox{\tt mp}$, $n=2$, $p$ is of the form $(q\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\mbox{\mbox{$q^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}})$, $p_{2}\equiv q$, and $\{p_{1},p_{2}\}\subseteq\text{$\beta$}$; }\\
(p_{1}\>\&\>p_{2})&\mbox{if $R\equiv\mbox{\tt both}$, $n=2$, and $\{p_{1},p_{2}\}\subseteq\text{$\beta$}$; }\\
q_{1}&\mbox{if $R\equiv\mbox{\tt left-and}$, $n=1$, $p_{1}$ is of the form $(q_{1}\>\&\>q_{2})$, and $\{p_{1}\}\subseteq\text{$\beta$}$; }\\
q_{2}&\mbox{if $R\equiv\mbox{\tt right-and}$, $n=1$, $p_{1}$ is of the form $(q_{1}\>\&\>q_{2})$, and $\{p_{1}\}\subseteq\text{$\beta$}$; }\\
(p\>\mbox{$\;|\;$}\raisebox{3.01385pt}{\texttildelow}\mbox{$\,$}{}p)&\mbox{if $R\equiv\mbox{\tt ex-middle}$, $n=1$, and $p_{1}\equiv p$; }\\
(p_{1}\>\mbox{$\;|\;$}\>p_{2})&\mbox{if $R\equiv\mbox{\tt left-either}$, $n=2$, and $\{p_{1}\}\subseteq\text{$\beta$}$; }\\
(p_{1}\>\mbox{$\;|\;$}\>p_{2})&\mbox{if $R\equiv\mbox{\tt right-either}$, $n=2$, and $\{p_{2}\}\subseteq\text{$\beta$}$; }\\
r&\mbox{if $R\equiv\mbox{\tt cases}$, $n=3$, $p_{1}=(q_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}q_{2})$, $p_{2}=(q_{1}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}r)$, $p_{3}=(q_{2}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}r)$, $\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}\subseteq\text{$\beta$}$; }\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
\mbox{error\/}&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{assume}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{\mbox{$D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}},\text{$\beta$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mbox{\mbox{$D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}},\text{$\beta$}\cup\{p\})

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\big\{D_{1};D_{2}\big\},\text{$\beta$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(D_{2},\text{$\beta$}\cup\{\mathcal{I}(D_{1},\text{$\beta$})\})

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{BY}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}D,\text{$\beta$})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}p&\mbox{if $\mathcal{I}(D,\text{$\beta$})=p$}\\
\mbox{error\/}&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}

Figure 48:
Definition of the NDL interpreter function.

Here is the main soundness and completeness result for NDL.

Theorem 1
:

If
\mathcal{I}(D,\text{$\beta$})=p
then
\text{$\beta$}\models p
. Conversely, if
\text{$\beta$}\models p
then there is some NDL proof
D
such that
\mathcal{I}(D,\text{$\beta$})=p
.

The first (soundness) half says that if a proof successfully derives
p
from some
\beta
, then
p
follows logically from
\beta
.
The second half (completeness) says that every logical consequence of
\beta

is derivable in NDL. We do not prove these results here, but briefly,
soundness is shown by a straightforward structural induction, while completeness is most easily established by showing that a logic
system that is already known to be complete can be embedded in NDL.

Along with a full listing of the various inference rules, this constitutes a complete description of NDL.
The lone bell-and-whistle that NDL allows beyond what we have already covered is this:
naming the results of intermediate subproofs and/or hypotheses.
Any element
D_{j}
of a composite proof
\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@lbrace}D_{1};D_{2};\cdots;D_{m}\mbox{\tt\big\lx@text@rbrace}

can be instead written as
I:=D_{j}
, and
then going forward (for all subsequent deductions
D_{j^{\prime}}
,
j^{\prime}>j
), the name
I
will be bound to the
conclusion produced by
D_{j}
. Likewise, any conditional proof
\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{assume}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}{}p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{\mbox{$D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}
can instead be written
as
\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4414,0.7383}\bf{assume}}}\mbox{$\;\>$}I\mbox{\tt:=}p\mbox{$\;\>$}\mbox{\mbox{$D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}
, in which case the name
I
will become bound to the hypothesis
p

inside (and only inside) the body
D^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
. Thus, for example, we can write:

⬇

1

l

:=

(A

&

B)

BY

left-and

on

hyp;

2

r

:=

(C

&

D)

BY

right-and

on

hyp;

3

(r

&

l)

BY

both

on

r,

l

4

}

and this will produce the conditional
(((A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}B)\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}(C\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}D))\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}((C\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}D)\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}(A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}B))))
.
103
103

103

Since
NDL is a proper subset of Athena, all proofs in this paper can be evaluated online at

https://tryathena.org/
. But note that Athena does not treat the names

A
,
B
,
\ldots
, as special in any way, so to declare these as atomic sentences,
the line
declare
A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H: Boolean
should be prepended to your input.

Strictly speaking, this means that the interpreter
\mathcal{I}
takes 3 arguments: a proof
D
, an
assumption base
\beta
, and a
lexical environment

\rho
that maps names to formulas. For those
with a background in programming language theory, extending the denotational-semantics definition of
\mathcal{I}

given here to account for
\rho
is a straightforward exercise.

For simplicity, we assumed that there is a single distinguished value
error
, but in practice
the NDL interpreter produces different types of error messages. As discussed elsewhere,
most errors
104
104

104

Some errors do not neatly fit into these categories (for instance,
references to out-of-scope identifiers).
fall into one of the following three categories:

•

Syntax errors
: These could involve either formulas (
e.g.
, unbalanced parentheses)
or NDL-specific constructs (
e.g.
, extra or missing curly braces, hallucinated syntax forms, and so on).

•

Structural errors
: These are caused by an incorrect application of an inference rule,
e.g.
, to the wrong number of arguments or to
arguments of the wrong form.

•

Logic errors
: These occur when a rule application appeals to a formula that does not currently hold (is not in the current assumption base).

Appendix B
Item Response Theory

B.1
Introduction

Item Response Theory (IRT)
[
38
,
73
]
is a family of statistical models for developing
tests and for analyzing task or benchmark data at the level of individual items/problems.
Although IRT was not used in the development of
ProofGrid
, we advocate for its use in analyzing
and understanding various tasks in AI benchmarks, in assessing their discriminating ability,
and in interpreting benchmark results. In this appendix we introduce IRT and illustrate its
application to a number of
ProofGrid
tasks. The theory is valuable for transparency because it
can reveal both the strengths and limitations of a benchmark. In our case, IRT indicates
that most
ProofGrid
tasks are highly effective at discriminating mid-tier models, and
several also separate top-tier models well, but none are particularly effective
at distinguishing much weaker models.

Instead of summarizing performance only with aggregate accuracy, IRT models the probability
that a respondent answers a particular item correctly as a function of two kinds of latent
variables: properties of the respondent (typically an unobserved
ability
or proficiency,
usually denoted by
\theta
) and properties of the item (such as its difficulty and, in richer models
like the ones we build here, its discrimination or susceptibility to guessing). In this way,
IRT treats observed responses as noisy measurements generated by an underlying interaction
between respondents and items, rather than as interchangeable Bernoulli trials.

We assume here that a model’s responses on a given task are driven primarily by one underlying latent variable.
This is known as
unidimensionality
, and it’s a standard assumption of IRT.
105
105

105

Multidimensional
IRT models are possible, but unidimensionality is the default in most applications.

This dimension is allowed to vary from task to task. Proof checking
may tap a different ability than proof writing, and proof masking and gap filling may
rely on other abilities still. Unidimensionality never quite holds in
practice, since there are always multiple factors impinging on any test taker’s performance, from
additional abilities (beyond the one that the task aims to measure) to more peripheral
factors like motivation (in the case of humans) or sampling parameters like temperature (in the case of
language models). The assumption can be considered sufficiently satisfied as long as there is
one
dominant
factor responsible for the test taker’s performance
[
37
]
[p. 17].
Essentially, what matters is that once we know a subject’s position on the relevant latent axis,
there should not be a large amount of systematic variation left over that would call for explanation
in terms of additional hidden skills. In addition, task items are supposed to be conditionally independent,
an assumption that can be more problematic for some AI benchmarks; we discuss this in greater
detail on p.
B.2
.

A key benefit of IRT is that it separates benchmark difficulty from model capability.
Two language models can have the same overall accuracy while differing in the kinds
of problems they can solve; likewise, two problems solved correctly by the same fraction of
models need not be equally informative. IRT makes these distinctions explicit by estimating item
parameters and placing models and items on a shared latent scale. This supports analyses
such as identifying unusually easy or hard items, finding items that best distinguish between
stronger and weaker models, and constructing score estimates that are less dependent on
the particular mix of items in a benchmark task.

For small to moderately sized benchmark tasks, like those in
ProofGrid
, all of which have fewer than 1K items,
Wright maps are a particularly useful part of the IRT toolbox.
A Wright map is a visualization that puts test takers and test items on the same scale.
On the left, it shows where the respondents fall, from lower-performing to higher-performing.
On the right, it shows where the problems fall, from easier to harder. The key idea is that
both are placed on one common line, so one can immediately see whether the questions are well
matched to the group being tested. If most questions sit far below the test takers, the test
is too easy; if they sit far above them, it is too hard. A Wright map also shows whether the
questions cover the full range of ability or bunch up in just one part of the scale.
In that way, it gives a quick visual summary of how informative and well targeted a task is.
We publish Wright maps for all
ProofGrid
tasks for which we have built IRT models; see
the last four sections of this appendix for the Wright maps of
PL1-PM
,
PL3-PC
,

PL4-PW
, and select
PL3-PW
subtasks.

In general, in the context of AI evaluation IRT provides a principled way to move from raw benchmark
outcomes to a measurement model of competence. This can be useful when comparing systems
across benchmark versions, studying whether some questions are redundant or poorly calibrated,
or asking whether improvements reflect broad capability gains versus progress on a narrow subset
of items.

B.2
Modeling and ICCs

We assume we have
N
respondents and
M
task items (problems). In this paper the respondents are language
models and
N=24
. Taking
PL1-PW
as an example, we would have
M=400
(the number of items
in
PL2-PW
). We also assume we have test data: a binary response matrix
\mbox{\bf y}\in\{0,1\}^{N\times M}
,
where
\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}
indicates whether model
i
solved problem
j
correctly. For
PL2-PW
,
\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}=1

means that model
i
wrote a proof that derived the expected result for problem
j
, and
\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}=0

means that the model’s output was incorrect.

In IRT we begin by specifying a probabilistic response model for each item
j
.
In the 2PL model,
106
106

106

In IRT (and psychometrics in general), a “2PL model” refers to a 2-parameter logistic model.

task item
j
is associated with an item response function of the following form:
107
107

107

We write
\sigma

for the sigmoid function,
\sigma(x)=1/(1+e^{-x})
.

P(Y_{j}=1\mid\theta,a_{j},b_{j})=\sigma(a_{j}(\theta-b_{j}))=\frac{1}{1+e^{-a_{j}(\theta-b_{j})}},

(19)

which gives the probability that a hypothetical respondent of ability
\theta

answers item
j
correctly, given the item’s key characteristics, namely its

discrimination

a_{j}
and its
difficulty

b_{j}
.

It is important to understand the intuition behind the algebraic form
a_{j}(\theta-b_{j})
. Why that
particular expression and not some other linear combination
c_{1}\theta+c_{2}a_{j}+c_{3}b_{j}
?
The first thing to note is that
(\theta-b_{j})
represents how much the respondent’s ability exceeds
the item’s difficulty. If
\theta
is greater than
b_{j}
, we should expect the probability of a correct
response to be greater than a random guess, i.e. greater than 1/2. If
\theta
is less than
b_{j}
, we
should expect the probability of a correct response to be less than 1/2. And if
\theta
is equal to
b_{j}
,
it should be anybody’s guess whether the response will be correct,
i.e.
,
\theta=b_{j}

should imply
P(Y_{j}=1\mid\theta,a_{j},b_{j})=0.5
. And indeed,
when
\theta=b_{j}
, the expression on the right-hand side of (
19
)
becomes 1/2. So this is the first key idea, that the chance of success depends on the margin
\theta-b_{j}
.
The second key idea is that
a_{j}
controls how sharply the probability changes as that margin changes.
If the item has high discriminating power, then a small increase in ability makes a big difference in the
odds of a correct response, whereas if
a_{j}
is small then the item is fuzzier and the probability of success
changes much more gradually. This is what multiplying
a_{j}
by the margin
(\theta-b_{j})
gives us.
To make this concrete, Table
31
shows how the probability of success varies for different sample values of

\alpha
and the margin
\theta-b
.

\alpha\backslash(\theta-b_{j})

-3

-2

-1

-0.5

0

0.5

1

2

3

0.2

0.354

0.401

0.450

0.475

0.500

0.525

0.550

0.599

0.646

0.5

0.182

0.269

0.378

0.438

0.500

0.562

0.622

0.731

0.818

1

0.047

0.119

0.269

0.378

0.500

0.622

0.731

0.881

0.953

2

0.002

0.018

0.119

0.269

0.500

0.731

0.881

0.982

0.998

3

0.000

0.002

0.047

0.182

0.500

0.818

0.953

1.0

Table 31:
Success probabilities under the 2PL model,

p=\sigma\!\bigl(\alpha(\theta-b_{j})\bigr)
,
for several values of the discrimination parameter
\alpha
and the
margin
\theta-b_{j}
. As the margin increases, the probability of success
rises; larger values of
\alpha
make this rise steeper.

In summary:

•

When ability equals difficulty, the item sits at its “middle point” and the probability of success is 1/2.

•

Keeping the difficulty
b_{j}
constant, higher ability increases the chances of success.

•

Keeping the ability
\theta
constant, higher difficulty decreases the chances of success.

•

Discrimination controls the sensitivity of the curve,

i.e.
, how sharply the margin matters.

We can visualize a probability model of the form (
19
) by plotting the
probability on the
y
-axis as a function of ability
\theta
on the
x
-axis.
By the definition of (
19
), the result will be a sigmoid curve.
The item’s difficulty
b_{j}
shifts the curve to the left or right, while
a_{j}

determines its slope. The resulting plot is called an
item characteristic curve
(ICC).
Figure
49
overlays three sample ICCs on the same axes, for these
three
PL2-PW
problems: items 2, 13, and 41.

Figure 49:
Three ICCs for three sample
PL2-PW
problems.

Again, the horizontal axis is model ability
\theta
and the vertical axis
is the probability of answering an item correctly. Each S-shaped curve
corresponds to one benchmark item. Where a curve crosses the 50% line marks that
item’s difficulty
b
: curves shifted to the right (like item 2,
b\approx 1.19
),
are harder—you need higher
\theta
to reach a 50% success chance. Curves shifted to
the left (like item 41,
b\approx-0.68
) are easier, with even lower-ability models having
a good chance at writing a correct proof. The thin vertical lines (in light blue color)
at each
b
visualize this “where
P=0.5
” point, and the darker vertical line at
\theta=0
plus the horizontal
line at
P=0.5
help to identify those crossings.

The steepness of a curve around its midpoint reflects the item’s discrimination
\alpha
—how sharply
it separates nearby ability levels. A steeper curve (e.g., item 13 with
\alpha\approx 2.45
) makes a big
probability jump with a small change in
\theta
, so it’s excellent for telling slightly weaker
from slightly stronger models near its difficulty. A flatter curve
changes more gradually, so it’s less informative for fine-grained ranking. In short, horizontal position
reflects difficulty (
b
), while slope reflects discrimination (
a
). Looking at all three together
shows how this task mixes easier and harder questions, and which ones are better at distinguishing models
that are close in overall skill.

We can estimate the
N+2\cdot M
parameters
\theta_{i},a_{j},b_{j}
(the ability of each respondent
plus each item’s discrimination and difficulty) by maximizing the likelihood of the observed data
y
:

\displaystyle(\{\hat{\theta}_{i}\},\{\hat{a}_{j}\},\{\hat{b}_{j}\})

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\{\theta_{i}\},\{a_{j}\},\{b_{j}\}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\>\prod_{j=1}^{M}P(Y_{ij}=\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}\mid\theta_{i},a_{j},b_{j})

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\{\theta_{i}\},\{a_{j}\},\{b_{j}\}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\>\prod_{j=1}^{M}\left[P(Y_{ij}=1\mid\theta_{i},a_{j},b_{j})^{\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}}\right]\cdot\left[1-P(Y_{ij}=1\mid\theta_{i},a_{j},b_{j}))^{1-\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}}\right]

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\{\theta_{i}\},\{a_{j}\},\{b_{j}\}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\>\prod_{j=1}^{M}\left[\frac{1}{1+e^{-a_{j}(\theta_{i}-b_{j})}})\right]^{\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}}\cdot\left[1-\left[\frac{1}{1+e^{-a_{j}(\theta_{i}-b_{j})}})\right]^{1-\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}}\right]

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\{\theta_{i}\},\{a_{j}\},\{b_{j}\}}\,\log\>\prod_{i=1}^{N}\>\prod_{j=1}^{M}\left[\frac{1}{1+e^{-a_{j}(\theta_{i}-b_{j})}})\right]^{\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}}\cdot\left[1-\left[\frac{1}{1+e^{-a_{j}(\theta_{i}-b_{j})}})\right]^{1-\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}}\right]

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\{\theta_{i}\},\{a_{j}\},\{b_{j}\}}\,{\sum}_{i=1}^{N}\;{\sum}_{j=1}^{M}\;{\mbox{\bf y}_{ij}}\cdot\log\>\left[\frac{1}{1+e^{-a_{j}(\theta_{i}-b_{j})}})\right]+(1-\mbox{\bf y}_{ij})\cdot\log\>\left[1-\left[\frac{1}{1+e^{-a_{j}(\theta_{i}-b_{j})}})\right]\right]

where we write
Y_{ij}
to denote the random variable that records
whether respondent
i
answers item
j
correctly, with
Y_{ij}=1
for a correct
response and
Y_{ij}=0
for an incorrect one.
We fix the latent scale of
\theta
by requiring
\theta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, i.e., that
\theta
is normally
distributed with a 0 mean and standard deviation 1; and we also impose
a_{j}>0
for all
j=1,\ldots,M
.
We describe our algorithm for estimating these parameters in Section
B.4
.
(Note that we introduce estimation here via the unregularized joint likelihood for conceptual clarity.
In practice, as described in Section
B.4
,
the models reported in this appendix are fit by joint MAP estimation with Gaussian priors,
which regularizes the item and respondent
parameters and improves stability when the number of items is large relative to the number of respondents.)

Once these parameters are estimated, we have built the desired model (
19
): we can plug in
an arbitrary
\theta
and compute the associated probability. The main use of such a model, however, is
not prediction (as is usually the case in ML applications), but rather explanation (
e.g.
, structured
explanations of which items are hard/easy, which discriminate, where each model lies on the ability scale,
and so on).

Note that the product on the first identity of the above chain
is justified only under an independence assumption:
once we condition on the latent trait, item responses should be independent. Informally, this means that
the only reason why two items might co-vary is that stronger respondents do better on both.
This is a fundamental assumption of IRT. Violations are common when items are paraphrases (or duplicates),
share context, depend on the same passage, or reward the same narrow trick.
One might think that this is not a concern for stateless language models, but the
issue is not about inter-item memory. The issue is that even after conditioning on latent ability,
success on item
j_{1}
might still tell us something about success on
j_{2}
, because there may be
additional shared factor(s) on which both items depend. That said, if interpreted strictly
the condition would be impossible to satisfy,
108
108

108

Consider two very different
items on a math test. While they may be testing different math skills and content, both may
depend on the ability to compute binomial roots or apply the Pythagorean theorem, both of which
have pervasive uses in mathematics.

so in practice it is usually understood as an idealization and its satisfaction
is considered a matter of degree.

Some tasks in
ProofGrid
appear to be more at
odds with the fundamental assumptions of IRT.
PL3-PW
performance, in particular,
has a nontrivial dependence on problem type.
This threatens conditional independence because many items from the same problem family are not just
“another draw of the same skill.” They often share the same hidden structure,
so a model that has the right trick for one item will tend to do well on related items,
and a model that lacks that trick will tend to fail on many of them.
Unidimensionality is also suspect here, since different problem families seem to
demand different types of competence (the ability to perform simple forward inference
vs counting arguments
etc.
). There may still be one
dominant
latent factor at play, namely
proof-writing ability, but it is an empirical question whether that
factor is strong enough to explain most of the covariance among item responses.
In
PL3-PC
, by contrast, problem families play a much less prominent causal role.
The model there is given a list of mini-SAT steps to verify. As long as these steps
are roughly of the same size and structure across two problem families, then the identities
of these families should not matter much. Proof length and formula size are
more important factors (and explain why
De Bruijn
proofs, for example, as so hard to verify).
We build IRT models for most
ProofGrid
tasks, except that in
PL3-PW
we build
different models for different problem families.
In this appendix we present IRT models for
PL1-PM
,
PL3-PC
,

PL4-PW
, and subsets of
PL3-PW
(see Section
B.5
—Section
B.8
).

B.3
Introducing Normalized Discrimination Scores

Our objective in this section is to introduce and motivate a single quantity that measures how strongly
discriminating a task is in a given band of ability. The quantity should be normalized to the
unit interval, with values close to 0 indicating very poor discriminating power and values close
to 1 indicating very high discriminating power. We base our development on the notion of Fisher
information
[
55
]
, which we discuss below in detail.

Figure 50:
Fisher information of a task item as a function of different
a
(discrimination) values.

For an item
j
with discrimination
a_{j}>0
and difficulty
b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
, equation (
19
)
specifies the probability that a language model of latent ability
\theta
solves the
item as

P_{j}(\theta)=\sigma(a_{j}(\theta-b_{j}))=\frac{1}{1+e^{-a_{j}(\theta-b_{j})}}\mbox{\raisebox{1.4457pt}{\mbox{\mbox{\hskip 0.57784pt}.}}}

(20)

The
Fisher item information
at ability
\theta
is defined as:

I_{j}(\theta)\;=\;a_{j}^{2}\,P_{j}(\theta)\,\bigl(1-P_{j}(\theta)\bigr).

(21)

To understand the intuition behind this quantity, consider the algebraic behavior of the expression

a_{j}^{2}\,P_{j}(\theta)\,\bigl(1-P_{j}(\theta)\bigr)
. First, the bigger the item’s discrimination,
a_{j}
, the bigger
the
I_{j}(\theta)
,
i.e.
, the higher the information conveyed by the item, and the growth is quadratic: doubling

a_{j}
quadruples
I_{j}(\theta)
, so this is a strong effect. The second and more important term is the product

P_{j}(\theta)\,\bigl(1-P_{j}(\theta)\bigr)
(which, as we note below, is really the variance of a Bernoulli random variable).
When
P_{j}(\theta)
is low, i.e., when a model of ability
\theta
is unlikely
to solve the item, this product is low. Likewise, the product is low when
P_{j}(\theta)
is high. The product is maximized
at 0.5. When we fix a value for
a
, the quantity
a^{2}\,p\,(1-p)
can be plotted as a function of
p
, and
Figure
50
does this for a range of
a
values. We thus see that:

•

if the item is very likely to be failed by a model of ability
\theta
, the information is low;

•

ff the item is very likely to be solved by a model of ability
\theta
, the information is also low; and

•

the information is highest when the response is most uncertain, i.e., when the response could be correct or incorrect
with roughly equal probability.

This holds for any value of
\alpha
, as shown in Figure
50
, although higher
a
values result in much higher
information for any fixed
P_{j}(\theta)
.

For a more statistical perspective: If we define
X^{\theta}_{j}
as a Bernoulli variable representing a hypothetical binary response
to item
j
from a respondent of ability
\theta
, then the success-probability parameter of that variable is precisely
P_{j}(\theta)
, i.e.,

X^{\theta}_{j}\sim\mbox{Bernoulli}(P_{j}(\theta))
, and its variance is
P_{j}(\theta)\cdot(1-P_{j}(\theta))
.
When
P_{j}(\theta)
is low or high, that variance is small because most responses will be either incorrect or correct, respectively.
The variance increases only when the probability enters middle-ground territory, in which case we can expect more diversity in the
correctness of the responses. So the variance term
P_{j}(\theta)\cdot(1-P_{j}(\theta))
measures the amount of uncertainty
regarding responses to item
j
from test takers of ability
\theta
, and Fisher information is exactly this
response variability weighted by the square of the item’s discrimination (
a_{j}
).
For a more analytical perspective, observe that the slope of the ICC of item
j
at a
given
\theta
is the derivative of (
20
) with respect to

\theta
, which is precisely
a_{j}\,P_{j}(\theta)\,\bigl(1-P_{j}(\theta)\bigr)
.
Therefore, in a 2PL model like this one,
I_{j}(\theta)=a_{j}\,\mbox{\mbox{$P_{j}^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}$}}(\theta)
and
item information is proportional to the local slope of the ICC, with
a_{j}
as the proportionality factor.

It’s worth stressing that this information quantity is not an intrinsic property of an item
j
; it depends crucially on the given
\theta
.
One and the same item could be highly discriminating (informative) for one ability level and quite ineffective for another ability level.
As a concrete example, consider item 2 in
PL2-PW
, with
a_{2}=1.53
and
b_{2}=1.19
. For
\theta=1.2
(roughly the ability of Gemini 3),
we have
P_{2}(1.2)=0.504
and thus
1-P_{2}(1.2)=0.496
, and hence

I_{2}(\theta=1.2)=a_{2}^{2}\,P_{2}(1.2)(1-P_{2}(1.2))=2.3409\cdot 0.504\cdot 0.496=0.58.

However, the information of the same item at the level of
\theta=-1.0
, roughly the ability of Mistral Magistral and Grok-3, is

I_{2}(-1.0)=a_{2}^{2}\,P_{2}(-1.0)(1-P_{2}(-1.0))=2.3409\cdot 0.034\cdot 0.966=0.07.

This represents an 88% relative drop in information content between these two ability levels.

In the 2PL models that we use here, the Fisher information
I_{j}(\theta)
of item
j
is always maximized
at
\theta=b_{j}
, since that is precisely the point where
P_{j}(\theta)=0.5
and

a_{j}^{2}\,P_{j}(\theta)\,(1-P_{j}(\theta))
attains the largest possible value.

Next, given a task
T
, we define the
task information
of
T
at an ability
\theta
as the sum of
I_{j}(\theta)
across all items
j
in
T
:

I_{T}(\theta)\;=\;\sum_{j=1}^{\mbox{$|T|$}}I_{j}(\theta),

(22)

where
|T|
is the number of items in task
T
(only counting
items contributing to the fit after filtering uninformative all-correct/all-incorrect columns).

In the general theory of Fisher information, the quantity represents how much a random observation
X
tells us about
an unknown parameter
\theta
, given a probability model
f(x\mid\theta)
. Its units are
1/(\mbox{units of}\>\theta^{2})
,
so if
\theta
is measured in meters, for example, Fisher information would be measured in
1/m^{2}
units.
In the current IRT context, however,
\theta
is a latent standardized ability scale, not a physical quantity
with real-world units. As a result, item information often feels unitless, and raw numbers like 0.4, 1.2 or 2.8
are hard to interpret. The rest of this section aims to turn this scale-dependent quantity that is hard to interpret
in isolation into a [0,1]-normalized quantity representing a relative efficiency score against a clearly defined upper bound.

Restricting to a target ability band.

As defined by (
22
), the Fisher information of a task
T
is local in
\theta
:
I_{T}(\theta)

tells us how discriminating the task is at one specific ability level, not overall.
In practice, we typically care about an entire ability region (say mid-tier models or elite models),
i.e., an interval
\Theta=[\theta_{1},\theta_{2}]
. Therefore, we need to turn the whole curve

I_{T}(\theta)
between
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
into a single summary number for that region.
The natural way to do that is to average the task information over the chosen band.
Conceptually, this is done with an integral, while numerically we implement the averaging over a fine uniform
grid
\{\theta^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{G}\subset\Theta
:

\overline{I}_{T}(\Theta)\;=\;\frac{1}{|\Theta|}\int_{\theta_{1}}^{\theta_{2}}I_{T}(\theta)\,d\theta\;\approx\;\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i=1}^{G}I_{T}(\theta^{(i)}),

(23)

where
\theta^{(i)}
denotes the
i^{\mbox{th}}
point in a numerical grid of size
G
laid across
\Theta
.
Thus,
\overline{I}_{T}(\Theta)
is just the average height of the task-information curve over the interval of interest.
If the curve is high throughout the band, the task discriminates well there.
If it is high only in a narrow spike but low elsewhere, the average will reflect that.
So this is just getting a band-specific single-number summary out of a location-dependent quantity,
thereby answering the following question: “On average, how much local discriminating power
does this task have over the ability range we care about?”
Intuitively, if many items
j
have their ICC “humps”
b_{j}
inside
\Theta
or at least near it,
then
\overline{I}_{T}(\Theta)
will be high, since these are the points that maximize
I_{j}
.
But
\overline{I}_{T}(\Theta)
will be low if many items have their information concentrated
far from
\Theta
. In that case there will be slim pickings for the
integral (
23
) to sum over.

Mean information per item.

To make the magnitude comparable across tasks of different sizes, we define
the
mean information per item over
\Theta
of a task
T
by dividing by the number of items in
T
:

\mbox{MI\/}_{T}(\Theta)\;=\;\frac{\overline{I}_{T}(\Theta)}{\mbox{$|T|$}}\mbox{\raisebox{1.4457pt}{\mbox{\mbox{\hskip 0.57784pt}.}}}

This removes the trivial advantage of longer tasks (otherwise, a 400-item task
will usually have much larger total information than a 50-item task, even if its
items are not individually better).

This quantity does not solve the deeper interpretability problem.
A value like 0.8 for
\mbox{MI\/}_{T}(\Theta)
still lacks a clean meaning.
It simply gives us a size-adjusted quantity, not yet a naturally interpretable one.
By itself, it is mainly useful for comparisons. If, for a given band
\Theta
,
this quantity is 1.2 for task
T_{1}
and 0.8 for task
T_{2}
, we can say
that task
T_{1}
has more average discrimination per item in
\Theta

than task
T_{2}
. This is a valid comparative statement, even if the raw
numbers have no intuitive absolute benchmark.

Normalized band-targeting score in
[0,1]

As already mentioned, raw Fisher information is useful but its scale is hard to
interpret directly. To get a clearer score, we compare the task’s band-averaged
information
\bar{I}_{T}(\Theta)
to an idealized upper bound defined over
\Theta
.

We define this idealized upper bound as follows.
For each item
j
, we keep its estimated discrimination
a_{j}
fixed and ask for
the largest band-averaged information that could be obtained by an imaginary
item with that same discrimination strength, but with difficulty chosen
to best match the target band. This gives:
109
109

109

The maximization operation
in (
24
) ranges over all
b\in\mbox{$\mathbb{R}$}
, so one may wonder
if it is well-founded. If we define

F_{j}(b,\Theta)
as
(1/|\Theta|)\int_{\theta_{1}}^{\theta_{2}}a_{j}^{2}\,\sigma\!(a_{j}(\theta-b))(1-\sigma\!(a_{j}(\theta-b)))\,d\theta
,
then the right-hand side of (
24
) is maximizing
F_{j}(b,\Theta)
over all
b\in\mbox{$\mathbb{R}$}
.
In fact, for any finite
\theta_{2}>\theta_{1}
and fixed
a_{j}>0
, the integral
simplifies to a unary function of
b
, namely
g(b)=a_{j}[\sigma(a_{j}(\theta_{2}-b))-\sigma(a_{j}(\theta_{1}-b))]

(this follows from elementary calculus, given that

\frac{d}{d\theta}\sigma(a_{j}(\theta-b))=a_{j}\sigma(a_{j}(\theta-b))(1-\sigma(a_{j}(\theta-b)))
),
so that
F_{j}(b,\Theta)=(1/|\Theta|)g(b)
.
We first note that this quantity is bounded from above, since for all
x\in\mbox{$\mathbb{R}$}
we have

0\leq\sigma(x)(1-\sigma(x))\leq 1/4
, and hence for every
b\in\mbox{$\mathbb{R}$}
,

0\leq F_{j}(b,\Theta)\leq a_{j}^{2}/4
, where
a_{j}
is fixed. So this optimization operation has a finite hard ceiling.
The other question is whether the maximum is actually attained at some finite
b
, and a simple continuity argument
will show that it is. Essentially:
F_{j}(b,\Theta)
is a continuous function of
b
, it is bounded (as we just showed),
and it vanishes at both tails (because, as
b
tends to either
-\infty
or
+\infty
, the
quantity
F_{j}(b,\Theta)
tends to 0). This shows that the maximization operation is well defined (and a little
more algebra will show that, assuming
\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}
, a maximum value must occur at the midpoint
(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})/2
).

\bar{I}_{j}^{*}(\Theta)=\max_{b\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{|\Theta|}\int_{\theta_{1}}^{\theta_{2}}I_{j}(\theta)\,d\theta=\max_{b\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{|\Theta|}\int_{\theta_{1}}^{\theta_{2}}a_{j}^{2}\,\sigma\!\bigl(a_{j}(\theta-b)\bigr)\Bigl(1-\sigma\!\bigl(a_{j}(\theta-b)\bigr)\Bigr)\,d\theta\mbox{\raisebox{1.4457pt}{\mbox{\mbox{\hskip 0.57784pt}.}}}

(24)

Summing over items gives the band’s attainable upper bound:

\mbox{UB\/}_{T}(\Theta)=\sum_{j=1}^{\mbox{$|T|$}}\bar{I}_{j}^{*}(\Theta).

We finally define the normalized discrimination score over a band
\Theta
as follows:

\mbox{$\mathcal{S}$}_{T}(\Theta)=\frac{\bar{I}_{T}(\Theta)}{\mbox{UB\/}_{T}(\Theta)}\in[0,1].

(25)

This can be read as follows: among all same-length comparison tasks with
the same per-item discrimination profile
(a_{1},\dots,a_{{\mbox{$|T|$}}})
, but with item
difficulties chosen to fit this band perfectly, what fraction
of the maximum attainable band-specific information is the actual task
T
achieving?

What does “fitting this band perfectly” mean? It means that the difficulty
b_{j}
of each
item
j
sits right at the midpoint of
\Theta
. (As explained in footnote
109
,
the maximum value specified by
24
is always attained exactly at
(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})/2
.)
So the normalized band-targeting score
answers the following question: how informative is
T
on
\Theta
compared with the unique
hypothetical task
T^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
obtained from
T
by keeping each item’s sharpness
a_{j}
fixed
but setting each item’s difficulty
b_{j}
exactly to the midpoint of
\Theta
?
Task
T^{\raisebox{0.72229pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{\mbox{$\mbox{\hskip 1.01231pt}\prime$}}}}
is a mathematical fiction, of course: every single item in it has the exact same difficulty
b_{j}
,
namely, the midpoint of
\Theta
, so all ICC curves are centered at the exact same place and the only
thing distinguishing them is their sharpness (the
a_{j}
values).
This is highly implausible, but
\mbox{UB\/}_{T}(\Theta)
is not trying to describe a realistic
benchmark design. It is a deliberately artificial upper bound for one specific band.
If the only question is “how much information could these same items deliver on this one target band,
if one were allowed to place their difficulties optimally for that band?”, then collapsing all
b_{j}

values to the midpoint is exactly the maximizing move (in the 2PL setting).

\mbox{$\mathcal{S}$}_{T}(\Theta)
values close to 1 mean that
T
is very well targeted to the ability band represented by
\Theta
.
A lower value suggests that, even if the items in
T
are reasonably sharp, their difficulties
are not as well aligned to
\Theta
as they could be in theory. However, this should not be
over-interpreted:
\mbox{$\mathcal{S}$}_{T}(\Theta)
is a fraction whose denominator sets an exceedingly high bar
(a very stringent idealized upper bound), so relatively moderate values may already
indicate strong targeting for practical purposes.

Finally,
\mbox{$\mathcal{S}$}_{T}(\Theta)
clearly depends on
\Theta
, so it is not a generic, band-independent measure
of task informativeness. Moreover, its interpretation is most useful for bands of substantive interest.
In one direction, if
\Theta
is very wide then high values become easier to obtain almost by construction.
For example, if
\Theta
is wide enough to cover essentially the entire
ability range for which the task was designed, then
\mbox{$\mathcal{S}$}_{T}(\Theta)
will often be
high because many items already have their difficulty levels somewhere inside that
broad band, so moving them all to the midpoint does not improve things much.
At the other extreme, if
\Theta
is very narrow then a high
\mbox{$\mathcal{S}$}_{T}(\Theta)
is
generally unrealistic, unless the task is unusually concentrated on that narrow slice
of ability. In that case the score is comparing the real task to an idealized version in
which every item is perfectly centered on that tiny interval, which is a very demanding
standard and one that a broadly useful task will usually not meet.
The measure is most useful for substantively meaningful ability ranges, such as mid-tier,
top-tier, or lower-tier models, or more systematically defined bands such as quartiles
or quantiles.

B.4
Fitting a 2PL IRT Model by Alternating MAP–MLE (EM–style) Updates

In standard psychometric uses of IRT, the number of respondents
N
is typically much
larger than the number of test items
M
. In many recent AI benchmarks, including
ProofGrid
,
the situation is reversed, as we often have
M\gg N
.
This can introduce certain technical complications, because we have few data points
from which to infer parameter values; with
N=24
in a
ProofGrid
task with
M
items,
we have to estimate 2 parameters (
a_{j}
and
b_{j}
) for each
j=1,\ldots,M

from only 24 binary responses. In this setting, item parameters can become noisy
(discrimination parameters, in particular, can be especially fragile). In addition,
estimation in this setting can be subject to separation pathologies and overfitting.

Here we mitigate the data asymmetry by fitting 2PL models not by gradient ascent
but by using an alternating MAP/Newton procedure that is well suited to the regime of
many items and relatively few respondents: Gaussian priors regularize the respondent and
item parameters, optimization in
\log a_{j}
enforces positive discriminations and mitigates
separation pathologies, and blockwise damped Newton updates yield stable low-dimensional
subproblems for items and respondents. This doesn’t quite eliminate the fundamental data asymmetry,
but it makes estimation substantially more stable and interpretable than unregularized joint
maximum likelihood in the same regime. The remainder of this section describes our fitting
algorithm in greater detail.

Goal and data.

We observe a binary response matrix
Y\in\{0,1\}^{N\times M}
, where
Y_{ij}=1
if
model (respondent)
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
solves item
j\in\{1,\dots,M\}
and
0
otherwise.
The 2-parameter logistic (2PL) model posits for each item
j
a discrimination
a_{j}>0

and a difficulty
b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
, and for each model
i
a latent ability
\theta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,

P(Y_{ij}=1\mid\theta_{i},a_{j},b_{j})=\sigma\!\big(a_{j}(\theta_{i}-b_{j})\big)\quad\text{with}\quad\sigma(z)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-z}}.

Objective.

The algorithm maximizes a regularized (MAP) joint log-posterior over all parameters,

\mathcal{L}(\theta,a,b)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Big[\,Y_{ij}\log P_{ij}+(1-Y_{ij})\log(1-P_{ij})\,\Big]-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{i}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\frac{(\log a_{j}-\mu_{a})^{2}}{\sigma_{a}^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\frac{b_{j}^{2}}{\sigma_{b}^{2}},

where
P_{ij}=\sigma(a_{j}(\theta_{i}-b_{j}))
,
\mu_{a}=0
, and
\sigma_{a},\sigma_{b}>0
are prior
scales. The Gaussian priors
\,\theta_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
\log a_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{a},\sigma_{a}^{2})
,

b_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{b}^{2})
stabilize estimation (preventing infinite slopes from separation) and enforce
a_{j}>0
by optimizing in
\log a_{j}
.

Why an EM–style algorithm?

The likelihood couples language models and items:
P_{ij}
depends on both
\theta_{i}
and
(a_{j},b_{j})
.
Direct Newton on
all
parameters is brittle on sparse/balanced items and under separation.
Instead, we use an alternating conditional maximization strategy (a close cousin of
ECM/EM): holding
\theta
fixed we maximize over
(\log a,b)
item-by-item; then holding
(\log a,b)
fixed we maximize over
\theta
model-by-model. Each conditional subproblem is a tiny, well-behaved logistic regression, so Newton steps are reliable; the alternation is a coordinate-ascent on
\mathcal{L}
.

Preprocessing and initialization.

We drop non-informative columns (items with all
0
or all
1
).
We initialize
\theta_{i}
from smoothed logits of per-model accuracy and standardize to mean 0, variance 1. We draw
\log a_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,0.25^{2})
and
b_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
We also enforce a practical box constraint
a_{j}\in[a_{\min},a_{\max}]
after each update.

Item (M-step for items) updates.

Fix
\theta
. For each item
j
we maximize the
itemwise
objective

\ell_{j}(\log a_{j},b_{j})\;=\;\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big[\,Y_{ij}\log P_{ij}+(1-Y_{ij})\log(1-P_{ij})\,\Big]-\frac{(\log a_{j}-\mu_{a})^{2}}{2\sigma_{a}^{2}}-\frac{b_{j}^{2}}{2\sigma_{b}^{2}},

where now
P_{ij}=\sigma\!\big(e^{\log a_{j}}(\theta_{i}-b_{j})\big)
.
We use a single (damped) Newton–Raphson step on the
2\times 2
Hessian for

(\log a_{j},b_{j})
, with gradients/Hessians (letting
a_{j}=e^{\log a_{j}}
,

t_{ij}=\theta_{i}-b_{j}
,
p_{ij}=P_{ij}
,
w_{ij}=p_{ij}(1-p_{ij})
):

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ell_{j}}{\partial\log a_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}(Y_{ij}-p_{ij})\,a_{j}t_{ij}\;-\;\frac{\log a_{j}-\mu_{a}}{\sigma_{a}^{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ell_{j}}{\partial b_{j}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}(Y_{ij}-p_{ij})\,a_{j}\;-\;\frac{b_{j}}{\sigma_{b}^{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{j}}{\partial(\log a_{j})^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}w_{ij}\,(a_{j}t_{ij})^{2}\;-\;\frac{1}{\sigma_{a}^{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{j}}{\partial b_{j}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}w_{ij}\,a_{j}^{2}\;-\;\frac{1}{\sigma_{b}^{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\ell_{j}}{\partial\log a_{j}\,\partial b_{j}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}w_{ij}\,(a_{j}t_{ij})\,(-a_{j}).

We solve the
2\times 2
linear system for the Newton step, damp it by a factor

\in(0,1]
, then clip
a_{j}
into
[a_{\min},a_{\max}]
(by re-adjusting
\log a_{j}
).
This is done independently for each item
j
.

Model (M-step for models) updates.

Fix
(\log a,b)
. For each language model
i
we maximize

\ell_{i}(\theta_{i})\;=\;\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Big[\,Y_{ij}\log P_{ij}+(1-Y_{ij})\log(1-P_{ij})\,\Big]-\frac{1}{2}\theta_{i}^{2},\quad P_{ij}=\sigma\!\big(a_{j}(\theta_{i}-b_{j})\big),

by one (damped) Newton step using

\frac{d\ell_{i}}{d\theta_{i}}=\sum_{j}(Y_{ij}-P_{ij})\,a_{j}\;-\;\theta_{i},\qquad\frac{d^{2}\ell_{i}}{d\theta_{i}^{2}}=-\sum_{j}a_{j}^{2}\,P_{ij}(1-P_{ij})\;-\;1.

Again, the update is scalar per model and therefore stable.

Identifiability rescaling.

The 2PL model is not identifiable up to linear transforms of the latent scale:

(\theta,a,b)\mapsto\big((\theta-\mu)/s,\;sa,\;(b-\mu)/s\big)
yields the same probabilities.
After each iteration we fix the scale by standardizing
\theta
to mean 0 and variance 1:

\theta_{i}\leftarrow\frac{\theta_{i}-\bar{\theta}}{s},\qquad b_{j}\leftarrow\frac{b_{j}-\bar{\theta}}{s},\qquad\log a_{j}\leftarrow\log a_{j}+\log s,

where
\bar{\theta}
and
s
are the current mean and standard deviation of
\theta
.
(This keeps
a_{j}(\theta_{i}-b_{j})
invariant, hence leaves
P_{ij}
unchanged.)

Monotonicity, damping, and clipping.

The Newton steps are
damped
to avoid overshooting on items with near-separation,
and
a_{j}
is clipped to
[a_{\min},a_{\max}]
to prevent extreme slopes.
The Gaussian priors play the role of ridge regularizers on
\theta
,
\log a
, and
b
,
ensuring negative-definite Hessians and preventing degeneracy.

Convergence check.

We track the relative change of the stacked parameter vector

\big(\theta^{\top},\log a^{\top},b^{\top}\big)^{\top}
and stop when the
\ell_{2}
change
falls below a tolerance. Because each block step increases a local quadratic
approximation of
\mathcal{L}
, the procedure behaves like a block–Newton EM/ECM and
converges rapidly in practice.

Relation to EM / MMLE.

Classical marginal-maximum-likelihood (MMLE) IRT treats
\theta
as latent
random

effects, integrates them out in an E-step (via quadrature), and maximizes the marginal
likelihood over
(a,b)
in an M-step. Our implementation instead performs
joint

MAP estimation (sometimes called MAP–JML): we maximize over both
\theta
and
(a,b)
,
alternating between them. This is an EM-style coordinate-ascent on the
posterior, with the Gaussian priors acting like the penalty that stabilizes each block.
Both approaches are standard; MAP–JML is simple, fast, and well-suited when
M
is large
and
N
is modest.

Algorithm summary

1.

Build
Y
from the input dictionary; optionally drop all-0/all-1 items.

2.

Initialize
\theta
from logits of accuracy; initialize
\log a
,
b
from Gaussians.

3.

Repeat until convergence:

(a)

(
Item step
) For each
j
: take one damped Newton step on
(\log a_{j},b_{j})

for
\ell_{j}
, then clip
a_{j}
.

(b)

(
Language model step
) For each
i
: take one damped Newton step on
\theta_{i}
for

\ell_{i}
.

(c)

(
Rescale
) Standardize
\theta
to mean 0, var 1 and adjust
(\log a,b)
.

4.

Return dictionaries for
\theta
,
a
, and
b
(in original item indices).

Why this works well in practice.

Each subproblem is low-dimensional with closed-form gradients/Hessians, so one Newton step
per block already yields large ascent. Regularization tames separation and keeps slopes
finite; re-centering fixes the latent scale; and clipping prevents pathological updates.
Empirically, with hundreds of items and a few dozen models, this EM-style
alternating MAP procedure converges quickly and produces stable, interpretable estimates
of abilities
\theta_{i}
, item difficulties
b_{j}
, and discriminations
a_{j}
suitable
for our downstream analyses (ICCs, Wright maps, information curves, and the normalized discrimination
scores introduced in Section
B.3
).

gemini-3.1-pro-preview

A line like this one:

means that items 48, 51,
\ldots
, 244, have difficulties
b_{j}
roughly around -0.87,
or more precisely, that their estimated difficulties
b_{j}
fall in the bin centered
around -0.87. This means models of ability
\theta\approx-0.87
(or more precisely, models
whose estimated abilities fall into that same bin), such as DeepSeek-V3 and Grok-3,
are just as likely to solve these problems as they are to fail them.

The shape of the Wright map is roughly the discrete counterpart of the task-information curve,
rotated clockwise by 90 degrees. Where the items bunch up on the Wright map, the information curve tends to be high. The peak near 0 means
that many items sit there, so the task becomes especially sensitive in that region.
This obviously resembles a normal distribution centered at 0, but this is not because
task information curves are inherently normal-shaped, nor is it because
\theta
is standardized
to have mean 0 and variance 1. Several tasks in
ProofGrid
have task-information curves that are skewed
or irregular; see the task-information curve of
PL4-PW
as an example, Fig.
52
.
The near-normal appearance observed in Fig.
51

tells us something specific about the
PL1-PM
task items: their difficulties are concentrated
around the middle of the ability scale, with increasingly fewer items as we move towards either tail end.
Again, this matches the Wright map: item density is heaviest around the central region and thins out toward both ends.
This means that the items of this task have difficulties that are nicely distributed on the
\theta
scale, and that the
task’s ability to distinguish models is also nicely distributed across that same central region,
i.e.
, the task
is most informative near the middle and its informativeness tapers off toward both ends.
So this is a particularly well-balanced task, with items that are spread broadly across the ability range,
instead of being piled up in one narrow hard or easy region. In simpler terms, this task has problems
for weaker, middle, and stronger models, not just for the very strong or weak models.

This is one of the few
ProofGrid
tasks that has good discriminating power in the lower half of the ability scale,
with
\mbox{$\mathcal{S}$}_{T}(-2,0.2)=0.63
.

|

282

We see that the task has items at many nearby difficulty levels across most of the respondent range,
with a particularly dense item mass from about
\theta\approx-0.45
up to
\theta\approx 0.49
,
plus additional coverage through 0.73 and 0.96.
The map is clearly strongest in the lower-mid to upper-mid region, and thinner in the extreme tails.
On the low end, there are only a couple of very easy items below
\approx-0.9
,
and on the high end there are only a handful of hard items above
\approx 1.2
, with two
ultra-hard items at 2.13 and 2.37 (items 290 and 282, the only 2
De Bruijn
problems that are not
failed by all models). So the task covers the middle region beautifully, but the very weakest
and very strongest models are measured with fewer anchor items. This means the task
should separate the broad population very well, as well as the mid-ability region,
while the extreme-tail ordering is less stable.

The task is also good at separating weaker models (below 0 ability),
as long as they are not too weak,
e.g.
, we have

\mbox{$\mathcal{S}$}_{T}(-0.5,0)=0.81
. For
very
weak models the discriminating power drops drastically,

e.g.
,
\mbox{$\mathcal{S}$}_{T}(-1.9,-0.6)=0.21
.
Finally, as the Wright map suggests,
PL3-PC
is exceptionally
good at separating mid-tier models,
e.g.
, for
\Theta=(-0.7,0.7)
we have
\mbox{$\mathcal{S}$}_{T}(\Theta)=0.93
.

Next, we analyze some tasks that are better at discriminating top-tier models.

B.7

PL3-PW

discriminating the proof writing abilities of top-tier models: Tseitin-formula problems
and relativized-pigeonhole problems.

Appendix C
Technical and Operational Details

This appendix summarizes the main operational details of the reported experiments: model identities,
API routes, prompt formats, decoding and reasoning settings, confidence intervals for our results,
batching policies, data generation, and evaluation procedures.
We do not include the prompts here, since they tend to be fairly long; they can be found on

ProofGrid
’s Github page
.

C.1
Data Generation for
\textsc{PL}_{1}

Data generation for
\textsc{PL}_{2}
,
\textsc{PL}_{3}
, and
\textsc{PL}_{4}
is already discussed in the paper.
For
\textsc{PL}_{1}
, premises and goals for all problems were generated randomly as ASTs (abstract syntax trees)
by choosing a logical operator as the root (or an atom for a leaf) and then recursively generating subtrees up to a maximum depth.
To minimize degenerate problems, we imposed the following constraints on
any premises
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
and goal
p
:

1.

all
n+1
formulas must be distinct;

2.

\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\}
must logically imply
p
;

3.

every premise
p_{i}
must be necessary, meaning that if we remove
p_{i}
, the goal
p
is no longer entailed by the remaining premises;

4.

the goal does not contain any atoms that do not occur in the premises; and

5.

the premises by themselves are logically consistent (as is the negation of the goal).

We used a SAT solver to enforce 2, 3, and 5.

We limit duplicate entries by normalizing a problem’s representation
as follows: sort the list of all premises, conjoin the resulting elements, form the conditional between that conjunction and the conclusion, and then universally quantify over all atoms in that conditional. We then identify two problems iff these universally quantified normal forms are alpha-equivalent (identical up to renaming of bound variables). Thus, for instance, the argument with
p_{1}=(A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}B)
,
p_{2}=(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C)
,
p_{3}=\neg C
,
and goal
\neg A
is represented by the QBF (Quantified Boolean Formula):

\forall\>A,B,C\mbox{$\ .\ $}(((A\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}B)\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}(B\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}C)\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg C)\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}\neg A)

and is thus considered identical to the problem with
p_{1}=(C\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}D)
,
p_{2}=\neg E
,
p_{3}=(D\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}E)
, and goal
\neg C
.

Terminological note: To improve readability, this paper often uses self-explanatory Latex-friendly
notation for formulas instead of the plain-ASCII notation actually used in the tasks,

e.g.
, writing
(A\mbox{$\>\wedge\>$}\neg B)
instead of
(A

&

~

B)
,
(p_{1}\mbox{$\>\Rightarrow\,$}p_{2})
instead
of
(
p_{1}

==>

p_{2}
)
,
(p_{1}\mbox{$\>\vee\>$}p_{2})
instead of
(
p_{1}

|

p_{2}
)
, and so on.

The Athena theorem prover that generated proofs for the
PL1-PC
problems uses a combination of backward and forward heuristics,
ordered according to the strategy in Section 4.1.14 of the textbook
Fundamental Proof Methods in Computer Science

[
3
]
.
The theorem prover is written as an Athena
method
, which is automatically instrumented so that a successful run produces a low-level

certificate
: a fully detailed proof comprising all applications of primitive inference rules that were made during
the run, as well as all conditional (sub)proofs and (sub)proofs by contradiction, properly scoped. This certificate is typically
a very long proof (many thousands of lines), because it includes all inferences
made even during paths of the search tree that were ultimately dead ends. These proofs are then simplified by an aggressive
proof-optimization algorithm
[
5
]
that removes detours and other inefficiencies
from natural-deduction proofs, radically simplifying their structure.

The deliberate corruption of a proportion of these proofs used interventions such as altering the logical form of
an argument to an inference rule (
e.g.
, applying conjunction elimination to a disjunction), or the number of arguments,
removing a step from a chain of subproofs, and so on.

C.2
Result Archives

We release result archives for all 15 tasks currently in
ProofGrid
, in the form of one Yaml file per task:

The released Yaml files contain data documenting our results for all these tasks:
problem descriptions and metadata, model identifiers, timestamps, and, for most runs,
raw model outputs, processed model outputs, as well as partial token-usage and/or intermediate
reasoning information.

Conceptually, a Yaml result file for task
T
is a list of
result dictionaries, one per problem/item in
T
. Each such dictionary has a numeric
index that serves as an ID, uniquely identifying that particular problem and associated result data
in the context of
T
. For example, if our IRT analysis talks about “item 28” in
PL2-PW
,
it means precisely that record
r
in the Yaml file for
PL2-PW
that has
r[’index’] == 28
.

In addition, each result record typically has keys like
problem
, describing the input problem,
metadata about the problem (
e.g.
, whether or not the input proof to be checked is in
fact correct), and a results dictionary mapping each of the 24 model identifiers
M
to
a corresponding result dictionary for
M
. That model-specific dictionary contains a host of information
about the response of
M
to the given problem: API call timestamps, the raw output that
M
generated,
the processed/evaluated output (as carried out by our pipeline), evaluation results and details,
and often a metadata key whose value indicates whether
M
’s response was ultimately counted as correct.

The
problem
field indicates the argument/sequent targeted by the relevant
proof

(the proof that was masked). The
conditionalized
flag indicates whether the problem was conditionalized (whether its
premises were imported into the conclusion). The
correctProof
and
complexProof
flags
are metadata about the starting
proof
: whether or not it solves the problem correctly and whether or not is has
any complex/nested syntax forms. The value of
maskedProof
is the main problem-specific input given to the models,
along with the general prompt for the task; it is obtained
by masking the starting
proof
. The full mask assignment that recovers
proof
from
maskedProof

is given by the value of the
masks
dictionary. Model results are provided under
llmMaskFillingResponses
,
a dictionary mapping our model identifiers to the full corresponding results. One such individual result dictionary,
for Claude Opus 4, appears between lines 23 and 57 in the above listing (with truncated values for some of the keys).
The dictionary contains timestamps (lines 23/24), the model’s response (lines 25–28), the unmasked proof obtained
by carrying out the assignments specified in the model’s response (lines 29–32); a final
correctMasks
field
indicating whether the model’s response was ultimately correct; and detailed evaluation results in the dictionary

instrumentedCheckerResult
. The main payload of the latter is the
result
key, which can take the 3 values
correct
,

incorrect
, and (very rarely)
unknown
. If the result is
incorrect
, error details provided by the instrumented
proof checked are given, including all syntax and/or semantic repairs.

Result files for other tasks are similarly structured.

C.3
Result Timeline and Interaction Protocol

The results were generated from April 2025 through March 2026; the distribution of API calls is shown in
Fig.
53
.

Figure 53:
API call timeline.

All systems were queried as plain language models; we did not use external
tools, retrieval, web search, or agent tooling. The standard interaction pattern
was the single-call paradigm used by most classic benchmarks, without additional dialog turns.
The reported outputs were not obtained by techniques like self-consistency, majority voting, or
pass@
k
aggregation.

).

C.6
Wilson Confidence Intervals

The parenthetical numbers in our tables are 95% confidence intervals for each reported accuracy,
computed using the Wilson score interval for a binomial proportion. Concretely, for each model
we treat every task item as a binary outcome: either the model got it right or it did not.
Thus, if a model answers
k
of
n
items correctly, its observed accuracy is
\hat{p}=k/n
.
Our tables report this point estimate together with the uncertainty due to the finite sample size.
In the compact notation used in the tables, an entry such as
0.87\mbox{$\;(\pm\>0.02)$}
means that the
estimated accuracy is 0.87 and the Wilson interval has half-width 0.02, so the underlying
95% confidence interval is approximately
[0.85,0.89]
after rounding to two decimals.

We used Wilson intervals rather than the simpler normal approximation because it behaves better
for proportions near 0 or 1 and for moderate sample sizes. Our code uses the standard 95% Wilson
formula with
z=1.96
: it computes an adjusted center
\tilde{p}
and a half-width
h
, then forms
the interval
[\tilde{p}-h,\tilde{p}+h]
. The numbers shown in parentheses are this half-width
h
,
rounded to two decimal places; the code also computes the full lower and upper bounds explicitly.
These intervals should be read in the usual frequentist way: if we repeated the same evaluation
procedure many times on comparable samples, about 95% of the intervals constructed this way
would contain the model’s true accuracy on that task distribution.

Table 37:
Compact summary of the main decoding, reasoning, and token-budget policies.

OpenAI models

Models from the GPT-5 family and the o-series were queried through the Responses API in serial mode. Temperature were not set explicitly for these models.
Instead, we specified the values of reasoning parameters. GPT-5, GPT-5.1, GPT-5.2, GPT-5.4,
and the o-series models used high-effort settings.
GPT-5.2 was typically given an automatic extra-thinking budget of 64K tokens in tasks that expose this field.

For
gpt-4o
and
gpt-4.1
, we used the Chat Completions API. These models therefore inherited
our task-level temperature settings (reasoning parameters were inapplicable to these models), which
are 0.6 for generative tasks like proof writing and 0.01 for discriminative tasks like proof
checking.
111
111

111

While 0.6 might seem high for a reasoning task, we have empirically found that
it outperforms lower temperatures for proof writing. In general, proof checking is a low-entropy
judgment task, while proof writing is a high-entropy search task. Low temperature helps the former
by reducing noise, wile moderate temperature helps the latter by improving search over possible proof
trajectories.

Anthropic Claude models.

Serial Claude calls used the Anthropic Messages API. When explicit thinking was enabled,
temperature values were omitted and thinking was requested directly. For the Claude 4.5 family
in serial mode, we used a 12K thinking budget with a 21,333-token output cap. (These caps were
imposed by Anthropic, which only supports higher values for these parameters in streaming mode.)
Older Claude models were run without explicit thinking controls.
Serial Claude calls also recorded the provider stop reason when available.

Batch Claude calls used Anthropic Message Batches. We requested provider-side
thinking for the Claude 4.5 family and for explicit “extended thinking” aliases.
In practice, this meant substantially larger batch-time output budgets than in serial mode.
For example, the Opus 4.5 batch path used a provider-side 64K token cap together with a
32K internal thinking budget after reserving buffer space for the visible answer,
whereas the Sonnet 4.5 batch path used a smaller fixed thinking budget.

Google Gemini models.

Our Gemini calls used Google’s
genai
client. We did not explicitly
set temperature, output-token limits, or reasoning controls, instead using provider defaults
(high by default).

DeepSeek models.

DeepSeek reasoner models were run through the provider’s reasoning-enabled path. When JSON
output was requested, the wrapper also requested JSON formatting and enabled the provider’s
explicit thinking mode. In both cases, the wrapper’s own defaults governed these calls rather
than our task-level temperatures.

xAI models.

xAI models were called through an API compatible with Chat Completions.
We specified temperature values and when JSON output was needed, requested JSON formatting.
We did not set reasoning-effort parameters or output-token caps.

OpenRouter-backed models.

For models routed through OpenRouter, we used two related but distinct paths.
In the default raw-HTTP path, we used the task-level temperature and requested JSON output
whenever the task required it, capped output at 32K tokens, and enabled OpenRouter’s
reasoning mode with
high
effort. The wrapper also used a 30-second connect timeout,
a 30-minute read timeout, and one additional transport-level retry in the case of whitespace-only
or apparently truncated HTTP bodies. In the client-library path, used only for
PL1-GF
,
we did not specify reasoning or token-cap settings.

C.8
Batching, Partial Results, and Failure Policy

In the reported experiments, the only models run in batch mode were Anthropic models.
These were run mostly through Anthropic Message Batches, with serial fill-ins used
in most tasks to complete missing items or rerun failures. Batched outputs were matched
back to problems through explicit custom identifiers rather than through positional order.

Malformed outputs were generally not discarded silently. Instead, we preserved them
either as raw outputs or through explicit flags such as
illFormattedProofOutput

or
illFormattedResponse
, or via task-specific parse-failure markers.
Proof-checking tasks also recorded explicit API-call failures.
These malformed or missing outputs remain visible in the released output files.

Our rerun policy was necessarily variable across models. Many models returned valid outputs
consistently and required little manual intervention. Others, especially some reasoning-oriented
models on difficult tasks, frequently exhausted their output budget, returned malformed answers,
or resulted in other API call failures. Grok-4 was especially difficult in this respect,
and some advanced GPT models also often declined to return an answer on the hardest problems.
For such models, we used a best-effort fill-in policy: malformed outputs and API-level
failures were rerun, typically at least three times and sometimes more.

If a problem instance did not yield any response (
e.g.
, because of a server-side API-call error),
that was not counted against the model (the data point was removed from the denominator), but
if an incomplete or ill-formed response was returned, it was counted as an answer.
When possible, invalid or partial results were either cleaned with postprocessing or
the API calls were repeated.

C.9
Evaluation

Performance is not computed directly from raw model outputs.
Each task has a deterministic evaluation pipeline, which forms an
important part of the experimental design. These are described in our
discussions of the individual tasks and released in full operational detail in
the Github code base.

C.10
Reasoning Metadata in the Released Result Files

Reasoning metadata is present with limited coverage.
Many GPT- and Claude-based runs preserve one or more of the following:

•

provider usage summaries;

•

extracted reasoning summaries;

•

stored thinking traces;

•

thinkingTokens
;

•

outputTokens
;

•

provider stop reasons.

However, these fields are not uniformly available across all providers and all tasks. They are common in several proof-checking runs and in subsets of the masking, gap-filling, and equational results, but they are less common for Gemini, xAI, and some OpenRouter-backed models.
